 culture, of which Yue opera is only one, but distinct, example. 
26


=== PDF PAGE 48 ===

Beijing Opera and the Male-Centered Opera Culture of the Qing Dynasty
In order to understand the historical signiﬁcance of the rise of this mod-
ern feminine opera, it is necessary ﬁrst to historicize its predecessor, Bei-
jing opera, and the male-centered opera culture it represented. Beijing opera
has been commonly understood as the standard Chinese opera, and its all-
male cast is considered typical of this peculiarly traditional cultural form.
Studies of Chinese opera in general and Beijing opera in particular have
often overlooked the fact that Beijing opera developed into an all-male the-
ater in order to serve a predominantly male audience only during the sec-
ond half of the Qing dynasty, and that its homo-social character represented
a rupture from the hetero-social opera culture of the seventeenth century.
The most popular opera form during the late Ming (1368–1644) and
early Qing (1644–1911) dynasties was a genre known as chuanqi, which lit-
erally means “romance,” “legend,” or “bizarre story.” Like some other liter-
ary forms of the time, chuanqi celebrated the cult of qing, meaning “feelings”
or “passion,” and featured romantic stories about talented scholars and faith-
ful beauties.
1 The conquering Qing state, however, deemed the late Ming
indulgence in sentimental love stories decadent and a cause of the fall of
the Ming house; it therefore imposed strict moral codes on public culture
by cracking down on what it considered “obscene theater” ( yinxi).
2 The
Qing state’s effort to purge the public morality of sexually provocative ele-
ments won the support of the scholar-ofﬁcial elite and local Confucian gen-
try, who shared the Manchu criticism of the late Ming cult of qing.
Emerging amid these circumstances, Qing opera culture assumed distinc-
tive masculine characteristics that set it apart from the heterosexual Ming
theaters that preceded it and the more feminized Republican opera culture
that followed it. 
The Qing state adopted a strict Confucian ideology that deﬁned
women’s proper place as within the home, while social customs discour-
aged women from entering public arenas.
3 Women were, by law and cus-
tom, largely excluded from public entertainment venues, such as theaters,
teahouses, and gambling halls, where opera was staged. Upper-class women
could view performances only inside their homes or in the homes of rela-
tives and friends, while lower-class women had access to shows only dur-
ing local festivals, lineage or religious ceremonies, and community
celebrations, when operas were performed in public, free of charge. Even
during these public events, women’s attendance was minimal. While house-
hold responsibilities and custom required wealthier women to stay at home
The Origins of Yue Opera    27

=== PDF PAGE 49 ===

(especially after dark), poorer women had to work long hours to earn a liv-
ing and hardly had time to remain for long at the few shows they could
attend; additionally, they were also bound by the social customs that dis-
couraged all women from appearing in public.
4 Women in general, there-
fore, rich and poor alike, had limited access to public opera shows and were
entirely excluded from performances in commercial venues.
5 Since they
were not signiﬁcant patrons or paying customers, they had little inﬂuence
in shaping this major form of entertainment. 
Moreover, as the Qing state considered women performers to be a kind
of prostitute and the source of sexually provocative performances, its sup-
pression of so-called obscene theaters went hand in hand with repeated
prohibition of women performers. The earliest such prohibition was issued
in 1671, the tenth year of the reign of the Kangxi emperor. Similar bans
appeared many more times during the Kangxi reign, as well as during the
Yongzheng and Qianlong reigns, and the last ban was issued in 1774, the
thirty-ninth year of the Qianlong reign.
6 In the countryside, female per-
formers were prohibited from participating in religious and lineage cere-
monies, the most popular occasions for staging opera. Owing to relentless
central and local government crackdowns, women performers generally dis-
appeared from public view after 1774, except for a small number who
appeared in the foreign settlements in Shanghai and Tianjin in the late nine-
teenth century.
7
In the absence of female performers, boy actors and female imperson-
ators came to occupy prominent roles in popular theater. The most famous
female impersonator was Wei Changsheng, a Sichuan native who played
young female roles in “Shaanxi clapper opera” (Shaanxi bangzi ). Wei made
his ﬁrst appearance in Beijing in 1779, and his beauty, skill, and unrestrained
eroticism immediately created a sensation. Wei and his cohorts made
Shaanxi clapper opera the most popular theater in the capital, until the court
expelled him from Beijing and disbanded his troupe in 1785.
8 Wei then
joined a Kun opera troupe and soon left Beijing for Yangzhou and the Jiang-
nan area (the Yangzi River delta surrounding Suzhou, Hangzhou, and Shang-
hai), while Shaanxi clapper opera eventually retreated to its home in the
northwest. In the 1790s, four pihuang opera troupes from Anhui,
9 who had
entered the capital for the celebration of the Qianlong emperor’s  eightieth
birthday, created a similar sensation with their leading female imperson-
ators, resulting in another crackdown on “obscene” opera in the capital by
the new Jiaqing emperor in 1798.
10 The opera troupes from Anhui even-
tually won ofﬁcial approval by shifting the focus of their performances from
28 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 50 ===

women’s to men’s roles and thereby managed to take root in Beijing. Over
the ensuing decades, as the Anhui troupes absorbed the inﬂuence of ofﬁ-
cial ideology and catered to the tastes of a capital-based political and intel-
lectual elite, they developed a new opera form known as Jingju, or opera
of the capital city of Beijing. 
Thus, by the nineteenth century, through state and elite intervention,
a new opera culture took shape. Not only did operatic representations of
women become the exclusive purview of men, as female roles were per-
formed by and for men, but female roles were further reduced to support-
ing positions, as love stories gave way to plays that accorded with Qing
ideology. In contrast to the Ming chuanqi genre, Qing opera typically told
grand narratives about the empire and the dynasty and featured emperors,
ministers, and generals as protagonists. The form of the performance grew
rigid and formulaic, as a strict system of role designs and highly formal-
ized postures developed for portraying stereotypical characters. In general,
male roles included laosheng (bearded males), for moralistic civil ofﬁcials;
wusheng (military males), for spirited generals; jing (known as hualian [lit.,
“painted faces”]), for simple-minded or mean ofﬁcials; and chou (clowns).
Typical female characters were serious young women known as qingyi (lit.,
“blue-clothed ladies”); outgoing young women, or huadan (lit., “colorful
women”), who were in most cases servants of the main female characters;
and old women, or laodan. 
The artistic transformation of Anhui opera into a capital-city form was
complete when it made imperial narratives the main theme of its per-
formances while establishing male roles as the leading roles. Soon there-
after, a cluster of leading actors rose from the status of lowly entertainers
to cultural celebrities who enjoyed imperial patronage and the support
of a sophisticated male audience composed of ofﬁcials, generals, imperial
students, courtiers, clerks, government runners, examination candidates,
and Manchu aristocrats.
11 A dozen or so famous actors—the most famous
of whom were Cheng Changgeng, Tan Xinpei, Yang Yuelou, Yang Xiaolou,
and Li Chunlai—personiﬁed ministers and generals and played out sto-
ries drawn from ofﬁcial as well as popular histories. Even a cursory exam-
ination of the plays developed during this period leaves no doubt that
they dealt mostly with historical battles and major events, as the follow-
ing titles indicate: The Battle of Chengdu (Zhan Chengdu), Borrowing Arrows
from the Enemy (Caochuan jie jian), Dingjun Mountain (Dingjun shan), and
Capturing and Releasing Cao Cao (Zhuofang Cao).
12 One of the most fre-
quently performed plays, Generals of the Yang Family(Yangjia jiang), tells the
The Origins of Yue Opera    29

=== PDF PAGE 51 ===

story of a family that sacriﬁced almost all its men to the Song dynasty’s
war against invading barbarians. In the latter half of the nineteenth cen-
tury, Beijing opera spread throughout the country with the support of a
national political and intellectual elite, from provincial capitals to local
market towns and villages, setting new standards of content and style for
local operas to follow. 
Major regional opera genres of the time, including clapper operas in the
north and luantan(southern variations of clapper operas) and pihuang operas
in the southern and central provinces, underwent similar transitions dur-
ing the nineteenth century. Although regional major operas maintained
their local color by singing tunes in local dialects, they shared a repertoire
of plays and acting styles with Beijing opera. Together, these major operas
and their patrons fashioned the male-dominant opera culture of the Qing
dynasty. 
Parallel to these major operas were folk operas active at the lower level
of society.
13 The term “minor operas” (xiaoxi) has been used loosely in opera
circles in China in order to distinguish the numerous folk operas from “major
operas” ( daxi), which encompassed more sophisticated and inﬂuential
national and regional genres such as Beijing opera and various clapper
operas.
14 Minor operas emerged in rural areas throughout China during
the nineteenth century; they were part of the local peasant economy and
offshoots of local peasant culture. The best-known genres include yangge
in Shaanxi and other parts in the north, “ﬂower-drum” ( huagu) in Hunan,
“ﬂower-lantern” (huadeng) in Yunnan, various “storytelling” (tanhuang) gen-
res in Zhejiang and Jiangsu, and “tea-picking” (caicha) among the Hakkas,
as well as boy-singers’ opera in Fujian, “lotus tone” (lianhua luozi) in Hebei
(predecessor of pingopera), and “little opera” (xiaogeban) in Shengxian, Zhe-
jiang (a forerunner of Yue opera).
15 Often started by poor peasants trying
to scrape together extra income during the agricultural off-season, minor
operas held low social status and were usually not allowed to be performed
at religious ceremonies or in ancestral halls. The peasant artists staged their
performances mainly in local gambling houses, low-end teahouses, and
sometimes in the homes of rich local families on celebratory occasions,
charging low fees so that they could stay in business in the poorer rural
areas. Indeed, many minor operas were known locally as “the beggar’s opera”
(taofan xi), as the peasant performers sang while begging for food and money
in the streets and in front of wealthy households. The performances were
rough around the edges: costumes, if they were worn at all, were shabby;
30 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===

lyrics were composed in local colloquial dialects; melodies were simple and
repetitive; acting was unpolished and bawdy.16
Minor operas also differed from major operas with respect to their reper-
toires, narrative focus, and style. The repertoires of minor operas were drawn
from a variety of sources, including popular religious scripts, folk songs
and tales, popular ballads and storytelling traditions, and accounts of the
daily lives of the peasantry. The main roles in minor operas were a young
man and a young woman who played out scholar-beauty love stories such
as Pearl Pagoda (Zhenzhu ta), or moments of local, real-life drama, as sug-
gested by such titles as Selling Charcoal (Mai qingtan) and Child Bride Returns
Home (Yangxifu hui niangjia).
17 Minor-opera plays were concerned with indi-
vidual fortunes, not the fate or vicissitudes of the empire, and told stories
about love and desire, loyalty and betrayal, and fortune and misery. Draw-
ing on folk religious motifs and local superstitions, such as retribution and
ghost stories, as well as such ofﬁcially endorsed moral themes as ﬁlial piety,
these stories delivered mixed messages and ambiguous moral lessons. Good
performances were both entertaining and full of folk wisdom. Although
not always clear-cut in practice, a general difference between minor and
major operas existed beyond doubt. 
Minor operas were part of the male-dominant opera culture of the time,
with all the ambiguities and contradictions that attended the local practice
of gender. In minor operas, as in major operas, all the performers were men.
Even within all-male companies, gender hierarchies were prevalent, as male
roles were higher in status than female roles. The bias against female roles
was closely related to local superstitions but had little to do with actual pro-
fessional ranks. A popular actor specializing in female roles, for example,
was often the leading actor in the company and earned the highest salary,
but he was still not allowed to sit on the dressing boxes backstage or walk
in front of actors playing male roles when the troupe hiked to the next site.
18
On the whole, the gender hierarchy was less rigid in minor-opera cul-
ture, which absorbed less ofﬁcial ideology and existed more as part of the
daily life of the countryside. As seen in Shengxian little opera, the content
of minor opera, which focused on everyday peasant life and scholar-beauty
love stories, was more appealing to rural women, while the performances
were more accessible. Because of their small size and light instruments,
minor-opera troupes could perform inside homes or in the open space in
front of a sponsor’s house, and neighbors and villagers could attend per-
formances for a token contribution. Such performances, unlike the much
The Origins of Yue Opera    31

=== PDF PAGE 53 ===

more elaborate events of “major-opera performance” ( chang daxi ), were
informal and required much less consideration of the status and propri-
ety of village women than did elaborate public performance of major
operas. 
Both historical records and oral histories point to periodic ofﬁcial pro-
hibitions of minor operas for their heterodox content and vulgar style, while
local elites viliﬁed them either out of ideological conviction or for personal
gain.
19 Authorities failed to eliminate minor opera, however, for it was part
of the social and economic structure of late Qing rural life. The perform-
ers often desperately needed whatever income they could scratch together
from their seasonal performances, and the local population needed enter-
tainment at the lowest possible cost. For the same reasons, however, minor
operas remained minor and relatively undeveloped during the nineteenth
century. Their audiences had little purchasing power, their performances
drew the wrath of ofﬁcials, and their styles were too local to attract an audi-
ence beyond regional colloquial boundaries. 
The Feminine T ransformation of Opera Culture during the Republican Era
Opera culture underwent a major transformation during the twentieth cen-
tury, as industrialization, urbanization, and revolution changed Chinese
society. The decline of major operas, the rise of minor operas, and a gen-
eral trend of feminization marked the century’s new opera culture. This
new opera culture came about ﬁrst and foremost by feminizing the oper-
atic body, which occurred when a critical mass of actresses and female
audience members entered the opera world. When the 1911 Republican
Revolution overthrew the imperial system and opened up Chinese society
to wide-ranging social and political changes, women’s liberation became
one of the most championed issues within a broader national discourse
about making China a strong and wealthy nation. The May Fourth New
Culture Movement that followed made women’s liberation a benchmark
of China’s social reform and cultural modernity and sought to popular-
ize its rhetoric among a nationwide reading population. Related issues,
such as free love and marriage, women’s education, women’s work and
economic independence, and women’s position in the family and society
all became heated topics in popular and intellectual publications. Women
were eager to take advantage of the opportunities these changing cir-
cumstances offered them. Upper-class women began assuming new roles
and responsibilities outside the home. Some joined revolutionary  orga -
32 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===

nizations, others devoted themselves to education, and young women from
wealthy families enrolled in new schools. Still others enjoyed newfound
freedoms by going to theaters. At the same time, working-class women
found in acting a means of earning a living. Gradually, a great number of
women became participants in the entertainment industry, as audience
members and performers. The presence of women eventually made oper-
atic explorations of women and women’s issues no longer the exclusive
purview of men. 
The increasing numbers of women operagoers brought about a shift in
emphasis from male to female roles in Beijing opera in the early Republi-
can period, as is evident in the emergence of the “four great female imper-
sonators” (sida mingdan): Mei Lanfang, Shang Xiaoyun, Cheng Yanqiu, and
Xun Huisheng. Qi Rushan, an intellectual and expert in European and Chi-
nese operas who was much involved in Mei Lanfang’s Beijing opera reforms,
reasoned that the success of any actor depended on his ability to attract
women spectators. Qi considered that an actor could become popular and
successful only if he appealed to women, and that his success would be
compromised if he appealed only to male patrons. Qi paraphrased popu-
lar sayings of the time to explain: 
Take a couple: if the husband wants to see a show but his wife is not inter-
ested, the wife will prevent the husband from going. If, however, the wife
wants to go but her husband does not, the wife will make her husband go
with her. In a family situation, a husband could go to the theater by himself
without bringing the children along. But if the wife wants to go, then she
would most likely bring her children with her. Among circles of friends, men
often go to the theater by themselves or with one other friend, but they rarely
treat their friends to opera shows. Women, on the other hand, like to gather
in groups and go together. Thus, if an actor can make one woman come, she
will bring many more with her to the theater. If an actor can make one man
interested, the man may not come at all if his wife is not interested. As the
saying goes: “A popular, good actor is one who can appeal to women.”
20
Lending support to Qi’s views, Mei Lanfang attributed his own popularity
to the growth of female audiences and testiﬁed to the profound impact of
female operagoers on Beijing opera:
In the past, neither night opera performances nor female audience members
were allowed in Beijing’s theaters. Social custom deemed the mixing of men
The Origins of Yue Opera    33

=== PDF PAGE 55 ===

and women in public injurious to public morality, as if opera were speciﬁ-
cally sung for men, and women did not have the right to enjoy this kind of
legitimate entertainment. This was indeed a stubbornness of the feudal age.
Since the Republican era, great numbers of female patrons have poured into
theaters and thus brought about sudden changes to the performing arts as
a whole. In the past, bearded characters [ laosheng] and male military char-
acters [wusheng] reigned in Beijing opera, because members of a generally
male audience had become connoisseurs of the art of performers of bearded
men and military ﬁgures, due to their long-standing patronage of Beijing
opera. Female audience members were new to the theater and thus naturally
lacked expertise. Since they came only to view the lively atmosphere [ kan
re’nao], they would certainly pick out the pretty ones ﬁrst. Therefore, female
characters [dan] became their favorites. In a few years, qingyi [or serious young
female characters] were attracting large crowds and in one jump became the
leading roles in theatrical art.
21
Aside from their lack of expertise, which prevented them from appreciat-
ing male roles, as Mei explained, women’s interests in female roles may also
have had to do with their greater facility in relating to female characters
than to emperors, generals, and court ofﬁcials. In any case, the corre lation
between the rise of women audiences and the new emphasis on female roles
is unmistakable. 
The rise of the four great female impersonators was accompanied by
new plays centered on female protagonists, which created a twentieth-cen-
tury dimension in Beijing opera and changed both its repertoire and its act-
ing style. During the ﬁrst two decades of the Republican period, Mei
Lanfang, a pioneer of Beijing opera’s modern reforms, created a dozen or
so new works in an effort to explore various historical and literary female
ﬁgures, including the mythological ﬁgure Chang’e, the Tang dynasty
beauty Yang Guifei, the legendary female soldier Hua Mulan, and Lin Daiyu,
the sickly beauty in the Qing dynasty novel Dream of the Red Chamber. In an
attempt to present his characters as real and complex human beings, rather
than as stereotypes, Mei was forced to break the strict codes of stylized
performances pertaining to qingyi, or serious young women, and huadan, or
lively young women, characters. Mei’s reforms made his characters capa-
ble of expressing both sorrow and happiness and being both reserved and
outgoing; the personalities of his characters thus blurred the traditional
line between qingyi and huadan.
22
When the female impersonator Shang Xiaoyun staged the new plays A
34 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 56 ===

Modern Nun (Modeng jia’nü) and Zhuo Wenjun(Zhuo Wenjun), he was hailed
by a commentator, in typical May Fourth hyperbole, as follows: “I think
that Zhuo Wenjun was the ﬁrst woman in several thousand years of Chi-
nese history who believed in free love. The fact that she worked as a cook
in her own restaurant proves that she was the ﬁrst woman who embraced
the idea of [economic] independence. Now Shang Xiaoyun has put this
history onstage, in an effort very much in accordance with the trend toward
women’s liberation.”
23 Cheng Yanqiu was known for his impersonation of
unfortunate but virtuous women in traditional society, such as Su San in
Spring of the Jade Hall (Yutang Chun). One commentator wrote about the
signiﬁcance of Cheng’s characters by combining May Fourth rhetoric with
an old ethic, as if no effort were required to reconcile the May Fourth con-
demnation of the old society that had made women suffer and the tradi-
tional female virtue of self-denial: “Chinese women have been suppressed
by men for the past several thousand years under the feudal system. All of
the commemorative arches erected in the past in honor of chaste women
actually represent countless sorrowful life histories. Cheng Yanqiu’s char-
acters indeed represent the most precious female role models in Chinese
history.”
24
As female audiences grew in size and female roles and protagonists
became increasingly popular, female performers also began to prosper in
Beijing opera circles. The ﬁrst generation of female performers was born
around the time of the 1911 Republican Revolution and began to study
the art during the May Fourth period. Meng Xiaodong was perhaps the
ﬁrst accomplished female Beijing opera performer who specialized in
laosheng, the leading male role. Other well-known women performers of
the period—Xue Yanqin, Xin Yanqiu, and Wang Yurong—specialized in
leading female roles and began to make a name for themselves around
the mid-1920s. The next generation, including some of the best Beijing
opera performers, such as Yan Huizhu, Tong Zhiling, and Li Yuru, studied
with the four great female impersonators and became established in the
1940s.
25 From that time onward, female performers generally replaced the
much celebrated male masters in the performance of female roles.26
Other major local operas from the Qing period also opened up to accom-
modate female performers and women’s taste. These pro-women reforms,
however, could not reverse a general decline of major operas in the early
Republican period.
27 The dilemma for major operas was as follows: on the
one hand, with the dismantling of the emperor system and the replace-
ment of ministers and generals with new kinds of political representatives,
The Origins of Yue Opera    35

=== PDF PAGE 57 ===

imperial narratives became increasingly irrelevant and unappealing to the
new Republican audience, half of which now consisted of women; on the
other hand, however, the masculine style of major operas, which developed
with their male-centered content, was too integral a part of these operas’
artistic identity to be cast away or sufﬁciently reformed to accommodate
more feminine narratives. In short, the male-centered Qing major operas
could not adapt to the new era without losing their identity, and thus their
decline was inevitable. By 1949, at the founding of the People’s Republic
of China, many major operas were on the verge of extinction and were
rescued only because of the new government’s efforts to preserve the
national cultural heritage.
28
Beijing opera retained its popularity a little longer owing to its high
political and social status, its historical and ongoing association with ofﬁ-
cials, military ﬁgures, and men of letters, and its evolving artistic sophis-
tication as perhaps best exempliﬁed by the modern reforms of the four
great female impersonators. During the Republican period, patronage of
Beijing opera became a marker of taste and status for rich and powerful
men, and male elites in political, intellectual, and business circles sup-
ported Beijing opera for various reasons. While old-fashioned wealthy
men found nostalgic pleasure in the opera, reform-minded intellectuals
and Nationalist government ofﬁcials found in it authentic Chinese aes-
thetics useful for the construction of a national identity that could bal-
ance the rapid trend toward Western-style modernization. Yet, despite
such political and intellectual support, Beijing opera’s popularity was ulti-
mately challenged and surpassed by emerging folk forms of minor operas,
most dramatically by ping opera in the Tianjin-Beijing area and Yue opera
in Shanghai and the Jiangnan region, but also by numerous other local
minor operas, such as Lü opera in Shandong, Yu opera in Henan, and Chu
opera in Hubei.
29
With the decline of masculine major operas, minor operas rose to
answer the call of the new era. Although minor operas shared the male-
centered characteristics of Qing opera, they proved capable of accom-
modating the trend of feminization, as their content was more “feminine”
to begin with and their styles less formulaic. Given the importance of
Western inﬂuences on China’s modern changes, much early moderniza-
tion was concentrated in treaty-port cities where new ideas and cultural
forms were ﬁrst tried out. Shanghai, Tianjin, and Xiamen, therefore,
became exciting places for minor operas where a rising urban mass audi-
36 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 58 ===

ence, of which women made up a large part, helped shape their trans-
formation into popular urban forms. 
Broadly viewed, the rise of minor operas in urban centers was a direct
response to mass urban migrations, the rhetoric of women’s liberation, and
women’s entrance into the popular-culture market. The early twentieth cen-
tury witnessed the growth of lower-income urban populations, owing to
large-scale migration to, and industrialization in, coastal cities. Peasants
moved to cities to become factory workers, clerks, barbers, and laborers
such as rickshaw pullers and porters. There were also large numbers of female
migrants, whose occupations ranged from housewives, to domestic helpers,
to factory workers. Minor operas moved into the city along with their native-
place and lower-class constituencies and developed rapidly in urban cen-
ters. The connection between minor operas and their constituencies within
this modern urban transition was partially captured in a contemporaneous
commentary on the “ didu opera” (diduban or diduxi, nicknames for Sheng-
xian little opera among Shanghai residents of the time) troupe.
30 The author,
Xiao Wo, proposed taking a modern view:
The twentieth century is an age of the people. For this reason, everything
reﬂected in the arts is simple, easy to understand, and hence of the people.
One example in our country is that our written language has changed from
classic to vernacular, and then to the language of commoners. In painting,
the popular cartoon has appeared, while in theater, there is a call for popu-
lar drama. In short, all these changes represent the spirit of the age, which
is to make the masses understand the arts. Then, how can you say diduxi [didu
opera] is not a product of the age? If someone thinks this statement is a slight
exaggeration, then let’s ask why diduopera is so popular with the masses nowa-
days. Why do we see the growing number of troupes and audiences only of
didu opera and not of any other opera? The fact is undeniable. Didu opera’s
singing is simple, easygoing, and straightforward, which is not only in accor-
dance with the spirit of the age but moreover ﬁts the taste of the masses, as
it is easy to follow.
31
Presenting didu opera as in tune with the Republican ideology of popular
rights, Xiao defended and promoted the opera of his native place in the
face of the traditional class bias against minor operas. Moreover, he sought
further to enhance minor operas’ political legitimacy by arguing, in the
new rhetoric of theatrical reform, that they were valuable tools for mass
The Origins of Yue Opera    37

=== PDF PAGE 59 ===

education. Xiao continued by comparing Shengxian opera and Beijing opera,
pointing out that the long, complicated tunes of Beijing opera made it
difﬁcult for the audience to understand the lyrics and follow the plots. As
a tabloid author, who hailed Shengxian little opera as a purveyor of mass
education for poorer urban constituencies, put it:
Didu opera is the most valuable and most educational drama. Didu opera’s
simple lyrics are like words as elementary as “people, hand, foot, knife, ruler”
in the ﬁrst lesson of a textbook, enlightening young students and educating
the masses. Didu opera is particularly useful in our country where education
has not yet become popularized. Kun opera, in contrast, declines rapidly
because it is too abstruse [for the populace] to understand.
32
At the same time, the new Republican ideology of women’s liberation
lifted traditional imperial sanctions against minor opera and its love sto-
ries. Supported by the May Fourth New Culture idea of free love and mar-
riage, as opposed to Confucian puritanism and the traditional institution
of arranged marriage, the love theme became prominent in theatrical rep-
resentations. Minor-opera artists needed only to recast traditional scholar-
beauty stories in the new rhetoric of free love and marriage and women’s
rights to adapt to the new fashion. 
The entrance of women into the entertainment market as both performers
and paying consumers greatly affected the modern transformation of
minor operas. Women’s impact is clearest in the cases of women’s Yue opera
in Shanghai and ping opera in Tianjin. More than other minor operas, Yue
opera and ping opera were centered on love stories, women performers,
and female or feminized male roles and were aimed at women audiences.
Ping opera was originally an all-male theater, and women appeared on its
stages only in the foreign settlement of Tianjin around the turn of the
twentieth century. However, women performers and female characters
soon thereafter took center stage, with the opera becoming increasingly
popular in the Beijing-Tianjin area and spreading to the northeastern
provinces and Shanghai in the 1930s and 1940s. During roughly the same
period, all-female Shengxian opera troupes created a sensation in Shang-
hai, quickly displacing the all-male Shengxian troupes and making Yue
opera exclusively an actresses’ domain. In the process, ping opera over-
took both Beijing opera and Hebei clapper opera in popularity in the north,
and women’s Yue opera overtook Beijing opera and the local Shanghai
opera to become the most popular genre in Shanghai and Zhejiang.
33 That
38 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 60 ===

these two forms of theater should have become the most popular operas
in their respective areas strongly suggests a close connection between
women’s entrance into the cultural market as consumers and the rise of
minor operas.
The Story of Shengxian Little Opera
It was within this context that the early history of Yue opera unfolded. 
“In the thirty-second year of the reign of the Guangxu emperor, 1906,
Yue opera was born,” or so Ma Chaoshui (1895–1975) declared in his On
the Early Development of Yue Opera (Yueju fazhan shu).34 This proclamation is
part of an oral history transmitted through three generations of story-singers
and performers and collected in the late 1950s, when a group of twenty-
ﬁve retired Yue opera actors gathered together for a government-sponsored
oral-history workshop in Shengxian. Ma Chaoshui, one of the twenty-ﬁve
retirees, put this oral history on paper in 1960. This and a cluster of indi-
vidual oral histories, studies of family and lineage books published in Tr a c -
ing the Origins of Yue Opera(Yueju suyuan), a collection of original lyrics from
the story-singing tradition,
35 and supplementary historical documents
provide a glimpse of the opera’s origins and early development. 
Yue opera went through several stages in its early development. Not
unlike the hip-hop originating among entertainment-hungry black youth
in the inner city ghettos of the late twentieth-century United States, Yue
opera began as a local tune of peasant self-entertainment in the country-
side of Shengxian, Shaoxing prefecture, Zhejiang. Talented young peas-
ants would sing about everything in a simple tune based on the sigongscale
system, or sigong diao.
36 Jin Qibing, an admirable talent from a certain Horse
Pond Village (Matang Cun) who was later remembered as the founding
father of Yue opera, was said to be skilled at not only ﬁlling in original
lyrics but also telling stories drawn from historical novels and Buddhist
literature that he heard from storytellers. Jin apparently inspired and taught
many young peasants, and when harvests were poor, he and his disciples
made some extra income with their singing. At ﬁrst, Jin and his followers
sang at the doors of better-off households in villages and towns not too
far from home during the lunar New Year. In return for the auspicious words
they sang, they hoped to be rewarded with a couple of New Year cakes
or dumplings. If all went well, they would return home with enough food
to sustain their families over the holiday season. The singers thus earned
the nickname “beggar’s stories” (taofan shu). Before long, story-singers started
The Origins of Yue Opera    39

=== PDF PAGE 61 ===

to take advantage of the agricultural off-seasons and went out regularly
on singing tours; it is said that some masters of the younger generation
spent more time singing than plowing and became semiprofessional
singers.
37
The younger generation, according to oral histories, managed to increase
the professional level of their art signiﬁcantly, with singers from artisan back-
grounds, such as Jin Zhitang and Xiang Jintang, playing important roles in
this process. Jin Zhitang, it is said, used to station his shoe-repair stand out-
side major teahouses so that he could listen to the master singers inside.
Apparently, Jin learned a great number of long stories and became familiar
with storytelling skills while repairing shoes for his customers. Meanwhile,
increasing numbers of Shengxian natives were turning to story-singing as
a form of livelihood, making it into a countywide industry in the late nine-
teenth century when the rural economy deteriorated. 
As their numbers increased, Shengxian story-singers began to expand
into market towns in the rich area centered on Hangzhou, Jiaxing, and
Huzhou in the Yangzi River delta, the Hang-Jia-Hu area. Shengxian mas-
ters competed with storytellers of the Hangzhou and Suzhou styles, two
major storytelling genres long established in this area, and, when neces-
sary, used their ﬁsts to ﬁght for their share of the market.
38 A serious alter-
cation apparently broke out in 1869 between Hangzhou-style masters and
newly arrived Shengxian masters over the right to perform in a major tea-
house in Yuhang, a market town near Hangzhou. Local ofﬁcials, interest-
ingly, ruled that both parties had the right to perform under the condition
that they pay a tax to local government. An ofﬁcial stone tablet was there-
upon erected and inscribed with four large characters that read “Sing sto-
ries and pay taxes” (Chang shu wan liang).
39 The Shengxian story-singers,
as oral histories have it, then started a golden age of performing at market
towns in the Hang-Jia-Hu area.
40
By the early years of the 1900s, however, Shengxian story-singing was
losing ground in market towns, and Ma Chaoshui and his colleagues had
to move back to the countryside. While oral histories do not provide any
speciﬁc reason for this development, it is likely that the illiterate story-
singers from Shengxian were at the end of their wits in attempting to com-
pete with the more learned and sophisticated Suzhou and Hangzhou
masters. It was at this moment, however, that Shengxian story-singing mas-
ters began to turn to operatic performance. 
Shengxian storytelling had its own style, and performers were aware of
40 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 62 ===

the difference between their style and that of major forms such as Suzhou
and Hangzhou storytelling. As Ma Chaoshui noted:
In major storytelling, one master told the whole story by himself, while in
our minor storytelling, there was the master and one or two companions
who sang ﬁxed repetitive melodies on the side. Major-storytelling forms fea-
tured tales like Romance of Yue Fei [Shuo Yue quanzhuan] and Romance of the
Three Kingdoms[Sanguo yanyi]. Minor storytelling told mostly tales like Story
of the White Snake [Baishe zhuan], The Golden Dragon Whip (Jinlong bian), Seven
Beauties (Qimei tu), and Pearl Pagoda.
41
The division between major- and minor-storytelling traditions was as clear
as the division in opera traditions. While major-storytelling forms focused
on historical, military, and political narratives, minor-storytelling forms
elaborated on love, betrayal, and personal fortunes. Other differences were
that the Shengxian style combined speaking with singing and chanting
on the side and required two or three performers who had to remain stand-
ing onstage to carry out the story. Compared with the conventional story -
telling method featuring one master who relied primarily on his oral skills
to tell the story, the Shengxian style involved more physical movements,
which made its transition to opera quite easy. 
Early in 1906, two or three groups of Shengxian story-singers began,
separately but simultaneously, to perform the stories they had thus far only
been narrating and thus gave birth to Shengxian little opera. Ma Chaoshui
recounted how this occurred on a cold winter night in the countryside of
Yuhang, when Ma and his master ran into two pairs of touring singers. They
all stayed at the patron Chen Wanyuan’s home, which was, as they knew,
always open to singers. Excited by the presence of three pairs of singers,
villagers gathered at Chen’s home and asked to be entertained. In order to
include all the singers in the performance, Chen suggested they act out a
story together, instead of each pair singing its own story. Apparently, the
suggestion was well taken. Using Mr. and Mrs. Chen’s clothes as costumes,
the singers staged a four-hour performance of Pearl Pagoda, punctuated by
occasional outbursts of friendly laughter from the audience, in Chen’s liv-
ing room.
News of the innovation spread fast, and other story-singers also turned
to acting. Some singers, upon returning home in the spring to work in the
ﬁelds, met with requests from fellow villagers that they put on plays, and
The Origins of Yue Opera    41

=== PDF PAGE 63 ===

the villagers promised to take care of the singers’ lands in exchange. Quickly,
all storytellers turned to acting, and a new minor opera began to take shape.
Because of its unpretentious style and small scale, the villagers gave it the
endearing nickname “little opera,” in contrast to “Shaoxing major opera”
(Shaoxing daban). 
Over the next few years, little-opera troupes multiplied in Shengxian,
but the opera remained part of the local peasant culture. Actors were mostly
peasants who worked in the ﬁelds during busy agricultural seasons and
performed only during off-seasons or on special occasions. Troupes usu-
ally had seven or eight members; troupe formation and performances were
rather casual. An actor might join an ongoing performance, and another
actor might leave one performance to join another. The only musical instru-
ments used in these days were a pair of small drums and a wooden clap-
per, played by any performer who was not onstage, to keep the rhythm.
Costumes were mostly everyday clothing, and audiences did not mind
when the plaster casts that some performers used to heal muscle and ten-
don injuries were exposed. There were few props to speak of, and troupe
members carried the few they owned from village to village on their shoul-
ders. Later, some troupes accumulated small funds and rented costumes
and instruments from Shaoxing major-opera troupes, but there was never
anything elaborate about little-opera performances. The troupes also
charged very low fees for a full program, which lasted from afternoon to
dawn. Their fees were so low that even small villages could afford them.
Performances at gambling gatherings, commonly known as xing pengzi (lit.,
“to raise an awning [for a gambling gathering]”), were another source of
income for little-opera troupes.
42
Because of its high mobility and low overhead, little opera ﬂourished
at the lowest level of rural society, and the nature of the rural market in
turn informed the opera’s style. Villagers appreciated major-opera per-
formances for their bustling atmosphere and the variety of snacks sold by
food peddlers; they did not mind if they could not actually hear the lyrics,
because everyone was already familiar with the stories. Their reasons for
appreciating little opera were very different. Little-opera performances
created a much more intimate relationship with audiences, as perform-
ances were casual and small in scale. Moreover, little opera used plain ver-
nacular language, simple local tones for speech and singing, and hardly
any musical instruments. All these features made it easy for local audi-
ences to hear the lyrics and speeches clearly and to follow the plot more
closely. 
42 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 64 ===

The content of little opera, which inherited a generous repertoire of
established stories from its story-singing predecessor as well as drew on
local peasant culture and Buddhist texts, was also of great interest to rural
residents. From story-singing, little opera inherited a set of stories known
as “news” stories, which vividly described local peasant life, as seen in such
titles as Ten Rare Things(Shi xiqi) and Ten Weird Things(Shi guguai).
43 “Sing
the news” ( chang xinwen) segments often served as a popular prelude to a
program. In a well-known piece, Selling the Mother-in-Law (Mai po ji), a
wicked daughter-in-law plots to get rid of her aged mother-in-law. She
bribes a matchmaker to marry off the old lady, and the greedy match-
maker cheats a poor young peasant by making him spend his life savings
for the bride. The young man happily takes the bride home, only to dis-
cover that she is a teary-eyed old woman. After hearing the woman’s sad
story, the young man’s anger turns to sympathy, and he decides to adopt
her as his mother. Deeply moved, the old woman tells him about a for-
tune her late husband secretly handed to her just before his death, and
she advises the young man to ﬁnd a nice wife and start a small business
with the money. The bad daughter-in-law regrets that she has thrown
away her good fortune, the matchmaker repents and ﬁnds the young peas-
ant a nice bride, and the old woman and young couple live a happy life
thereafter.
44
Little opera also inherited the romance from its story-singing predecessor.
When Shengxian story-singers entered market towns, they learned longer
pieces from the rich repertoires of storytelling traditions, and one of these,
among the most performed tales in both Shengxian story-singing and lit-
tle opera, is Pearl Pagoda. It tells a long, complex story about a young scholar
named Fang Qing. Fang hails from a scholar-ofﬁcial family in Henan. The
family becomes impoverished after Fang’s father dies, and Fang visits his
aunt to ask for a loan so that he can go to the capital and take the civil-
service examination. His snobbish aunt humiliates him and gives him noth-
ing. The aunt’s daughter overhears their conversation and, sympathetic to
her cousin, secretly gives the young scholar her pearl pagoda to pawn for
the money he needs. His uncle also sees a promising scholar in Fang and
grants the young man his daughter’s hand in marriage. On his way home,
Fang is robbed and almost freezes to death in the snow. The person who
stole from Fang is exposed when he takes the pearl pagoda to the local
pawnshop, and Fang is presumed dead. But Fang had in fact been rescued,
passes the exam, taking ﬁrst place, and is appointed to an ofﬁcial position.
One day, Fang disguises himself as a Daoist priest and is invited into his
The Origins of Yue Opera    43

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===

aunt’s house. In the process of telling her fortune, he purposefully criti-
cizes his aunt while hinting at his true identity. In the end, Fang’s iden-
tity is revealed, the aunt repents, and Fang happily marries his loving
cousin.
45
Little opera also drew on Buddhist traditions for its materials. Buddhist
Daughter of the Liu Family (Liu Xiangnü), a story adopted from the popular
Buddhist text Precious Volume about Liu Xiangnü (Liu Xiangnü baojuan), tells
the story of the enlightened daughter of the Liu family, who practices ﬁlial
piety, saves her parents from misfortune, and, at long last, enlightens them.
Historians of popular religion in late imperial China have shown that Bud-
dhism had considerable inﬂuence on the lives of rural women, and the Liu
Xiangnü story is but one example of the spread of Buddhist messages
through forms of peasant entertainment.
46
Oral accounts of the early history of little opera describe the opera’s
difﬁcult relationship with authorities and some members of the local elite.
While local moralists may have disapproved of the opera’s heterodox mes-
sage and bawdy style, local bullies considered the actors easy prey in their
power games, and government agencies ﬂexed their muscles by going after
the actors. According to Ding Yi, a local Yue opera researcher in Sheng-
xian, a little opera show at its home village in the summer of 1906 was almost
ruined by a bully, Fan Jinhuan, who controlled an eighteen-man militia. It
was an unspoken rule that anyone planning a show in this area had to send
gifts to Fan in advance. The show, Ding Yi said, was put together at the
last minute by local actors, who had just returned from a performance tour,
to entertain their fellow villagers. No gift was sent to Fan, and Fan dispatched
his men to arrest the singers. Outraged, the villagers selected eighteen young
men from the peasant association to protect the singers and also plead their
case with a member of the local gentry, Li Haifa, who was well connected
to ofﬁcials in both Shengxian and Dongyang Counties. Li, who had been
disgusted by Fan’s behavior for some time, took the opportunity to lead a
rebellion against Fan. On the day of the show, a big audience, including
many supporters from nearby villages, gathered around the stage. Li sat
right in the front row, and the eighteen young men were positioned on
both sides of the stage. When Fan’s men heard about this, they ﬂed before
they reached the spot.
47
In 1908, Regulations of Self-Government of Shengxian (Shengxian zizhi
zhang cheng), an ofﬁcial edict issued by the local government as part of
the national Constitutional Reform Movement, prohibited minor-opera
44 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===

performances of any kind, and the newly formed police force welcomed
this opportunity to show its power.48 Ma Chaoshui recounted another dra-
matic incident in which he and his troupe were arrested in a small village
and taken to the police station in Sanjie. An elderly gentry man bailed
them out, and then the man’s wife wanted to see them perform. A simple
stage of planks placed on top of haystacks was erected outside the man’s
yard, facing the police station, and the actors performed two plays for the
villagers, Double Golden Flowers (Shuang jin hua) and Pearl Pagoda. The show
was well received, and the audience was moved to tears by the scene in
which the daughter gives her pearl pagoda to her beloved cousin. The
show went on for several days, until the head of the police force became
so embarrassed that he begged the old man to end it. “We felt victorious,”
Ma commented, and he even dared to lecture the head of the police force,
or so he claimed in his account, arguing: “Opera is one of the three per-
suasions. The ﬁrst is the emperor’s law, a tough measure that promises cap-
ital punishment for those who dare to violate it. This is not the best way.
The second is the Buddhist way that makes people study sutras and learn
about retribution. The third is playful persuasion through opera. We show
the people the crystal-clear [difference between] good and evil by means
of our vivid performance.”
49 Although Ma’s depiction of the event need
not be taken on faith, his speech is a clear example of peasant appropria -
tion of the elite idea of “persuasion of the people” ( jiaohua) with which to
argue his case.
50
The troupes were not so lucky when performing outside of Shengxian.
Once, in Paito, Zhuji County, Ma Chaoshui and seven fellow actors were
charged with obscene performance and arrested in the middle of a show
by the local police chief, Zhou Zhongzen. Zhou, a Shengxian native him-
self, seems to have been ashamed of his native-place fellows’ lowly trade
and shows. He ridiculed the actor who played young female roles by parad-
ing him through the streets in his womanly makeup and stage costumes.
Then he had all the costumes burned. According to Ma, those who had
attended the show were not convinced that Zhou was in the right, as they
had witnessed nothing obscene, and so they stormed the police station.
“I took my men and ﬂed to Sandu,” Ma wrote. “We did not have any money
but still had seven or eight mouths to feed, so we had to perform some
story-singing in teahouses.” Later, they borrowed some costumes and
props and staged an opera; again, “the police objected and imposed a
four-yuan tax for each night of performance, while all we could earn from
The Origins of Yue Opera    45

=== PDF PAGE 67 ===

one night’s show was about three or four yuan. We had no choice but to
leave.”51
Ma’s account is rife with similar incidents of persecution but also reveals
a sense of pride at little opera’s rise from such adversity. Despite repeated
persecution, Shengxian little-opera troupes mushroomed all over the coun-
tryside, attesting to, among other things, the deep rural market for minor
operas. 
The rapid increase in the number of little-opera troupes in Shengxian
was also closely related to a downturn in the local economy. From 1907
to 1911, a drought in Shengxian destroyed all the crops. Many young men
joined little-opera troupes and left the county in search of a new liveli-
hood. Opera singing became a countywide industry, and little opera was
the main Shengxian export to adjacent areas.
52 The troupes traveled along
three traditional trade routes: through Xinchang and Yuyao to Ningbo in
eastern Zhejiang; through Dongyang and Zhuji to Jinhua in central Zhe-
jiang; and through Shangyu, Shaoxing, and Xiaoshan to the Hang-Jia-Hu
area in western Zhejiang.
53 The Hang-Jia-Hu area, highly developed and
a long-term base for Shengxian story-singers, was the most popular desti-
nation for little-opera troupes. Such a troupe performed in Hangzhou for
the ﬁrst time in 1910, and in Ningbo and Jinhua in 1915. Two years later,
in 1917, little opera staged the ﬁrst show in Shanghai.
54
Shengxian Opera in Shanghai
Shengxian little opera entered Shanghai along with a large ﬂow of immi-
grants from Zhejiang, especially the Ningbo-Shaoxing area. Yang Shao -
chun, a Shengxian native and a Beijing opera agent in Shanghai, brought
the ﬁrst little-opera troupe to Shanghai in 1917 . The twenty-odd mem-
bers of the troupe boarded a Ningbo-Shanghai steamboat and arrived at
the Shiliupu dock along the Huangpu River, where Zhejiang immigrants
dominated. As news of their arrival spread, Zhejiang natives crowded the
New Theater (Xin Wutai) to watch the city’s ﬁrst show of Shengxian lit-
tle opera. When the actors appeared onstage in their funny-looking cos-
tumes and began their clumsy singing and acting routines, the audience
burst into laughter. Many left before the end of the program, and few came
back for a second viewing. Yang Shaochun held out for a few weeks but
eventually gave up and disbanded the troupe; most of the actors sold their
bedding to buy tickets home.
55 A few months later, a Shaoxing major-
opera agent in Shanghai sponsored a second little-opera troupe. Despite
46 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 68 ===

an initial success that lasted a few months, the troupe disbanded within
half a year. A few further attempts were made in 1918 and 1919 without
much success.
56
The turning point came in the winter of 1919, as little opera’s effort to
enhance its production values converged with a new wave of social aware-
ness related to such issues as women’s liberation and free love and marriage
after the May Fourth Movement. Shengxian opera returned to Shanghai
with new plays boasting better-developed plots and characters as well as
more-reﬁned music, costumes, makeup, and acting.
57 Most plays during the
foundational phase of Shengxian opera in Shanghai were centered on love
stories, and the most successful plays included Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtai
(or The Butterﬂy Lovers),
58 Emerald Hairpin(Biyu zan), Tale of the Lute (Pipa ji), and
Meng Lijun (Meng Lijun), all with positive portrayal of the heroines. 
The development of Emerald Hairpin and Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtaibest
illustrates the success of Shengxian opera in Shanghai. Set in the Ming
dynasty, Emerald Hairpin tells the tragic story of a young couple. Minister Li
Tingfu marries his only daughter, Li Xiuying, to Wang Yulin, son of a mem-
ber of the imperial academy. Li Xiuying’s cousin, Gu Wenyou, who is secretly
in love with her, conspires to separate the new couple. Gu arranges to have
a forged love letter supposedly written by Li and a stolen emerald hairpin
of hers placed in the new couple’s bedroom on their wedding night. When
Wang sees the items, he falls for Gu’s trick and concludes that Li is having
an affair with Gu. Furious, Wang rejects Li and berates her repeatedly or
ignores her. Unaware of the source of Wang’s anger, Li submits to his abuse
with the hope that her loyalty and love will eventually win his heart. 
In the original Wu opera (from adjacent Dongyang County) version of
this story, Li’s father kicks his daughter to death upon hearing about her
alleged violation of chastity. Then, after the truth comes to light, Wang
commits suicide in remorse and Gu is arrested and executed. In a previous
Shengxian version of this play, Li Xiuying eventually dies of melancholy,
while Gu Wenyou succumbs to lovesickness.
59 The play had never been
popular because the tragic ending involved the deaths of all the main char-
acters and the loss of heirs for all three families. But in Shanghai in the
winter of 1919, it was presented with an innovative and completely dif-
ferent ending. As Ma Chaoshui, who participated in the Shanghai adven-
ture and was in fact responsible for the revised ending, recalled:
The fourth time we went to Shanghai to perform in the Star Theater [Min -
xing] in Tianbao Lane, off Haining Road, I thought to myself: “Even though
The Origins of Yue Opera    47

=== PDF PAGE 69 ===

Shanghai’s market is really good, we still have to ﬁnd our way into it.” I
thought that the Dongyang Wu opera Emerald Hairpin was a moving story
and maybe a good choice. We had more than forty actors at the time. I went
out to the street to look for scripts and found a storybook in which Wang
Yulin abuses Li Xiuying until she dies. I thought that the ending was too
negative. Later I found a Buddhist edition that has Wang Yulin become a
zhuangyuan [ﬁrst place in the metropolitan civil-service examination] and
present the phoenix cap to Li Xiuying [seeking reconciliation]. I then wrote
the act “Presenting the Phoenix Cap” and had Zhang Yunbiao play Wang
Yulin. During the show, the audience was moved to tears as the story
unfolded.
60
Ma Chaoshui and his actors changed the story line and gave the play a
happy ending. Instead of simply accepting Wang’s allegation regarding
his daughter’s chastity, Li Xiuying’s father investigates and discovers that
the handwriting in the love letter, the evidence on which Wang based his
accusation, does not match his daughter’s. The story that follows was then
completely altered: Gu’s conspiracy is exposed, while Li Xiuying returns
to her natal home to recuperate, and Wang is left alone to rue his bad behav-
ior. In an effort to redeem himself, he studies hard and wins the title of
zhuangyuan. Upon his return from the capital after the examination, Wang
presents Li Xiuying with a phoenix cap, a gift he solicited specially from
the emperor in order to honor his wife. Li, however, refuses to accept it,
nor is she willing to go home with Wang, declaring instead that she wants
to live in a monastery for the rest of her life. In the closing act, everyone,
including all the characters in the play as well as members of the audi-
ence, must listen to Li Xiuying complain at length about her sufferings,
while Wang, the honorable zhuangyuan, is made to kneel at Li’s feet and
apologize. At the end of the play, the two are gloriously and happily
reunited.
61
This positive ending to an otherwise tragic story, with its vindication
of the heroine and criticism of the male protagonist, was most sympathetic
to women and accorded with the new concept of women’s liberation, rep-
resenting a clear departure from the misogynist operatic tradition of the
Qing period. The play was Shengxian opera’s ﬁrst big hit in Shanghai and
subsequently became a classic of the Yue opera repertoire. 
Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtaiwas a celebration of love that originated from
a folktale set in neighboring Shangyu County.
62 Some scholars believe that
48 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===

the ﬁrst written record of the story appeared during the Tang dynasty (618–
907) and told the following tale: 
Yingtai, daughter of the Zhu family in Shangyu, disguised herself in men’s
clothing and traveled in search of a school education. At school, she met
Liang Shanbo, a classmate from Guiji [present-day Shaoxing], whose style
name was Churen [lit., “with benevolence”]. Yingtai returned home ﬁrst;
one year later, Shanbo visited her at her home, only to discover that Ying-
tai was a woman. He was deeply affected. Upon his return home, Shanbo
asked his parents to arrange for his marriage with the Zhu family, but Ying-
tai had already been betrothed to the son of the Ma family. Shanbo later
served as the magistrate of Yin county [present-day Ningbo], where he passed
away from sickness. He was buried in the western side of Maocheng [east
of Yin County]. On her way to Ma’s house as a bride, Yingtai’s boat was
held up by storms and big waves when it passed the site of Shanbo’s tomb.
Having learned that Shanbo was buried nearby, Yingtai landed and cried
passionately before his grave. Suddenly, the ground below her split open
and collapsed, and Yingtai was thus buried with Shanbo. Prime Minister Xie
An [320–385] of the [Eastern] Jin dynasty [317–420] issued a memorial to
the throne on the matter and accordingly honored the grave as “the grave
of a righteous woman.”
63
A Southern Song (1127–1279) version of the story transforms the two lovers
into a pair of butterﬂies ﬂuttering over the gravesite.64 The story later spread
widely, taking different forms in various entertainment media; modern exam-
ples include the Sichuan opera play Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtai (Liuyin ji)
and the Huangmei opera play by the same title. 
In the Shengxian opera version premiered in Shanghai in the winter of
1919, Zhu Yingtai, disguised as a man, meets Liang Shanbo at Straw Bridge,
outside of Hangzhou, on her way to school in the city. The two young
students are so taken with each other at their ﬁrst meeting that they imme-
diately become sworn brothers. They then study and live in the same room
for three years without Shanbo ever suspecting Yingtai’s true sex. Upon leav-
ing for home, Yingtai secretly asks the teacher’s wife to arrange a marriage
between her and Shanbo. Soon after Yingtai returns home, however, her
father accepts a request from a certain wealthy Ma family and betroths
her to Ma Wencai. When Shanbo ﬁnally arrives with his request for mar-
riage, Yingtai can only promise to be with him in their next life. Returning
The Origins of Yue Opera    49

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===

home, Shanbo soon dies of lovesickness. On her way to Ma’s house on
the wedding day, Yingtai stops to pray in front of Shanbo’s tombstone,
and when it opens up in response to her prayers, she jumps in to join him.
Ma Wencai tries to hold her back, only to be dragged into the tomb along
with her, and the two go thereafter to the court of the underworld. Check-
ing the book of life and death, the King of the Underworld ﬁnds that Shanbo
and Yingtai are actually the Golden Boy and the Jade Girl who serve the
Jade Emperor in heaven. The Jade Emperor had sent them down to earth
to experience a few cycles of incarnation, and they have just completed
the last reincarnation. The king accordingly sends the two back to the Jade
Emperor and returns Ma W encai to earth, where he marries his own pre-
destined bride.
65
The play’s highlights came in four separate acts: “Eighteen-LiFarewell”
(Shibali xiangsong), “Remembering the Eighteen-Li Farewell” (Hui shiba),
“Meeting at Yingtai’s Chamber” (Loutai hui), and “Yingtai Crying at
Shanbo’s Bier” (Yingtai kuling). In “Eighteen-Li Farewell,” Liang Shanbo sees
Zhu Yingtai off as she heads home, accompanying her for eighteen li
(roughly six miles). Along the way, she tries to reveal her true sex and her
love for him by invoking eighteen metaphors of love, including ﬂowers,
butterﬂies, mandarin ducks, the story of the Cowherd and the Girl Weaver,
monks and nuns, and the hall of the bodhisattva Guanyin where young
couples pray for sons. Yingtai cannot keep herself from expressing her
feelings for Shanbo before they depart. But, as a well-educated woman,
she knows how a proper lady must carry herself, and as a young girl, she
is also too shy to express her love and desire in a straightforward manner.
“Eighteen-Li Farewell” captures Yingtai’s dilemma and beautifully illustrates
the young girl’s grace and complex psychology, a mixture of desire, pas-
sion, shyness, determination, and intelligence. “Remembering the Eight-
een-Li Farewell” is a solo act in which Liang, having learned from the
teacher’s wife about Yingtai’s true identity and her wish to marry him, leaves
school for the Zhu Family Village (Zhujia Zhuang) to ask for Yingtai’s hand
in marriage. As he encounters familiar scenes along the way, a happy Liang
Shanbo recalls the eighteen metaphors Yingtai used to hint at her love for
him. “Meeting at Yingtai’s Chamber” and “Crying at Shanbo’s Bier” depict
the lovers’ passion and desperation. 
The rich local oral tradition was most inﬂuential in the development of
the Shengxian opera play. According to the recollections of veteran
actors, “Eighteen-Li Farewell” and “Meeting at Yingtai’s Chamber,” the two
most popular acts, took shape in Shengxian story-singing as early as the
50 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===

late nineteenth century. When Shengxian story-singing was turned into
opera in the early twentieth century, these two acts were staged as inde-
pendent pieces. It was not until the winter of 1919 in Shanghai, however,
that the parts were united into an integral whole as described above.
66 In
this early version of the play, folk superstitions, Daoist heavens, Buddhist
notions of reincarnation, and peasant forms of sexually suggestive gestures
and lighthearted ﬂirtation are mixed with love motifs alluding to the May
Fourth rhetoric of women’s liberation and free love. Despite these mixed
messages, the story of the butterﬂy lovers was best known in the early
Republican era as a popular celebration of love and loyalty. 
With these additions to its repertoire, the all-male Shengxian opera expe-
rienced a golden age in the 1920s. The rise of little opera in Shanghai was
coterminous with the decline of Shaoxing major opera. As the native say-
ing of the time put it: “The little brother defeated the big brother.” In 1923,
Shengxian little opera was performed at four permanent theaters, and by
1925, the number of venues had increased to more than ﬁfteen, including
amusement parks, large teahouses, native-place halls, inn lobbies, and new-
style theaters. During the same period, the number of theaters that staged
Shaoxing major opera declined from four to two.
67 Upon its initial success,
little opera began to call itself “Shaoxing civil opera” (Shaoxing wenxi), claim-
ing thereby to be the representative opera from Shaoxing prefecture, of
which Shengxian was a part. The term “civil opera” (wenxi) indicates little
opera’s emphasis on love stories and the fact that love drama, not military
romance—a particular strong point of Shaoxing major opera—was what
made little opera popular in Shanghai. 
Changing Gender Norms and Girls’ Opera Schools
Sometime in the 1930s, the term “women’s Shaoxing civil opera” (nüzi Shao -
xing wenxi ) began to appear frequently in advertisements, posters, and
prints. Then, in the 1940s, “women’s Yue opera”, or simply “Yue opera,”
became the standard name for this newcomer to the Shanghai opera scene,
signaling the coming of age of all-female Shengxian opera troupes and the
passing of all-male troupes. The sudden rise of women’s Shengxian opera
and the disappearance of male actors around 1938 have been a mystery in
Yue opera history and in the history of modern Chinese opera more gen-
erally. Moreover, that Yue opera was the only genre to evolve into an all-
female theater, and one of the most popular theaters in modern China, begs
for historical explanation. The ofﬁcial Shanghai Yue Opera Gazetteer (Shang-
The Origins of Yue Opera    51

=== PDF PAGE 73 ===

hai Yueju zhi) devotes only one sentence to the decline of all-male troupes:
“As actors became older and lacked successors, all-male troupes eventually
declined and were ﬁnally displaced by all-female troupes.”
68 Evidence sug-
gests that men’s Shengxian opera lost ground to women’s opera on both
artistic and institutional fronts. As the artistic transformation of Shengxian
opera and the advantage of the all-female cast in this process are discussed
in the next chapter, this chapter addresses the institutional aspect, focus-
ing on girls’ opera schools and changing gender norms in performance art
in general. 
The entrance of women into the entertainment market had a dramatic
impact on gender arrangement in theatrical art in the early Republican
period. In 1914, only two years after the founding of the republic, female
performers made a powerful appearance in China’s ﬁrst modern theater,
“new drama” (xinju or wenmingxi), which was imported from the West via
Japan and prospered in China as “family drama” ( jiating ju) and “live news
drama” (huobao ju). Among some thirty new-drama companies, at least six
were all female.
69 The all-female companies represented a break from the
late Qing tradition of male-dominated theater but perpetuated the use of
single-sex casts onstage. 
Sex desegregation in theatrical arts was taking place in more traditional
revues, ﬁrst as a way of returning to a cross-gender acting tradition. In Shang-
hai’s foreign settlements, women had begun performing Suzhou story-
singing and “Shanghai storytelling” (bentan) in teahouses and Beijing opera
in the late nineteenth century.
70 During the early Republican period, women
performers became popular and fashionable; they took on both male and
female roles and acted onstage with male as well as female partners. Cross-
gender performance was common at the time for male and female actors.
71
Gender-straight performance was ﬁrst established in elite spoken drama
in 1923. A coterie of avant-garde intellectuals, dissatisﬁed with the com-
mercial and vulgar qualities of the new drama, formed the Popular The-
atrical Association (Dazhong Jushe) in Shanghai in the early 1920s. The
devotees called their new spoken drama “amateur theater” (aimeiju); the term
uses a phonetic transliteration of the English word “amateur,” rendered in
Chinese characters that mean “love of beauty” (aimei ) so as to emphasize
its noncommercial character. These Western-inﬂuenced intellectuals con-
sidered single-sex theaters and cross-gender acting embarrassingly unreal-
istic and thought traditional theater’s formulaic style was old-fashioned and
feudal. In an effort to modernize Chinese theater, intellectuals adopted the
52 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 74 ===

Western realistic mode of acting and initiated a gender-straight acting sys-
tem by which men and women played the roles of their own sex. In 1923,
the director and playwright Hong Shen staged Marriage: A Lifelong Commit-
ment (Zhongsheng dashi), Hu Shi’s inﬂuential May Fourth adaptation of
Ibsen’s A Doll’s House, with a gender-straight cast. The next year, Hong Shen
presented another gender-straight production, Oscar Wilde’s Lady Winder -
mere’s Fan, which became one of the most frequently performed plays of the
time.
72
During the ensuing decades, the elite practice of gender-straight act -
ing was to become the rule for all Chinese theaters, and although cross-
gender performances continued to exist in various native operas, the
practice became marginalized. Even Beijing opera could not escape when
new generations of actresses eventually took over women’s roles. Yue opera,
like other native forms, freely adopted a variety of gender arrangements,
from gender-straight to cross-gender and from single- to mixed-gender
modes of acting. Signiﬁcantly, when most native operas eventually were
transmuted into gender-straight theaters, Yue opera evolved into a theater
dominated by all-female troupes. Thus, the spectrum of possible gender
arrangements displayed on Republican Shanghai’s stage may be seen as a
response to the rise of female performers and audiences, with women’s Yue
opera being a radical representative of this modern transition. Women’s
opera was, therefore, an exception in a general move toward gender-straight
acting, one that proved the rule of the feminization of performing arts in
the modern age. If the preference for gender-straight acting was much
inﬂuenced by the West, then the rise of women’s opera was evidence of
various native and traditional inﬂuences in the shaping of China’s modern
theater. 
Women’s Yue opera was deeply connected with a cultural legacy of the
region—the “all-girls training schools/troupes” ( nü ban, also nü xiand nü yue),
a tradition peculiar to the Jiangnan area that dated back to at least the Ming
dynasty. The prosperous late Ming and early Qing literati in Jiangnan were
fond of opera, and it was just as fashionable for men of means to train and
keep private opera troupes as it was to build private scholars’ gardens. Many
of the best private troupes, such as those owned by Zhu Yunlai and Yu Shui-
wen, consisted of women only.
73 During the late nineteenth century, after
the government prohibited adult women performers, all-girls opera troupes,
known as mao’erxi (lit., “children’s opera”), emerged. The all-girls opera fea-
tured girls mostly in their early teens performing all roles in a highly pro-
The Origins of Yue Opera    53

=== PDF PAGE 75 ===

fessional manner, and the performers were known to be especially skilled
in acrobatic ﬁghting. All-girls opera enjoyed great popularity for a few
decades and then disappeared completely by the end of the 1920s, when
adult actresses reappeared in great numbers.
74 In the early 1920s, however,
a combination of the popularity of female performers, all-girls opera troupes,
and men’s Shengxian opera seems to have inspired the idea of a girls’ Sheng-
xian opera.
75
In 1923, a man by the name of Wang Jinshui, a Shengxian merchant
sojourning in Shanghai, decided to create an all-girls Shengxian opera school
in his hometown in order to prepare the girls for Shanghai’s market.
76 Wang
had been in the theater business for some years and had recently become
manager of the Shengping Theater in Shanghai, one of the major theaters
featuring all-male Shengxian opera. With three hundred silver dollars, Wang
started an all-girls training school in early 1923 in Shi’s Village (Shijia Ao),
a poor village located in a mountainous area. Residents greeted him with
suspicion, however.
77 Older moral imperatives and customs held sway there,
and local people voiced a long-standing bias against the “three zi”: “actors”
(xizi), “soldiers” (liangzi), and “prostitutes” (biaozi). Taking up acting, they felt,
was bad enough for men, let alone for women. Wang, however, had such
conﬁdence in Shanghai’s lucrative market that he was determined to carry
out his plan. He ﬁrst recruited his daughter and his niece to demonstrate
that he was serious and promised an attractive package for the students: free
room and board and a gold ring, a dress, a pair of leather shoes, and one
hundred silver dollars after three years of apprenticeship. In three months,
Wang recruited about two dozen girls between the ages of nine and thir-
teen; daughters of peasants, artisans, and rural opera actors accounted for
the majority of the apprentices, and many were family members, relatives,
and neighbors of Wang and the instructors.
78
Instruction began in the early summer. The school hired three teach-
ers, all retired actors. Most girls were illiterate and had no previous act-
ing experience, but they were nonetheless able to endure the arduous
training program. The school safeguarded the girls’ well-being in general,
since most students had close ties to the men in charge, but the strict train-
ing program had the girls working day and night. Students got up early
for physical ﬁtness training before breakfast. Then they studied scripts and
memorized parts of plays. In the afternoon, they studied performance skills
until dark, and diligent students rehearsed additional work on their own
by moonlight. The curriculum consisted of three basic training courses:
“acrobatics” (wugong), “singing” (changgong), and “posture” (zuogong). Along
54 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===

with their basic training, they learned and memorized several plays. By
the end of the third month, Wang was ready to put the girls onstage for
a formal rehearsal.
79
The ﬁrst rehearsal was held in the open space in front of the school.
The temporary stage was made up of several planks. The performance
featured a costume play, A Pair of Pearly Phoenixes(Shuang zhu feng), a typ-
ical scholar-beauty love story, which begins with Wen Bizheng, a secondary-
degree holder from Luoyang, seeing Huo Dingjin, the beautiful daughter
of a high ofﬁcial, on his visit to a temple. He immediately falls in love but
has no way to approach her. Wen goes off to a village to consult a go-
between, and there he falls in love again, this time with the go-between’s
daughter. The go-between manages to sell Wen to the ofﬁcial’s house-
hold as a slave-servant. While there, he ﬁnds an opportunity to meet the
young lady he had seen at the temple and reveals his true identity and his
love for her. At the end, Wen restores his status and happily marries both
women. Although the stage and costumes were simple and the acting
lacked sophistication, the villagers responded positively to the girls’
efforts.
80
Wang soon exhausted his initial investment of three hundred silver dol-
lars and was eager to test the market in Shanghai. Early in winter 1923, he
took his troupe to Shanghai and put on a show at the Shengping Theater.
But the result could not have been further from the success he dreamed of.
Three months of training proved insufﬁcient, as the girls appeared tech-
nically inadequate as well as shy and immature. After few more tries per-
forming without success at teahouses in Shanghai and Hangzhou, Wang
brought the troupe back to Shengxian in spring 1924.
81
The villagers welcomed the girls back with great enthusiasm. That the
children had performed in Shanghai gave them a certain cachet in the minds
of the villagers. The clansmen even deﬁed rules and opened the stage inside
Shenwu Hall, the Shi lineage hall, for the girls’ homecoming show. The
whole village was exhilarated. In the past, Shi’s Village had not had the
resources to invite opera companies; now the village had its own opera
troupe, and the native daughters put on shows for the community free of
charge. Villagers invited relatives and friends from other locations and even
slaughtered domestic animals so that there would be sufﬁcient provisions
to treat their guests. On the day of the show, Shenwu Hall was packed
with enthusiastic peasants. The program consisted of typical scholar-beauty
love stories, including A Pair of Pearly Phoenixes , A Pair of Jade Bracelets (Yu
lianhuan), and Pearl Pagoda. The girls were also excited. They had become
The Origins of Yue Opera    55

=== PDF PAGE 77 ===

more experienced during their several months of performing in big cities,
and the warm and eager welcome they received helped mitigate any resid-
ual shyness. The girls put on a bold and lively performance and greatly
pleased the audience. The troupe then performed a few successful shows
in nearby towns.
82
In the autumn of 1924, the school undertook a long-distance tour in
the eastern Zhejiang countryside. On land, they traveled by foot, but most
of the journey was on water, by raft and “riverboats covered with black
awning” (wupeng chuan). Such tours became routine over the next few years.
In 1927, when the school’s four-year contract with the students expired,
Wang turned the school into a professional troupe, adopting a salary sys-
