tem that put the leading actresses at the top of the payroll. By the late
1920s, the ﬁrst all-girls Shengxian opera troupe had become a ﬁxture in
Shaoxing and had acquired name recognition in Ningbo and Hangzhou,
replacing the declining all-girls Beijing opera.
83 The popularity of three
leading actresses, known as “the three ﬂowers” (san hua)—Shi Yinhua and
Zhao Ruihua, who specialized in young female roles, and Tu Xinghua, who
specialized in young male roles—marked the commercial success of the
girls’ Shengxian opera troupe. A 1927 article in the Shaoxing Evening News
(Shaoxing wanbao) noted: “[The girls] performed in the Juemin Theater.
Shi Yinhua and others already had great marketing power to attract audi-
ences. The audience was reluctant to leave after the show was over, and
the opera got a nickname, diaojiaoxi [lit., ‘the opera you are not able to
walk away from’]. Shi Yinhua’s salary was as high as nine hundred yuan
[per month].”
84 In 1929, the ﬁrst all-girls troupe came to a glorious end
when the actresses left the troupe one by one for marriage. Among the
ﬁrst to leave, in 1928, was Shi Yinhua, who married the son of a rich fam-
ily in the market town of Chongren. Wang Jinshui took the remaining
twelve girls back to Shi’s Village, where rural people had long been await-
ing the troupe’s return. During the troupe’s last year of operation, people
from near and far came to Shi’s Village to contract with Wang to bring
the troupe to their villages and towns. In one incident, the owner of a cloth
shop in the market town of Huangze sent ﬁve sedan chairs to bring the
actresses to perform at his mother’s birthday celebration. The troupe trav-
eled, or rather paraded, about twenty-ﬁve miles in sedan chairs, stopping
to perform at several places and creating a sensation along the way. The
troupe’s splendid end instilled a dream in the minds of many young girls
and also effectively demonstrated to the country folk that being an actress
might not be such a bad idea after all.
85
56 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 78 ===

All-girls schools proliferated, however, only in the 1930s when the Great
Depression began to take a toll on China’s silk economy. Shanghai and
Zhejiang, with silk industries closely linked to the international market,
were hit hard. Sericulture and the silk industry had been an important part
of Zhejiang’s economy for centuries. The northern Zhejiang area (mainly
the Hangzhou-Jiaxing-Huzhou plain) was the leader in the province’s silk
industry, supported by eastern Zhejiang’s sericulture and cheap labor.
Shengxian’s mountainous terrain was unsuitable for agriculture, and as the
economy relied heavily on sericulture, the area was especially affected. In
the early 1930s, Shengxian endured a lasting depression.
86
Economic disaster impoverished local families and changed the lives of
many native daughters, inadvertently contributing to the rise of women’s
opera. Sericulture had been an important source of cash income for peasant
households in Shengxian, and peasant girls traditionally labored alongside
adult women in raising and harvesting silkworms, all the while dreaming
of becoming wageworkers in the silk mills of Hangzhou and Shanghai. The
outcome poor peasant girls most feared was being sold or given away as
child brides by desperate parents, a common practice in eastern Zhejiang’s
poor countryside. Ironically, in the face of the bankrupt rural economy, a
daughter’s cash-earning ability became increasingly important to rural fam-
ilies. All things considered, opera singing suddenly did not look like such
a bad alternative for a little girl whose family desperately needed her to
bring in cash.
87
Thus, just as a local natural disaster in the early twentieth century had
pushed the development of Shengxian little opera, so an international dis-
aster in the early 1930s resulted in the phenomenal growth of all-girls opera,
ﬁrst in Zhejiang and then in Shanghai. In 1929, a second all-girls school,
Xinxinfeng Theater, was established in Huangze township. Then, from 1930
to 1934, at least thirteen girls’ schools came into being in Huangze alone.
In Shengxian as a whole, major schools during the period between 1930
and 1937 numbered at least forty, while smaller schools were even more
numerous. Girls’ opera schools also appeared in great numbers in such nearby
counties as Xinchang, Xindeng, Lin’an, Shaoxing, Sanmen, Yuyao, and Long -
you.
88 Girls’ schools and subsequent all-female troupes vastly outnumbered
men’s Shengxian opera troupes; moreover, with their highly competitive
fees and the quality performances presented by a cluster of well-known
stars, girls’ Shengxian opera marginalized all other genres active in the prov-
ince, including the long-standing Shaoxing major opera, Hangzhou opera
(Hangju), and Beijing opera. Like men’s Shengxian opera, girls’ Shengxian
The Origins of Yue Opera    57

=== PDF PAGE 79 ===

opera was deeply rooted in the peasant culture of Zhejiang’s countryside
and bespoke the general lack of entertainment in rural life. Backed up by
the vast rural market, all-female troupes were able to test the waters at urban
centers from time to time and eventually emerged as powerful contenders
in the entertainment markets of Ningbo, Shaoxing, Wenzhou, Hangzhou,
and, ﬁnally, Shanghai. Girls’ troupes began to frequent Shanghai after 1935
and already enjoyed better market value than all-male and mixed-gender
Shengxian opera troupes. In the meantime, all-male and mixed-gender
troupes were demoralized by their declining market value and lack of new
recruits. 
The institution of the girls’ opera school was vital to the success of
women’s opera for several reasons. The school gave its students systematic
technical training in acting, singing, and acrobatic ﬁghting, a beneﬁt many
peasants who became minor-opera performers never had. The school-
trained troupe also enjoyed a strong institutional and technical structure
with a team of well-trained long-term partners in all roles, crucial for a well-
balanced troupe. Moreover, girls who lived, traveled, and worked together
for months and years developed a sense of belonging that kept them going
and overcoming all sorts of hardships in their career. Finally, the sheer num-
ber of girls’ schools and women performers overwhelmed existing male
 performers and discouraged boys from taking up acting careers, effectively
making Shengxian opera a women’s realm. 
The outbreak of the War of Resistance against Japan in 1937 gave women’s
Shengxian opera troupes one ﬁnal push toward establishing them selves per-
manently in Shanghai. In August 1937, Japanese forces attacked the city,
and the Battle of Shanghai began. It lasted for three months, and most Sheng-
xian opera troupes left Shanghai to continue their performances elsewhere.
When peace returned to the fallen city, entertainment quickly picked up
in “island” Shanghai, which became crowded with refugees from the sur-
rounding areas. On January 31,1938, the lunar New Year’s Day, the Yuesheng
Theater, the ﬁrst all-female troupe to arrive in the city after the Battle of
Shanghai, successfully staged a performance at the Commercial Inn (Tong-
shang Lüguan), a hostel owned by a Shengxian native where many sojourn-
ers from Zhejiang congregated. Within two months, the troupe moved to
a real theater, the Old Dam Theater (Laozha Xiyuan), where an enthusi-
astic audience ﬁlled the three hundred seats. On the day of the Lantern
Festival (the ﬁfteenth day after the lunar New Year), another all-female
troupe arrived and performed at the Shanghai Little Theater. In April came
58 The Origins of Yue Opera

=== PDF PAGE 80 ===

the third troupe, which performed at the Empire Theater. Encouraged by
the success of these pioneers, more troupes and actresses followed suit; as
women’s troupes moved into the city one by one, male performers and all-
male Shengxian opera disappeared from the scene.
89 Within a few years,
women’s Shengxian opera had managed to become the city’s most popu-
lar theater, while creating a distinctly women’s culture in the city’s public
space. 
The Origins of Yue Opera    59

=== PDF PAGE 81 ===

Chapter 2
The Rise of Feminine Opera
The story of women’s Yue opera is primarily a story about the actresses.
At the beginning of 1938, when the historical stage was set for the rise of
women’s Yue opera in Shanghai, it was the many young peasant girls from
Shengxian who made it happen, but not without casualties.
It was February 21, 1942. In a funeral parlor at 838 Xujiahui Road in the
former French concession, people from all over Shanghai came to watch
the funeral ceremony for Ma Zhanghua, a twenty-one-year-old women’s
Yue opera star. Ma died of tuberculosis, but rumor attributed her illness to
melancholy brought on by slanderous allegations of promiscuity. It was
commonly believed that the slander was spread by Lu Gendi, boss of the
Dalai Theater where Ma performed, in angry response to Ma’s recent mar-
riage and retirement. 
Five years later, when another tragic death of a popular Yue opera star,
Xiao Dangui, captured the headlines, some twenty thousand Yue opera fans
crashed the Leyuan Funeral House, where the funeral ceremony was held.
Xiao had committed suicide when her boss Zhang Chunfan accused her
of having an affair with her male director. Xiao had lived with Zhang for
many years, ostensibly as a concubine. Before she committed suicide, Xiao
Dangui is reported to have stated to a friend: “My body is pure and clean”
(Wode shenti shi qingbai de).
1 This statement reminded the public of the
suicide of Ruan Lingyu, a popular movie star, which occurred in 1935, also
amid a sexual scandal.
Ruan Lingyu’s suicide was one of the ﬁrst instances in modern Chinese
history of a famous actress’s tragic death becoming a topic of widespread
public interest. Ruan’s personal life became the subject of gossip when her
60


=== PDF PAGE 82 ===

ex-husband ﬁled a lawsuit against her and her second husband, accusing
them of adultery and of misappropriating his property. Newspapers pub-
lished stories portraying Ruan as a playgirl who had abandoned her ex-
husband for a wealthier man. On March 8, 1935, International Women’s
Day, one day before she was scheduled to appear in court, Ruan commit-
ted suicide, stating in a suicide note: “Gossip is frightening” (Renyan kewei),
and “I cannot wipe out the wrong done to me if I do not die.”
2
Public fascination with the lives and deaths of actresses involved in
“peach-colored news” (taose xinwen), or sexual scandals, was common to the
cultural scene of Republican era Shanghai. Gossip, as well as reports on
actresses’ personal lives, ﬁlled the pages of tabloids and mainstream news-
papers, feeding the curiosity of a large urban public. Subject to such expo-
sure, actresses often went out of their way to defend their good name and,
sometimes, died for it. If Ruan Lingyu’s statement that “Gossip is fright-
ening” was a protest against the public’s scrutiny of her personal life, then
Xiao Dangui’s qingbai (purity and cleanliness), the description she applied
to her body, represented a moral quality that actresses wished to cultivate
as their public image.
The concept of qingbai, as a moral imperative of female bodily practice,
was derived from a Confucian discourse on female chastity prevalent dur-
ing the Ming and Qing dynasties. It was commonly used to describe women
of good families, but rarely, if ever, public women who worked as prosti-
tutes or entertainers.
3 The gender system during the Ming–Qing periods
formally restricted a woman’s role to provider of reproductive and sexual
services to men, while a corollary concept of chastity valued female bod-
ily purity as opposed to promiscuity. The virtuous woman remained in the
inner chambers, her body belonging only to her husband; public women
who offered sexual services and entertainment as consumable goods were
at the other end of the spectrum. Yet, even among public women, there
was a moral hierarchy structured according to the criterion of chastity. It
is not difﬁcult to ﬁnd, for example, literary descriptions of morally supe-
rior courtesans who remained loyal to the dynasty and faithful to their literati
lovers.
4 Since qingbai was traditionally reserved for certain categories of
women, a woman’s capacity to invoke this notion became a mark of her
high social status in itself. In the 1930s and 1940s, however, female public
entertainers became increasingly concerned with the idea of qingbai. Some
even died after having been accused of being unchaste. This raises several
questions: How did the notion of qingbai become central to the public image
of the actress, a woman considered marginal in traditional social hierar-
The Rise of Feminine Opera    61

=== PDF PAGE 83 ===

chies? What social and cultural conditions of the Republican period ren-
dered qingbai a desirable virtue for public women? And how did this notion
affect the construction of women’s Yue opera?
During the Republican period, actresses were caught up in the cultural
conﬂict between traditional popular eroticism focused on the sexuality of
public women and a reformist ideology that considered the cleansing of
this popular eroticism to be part of the task of building a modern nation.
The new ideology, which called for a redeﬁnition of women’s social role
in general, required that women in theater be redeﬁned as art workers who
would move audiences to contemplate the well-being of the nation, instead
of sexual objects indulging viewers with titillating entertainment. This cul-
tural conﬂict was intensiﬁed by the emergence of mass media that provided
a public domain in which the city’s culture was being formed and re-formed. 
The actress was a new type of woman born in the Republican period,
an era with the slogan of women’s liberation painted on its banner.
Although, historically, there had been numerous female public entertainers,
the Qing state adopted a strict Confucian gender ideology and excluded
women from public entertainment. Female performers of the early Repub-
lican period came from a wide spectrum of social backgrounds, ranging
from revolutionary new women and vocational performers to courtesans
and prostitutes. While female students acted out revolutionary ideas in the
form of Western drama, and upper-class ladies spent their time as Beijing
opera amateurs, for lower-class women entertainers, the stage, only recently
made accessible to them, presented a new opportunity to earn a living. In
the 1920s, female entertainers began to reappear in numbers in teahouses,
on stages, and on screens, adding a new category of performer to the two
traditional types of entertainers, the actor and the prostitute. Despite ter-
ritorial boundaries and sexual differences between actors and prostitutes,
both provided similar services for their male customers. While young male
actors often served as prostitutes, courtesans generally performed music
and dance as well as provided sexual services. The new female actors occu-
pied a niche between the traditional categories of male actors and female
prostitutes, but they were easily viewed as a mixture of the two. Weikun
Cheng, in his article on actresses in Republican Beijing and Tianjin, has
also noted: “Contemporaries more or less understood stage performance
by women in the early twentieth century as a form of urban amusement
associated with erotic indulgence and immoral lifestyles, and believed act-
ing to be a profession full of abuse, exploitation, and social control.”
5
Moreover, historically and also in the case of women performing in for-
62 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 84 ===

eign settlements during the late Qing period, the line separating female per-
formers and courtesans was ambiguous, as public women’s sexuality was cus-
tomarily considered part of their vocation, available for sale alongside their
other entertainment skills. The Republican rhetoric of women’s liberation
may have opened a new niche for vocational female performers, but it hardly
gave them a wholly new, ready-made social status. Centuries-old images of
female entertainers were easily invoked to deﬁne public perceptions of these
new actresses. Traditional gender-role ideas persisted as well.
The traditional consuming public’s voyeuristic gaze, which manipulated
female entertainers’ sexuality, intensiﬁed dramatically in the ﬁrst half of
the twentieth century with the emergence of mass media. Newspapers, mag-
azines, and tabloids ﬁlled Shanghai’s streets, carrying, among other things,
gossip about female stars. If female entertainers’ performances and sexual-
ity were consumable goods available only to a limited number of people
before the emergence of mass media, they were now for sale as a commodity
to a large urban audience. In a sense, the actress became an object of public
erotic fantasy whose sexuality was the business of anyone with access to
media-created gossip.
Being such a heavily eroticized commodity, the actress could not help
but feel the gaze of her fans and the tabloid-reading public in general.
Admittedly, this phenomenon is not uncommon to urban popular cultures
elsewhere. Yet it remains important to inquire into the ways in which China’s
traditional gender system yielded different expectations of actors and
actresses during the Republican period. Since the traditional Chinese fam-
ily system was polygamous, multiple sexual relationships were considered
normal for men. By contrast, women in the traditional family system were
expected to be monogamous. Thus, while actors could engage in multiple
sexual relationships without being stigmatized, the public imagined the
actress as if it were involved in a love affair with her and therefore had a
claim on her sexuality and loyalty.
6 In other words, the public paradoxi-
cally demanded chastity from its favorite actresses as it simultaneously con-
sumed their sexuality.
The public further demonstrated its gender bias in its refusal or failure
to distinguish between an actress’s stage role and her real life. While an
actress’s alleged promiscuity could ruin her reputation and her professional
career, her stage persona as an amorous character could irreversibly dam-
age her personal reputation, making her more vulnerable to slander.
Actresses who played loose characters were somehow more vulnerable to
public gossip about their personal lives than were those who played right-
The Rise of Feminine Opera    63

=== PDF PAGE 85 ===

eous roles. This pattern is evident in many cases, including those of the
three actresses whose deaths were described at the beginning of this chap-
ter: Ma Zhanghua was famous for impersonating amorous young men; Xiao
Dangui was known for playing erotic women; and Ruan Lingyu began her
screen career playing loose women, and many considered her role as a pros-
titute in The Goddess (Shen nü), released in 1933, her most successful. The
onstage persona of an actress often inﬂuenced public perception of her per-
sonality in the real world. In a sense, for the public, the actress became the
characters she performed. The actress was therefore considered a sexual
object during the early Republican period not only because of the tradi-
tional confusion between the actress and the courtesan but also because of
the sexually suggestive performances she often gave. 
These actresses, most of whom came from lower-class backgrounds and
struggled to make a living, had little control over the program and roles
they performed. The basic fact was that they were hired by their male
superiors—a boss, teacher, or employer—for the sole purpose of pleas-
ing a predominantly male audience. Thus, the relationship between the
theatrical representation of women and the alleged moral character of
actresses was embedded in a power relationship informed by class and
gender inequality. The social position of female entertainers is thus artic-
ulated by the visually based, erotic public culture prevalent in early
Republican Shanghai that made female sexuality a commodity for sale in
theaters and mass media.
This public culture of erotica was, however, challenged by a rival intel-
lectual ideology of social and cultural reform. During the early Republican
period, a reformist discourse, supported to a large extent by the National-
ist Republican government after the May Fourth period, elevated theatri-
cal performance to the status of art while degrading prostitution to the level
of a social disgrace.
7 Within this discourse, the victimized Chinese woman,
typically portrayed with bound feet and a weak body, represented the weak-
ness and suffering of the Chinese nation, while the bodies of prostitutes
and female entertainers symbolized the degradation of Chinese society.
Liberating the female body therefore became a central metaphor of a Chi-
nese revolution aimed at transforming the old society and building a wealthy
and powerful modern nation.
8 The bound female body had to be emanci-
pated in order to make healthy women, who would then plant the seeds
of a healthy nation. Traditional eroticism, materialized in the bodies of pros-
titutes and female entertainers, in theaters and in print, had to be suppressed
so that public morality could be attained.
64 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 86 ===

Moreover, the theater itself became a vehicle by which enlightened intel-
lectuals could educate the masses with messages of reform and modernity.
Intellectuals in the early twentieth century experimented with spoken drama
and initiated theatrical reform in Beijing opera. They scripted, directed,
performed, and produced plays in both new and traditional theaters; many
made a name for themselves as performing artists, the most famous being
Ouyang Yuqian, Hong Shen, and Yu Shangyuan.
9 Intellectuals’ participa-
tion in the theater enhanced the status of theatrical performance and exem-
pliﬁed the ideal of the Republican actor who not only entertained the
like-minded with pure art but also enlightened the masses with messages
of national salvation and social reform. Thus, although actors in the past
were placed in the same debased social category as prostitutes, a reformist
discourse now elevated these performers to full social status, while reduc-
ing prostitutes to the level of a social disgrace. 
The actress thus occupied a particularly ambivalent position between
vocational actors, who were striving to achieve higher social status as pro-
fessional artists, and prostitutes, whose livelihood still depended on sell-
ing sexual services. As a commercial performer who needed to make a living,
the actress had to seek favor from a general audience. Yet, at the same time,
she had to reject the public’s voyeuristic gaze, habitually cast on the tra-
ditional female entertainer, which dragged her down to the prostitute’s level.
She was caught between the conﬂicting demands of a public culture of erot-
ica, which viewed her as a sexual object, and a reformist discourse that would
grant her social status only if she could clearly distinguish herself from the
prostitute. 
Ruan Lingyu’s suicide in 1935 and the media event around it illustrate
the dilemma of the Republican actress and the environment Yue opera
actresses were discovering. Ruan Lingyu entered China’s young ﬁlm indus-
try in the mid-1920s. The daughter of a poor commoner family that had
barely been able to put her through middle school, Ruan grew up with the
industry under the guidance of her directors. She began her career playing
prostitutes and “loose women” in ﬁlms such as the 1927 A Nominal Couple
(Guaming de fuqi), directed by Pu Wancang. During the early period, sen-
sational themes dominated the ﬁlm industry, but with the establishment
of Lianhua Studio in 1929, a group of reform-oriented directors and screen-
writers shifted to new themes of social-realistic critique. The ﬁlm industry,
as a form of modern art, was then much inﬂuenced by reformist intellec-
tuals in the 1930s. Perhaps because of her lowly family background, Ruan
Lingyu responded positively to the reform rhetoric. In 1932, she success-
The Rise of Feminine Opera    65

=== PDF PAGE 87 ===

fully played the role of a factory worker in Three Modern Women (Sange
modeng nüxing), directed by Pu Wancang. Over the next two years, she
continued to create images of new women in several other movies. Her
last role, in The New Woman(Xin nüxing), directed by Cai Chusheng, was that
of a progressive journalist who was slandered by the tabloids and ultimately
committed suicide. Ruan’s best performance was arguably in the 1933 ﬁlm
The Goddess(Shen nü), directed by Wu Yonggang, in which she played a young
woman forced into prostitution, after being abandoned by her boy friend
and rejected by family and society, in order to provide for her son’s edu-
cation. Her sympathetic portrayal of the prostitute illustrated the reformist
construction of “the prostitute” and presented a criticism of the social sys-
tem that produced such unfortunate women.
10
From A Nominal Couple to The New Woman, Ruan’s popularity increased;
her art matured, and she became a superstar in Shanghai’s ﬁlm culture. A
pioneer in creating images of new women on-screen, Ruan began to
develop the identity of the professional artist—a female artist who had
emerged from the murky milieu of prostitution to become a new woman.
At the same time, however, gossip about her two marriages and her ex-
husband’s lawsuit against her became a familiar feature in tabloid and news-
paper headlines. Tabloid writers, irritated by the criticism of tabloid
journalism in The New Woman, took revenge on Ruan by producing even
more damaging gossip. Somehow, she came to be portrayed as a loose
woman who lived with or abandoned men for their money, and who did
not care about formally marrying either of her two husbands. These sto-
ries created a sensation among movie fans and sold well to the tabloid-read-
ing public. Shanghai’s shining star fell into moral disgrace. Certainly, her
court appearance, scheduled for March 9, 1935, would have created an even
bigger stir in the mass media. But in the early morning of March 8, Inter-
national Women’s Day, Ruan swallowed an overdose of sleeping pills. On
that very day, most of which she spent barely alive in hospital emergency
rooms, she had been scheduled to meet with a group of middle-school girls
to talk about the meaning of Women’s Day and how to become a new
woman. She died later that day.
Ruan’s death shocked the public. A sense of loss and sympathy for her
dominated public attention and sparked a debate on who was responsible
for her death. Seizing the opportunity, Shanghai’s sympathetic liberal intel-
lectuals launched an attack on the public culture of erotica, blaming a vul-
gar tabloid culture and old-fashioned gender biases for Ruan’s death.
Throughout the debate, liberal intellectuals represented Ruan Lingyu as
66 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 88 ===

not only a typical suffering Chinese woman victimized by a lingering feu-
dal culture but also a talented artist who exempliﬁed a new professional-
ism among actresses. “Ruan Lingyu’s death was not a suicide but a murder,”
one writer asserted. “The gun that killed her may have been made of Zhang
Damin’s [her ex-husband] lawsuit and Tang Jishan’s [her second husband]
deception. But the ones who pulled the trigger were China’s backward (not
progressive) audience members, the old feudal society, and the restora-
tionists who preached the return of the ghost of Lady Meng Jiang
11 urged
concubines to follow a deceased husband by killing themselves, and cursed
women who remarried.”
12
Another commentator pointed out that Ruan’s death was a tragedy char-
acteristic of the age, an age of transition. Living in this transitional age, Ruan
was a victim of lingering feudal values, stigmatized by her remarriage and
alleged loose lifestyle, and a victim of capitalist materialism, betrayed by
her own material desires and seduced by Tang’s wealth. Perhaps more impor-
tant, the author went on, Ruan herself was in a process of spiritual transi-
tion. Just before her death, she had become enlightened through her
participation in social-realistic ﬁlms. This new consciousness created tremen-
dous tension between old and new within her, and, according to this com-
mentator, her weak character failed in trying to deal with the assault of
external forces and inﬂuences.
13 For Lu Xun and Cai Chusheng, director of
The New Woman,in which Ruan had played her last role, it was the “obscene
tabloid press” (huangse xiaobao) that stigmatized Ruan in the public eye and
thereby killed a defenseless woman and a brilliant artist.14
These comments represent the liberal intellectuals’ admonition of public
morality and their reconstruction of the public woman. Reformist discourse
linked the tasks of delivering the actress from the sexual gaze and turning
her into a new professional woman, making them into one process. The-
oretically, the reformist ideology that included the actor while excluding
the prostitute opened the door to full social status for the actress, as long
as her cause was to change the old culture. In reality, however, only when
the actress could establish herself in the public eye as no nü xizi, the pejo-
rative term for the traditional female entertainer/prostitute, would she be
considered worthy by the reformist cause. 
The actress’s social status was therefore shaped largely by her public per-
sona, and it was in this context that qingbai, or purity and cleanliness, as a
moral description of strict female bodily practice became central to the
actress who strove for high social status. The term qingbai became a trope
describing an actress’s morality and hence signiﬁed her relation to the
The Rise of Feminine Opera    67

=== PDF PAGE 89 ===

reformist cause. The ability of an actress to appropriate this term became
crucial to her professional career as an art worker as opposed to a tradi-
tional female entertainer. Failure to appropriate this term could sometimes
be fatal, as the deaths of these three actresses testify. 
Building a public persona of qingbai , however, required the actress not
only to restrict her sexual conduct in her personal life but also to perform
the idea of qingbai onstage. Although the two were related, the stage per-
formance had more inﬂuence in constructing an actress’s public persona,
probably because stage performance was the most direct form of commu-
nication between the actress and the public. Thus, while Ruan Lingyu’s
vulnerability to tabloid attacks on her personal life stemmed to a large extent
from her earlier loose-woman roles, the re-creation of Ruan Lingyu as a
model new woman and artist after her death was also grounded primarily
on the pro-reform new female images she had created on-screen. Performing
qingbai thus became the focal point at which the actress’s need to build her
pure and clean public persona converged with the reformist cause of remak-
ing the theater into an educational tool for nation building and a showcase
of modernity.
This complex relationship among reform discourse, a public culture of
erotica, and the actress’s struggle for survival and status produced a dilemma
that many actresses would not survive. But this situation was also an oppor-
tunity for the actress to rise out of the social and cultural gutter. The con-
cept of qingbai as a traditional moral imperative of women’s bodily practice
had dogged many public women, but, for others, it also provided a strate-
gic weapon they could use to improve their social status. Performing qing-
bai onstage, in the printed media, and in real life helped the actress preempt
public fantasy about her sexuality, fend off unwanted sexual advances from
men, distinguish herself from traditional female entertainers or prostitutes,
and ultimately join with the mainstream reformist cause. 
The concept of qingbai indeed played a crucial role in the rise of women’s
Yue opera in Republican Shanghai. During the 1930s and 1940s, an axiom
circulated among Yue opera actresses: “Carry yourself pure and clean; per-
form honestly and earnestly” (Qing qing bai bai zuoren; ren ren zhen zhen
yanxi). With this axiom, in the course of two generations of Yue opera
actresses, a “traditional” lowbrow form was reshaped by modern conditions,
and the traditional female entertainer was transformed into the legitimate
public ﬁgure of the art worker. Although this dual process took place to
varying degrees in many other minor-opera forms, including Shanghai opera
and Huai opera, women’s Yue opera, because it was an all-woman theater
68 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 90 ===

and greatly popular, most clearly illustrates the actress’s struggle during this
period of transition and the ways in which it informed the re-formation of
modern Shanghai’s public culture. 
Yue Opera Actresses in the 1930s
In the 1930s,Yue opera actresses appeared to many as young peasant girls
performing curious little shows to entertain a carefree metropolitan audi-
ence. The opera was becoming increasingly popular, especially as more
women began to attend, but Yue opera’s status did not compare to that of
Beijing opera and spoken drama. Beijing opera was honored in reformist
discourse as the national opera that symbolized a national cultural heritage.
15
Its patrons were largely members of elites, including government ofﬁcials,
military ofﬁcers, businessmen, and intellectuals, while the famous Beijing
opera stars, such as Mei Lanfang and Zhou Xinfang, were no longer mere
actors in the traditional meaning of the word. These stars were enthroned
as “artists,” and they were also capitalists, in the sense that both owned their
own companies; Zhou also held a long-term lease on one of the best the-
aters in Shanghai, the Grand Golden Theater (Huangjin Daxiyuan). Spo-
ken drama was basically a theater by and for intellectuals. Women’s Yue
opera, in contrast, a theater by women and increasingly for women, was
of marginal signiﬁcance to male elites and was neglected by mainstream
newspapers, intellectuals, and art critics.
The comparative status of the performers was similarly unbalanced. Yue
opera’s women were, in the eyes of the cultural elite, not artists but tradi-
tional entertainers earning a living. Most women’s Yue opera actresses started
in their early teens as apprentices at a training school.
16 During the appren-
ticeship, they received only meager stipends in addition to room and board.
At the end of their apprenticeships, the school often turned into a com-
pany, and the girls became employees of the company owner. The owner
paid the actresses small salaries and pocketed whatever fees he received
from contracting with theaters in Shanghai and Zhejiang for performances.
Toward the end of the 1930s, as large numbers of actresses emerged from
training schools and women’s Yue opera became a regular feature in the-
aters everywhere in the region, theater owners began to contract directly
with actresses, replacing company owners and taking on the role of bosses.
The traditional “company system” (xiban) was replaced by a system in which
the theater owner took control of his own performing team by contract-
ing with individual actresses for ﬁxed salaries over a half-year period. The
The Rise of Feminine Opera    69

=== PDF PAGE 91 ===

company was therefore no longer a ﬁnancially independent entity but was
on the theater owner’s payroll.17 Companies were organized around a few
leading actresses, by the stars or by the theater boss, but few actresses had
their own theaters and none owned her own company.
18
Earning a livelihood was hard work for most Yue opera actresses, per-
haps harder than for actors in Beijing opera and other traditional major operas.
For one thing, unlike many Beijing opera actors, who inherited family tra-
ditions or had relatives in the vocation, most Yue opera actresses came from
poor peasant families and had few family connections in opera circles. As
ﬁrst-generation immigrants from the countryside of Shengxian, Yue opera
actresses also lacked family support in the metropolis. Most of them scraped
by on low salaries, sending the little they could save back to their families
in Shengxian. Those who managed to put together sizable savings usually
brought their families—parents, siblings, and close relatives—to Shanghai.
In order to provide for their families, they often pushed themselves to the
limit. Yue opera theater, unlike Beijing opera, which usually had only night
shows, offered matinee as well as evening performances every day, and each
show regularly lasted three or four hours. This put actresses on a tight sched-
ule and left them little time for other activities, such as going to night school
or socializing. The majority of actresses were illiterate, while the most
schooled among them could be considered only semiliterate (though many
eventually learned enough to do some simple reading and writing).
19 Many
actresses slept backstage in the theater for years after they arrived in Shang-
hai, in part to save rent but also because they generally did not know any-
one who could take them in.
20
This lack of social support networks, shortage of economic resources,
and low literacy severely limited actresses’ cultural and political horizons
and left them with little power to negotiate with various social elements,
such as theater bosses, hooligans, and tabloid reporters. Theater bosses,
many of whom had ties to secret societies or were local bullies themselves,
were the most powerful controlling element in an actress’s life. Often,
actresses were more than employees to their bosses; their bodies and pri-
vate lives might also be subject to the control of these men. It was not
uncommon for a theater boss to take a wife or a concubine from among
the actresses he employed. Given the extremely imbalanced power rela-
tions between the boss and his female employees, a boss’s control over a
famous actress’s sexuality often meant virtual ownership of her person and
the opportunity to exploit her talent and labor for economic gain. An actress
could be very popular among audience members, therefore, but still have
70 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 92 ===

no social status and little control over her economic and personal situa-
tions. Overwork and pressure often led to illness. The death of Ma Zhang -
hua is only one example among many.
Among the most important and worrisome matters for an actress was mar-
riage. For a woman in traditional society, marriage was the most important
event in her life, because the social system made her reliant on her husband
and sons for her support and comfort. While this situation had recently begun
to change during the Republican period, with the slow emergence of pro-
fessional and working women, marriage was still more important than a career
for most women, socially, psychologically, and practically. The Republican
actress, one of these new professional women, enjoyed economic indepen-
dence to varying degrees. This independence, however, was compromised
by the lingering public culture of erotica, in which the voyeuristic gaze
favored young female bodies, effectively making a performing career a young
woman’s privilege and a transitional stage that would invariably end with
marriage. Marriage was also important because it gave the actress standing
in social and familial institutions, redeeming her from her dubious status
as a female entertainer. For all these reasons, getting married and marry-
ing well when still young were priorities for most actresses. Ordinary
actresses often found spouses among the male staff members of their com-
pany or opera circle, while popular actresses often became concubines of
rich men, a traditional practice still common during the early Republican
period. Yet, as the Republican era gender ideology slowly changed the
social practice of marriage, many Yue opera actresses tried to maintain
their ﬁnancial vitality and shunned the status of the concubine. Popular
Yue opera actresses who married during the 1940s, including Yao Shui-
juan, Xiao-Bai Yumei, and Ma Zhang hua, made a point of not becoming
the secondary wives of rich men. Both Yao and Xiao-Bai, as well as many
other popular actresses, suffered varying degrees of abuse and exploitation
at the hands of their husbands. Ma died young, and Yao and Xiao-Bai had
unhappy married lives and raised their children without the presence of,
or help from, their husbands.
21 Despite their ﬁnancial comfort, these
actresses suffered in their personal lives, unable to fully overcome the stigma
attached to women entertainers that left them vulnerable to scorn and abuse
from their often unruly and unreformed husbands.
The public image of Yue opera actresses was also unﬂattering. Portray-
als of actresses as sexual beings subject to the whims of powerful men were
common in contemporary popular literature. One such example is the char-
acter Shen Fengxi in Zhang Hengshui’s novel Fate in Tears and Laughter, pub-
The Rise of Feminine Opera    71

=== PDF PAGE 93 ===

lished in 1929. In this novel, a dagu (a form of story-singing in the Beijing
area) singer, Shen Fengxi, is forced to become the concubine of a warlord,
who tortures her until she goes insane and then abandons her. In addition,
as the fates of Ruan Lingyu, Ma Zhanghua, and Xiao Dangui testify, real-
life actresses also faced personal tragedies, which became fodder for liter-
ary portrayal and public gossip that further debased the image of the actress
in the public eye.
The popularity of Yue opera and its actresses, therefore, appears in sharp
contrast to their marginal social status. Insightful contemporaries were aware
of this contradiction. In his comments on Ruan Lingyu’s death, Lu Xun
remarked that actresses were “very famous but had no power” in the face
of slander and the tabloids.
22 Ma Zhanghua’s death in 1942 only made her
colleagues more acutely aware of their situation. A talented Yue opera actress
who specialized in young male roles, Ma was on the fast track to fame after
coming to Shanghai in the mid-1930s. Her handsome appearance, beau-
tiful voice, and articulate performances quickly made her the leading actress
at the Dalai Theater. In 1941, defying the will of her boss Lu Gendi, Ma
married the son of one of her fans, a certain Mrs. Bao, and retired from the
Dalai Theater. Ma’s father-in-law, a businessman, soon organized a com-
pany for her, and she staged a few successful performances at the Tianxing
Theater. Angered by the loss of his main source of revenue and the object
of his sexual desire, Lu set out to destroy Ma’s good name. He sent hate-
ful letters to the Bao family and paid tabloids to slander Ma by fabricating
stories about her having sexual relations with men prior to her marriage.
Ma was deeply hurt but had no way of retaliating. Eleven months after her
marriage, Ma died of tuberculosis. She was twenty-one years old. Although
a causal connection between Ma’s illness and her maltreatment by Lu was
never clearly established, it was nevertheless popularly assumed.
23
Ma’s death, which caused a public scandal, saddened her colleagues and
triggered Yue opera reforms led by Yuan Xuefen, Ma’s stage partner at the
Dalai Theater and one of the more self-aware and autonomous personali-
ties among Yue opera actresses. Born in 1922, Yuan was the eldest child of
a rural family in Shengxian. The family lived off the father’s meager income
as a primary-school teacher for a few years, but the father lost his liveli-
hood when the Great Depression hit Shengxian’s sericulture economy. Since
the family did not have a surviving son, Yuan was expected to help the fam-
ily as a son would. Her father thus took her to the school where he taught
and made her study for a couple of years, preparing her for work in a silk
factory in Shanghai. The young Yuan Xuefen, however, loved to sing opera
72 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 94 ===

and joined a girls’ opera school at age eleven on her own initiative. Yuan’s
father objected, based on traditional views against female performers, but
her mother backed her, hoping Yuan would eventually bring some cash
income to the family. Yuan did better. After her success in Shanghai, she
provided for the whole family back in Shengxian and properly buried her
father when he died of lung disease in 1942. She then moved her mother
and sisters to Shanghai to live with her and provided for her sisters’ edu-
cation and marriage expenses. The school Yuan joined in 1933 was called
the Four Seasons Company (Sijichun Ban), and Yuan’s basic literacy dis-
tinguished her among her classmates. Then, in 1938, Yuan came to Shang-
hai, her school having become a company, and performed as the second
leading actress at the Dalai Theater. From 1938 to 1941, Yuan collaborated
with the leading actress Ma Zhanghua in many plays on the Dalai stage. 
Yuan practiced the axiom “Carry yourself pure and clean; perform hon-
estly and earnestly.” While Ma played amorous young male roles, Yuan
refused to play loose women and consistently portrayed morally restrained
maidens. In real life, Yuan joined a religious sect and was a declared vege-
tarian and celibate in order to avoid unwanted social and personal engage-
ment. Ma’s conﬂict with Lu and her subsequent death probably shocked
Yuan more than any other Yue opera actress, as she had witnessed Ma’s ill-
fated attempt to control her sexuality, private life, and ﬁnances.
24 Eventu-
ally, it became clear to Yuan that actresses’ ambiguous social status made
them vulnerable to economic and sexual exploitation, and that this ambi-
guity was closely related to the low status of their opera, something for
which they were partially responsible as purveyors of cheap eroticism. Yuan
recalled:
Everyone who is familiar with history knows that Yue opera at the time had
no status to speak o f...  and could not claim to be decent entertainment
let alone to have any positive social utility. Even in a well-developed tra-
ditional play such as The Butterﬂy Lovers, beautiful folklore was riddled with
all kinds of feudal superstitions and fatalist trash....  T h e r e  w a s  a l s o  a  l o t  o f
erotic material in the lyrics that I felt uncomfortable singing. In the widely
circulated version of the story, a preponderance of dross overshadowed the
essence. As early as the late 1930s, when Ma Zhanghua and I were stage
partners, we agreed not to sing these obscene lyrics and did a preliminary
cleanup of the play ....  A t  the time, I could claim no enlightenment but
simply felt deeply hurt having to put on a performance like this. It was humil-
iating to the actresses. If it was only for the sake of making a living, then
The Rise of Feminine Opera    73

=== PDF PAGE 95 ===

there were a lot of other jobs one could choose! This was one of the rea-
sons why I felt reform was absolutely necessary.25
This passage speaks of the beginning of a historical process in which a
generation of young Yue opera actresses took control of their lives and
careers. By the late 1930s, as they established their star status, Ma and Yuan
began to rebel against what they deemed to be humiliating performances,
and in 1942, Yuan turned this spontaneous resistance into self-conscious
theatrical reforms. 
Like many other minor operas of the period, Yue opera production was
rough around the edges, and its content was old-fashioned, replete with
lighthearted ﬂirtations and crude sexual jokes. The theater itself was a noisy
place, and while actresses performed onstage, chaos unfolded in front of
them. The audience chatted, laughed, and told jokes; waiters hurried about
serving tea; and hot wet towels were tossed through the air. These famil-
iar aspects of theater life now made Yuan uncomfortable, especially as she
became aware of ongoing trends in theatrical reform and the connection
between the low social status of actresses and the practices of their the-
ater. In her memoir, Yuan wrote about her experiences in these few years
leading to her decision on Yue opera reform in 1942. Of Shanghai in 1938,
she recalled:
The performances on the stage were nonsensical, badly organized, and with-
out ﬁxed scripts, while the atmosphere in the audience hall and backstage
was simply foul. Patrons ﬂattering their favorite actresses was a prevalent
practice, and [Yue opera] company bosses regularly proﬁted by making
actresses perform at banquets and private homes. I regretted that I had stum-
bled into the wrong profession when I was young and naive, but it was very
difﬁcult to go back to school and change my career. Father was dying of lung
disease, and the responsibility of my family’s livelihood was so heavy that I
had no choice but continued to perform while enduring great pain and self-
sacriﬁce. In order to maintain a quiet and peaceful life, I became a Buddhist
vegetarian and shunned social life. I thereafter changed from a merry and
lively girl to a quiet person who paid attention only to the safeguarding of
her own moral standard....  B u t  bad news kept coming. First, talented Ma
Zhanghua had to leave the Dalai Theater and stopped performing with me;
then her separate performances lacked [personnel] support; ﬁnally, suffer-
ing from poisonous slanders, she fell ill and died in melancholy and bitter-
ness. She was only twenty-one! When the bad news came, I was shocked,
74 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 96 ===

sad, and full of bitter hatred! I was so angry and upset about Ma Zhanghua
dying uncleared of a false charge that I suddenly began to vomit blood. At
the same time, I could no longer stand the theater and company bosses and
my uncle “protector”—all of them disregarded my illness and forced me to
continue performing. I then asked a fellow townsman Liu Baoxiang to accom-
pany me and traveled back home.
26
While the recollection may have been neatened by the passage of time,
and inﬂuenced by the socialist ideology that guided Yuan’s career after 1949,
it indicates many tensions she felt at the time. At home, Yuan rested for
about half a year and kept her father company in his last days. When she
returned to Shanghai, Yuan declared her intention to reform the Yue opera
theater:
The bosses who invited me [to perform] had ideas that were very different
from mine. They would consider nothing but their proﬁt. The most impor-
tant question for me was, however, what had I gained in the past eight years
of performing with the opera school/company? All these meaningless per-
formances of nonsensical stories had given me nothing but hatred and
loathing of the backward and dark institution. I therefore determined to leave
the opera school/company and to start on a new path that would change my
personal life and the destiny of the genre.
27
Love Drama and the Rise of Women’s Yue Opera
The reforms Yuan Xuefen inaugurated in 1942 should be viewed as a phase
in an ongoing urban transformation of Yue opera that centered on two major
tasks. The ﬁrst was to cleanse Yue opera of the lower-class crude eroticism
that had always been part of the minor-opera tradition and to replace it
with a mode of intensiﬁed love drama prevalent in Republican Shanghai’s
popular literature. The second task, discussed in the next section, was to
enhance the production values of Yue opera performances and remake a
shabby peasant form into a dazzling urban spectacle.
Ridding Yue opera of the peasant eroticism typical of minor operas was
a prerequisite to successful performing careers in urban centers. Perform-
ers not only were under pressure to capitulate to the elite demand for moral
sanitization but were also seeking measures by which to cater to the tastes
of an emerging urban middle-class audience, of which women composed
the fastest-growing sector.
The Rise of Feminine Opera    75

=== PDF PAGE 97 ===

Historically, crude sexual themes, performed using a combination of sex-
ually explicit lyrics and gestures, had been a standard feature of male-cen-
tered minor operas. In catering to a lower-class male audience in rural
gambling halls and teahouses, the female body was often described at length,
part by part, creating a visual image of a composite sexualized body. Exam-
ples of such eroticism are abundant in minor-opera repertoires. One piece
of “Ningbo story-singing” (Ningbo tanhuang), predecessor of Ningbo opera,
Floating Boat (Danghu chuan), describes a young man’s overnight boat ride
from Changshu to Suzhou, Jiangsu. Along the way, the young man and
the two sisters who operate the boat each sing a folk song. The young man’s
song, “Eighteen Caresses” (Shiba mo), sung in plain local colloquial,
describes a woman’s body from head to toe, following the movements of
a man’s hand and emphasizing sexual organs.
28 Other examples are found
in such works as Little Cowherd (Xiao fangniu), Selling Coal (Mai qingtan), and
Selling Straw Cradles (Mai caotun), titles from the repertoires of various local
minor operas and their story-singing predecessors. Crude eroticism fea-
tured not only in short pieces about peasant life but also in more devel-
oped narratives, such as He Wenxiu (He Wenxiu), A Pair of Phoenix Jade Clasps
(Shuang zhufeng), and early versions of The Butterﬂy Lovers.
29
Evidence shows that sexually explicit performances were regular features
of minor operas during the 1920s and 1930s, and both actors and actresses
in Shengxian opera performed sexual scenes in Shanghai as well as in towns
and villages in Zhejiang. One good example is a scene from Marriage as a
Result of Three Smiles (Sanxiao yinyuan), performed by women’s Shengxian
opera in Shanghai in the 1930s. Zhou, a young scholar disguised in women’s
dress, plays a trick on a young mistress and, successfully passing himself off
as a woman, is allowed to sleep with her for the night. Zhou then delivers
the following lines in speech and song:
[Speech] Well, well, well. The young mistress has fallen sound asleep.
Let me feel her body all over. 
[Song] One hand opens the blue cotton bed curtain, two eyes steal a look
at the girl’s pretty face; gently feeling her body inside three layers of quilts,
from four directions to touch her breasts, ﬁve ﬁngers reach her privates. Ya!
So frightened am I that I lose control over my six senses, trembling as if I
have gone mad. 
[Speech] Ah ya ya! Feeling my way around, I checked her privates for
urine. Ha, ha, ha! Let me compose a poem: This part is truly amazing. Two
76 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 98 ===

peaks on both sides of a little stream, spring water dripping from the inside
of the cavity, lush grass outside the door. There is water but no ﬁsh; there
is no wood, but the bird can rest. Even if you spend a thousand pounds of
gold, it is not easy to get; many people lose their minds for it.
30
Such passages were scattered throughout the pages of the cheap lyric books
and scripts that recorded established stage performances.31
Educated men’s bias against this kind of unreﬁned eroticism was com-
mon at the time. One contemporary commentator had this to say about
Yuyao opera, a notoriously erotic minor opera developed a little earlier than
Shengxian opera in the same region:
Yuyao opera is the most spineless of all Shaoxing operas,32 for its lyrics are
the most crude, vulgar, and disgusting of them all. It does not care about
costumes or makeup, let alone ideas, and it boasts no acting skills. Ofﬁcials
constantly ban it, but they cannot possibly eliminate it. The opera is greatly
popular among the lower orders of society. They sing in small teahouses in
poor parts of the countryside and remote areas and charge very little, almost
nothing. After a whole day of hard labor, country bumpkins take it as their
chosen form of entertainment and crowd teahouses to see it. The perform-
ers are mostly unemployed wanderers. The opera corrupts public morals
beyond our imagination.
33
Cleansing itself of crude eroticism was only the ﬁrst step taken by any
minor opera aspiring to raise its status in the urban market. The larger ques-
tion was one of stylistic redeﬁnition, or how to turn minor opera’s plain,
rough style into sophisticated expressions of love and desire that would
appeal to urban tastes. Faced with this challenge, the male and female troupes
of Shengxian opera departed, and their respective choices for achieving
artistic sophistication resulted in the decline of all-male and the rise of all-
female troupes.
34 Male Shengxian opera, upon entering Shanghai, followed
the model of major operas, emulating the highly stylized movements of
Beijing opera and Shaoxing major opera while copying their costumes and
stage designs (see ﬁg. 2.1). As one contemporary reviewer observed: “I have
also seen diduopera performed by male actors. It seems as if actors like Wei
Meiduo and Ma Chaoshui sing a little faster than is done in women’s didu
opera. The play they put onstage, the performance, and the stage effect
look very much like that of Shanghai-style [Haipai] Beijing opera. I am
The Rise of Feminine Opera    77

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===

2.1  Stage photo of the all-male Shengxian opera The King of Zhou and His Favorite 
Concubine, Daji (Zhou Wang yu Daji), with Ye Qinfang as Daji (left) and Tang Degui 
as the King of Zhou, Shanghai, 1920s. In style, the all-male Shengxian opera is similar
to Beijing opera but quite different from women’s Shengxian opera, which developed
in the ensuing decades. (Shanghai Yueju zhi 1997) 

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===

afraid that this will shake the very foundations of didu opera.”35 In hind-
sight, it is apparent that male Shengxian opera was going against the cur-
rent of change, but at the time, the actors had few choices available to them,
as major operas, especially Beijing opera, represented the most sophisti-
cated forms of opera at a time when new forms had not yet been estab-
lished. Moreover, male actors of minor operas looked to major operas for
inspiration, because major-opera troupes also consisted mainly of male
actors, having only recently admitted a few actresses. The problem was
that the artistic style of major opera did not ﬁt well with the content of
minor opera. The highly stylized movements of Beijing opera, which por-
trayed the ritualistic and reserved manner of generals and high ofﬁcials,
were not effective when used to express passionate love by the young lovers
featured in minor opera.
The all-female troupes, on the contrary, developed a new feminine
mode of expressing sexual themes. Two mutually reinforcing factors
affected the course of women’s Yue opera in the late 1930s: changes in
the composition of opera audiences and the maturation of all-female the-
ater. The audience for Yue opera changed from a lower-class, male major-
ity to one anchored in a growing core of upper- and middle-class women.
While men of letters despised lower-class eroticism, educated women
probably felt at least uncomfortable with the ways in which minor opera
objectiﬁed the female body. Two points need to be made in analyzing
the tastes of Shanghai’s middle-class women audiences. First, the explo-
ration of sexual and love relations in the framework of marriage and fam-
ily proved more attractive for women, especially middle- and upper-class
women, because women’s sexuality had historically been constructed
within the conﬁned domestic settings of traditional patriarchal society.
They could not, for example, take seriously a sexual encounter such as
the one described in Floating Boat because it is so far outside the family and
marriage context. In fact, the genre, Ningbo story-singing, which featured
such pieces, was notorious for its low taste and was never popular among
Shanghai’s middle-class women audiences. In contrast, the play Marriage
as a Result of Three Smiles was a popular women’s Yue opera play largely
because the sexual scenes are grounded in a complicated narrative of the
traditional scholar-beauty love story that ends with happy marriage. Sec-
ond, explicit sexual scenes, even those presented in the context of love
stories, had to be replaced with more reﬁned expressions of love and desire.
Modern, educated middle-class women, especially those in Shanghai, the
modern metropolis and center for cultural production and consumption,
The Rise of Feminine Opera    79

=== PDF PAGE 101 ===

were trained by popular literature and Hollywood movies to appreciate
romantic love and artful expressions of sexual desire. 36
The rapidly maturing all-female theater met the demands of this new
audience. Actresses, who had long suffered from traditional biases against
women entertainers and the objectiﬁcation of their bodies by the male
erotic gaze, came to identify the sexual obscenities they had been per-
forming as a major source of their own social degradation. As a single-
gender theater, it was easier for women’s Yue opera to replace crude, poorly
integrated sexual jokes and gestures with a more sentimental form of expres-
sion capable of conveying love and passion, as well as erotic desire, as
part of the narrative of marriage and family. The contemporary com-
mentator Xiao Wo compared the different acting styles of women’s Yue
opera and Beijing opera:
With regard to singing, didu opera differs from Beijing opera. In Beijing opera,
different voices are strictly deﬁned for use by different roles, and all the
actors who specialize in a particular role sing in the same voice. In didu opera,
actresses sing with their own natural voices and thereby avoid such com-
mon drawbacks as exaggeration, overacting, and fabrication. On the Yue
opera stage, there is nothing but self-expression of one’s true nature, and
the performers have freed themselves of the shackles of traditional artiﬁcial
movements and singing styles.
37
Zhang Linghui, a Zhejiang native who moved to Taiwan with the Nation-
alist government in 1949, recalled that, in the late 1930s and early 1940s,
major women’s Yue opera companies in Shanghai all trained in Zhejiang and
periodically returned there to perform after settling in Shanghai. In light of
the frequent interactions between Yue opera troupes in Zhejiang and Shang-
hai, Zhang’s observation of women’s Yue opera performances in the Zhe-
jiang countryside should also hold true for women’s Yue opera in Shanghai:
Because it did not represent real life, Chinese opera often did scant work in
portraying the details of human feelings. When a character was supposed
to cry, for example, the actor would simply raise his arm and put his sleeve
in front of his eyes a few times, indicating weeping. All local operas acted
in this manner. When I saw Yue opera in the countryside, however, I found
that the actresses were all able to put themselves into the shoes of the char-
acters they were playing and convey the characters’ inner feelings. In the
case of a tragedy, they often performed the ﬁctional plots with their own
80 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 102 ===

true feelings and cried with abundant tears ﬂowing down their cheeks, mak-
ing the scene virtually real. Their earnest performances often won the hearts
of rural women and made them cry along with the characters onstage.
38
Feature ﬁlms, especially Hollywood ﬁlms, had much impact on minor
operas in search of their own acting style, different from that of the major
operas. Scenes common in these ﬁlms—a handsome young man holding
hands with, hugging, or kissing a pretty young woman, lovers exchanging
affectionate looks—exempliﬁed natural expressions of passion and intimacy
to minor-opera performers as well as to Shanghai’s romance-loving audiences. 
The opera stage was, however, very different from the movie screen. It
is one thing to perform before the camera, and it is another to perform pas-
sionate love before a live audience. It would indeed have been too embar-
rassingly real for both performers and audience members in a society in
which free expression of passion and intimacy between lovers was more
an ideal than a reality for most people. This gap between what people liked
to see and what they could actually do in real life gave women’s Yue opera
a tremendous advantage over other operas with both male and female per-
formers, because expressions of intimacy between women were commonly
displayed in public. As an all-female theater, Yue opera could use a  natural -
istic acting mode and thereby be more expressive when performing inti-
macy and passion in love melodramas, because everyone in the theater knew
that the performers were women (see ﬁg. 2.2). The contemporary Yue opera
critic Cai Yuying explained:
Normally there is nothing surprising about men and women perform-
ing together onstage. But men and women still do not socialize openly in
the countryside. As young girls in Shaoxing are shy when encountering
[male] strangers, it is naturally impossible for them to perform with actors
onstage. Even when they do, they merely perform their duty in a perfunc-
tory manner.
The essence of drama lies in the convergence of the performers and the
roles they play. The story is ﬁctional, but the performance has to be lifelike—
that is what makes for an excellent show. The acting seems more earnest and
real in all-male or all-female theaters. The plot almost always falls apart when
men and women perform together. For the actress certainly wants to avoid
getting too close to the actor she is performing with, and the actor is also too
restrained by various considerations to guarantee a well-acted play. In the case
of romantic love stories, the passion felt by the lovers is reﬂected in the story
The Rise of Feminine Opera    81

=== PDF PAGE 103 ===

line through ﬂirtation, elopement, or sexual intercourse and has to be ade-
quately performed in order to bring the play to a climactic ending. Look at
the performance put on by gender-straight theaters: actors and actresses are
indifferent, listless, and merely muddle through the show. That is why I always
oppose gender-straight theaters. It is not that I am old-fashioned, but rather
that the ﬁnal product does not encourage gender-straight performances.
39
Veteran Yue opera actresses support Cai’s remarks. When asked why all-
female Yue opera became more popular than gender-straight theaters,
82 The Rise of Feminine Opera
2.2  Actresses Xu Yulan (left) and Fu Quanxiang in Gold and Beauty
(Huangjin yu meiren), 1944. The display of intimacy between
heterosexual lovers was tolerated in women’s Yue opera but would
