=== PDF PAGE 104 ===

actresses who performed during that period, such as Xiao-Bai Yumei, Fu
Quanxiang, and Xu Yulan, all gave similar answers: In a women’s theater,
they could act more naturally and passionately without restraint, while it
was awkward to do so with male actors.
40
Thus, under the elite directive to cleanse traditional sexual “obscenity”
from minor operas, and in order to meet the demand of a changing urban
opera constituency that increasingly consisted of upper- and middle-class
female patrons, the all-female Yue opera developed a new mode of acting
that replaced old-fashioned, crude sexual scenes with reﬁned, sentimental
expressions of love and desire.
Modernity as Respectability: The Making of New Yue Opera
If being an all-female theater gave Yue opera a natural advantage in per-
forming the modern drama of love and desire, then it was the conscious
reform efforts of Yue opera actresses in the late 1930s and 1940s that fully
developed the potential of women’s Yue opera and transformed it into a
truly modern master theater of love drama. Before Yuan Xuefen initiated
her reforms, leading actresses Yao Shuijuan and Xiao Dangui had already
begun theirs. In 1938, Yao Shuijuan invited Fan Li, an unemployed for-
mer editor for Dagong Daily (Dagong bao), a mainstream newspaper, to
create new plays for her. Around the same time, Xiao Dangui invited Wen
Zhong, who had had a career in civil drama, to be her playwright.
41 Both
actresses also experimented with the modern stagecraft of ﬁlm and spo-
ken drama. Yuan’s reforms, however, consisted of a more conscious effort
to make Yue opera into a meaningful modern theater, like spoken drama,
and to modernize the Yue opera institution by establishing the opera’s artis-
tic staff.
More clearly than the reforms of Yao Shuijuan and Xiao Dangui, those
of Yuan Xuefen took spoken drama as its model, as Yuan considered it the
most respected theater in town.
I went to see spoken drama and felt totally refreshed. First, the new plays:
it was quite moving to see progressive spoken-drama workers put on edu-
cational, patriotic plays such as Wen Tianxiang[Wen Tianxiang], Ge Nenniang
[Ge Nenniang], and The Spirit of the Revolutionaries [Dangren hun]. Second,
the new forms: spoken drama used complete scripts, regulated rehearsals,
realistic and earnest acting, and modern stage sets. All these revealed a new
The Rise of Feminine Opera    83

=== PDF PAGE 105 ===

horizon to me, a person who lived in isolation, as if an oasis suddenly appeared
in the desert.42
Yuan had attended several spoken-drama shows, including Wen Tianxiang, a
patriotic play about the great general of the Southern Song dynasty who
fought heroically against invading barbarians. The play was staged a few
months before the Japanese entered “island” Shanghai at the end of 1941,
a heroic act given that the territory had been at the mercy of surrounding
Japanese troops long before the actual takeover. Spoken drama appeared to
Yuan to be a much more disciplined theater, as it produced progressive plays,
its stage sets were realistic, its directors exercised control over all details of
the production, its actors and actresses were serious performers, its audi-
ence was quiet and respectful, and it was devoid of obscenity and lighthearted
ﬂirtation. In short, the atmosphere in spoken-drama theater, in contrast to
the chaotic situation in traditional theaters, commanded respect. Spoken
drama had a small audience, but it had the social and cultural status Yuan
hoped to bring to Yue opera and its actresses.
43
Using her market power, Yuan cut a new contract with Lu Gendi, the
owner of the Dalai Theater, and began to implement her reforms in late
1942. Since Yuan was one of the most popular Yue opera actresses, theater
owners competed for her employment, and she agreed to return to the Dalai
Theater only when Lu consented to her reform plan. Lu and other old-
style theater owners had no interest in any substantial reforms, as they were
content with Yue opera’s current conﬁguration, which placed them at the
top of the system and made them the largest beneﬁciaries of the theater’s
popularity. As a local strongman, Lu also had little appreciation for the
reformist rhetoric that characterized people like himself as “feudal” elements
and saw reform as a threat to his power. But since he relied on Yuan Xue-
fen to bring in the proﬁts, he compromised.
44
Yuan’s aim was to reinvent Yue opera as a serious modern art form, along
the model of spoken drama. Her reforms were twofold—to create socially
redeeming content centering on, if not limited to, anti-feudal love stories
and to give the Yue opera stage a fresh appearance. 
She began with three main changes. The ﬁrst was to reorganize the struc-
ture of the theater and its backstage. During the nineteenth century, large
teahouses had been the traditional venues for opera; they served a rich and
powerful male audience who used the performances as occasions to social-
ize over food and drink. The traditional teahouse featured a three-dimen-
sional stage extending outward into the audience’s seats, which were set
84 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 106 ===

around tables, and interactions ﬂowed in all directions on and off the stage.
During the early Republican period, under the inﬂuence of various West-
ern-informed theatrical reforms, large teahouses either closed down or
restructured themselves in accord with Western-style theater forms; at the
same time, new theaters were built to screen ﬁlms and stage spoken dramas
as well as native operas from the 1920s onward.
45 Designed to focus atten-
tion on the performance, new theaters featured a framelike stage and audi-
ence seats facing the stage in rows that discouraged disruption of, and
distraction from viewing, the performance. These new structural features
of theater design met with popular resistance, however, as native-opera actors
continued to joke with the audience, while the audience chatted freely dur-
ing the performance, and actors and non-actors alike streamed freely in and
out of the backstage area during the performance. Theater owners set up
tables in front of the ﬁrst row of seats and attached wooden plates for teacups
and towels to the backs of the seats.
46 In such a traditional theater setting,
the performance was hardly the center of attention but only one of many
things going on in the theater. For Yuan Xuefen, there would be no dignity
for Yue opera and its actresses so long as they performed in such environ-
ments. She decided, therefore, that no one other than actresses and staff
were to be allowed backstage during the performance, and the theater was
to stop serving tea and distributing hot towels in the middle of a show. 
Yuan’s second change was to put the company in control of production
and programming; the theater owner was not to intervene so long as ticket
sales stayed high. The third was to hire literary men as directors and play-
wrights for the company. Lu agreed to the ﬁrst two changes but refused to
pay for directors, playwrights, or stage supervisors. From Lu’s viewpoint,
it was the stars that brought in the audience and there was no need for extra
personnel. Yuan was determined, however, and decided to donate a large
portion of her own monthly salary to pay for these men, whom Lu con-
sidered extra but Yuan considered essential.
47
The establishment of a “staff ” (juwu bu) composed of literary men was a
crucial step toward modernizing the institution of Yue opera—its internal
structure, production process, and productions. The staff served as an infor-
mal headquarters for Yue opera reform. Fan Ruijuan, a leading young male
impersonator and Yuan Xuefen’s stage partner, recalled the early reform period:
During the 1940s, Yuan Xuefen initiated Yue opera reform and recruited
many men of letters, the new-art workers [xinwenyi gongzuozhe]. These men
brought new ideas and new knowledge to local opera. Their involvement
The Rise of Feminine Opera    85

=== PDF PAGE 107 ===

greatly helped the reforms and pushed Yue opera into rapid develop-
ment. . . . At that time, we actresses were mostly young girls from poor peas-
ant families, and our literacy level was low. So we had great respect for the
cultured people in the staff. We addressed them as “teacher” [xiansheng] and
referred to the staff collectively as the “headquarters” of reform.
48
Most staff members were drawn from the ranks of the so-called new-art
youths ( xinwenyi qingnian)—young high-school and college graduates,
mostly from lower-middle-class families, who were deeply inﬂuenced by
the May Fourth New Culture and New Literature movements and who held
a left-leaning liberal outlook. They were fascinated with motion pictures
and actively participated in amateur spoken drama, but they were also
exposed to traditional Chinese forms, such as Beijing opera. When lead-
ing intellectuals and activists left the falling city before the outbreak of the
Paciﬁc War at the end of 1941, these talented petty intellectuals and vet-
erans of the amateur scene, lacking the ﬁnancial means and personal con-
nections to ﬂee, stayed behind and became invaluable resources for Yue
opera reform. During the heyday of the amateur spoken-drama movement,
from 1937 to 1941, patriotic young intellectuals and students took ama-
teur spoken drama to the streets in an effort to mobilize the general pop-
ulation for national resistance.
49 Their involvement, both as actors and as
audience members, in no small part created the spoken-drama boom. After
the fall of “island” Shanghai, however, the spoken-drama mass movement
in Shanghai came to a halt, and only a few professional spoken-drama com-
panies were able to survive on commercial performances.
50 This initial con-
traction of the reach of spoken drama left many spoken-drama veterans
unemployed and available to participate in Yue opera and other minor operas
as directors, scriptwriters, and stage designers. They thus became a vital
link between lowbrow operas and the New Culture theatrical reforms. 
Han Yi (b. 1922) was among the ﬁrst members of Yuan Xuefen’s new
staff.
51 His maternal grandfather, Ma Jianzhong, was the author of China’s
ﬁrst modern grammar book, Grammatical Dictionary by Mr. Ma (Ma shi wen-
tong), and a member of Prime Minister Li Hongzhang’s staff. By the 1930s,
however, the family had lost all its property, with the exception of some
small estates in nearby Kunshan County. When Han’s parents died in 1937,
his uncle absconded with what remained of the family property, and Han
took an apprenticeship with the British-American Tobacco Company, where
his father had worked. He joined a friendship association organized by
underground Communist Party members seeking to involve young employ-
86 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 108 ===

ees in the anti-Japan amateur spoken-drama movement. When the Japa-
nese took over the company after the outbreak of the Paciﬁc War, Han
refused to remain and found a job as a makeup technician for a spoken-
drama company, earning a small monthly salary of twenty to thirty yuan.
He soon moved to Yuan’s company when she offered him a monthly salary
of more than ﬁfty yuan. “To make a living was the ﬁrst concern, and per-
sonal interest was the second reason,” Han remarked, explaining why he
became interested in Yue opera, a lowbrow women’s theater that young lit-
erary men like himself generally looked down upon. 
As Yuan initiated her reforms, literary men such as Han Yi became her
indispensable lieutenants. “Yuan insisted on reform, and we pushed the trans-
formation,” Han stated. The main challenge of the reforms was, accord-
ing to Han, to “modernize” the art of Yue opera; cleansing Yue opera of its
feudal content had become such a natural task that it no longer required
special deliberation. His ﬁrst artistic reform was to replace the traditional
water-based makeup, which tends to be compromised by sweat and tears,
with Western-style waterproof oil-based makeup. Then, Han Yi and his
colleagues studied the history of clothing and designed different costumes
and hairstyles for characters of different dynasties. As both director and
stage designer, Han Yi was most noted for his inventive use of small devices
inﬂuenced by various Western aesthetic styles, which created a setting and
atmosphere that highlighted rather than overshadowed an actress’s per-
formance. In Sister Xianglin (Xianglin sao), of 1946, for example, Han cre-
ated a small half-bridge with an inclining streetlight standing next to it
against the backdrop of a blue sky—a typical Shaoxing street setting within
which the tragedy of Xianglin’s widow reaches its conclusion. Han’s
designs were described variously as “novel and pretty,” “fresh and imagi-
native,” and possessing “a special beauty.”
52
Remarkably, most of the actresses and staff members involved in the
reform process were young people around twenty years of age. “Big brother”
Lü Zhong was only about twenty-six. It was a youthful group, full of energy,
creativity, and a sense of mission. A cartoon appearing in a 1946 publica-
tion commemorating the founding of the Xuesheng Company, led by Yuan
Xuefen, depicted the relationship between the star actresses and the new
staff in terms familiar to urban youth: the cartoon, titled “Snow White and
the Seven Dwarfs,” shows Yuan Xuefen singing atop seven dwarfs—the
seven core staff members (see ﬁg. 2.3). Among the seven was Cheng Rong
(b. 1922), the only woman playwright in the company. A high-school grad-
uate, Cheng was the daughter of a lower-middle-class family from Ningbo.
The Rise of Feminine Opera    87

=== PDF PAGE 109 ===

Her father was a small merchant, and her mother, a housewife, loved Bei-
jing opera and Yue opera. When Cheng was young, her mother often took
her to Yue opera performances because the tickets were cheaper and admis-
sion was free for small children. In middle school and, later, in women’s nor-
mal school (the equivalent of high school), Cheng read Lu Xun, Guo Moruo,
Ba Jin, and other leading writers of the New Literature, but she also loved
classical poems and prose as well as works in translation by well-known Rus-
sian and Soviet writers. She became a fan of spoken drama, participated in
amateur performances, and stopped going to see Yue opera. For a New Lit-
erature student like Cheng, Yue opera appeared vulgar, old-fashioned, and
hopelessly meaningless in content; it was remote from the ideals of China’s
progressive New Culture. After graduating, she took a teaching position in
a primary school to support herself while attending Daxia University’s night
school, and eventually, her love of traditional opera and classical literature
drew her back to Yue opera. Cheng and her mother once attended a show
by Yuan Xuefen and were greatly impressed. Cheng became a great admirer
of Yuan and in 1944 joined Yuan’s company to become the ﬁrst woman
scriptwriter in Yue opera. In Cheng’s eyes, Yuan Xuefen was no ordinary
female entertainer. Unlike other Yue opera actresses, she asserted, Yuan was
more like a female student who loved to read and keep company with female
students, and her performance was earnest and socially meaningful.
53
The company’s only other female staff member, not pictured in the car-
toon, was the radio announcer Chen Shulian (1921–2000). Chen’s family
was originally from Shaoxing prefecture, but Chen was born in Huzhou,
which is also in Zhejiang. After high school, she became a primary-school
teacher and later a radio announcer at the Soviet Union Radio Station (Sulian
Diantai) in 1943.
54 In 1944, she met Yuan Xuefen when the actress came
to the station for a radio program and immediately befriended her. While
Chen still worked full-time at the radio station, Yuan enlisted her as a mem-
ber of the Xuesheng Company, the ﬁrst radio announcer in Yue opera. Each
day, Chen hosted Yuan and other actresses on her radio program, broad-
casting live from the theater or introducing new plays to the radio audi-
ence. Through her familiar voice and intimate approach, the audience was
introduced to the story lines, music, and songs of new plays while learn-
ing about the technical innovations used in the staging. Listeners could also
call in to comment, request a song, or reserve tickets. Chen’s radio pro-
gram became very popular among opera fans and provided an important
new channel of communication between the company and its audience.
55
Perhaps the most inﬂuential member of Yuan’s staff was Nan Wei (1921–
88 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 110 ===

1989), a talented playwright and director. Nan Wei was a native of Chang -
zhou, Jiangsu. His father, probably a small merchant sojourning in Shang-
hai, was fond of Suzhou story-singing. Nan Wei was familiar with the genre
as a child and became a Beijing opera fan in his teenage years, only to shift
his interest to motion pictures and spoken drama in the late 1930s. He
trained and worked as an accountant, but his love for the theater led him
out of business circles. Nan Wei ﬁrst took part in amateur spoken-drama
theater and later joined Yuan Xuefen’s company in 1943. Within three years
of joining the company, he had written and directed approximately thirty
new plays, including Sister Xianglin, and rearranged many traditional ones,
such as The Butterﬂy Lovers.
The reforms that began at the Dalai Theater in late 1942 led to a new
production process, according to which the newly established  staff—
The Rise of Feminine Opera    89
2.3  ”Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs,” a cartoon depicting Yuan Xuefen and her
staff, celebrates the fruitful collaboration between Yuan and new-art youths in 
furthering Yue opera reform. Top: Yuan Xuefen. From left: Xiao Zhang, Lü Zhong,
Hong Jun (Han Yi), Zhong Mei, Zheng Chuanjian, Nan Wei, and Cheng Rong. 
(Xuesheng jinian kan 1946)

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===

consisting of playwrights, directors, and other technical support person-
nel for lighting, costume, and stage design—assumed responsibility for pro-
duction, altering the traditional actor-centered system prevalent in lowbrow
entertainments. In the traditional system, predicated upon the actors’ illit-
eracy and lack of access to scripts, a “master of plays” (shuoxi shifu), usually
an experienced actor or musician with basic literacy who was familiar with
the repertoire of traditional plays, taught the performers the acts and plots
of a play. He assigned roles among the actors, making sure they under-
stood their roles and the timing of their stage entrances and exits. During
the performance, the master posted an “outline of acts” (mubiao) backstage
to remind the performers of the order of the acts. Because only a few fre-
quently staged plays developed ﬁxed lyrics (these were known as the “meat”
[rouzi] and were taught to young actors), it was up to the actors to impro-
vise onstage everything from singing and dialogue to acting. Without ﬁxed
scripts, the show’s quality relied almost entirely on the performances of a
few main actors. Talented actors were given the whole stage on which to
show off their originality and creativity and might sing for what seemed
like forever to those backstage, leaving little time for the others to act. Infe-
rior actors often embarrassed themselves by running out of lyrics and devices
when the more powerful star actors failed to appear onstage as scheduled.
Stage productions were thus crude and unpredictable, and plays were rarely
reﬁned through the accumulated experience of several generations. In order
to cater to the tastes of an increasingly literary urban audience, Yue opera
reform in the 1940s sought to replace this actor-centered system with a
director- and script-centered system modeled on ﬁlm and spoken drama,
at the expense of the traditional improvisation.
At ﬁrst, the new scripts were little better than the outlines of acts. The
playwright arranged stories and plots and wrote some lyrics and verses but
left the rest for the actors to ﬁll out. In the famous 1938 play Tears Over the
Lovesick Land (Lei sa xiangsi di), Yao Shuijuan improvised eighteen lines in
song to express the protagonist’s extreme sadness, although the original
script provided only four lines. In the mid-1940s through the late 1940s,
and after staging many new plays, playwrights and directors slowly began
to establish their authority over the actresses’ habit of improvising onstage.
But the authority of the script and the director would be ﬁrmly established
only much later, as a cultural policy of the new PRC state.
56
The staff also reduced the theater owner’s power over stage productions.
Theater owners were mostly Shaoxing natives familiar with the traditional
Yue opera repertoire and were the ones to decide their theaters’ programs.
90 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 112 ===

With the establishment of the staff and the modernization of the produc-
tion process, Yue opera companies began to obtain artistic autonomy vis-
à-vis the owners.
The combined reform force of actresses and new-art youths resulted in
the creation of a self-proclaimed New Yue Opera (Xin Yueju), which sig-
naled a higher level of professionalism and literary sophistication. Actresses,
with the help of the director, became self-conscious practitioners of mod-
ern artistic methods such as character development. Fan Ruijuan recalled
how Nan Wei helped her in this regard:
Nan Wei opened up the actresses’ minds by telling us to learn about dra-
matic theories and cultivate our artistic elements. During the 1940s reforms,
I heard that Stanislavsky’s books specially dealt with theater direction. Hav-
ing no idea how hard his books could be, I asked Nan Wei to get me a copy
of On Actors’ Self-Cultivation [Lun yanyuan ziwo xiuyang (in Chinese transla-
tion)] and started reading it. My literacy level was low, and theoretical works
were difﬁcult for me. From time to time, I sought Nan Wei’s help. He was
always patient. He taught us how to study, understand, and perform a wide
range of characters, especially how to create various characters through our
careful observation of people in real life.
57
Under Nan Wei’s direction, Fan played roles ranging from romantic young
scholars to opium addicts, bandits, and simpletons and emerged as one of
the best Yue opera performers of her time. Zhang Guifeng, another lead-
ing actress who specialized in old men’s roles, at ﬁrst hated memorizing lines
and following a script but eventually came to terms with the new practice.
When Nan Wei assigned her the role of Wei the Bald in Sister Xianglin, con-
ventionally a clown’s role, Zhang made two trips to a teahouse on a dock
in Pudong to observe how hooligans acted and spoke and was able “to cap-
ture the essence” of her role.
58 With the director’s encouragement, second-
and even third-tier actresses also became concerned about better acting,
and their efforts raised the quality of each play and the professional level
of Yue opera overall.
59
Though heavily inﬂuenced by spoken drama, New Yue Opera  none -
theless also took inspiration from such traditional theatrical arts as Kun
opera, another native opera of the Jiangnan region popular during the Ming
and early Qing periods and known for its elaborate love stories. For the
actresses, popular rejection of spoken drama in favor of Chinese operas
made it clear that Yue opera should not become spoken drama plus singing.
The Rise of Feminine Opera    91

=== PDF PAGE 113 ===

While rejecting the highly formalized postures of the Qing major operas
represented by Beijing opera, they found inspiration in Kun opera’s ﬂuid
dance style. Yuan Xuefen invited the Kun opera master Zheng Chuanjian
to be the company’s director of choreography, and Zheng taught Yue opera
actresses to use Kun opera postures in their performances. Kun opera’s
unique body movements and postures greatly enriched Yue opera body lan-
guage and contributed to a ﬂuid acting style that integrated song and dance.
This particular mode of traditional stylistic acting ﬁt well with the modern
naturalistic expression of love and desire, as Yue opera actresses creatively
used traditional singing and dancing to enhance the aesthetic intensity of
the modern romance.
60 As a result, a native opera that combined a Chi-
nese-inﬂuenced acting style with Western-inﬂuenced stage design and the-
ater discipline appealed to metropolitan audiences by means of its refreshing
modernity and familiar native sensibility.
With Yuan Xuefen’s company leading the way, other major Yue opera com-
panies implemented similar reforms, and New Yue Opera ﬂourished.
61 Dur-
ing the 1940s, more than one hundred modern productions of  anti-feudal
love dramas enhanced by innovative stage sets and ﬂuid singing and dance
reshaped Yue opera into an exciting and respectable modern theater.
Patriotism, Eroticism, and Love Drama in Women’s Yue Opera
The Yue opera repertoire formed during the late 1930s and 1940s contained
four main thematic subgenres: patriotic and revolutionary plays, family
morality plays, erotic plays, and romantic love plays. The development of
this repertoire further illustrates a dynamic relationship among Yue opera
actresses’ struggle for survival, status, and respect; the elite rhetoric of the-
atrical reform; and the demands of a changing urban audience that trans-
formed Yue opera into a key sector in Shanghai’s popular-entertainment
industry. 
In order to enhance the status of Yue opera as a serious theater, actresses
eagerly staged patriotic plays, though they often embellished these plays
with love stories in recognition of audience tastes. The ﬁrst patriotic Yue
opera play was Hua Mulan (Hua Mulan), staged in 1938 in “island” Shang-
hai at the height of the resistance culture. Not incidentally, Hua Mulan was
also the ﬁrst women’s Yue opera play that made use of a playwright and a
director and thus marked the beginning of Yue opera reforms. Hua Mulan
was based on a folk song that originated in the late sixth or early seventh
century. In the folk song, Mulan, the daughter of an old village couple, dis-
92 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 114 ===

guises herself in men’s clothing and joins the army in place of her aging
father. When she returns triumphantly after twelve years on the battleﬁeld,
the emperor wants to make her a minister, but Mulan wishes only to return
home. She surprises her fellow soldiers when she emerges from her home
in women’s dress. While the original folk song conveys close feelings between
Mulan and her parents and praises her talents in a simple and joyful tone,
subsequent renditions elaborated on Mulan’s moral perfection, noting that
she was both loyal to the state and ﬁlial to her parents.
62 In modern times,
Mulan came to be portrayed as a heroic woman who defended her nation
by ﬁghting alongside men. In the wartime context of the late 1930s, such
updated versions of the story of Mulan became popular, because they res-
onated with several concerns prevalent in “island” Shanghai: nationalism,
patriotism, women’s liberation, and, indeed, romantic love. The legendary
female heroine, who dressed in men’s clothing and fought against barbar-
ian invaders, found her way into ﬁlm, spoken drama, Beijing opera, women’s
Yue opera, storytelling, and many other forms of entertainment.
In 1938, two Yue opera companies staged the story of Mulan in “island”
Shanghai. One version was adopted from Mei Lanfang’s Beijing opera piece,
while the other, more well-known version was staged by Yao Shuijuan,
scripted by Fan Li, and directed by Zhang Zifan (see ﬁg. 2.4). Fan Li was
a native of Hangzhou and had been a local newspaper correspondent for
the Dagong Daily. After Japanese troops occupied Hangzhou, Fan ﬂed to
Shanghai, where he was introduced to Yao Shuijuan, who was seeking a
playwright to help her develop new plays. Hua Mulan was Fan’s ﬁrst play.
Yao Shuijuan’s show, which combined patriotism with romance, was well
advertised by both tabloids and radio stations. Advertisements also ran in
the entertainment section of the American-operated local English-language
newspaper, The China Press, which published a brief introduction to the play
alongside a photograph of Yao Shuijuan dressed as Hua Mulan in military
uniform. The introduction reads: “Miss Yao Shui-chuan [Shuijuan], noted
Chinese actress, who is known as the Queen of Shaoshing drama [Yue
opera], will make her debut tomorrow in ‘Hwa Mo-lan’ [Hua Mulan], China’s
Jeanne d’Arc, at Tien Hsiang Theater, in the Coal Guild Building.”
63 The
Yue opera play opened on schedule and ran for about a month, with per-
formances twenty-seven nights in a row. Given that Yue opera companies
usually changed programs every two weeks—sometimes every week if ticket
sales were slow—Hua Mulan was indeed a success. The play brought new-
found status to Yao Shuijuan and to women’s opera in general. By produc-
ing a patriotic opera, Yao Shuijuan and her actress colleagues demonstrated
The Rise of Feminine Opera    93

about a legendary woman warrior. (Courtesy Mr. Wei Shaochang)

their patriotism and progressive ideas and hence distinguished themselves
from old-fashioned popular entertainers (see ﬁg. 2.5).
The Japanese occupation made it impossible to stage patriotic plays, but
after the war, women’s Yue opera troupes continued to produce plays that
combined grand political narratives with love stories. For example, in Sad
Moon over the Liao Royal Palace(Qiliang Liao gong yue), of 1946, Yuan Xue-
fen depicted a patriotic court maid who fought off evil ofﬁcials for the good
of the state. In 1947, Prince Liu Chen(Guopo shanhe zai), with Xu Yulan cast
as Prince Liu Chen, criticized the corruption and decadence of the head
of an ancient state. Yin Guifang, a leading actress specializing in young
male roles who joined in the New Yue Opera reforms in the mid-1940s,
created a series of images of revolutionaries, including Shi Dakai, the

(Mulan congjun), 1944. The staging of this patriotic

=== PDF PAGE 117 ===

great general of the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom, in Shi Dakai(Shi Dakai),
in 1946; the Guangxu emperor, the tragic reformist emperor of the late
Qing, whose efforts were quashed by Empress Dowager Cixi, in Emperor
Guang xu and His Favorite Concubine Zhenfei(Guangxu Di yu Zhenfei), in 1946;
and Sun Kexian, the great Ming loyalist who fought the invading Qing
army to his death, in Ge Nenniang (Ge Nenniang), of 1946. These plays,
though usually not big hits, were noticed and warmly received by inﬂuen-
tial intellectuals.
64
Family morality plays had been the mainstay of the Yue opera reper-
toire, and these included traditional plays such as Pearl Pagoda and Emerald
Hairpin as well as such modern plays as The Dark Family (Hei’an jiating), of
1944,65 and The Prodigal Son (Langdang zi), of 1947 . These plays were
intended to criticize social ills, such as drug abuse and gambling.66 As most
traditional family morality plays had a love story at their core and were
organized within the structure of love drama, they may be considered a
variation in the growing subgenre of the love drama. 
The mainstay plays of the Yue opera repertoire developed around per-
sonal and private narratives centered on love and desire. The process went
in two directions: one continued the traditional public culture of erotica
by turning the “vulgar” (su) into the “reﬁned” (ya), while the other displaced
the earlier crude eroticism with a new mode of intensiﬁed love drama. While
the reﬁned eroticism maintained the prior emphasis on “sex” (xing), love
melodrama made qing, or feelings of love, its focus and sex a subtext. The
development of these two subgenres were, however, inﬂuenced by the May
Fourth ideology of social and cultural reform that sought to eliminate the
public culture of erotica but was friendlier toward love drama. 
The subgenre of erotic plays in Yue opera, performed most notably by
the famous actress Xiao Dangui, explored women’s sexual desires. Xiao, an
excellent singer, actor, and acrobatic dancer, became a Yue opera super-
star around the same time as did Yao Shuijuan. Between the late 1930s and
her tragic death in 1947, Xiao successfully played roles ranging from the
female warrior in White-Water Shore (Baishui tan), to a medusa in The City of
Sizhou (Sizhou cheng), to the cross-dressing ﬁgures Cai Lanying in The Eagle-
wood Fan (Chenxiang shan) and the title character in Meng Lijun. Her most
acclaimed roles, however, were amorous women, such as the lonely and
desirous Yang Yuzhen in The Imperial Concubine(Guifei zuijiu), the seductive
Widow Ma in Widow Ma’s Inn(Ma Guafu kai dian), and the oversexed Pan
Jinlian in plays adapted from the Ming dynasty novels The Water Margin
(Shuihu zhuan) and Plum in a Golden Vase (Jinpingmei).
96 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 118 ===

In the early 1940s, Xiao was among the ﬁrst women’s Yue opera actresses
to use playwrights and directors. She was slow, however, to incorporate
such intellectual themes as nationalism into her plays. While Yuan Xuefen
and Yin Guifang explored the path of Yue opera reform and cultivated a
new constituency in female students and professionals with new-style love
dramas, Xiao Dangui’s repertoire, composed largely of sexually provoca-
tive plays, was popular among middle-class men and housewives. The female
images she created onstage were of lustful, daring women seeking sexual
satisfaction, and her performances explored a type of female eroticism
focused on sexual desire as the basis for, not the result of, the sentiment of
love. Her approach was thus distinguishable from both traditional crude
eroticism focused on sex only and trendier sentimental love dramas. This
kind of eroticism was common in women’s Yue opera at the time. The fol-
lowing contemporaneous tabloid description of an erotic scene from a pop-
ular play put on by Xiao Dangui and her stage partner Zhang Xiangqing
provides some idea of Yue opera eroticism of the 1940s:
The famous Yue opera actresses Xiao Dangui and Zhang Xiangqing came
to perform at the Zhedong Theater, and the audience in this middle district
loved their shows, especially the two plays at which they especially excel,
Widow Ma’s Innand Stir Up the Sanya Garden[Da’nao Sanya Yuan]. Tickets for
these shows were always sold out. They are currently staging Stir Up the Sanya
Garden again. Xiao Dangui is exceptionally beautiful and sexy in her bright
red cheongsam and high-heeled leather shoes; Zhang Xiangqing, in her long
white silken gown and Panama-style hat, deserves to be called a handsome
young man. Their performance of the ﬂirtation scene in the bedroom is really
impressive; especially original is the way they take off each other’s clothes.
Xiao initiates it by taking off Zhang’s coat, and Zhang then proceeds to take
Xiao’s clothing off, until they both are wearing only their underclothes. Even
the queen of ping opera, Bai Yushuang,
67 would have to admit her inferior-
ity before such a “bold” performance.68
The Yue opera fan Wu Ping saw similar performances of the famously,
or notoriously, erotic Widow Ma’s Innin the 1940s, and she loved it.69 In the
story, Widow Ma desires a guest at her inn, Di Renjie, a young scholar on
his way to the capital to take the civil-service examination.70 At midnight,
the widow, driven by uncontrollable desire, goes into Di’s room and tries
to seduce him—hence, the famous seduction scene.
71 Xiao Dangui was not
the only actress to play Widow Ma, as the play was widely popular and was
The Rise of Feminine Opera    97

=== PDF PAGE 119 ===

staged by almost every Yue opera company, but she was considered the best
of them all—passionate and sexual, yet sensitive and digniﬁed.
Explicit sexual expression was garbed in enhanced stage sets and cos-
tumes, exquisite performances, reﬁned lyrics, and romantic settings, as seen,
for example, in Record of Retributions (Guobao lu), with Mrs. Diao’s desire
for Wang Wen, a young scholar next door and a friend of her husband’s.
In the garden scene, Mrs. Diao, played by Xiao Dangui, sings reﬁned lyrics
expressing sexual desire and lovesickness while playing her lute, and Wang
Wen, played by Jia Lingfeng, accompanies her on a xiao, a vertical bam-
boo ﬂute, from the little hill in his own garden. Both sing of their desire in
romantic but sexually explicit lyrics.
72
Such explorations of female sexuality were very popular but ill fated in
Shanghai in the 1940s. It was said that Xiao always had a full house when-
ever she performed Widow Ma’s Innand that she always performed it when-
ever box ofﬁce sales were slow. Widow Ma’s Inn and other similar plays
prospered throughout the 1940s, with the support of Shanghai audiences
and the absence of governmental attention: While the Japanese occupation
authority simply did not seem to care, the Nationalist government of the
postwar period was too busy cracking down on the Communists to clean
up eroticism in popular entertainment.
73 The ofﬁcial ideology of the
Nationalist government and the elite nation-building rhetoric of theatri-
cal reform, however, deemed eroticism harmful to public morality. As the
Republican discourse of women’s liberation generally neglected the ques-
tion of women’s sexuality, paradoxically, the traditional virtue of qingbai
continued to set the moral standard for good women.
74 Thus, in the late
1940s, both Republican government and elite leftist intellectual ideologies
increasingly pressured Yue opera, and for that matter any lowbrow enter-
tainment, to refrain from sexually explicit acting, be it crude or reﬁned.
This trend ﬁnally culminated in the banning of erotic plays after 1949 by
the newly established PRC state.
Because patriotic plays relied on love stories to sell tickets and popular
erotic plays gave Yue opera a bad name, love drama became the dominant
subgenre during the process of Yue opera reform. In an article published in
Yue Opera World (Yueju shijie), Xu Jin, a young Yue opera playwright, dis-
cussed three kinds of Yue opera plays. One was the conservative kind, which
preserved the essence of traditional Yue opera but was behind the times.
Another was the radical kind, which went too far toward making Yue opera
into an art, neglecting the fact that Yue opera as a popular form was never
just a showcase for “pure art.” The third type of Yue opera play, Xu Jin wrote,
98 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 120 ===

catered to both audience tastes and the needs of the age. He asserted that
it was wrong to say that catering to the audience meant that reform was not
taking place: “Yue opera is gradually progressing, and its audience is
 growing—this is reform. It would be a failure if we raised artistic style to a
level that the audience could not understand.”
75 By “conservative” plays, Xu
meant the reﬁned erotic play represented by the famous Xiao Dangui as well
as traditional lower-class eroticism; by “radical,” he meant new plays loaded
with too much political message. It is not surprising that, with such an under-
standing, Xu Jin became a master of the Yue opera love melodrama, con-
tributing to the creation of several masterpieces, including The Butterﬂy Lovers
and Dream of the Red Chamber. The lyrics he wrote and edited for these plays
were among the best in Yue opera history—they were of high literary qual-
ity and were neither so reﬁned that they intimidated ordinary audience mem-
bers nor so vulgar that they repelled those who were better educated.
The leading actress Yin Guifang also spoke about the ways in which the
relationship between ideological pressure and audience demands inﬂuenced
Yue opera reform. To answer the call that Yue opera incorporate the nation-
state narrative into its story lines, Yin placed patriotic generals, warriors,
and scholars on Yue opera stages. In response to audience demands, Yin
made her characters sentimental, caring, and loyal lovers. Yin Guifang’s
reforms stressed the importance of retaining the “essence” of Yue opera, as
suggested in an interview with the mainstream newspaper The New Citizen
Evening News(Xinmin wanbao) on August 22, 1947: “I am not only for reform
but moreover hope to eliminate and not just amend the obscenities com-
mon in Yue opera. I uphold a principle, however, that the Yue-ness of Yue
opera must be retained.”
76 What was the “Yue-ness” Yin wanted to uphold?
From her other writings, it is quite clear that she considered love stories to
be the essence of Yue opera. Earlier that year, she had written: “Looking
back, [I see that] the larger part of every play has always consisted of pas-
sionate love between young men and women. This is how Yue opera won
a mass audience.”
77
Love stories were not necessarily conventional and were hardly mean-
ingless, despite the opinion of the cultural elite. In fact, the ﬁxation on love
in modern popular culture reﬂected the psychological needs of an urban pop-
ulation witnessing drastic changes in gender and sexual relations. Contrary
to the impression that love stories in women’s Yue opera, or in all popular
genres, were monothematic pabulum, the subgenre of love drama included
varieties such as exotic plays, cross-dressing plays, and love tragedies. 
The exotic play was often a mix of love story, patriotic theme, and adven-
The Rise of Feminine Opera    99

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===

ture set in an exotic time and place. One of the most popular plays of this
sort, Desert Prince (Shamo wangzi), of 1946, had a script by Xu Jin and was
directed by Zhong Min. Adapted from a story in the Arabic classic The
Thousand and One Nights, it tells the adventures of a young Mongol prince
forced to ﬂee into the desert after a rebellious tribal chief murders the king
and usurps the kingdom. On his wanderings, the prince meets and falls in
love with a young princess. But the tribal chief captures the princess and
renders the prince blind by means of black magic. The prince then allies
with the princess’s father, another tribal leader, and eventually overcomes
the rebels. As soon as he restores the kingdom, the prince gives up his crown
to search for his lover. At the end, he ﬁnds her in the desert, and her pas-
sionate embrace restores his eyesight. The two live happily together there-
after, and there is great peace and prosperity under their rule. This play
took Shanghai audiences to the Mongolian desert, displaying an exoticized
Mongol people in colorful native costumes, all the while employing a fairy-
tale love-adventure story line that was not so different from that of more
conventional love stories. The images of the beautiful, passionate, and heroic
Mongolian prince and princess that Yin Guifang and her partner Zhu
Shuizhao created on the Yue opera stage were at once exotic and familiar,
adventurous and conventional, and catered to the urban audience’s passion
for a combination of fantasy and comfort. By creating patriotic Mongol
heroes, the play was also politically correct in terms of government pol-
icy regarding Mongolia and the multiethnic character of the Republican
Chinese state. This was a love story that also spoke directly to the recent
history of Japan’s failed attempt to make inner Mongolia an “independent”
state during the Second World War.
The Yue opera cross-dressing performance illustrates how love rela-
tionships are explored in the seemingly meaningless cross-dressing play.
Cross-dressing, mistaken identity, and cross-gender acting are common to
both Chinese and English theatrical traditions, the most notable English
example being the plays by William Shakespeare that employed all three
techniques. Women’s Yue opera also used all three techniques, albeit with
an all-female cast instead of the Shakespearean theater’s all-male cast. Bei-
jing opera, in contrast, featured female impersonators, but transvestite plays
were few and represented a relatively insigniﬁcant part of its repertoire, as
love drama was never a strength of Beijing opera. Transvestitism was not
only a common device in Yue opera but also an essential part of the plot
in several of its most popular plays, including two masterpieces, The Butter -
ﬂy Lovers and Meng Lijun. 
100 The Rise of Feminine Opera

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===

Although set in the Yuan dynasty, Meng Lijun was a modern play put
together and produced by Shengxian opera in Shanghai for a modern urban
audience. Shengxian opera ﬁrst staged this play in 1921, at the height of
the May Fourth New Culture Movement, when the discourse of women’s
liberation was still relatively new. Along with The Butterﬂy Lovers and Emer-
ald Hairpin, Meng Lijun was instrumental in making Shengxian opera a pres-
ence in Shanghai’s cultural market, and it became one of the most popular
plays of women’s Yue opera in the ensuing decades.
78
Meng Lijun tells the story of a talented girl, Meng Lijun, who ventures
into the world of men seeking justice and revenge for her father and her
ﬁancé, both high ofﬁcials persecuted by bad elements in the court of the
Cheng emperor. Lijun disguises herself as a man, passes the civil-service
examination, in which she takes ﬁrst place, and becomes the emperor’s
trusted prime minister. During her tenure, she manages to rehabilitate her
father and ﬁancé and punishes the wrongdoers. The emperor, however,
comes to realize that Lijun is a beautiful woman and desires to make her
his imperial concubine. Lijun remains loyal to her ﬁancé, however, and at
the end, she resumes her life as a woman and marries her ﬁancé with the
emperor’s blessing. 
In 1999, the Shanghai Yue Opera House staged a production faithful to
a late-1930s audio recording of the play, employing an all-female cast. The
double-layered sex play is intriguing: three actresses play out a heterosexual
romance involving two men and a woman pretending to be a man. The pro-
duction culminated in the act “Touring the Imperial Garden” (You Shanglin
Yuan),
79 by which point the emperor, who has become suspicious of his min-
ister’s true gender, orders Lijun to accompany him on a tour of the imperial
garden, where he tries to seduce her. As played by the actresses Zheng Guo -
feng (as the emperor) and Shan Yangping (as the transvestite minister), the
emperor has the upper hand, and the minister must rely on her wit and moral
courage to protect her chastity—the foundation of her dignity as a person.
The young emperor is affectionate and lustful, and he genteelly seeks his
objective by means of teasing, tricks, and a little coercion at times. Since
the emperor is played by a woman, the character’s desire for Lijun may be
seen as same-sex as well as opposite-sex staging. The minister is shy and
reserved, as is her nature as a woman, but at the same time brilliant and obe-
dient, as beﬁts her adopted nature as a male subordinate. Signiﬁcantly, while
her obedience also strongly suggests her femininity, when in masculine guise,
her display of wit only highlights her desperate situation—she has no way
out but to become the emperor’s sexual captive. Lijun is rescued at the last
The Rise of Feminine Opera    101

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===

moment when the emperor’s mother puts a stop to her son’s mischievous
behavior and returns everything to order. The performance not only com-
forts audience members by driving home the traditional virtues of loyalty
and chastity but also takes them into a fantasy world of dangerous pleasures,
namely, seduction involving ambiguously gendered characters carried out
according to the rules of intricate and ﬂuid sex games (see ﬁg. 2.6). 
Love tragedies, represented by the three classic masterpieces The Butter -
ﬂy Lovers, Tale of the Western Chamber (Xixiang ji), andDream of the Red Chamber,
were perhaps the most inﬂuential of Yue opera’s love dramas. The remak-
ing of The Butterﬂy Lovers illustrates the transformation of Yue opera into a
modern master theater of love drama, which took place during the Yue opera
reforms. In 1945, the Xuesheng Company restaged the story of the but-
terﬂy lovers as The T ragic Story of Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtai (Liang Zhu
aishi), scripted and directed by Yuan Xuefen. The main modiﬁcation was
the use of modern stage design to enhance production values. The play
was staged again in 1946 with further changes in content and lyrics.
80 As
Yuan Xuefen explained:
The T ragic Story of Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtai had been staged at the Nine-
Star Theater [Jiuxing] last year. This new version of the play is again dif-
ferent. Last time, as we were in such a hurry, we only reformed the stage
designs but left the lyrics and structure unchanged. I felt that further reform
of many old elements was needed to make the play more ideal, and that is
why I rearranged it again.
It is hard to say if the new T ragic Story of Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtaiis sat-
isfactory, and I myself am not sure. But we tried hard to make the story more
real and exciting, by eliminating many superﬂuous and obscene elements
and by integrating new ideas into the play as much as possible. 
. . . If the story of Liang and Zhu were real, I believe people would most
likely be sympathetic to their tragic story and lament the perils of arranged
marriage.
81
Emphasizing the theme of free love and marriage, the play eliminated such
“feudal” elements as bawdy eroticism and plots related to reincarnation and
other ideas from popular religions. It ended with an emotional climax, with
Yingtai crying and pouring her heart out before Shanbo’s bier.
82
The play created an image not only of an independent-minded woman
in Zhu Yingtai, played by Yuan Xuefen, but also of a woman’s man in Liang
Shanbo, played by Fan Ruijuan (see ﬁg. 2.7). Fan came into her own as an

artist when she reinterpreted the role of Liang Shanbo and subsequently
became known for portraying sincere and kind young scholars. In folk ver-

Shanbo is comically cunning, salacious, and dull at the same time because
his soul has been stolen from him by order of the Jade Emperor so that he
will be unable to ﬁgure out Zhu Yingtai’s true gender during the three years

based on superstition, Fan Ruijuan transformed Liang Shanbo into a book-
ish young man, a little naive regarding women, but sincere, tender, and
affectionate upon realizing his love for Yingtai. Fan’s portrayal earned Liang
Shanbo a nickname from the audience, “the adorable fool” (keai de shagua).
83
104 The Rise of Feminine Opera
2.7  Yuan Xuefen as Zhu Yingtai (left) and Fan Ruijuan as Liang Shanbo in The T ragic
Story of Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtai (Liang-Zhu aishi), 1946. In the 1940s, Yuan Xuefen
and her stage partners transformed this centuries-old folk story into a celebration of
love and loyalty. (Yuan 2002)

=== PDF PAGE 126 ===

In the 1949 version of The Butterﬂy Lovers, staged by the Dongshan Com-
pany, Fan Ruijuan, as Liang Shanbo, and Fu Quanxiang, as Zhu Yingtai,
added a ﬁnal act to their performance. On her way to Ma’s household as
the new bride, Zhu Yingtai stops at Liang Shanbo’s tomb to mourn him
one last time. As Yingtai weeps, heaven responds with a thunderstorm, and
a bolt of lightning strikes the tomb and splits it open. As Yingtai jumps
into the grave, Ma Wencai and others try to hold her back by grasping her
skirt. After Yingtai disappears into the tomb, pieces of fabric torn from her
skirt become a pair of beautiful butterﬂies (played by the two stars garbed
in colorful butterﬂy dresses), happily courting each other under a rainbow
in a graveyard of ﬂowers in full bloom.
84
The artistic achievement of New Yue Opera in the 1940s also laid the
foundation for Yue opera’s continuous development in the 1950s when, with
the support of the PRC state, the genre gained national and international
inﬂuence. At a 1952 opera conference held in Beijing, The Butterﬂy Lovers
thrilled a national audience and brought home multiple awards for script,
music, stage design, and best actress. In 1953, the play was made into China’s
ﬁrst 35 mm color ﬁlm, and its 1954 premiere in Shanghai attracted a record
1.55 million viewers. It won the award for musical ﬁlm at the Eighth Inter-
national Film Festival in Czechoslovakia in July 1954 and an award at the
Ninth Edinburgh International Film Festival in Scotland in August 1955.
85
The ﬁlm was distributed internationally and caused a sensation in Hong Kong,
while the play toured nationwide and traveled to North Korea, the Soviet
Union, and Eastern Europe as part of a mission representing theatrical arts
from China. One performance in Berlin evoked such enthusiasm from the
audience that the actresses had to answer twenty-eight curtain calls.
86
The Rise of Feminine Opera    105

Chapter 3
Patrons and Patronage
The rise of Yue opera in Shanghai in the mid-twentieth century was not
just a cultural phenomenon but also a social phenomenon that entailed
the emergence of new social groups in the hierarchy of the city’s public
space. The opera’s upward mobility was closely tied to its ability to build a
core of upper- and middle-class patrons, and Yue opera grew by enlarging
its audience base, keeping its lower-class audience while capturing rising
numbers of urban middle-class men and, especially, women. These upper-
and middle-class patrons of Yue opera were mostly from the rich Ningbo-
Shaoxing area of eastern Zhejiang, the native home of Yue opera, in which
Shengxian was a poor sibling. 
Yue opera’s ascent was thus closely related to the rise of three some-
times overlapping social groups in Republican Shanghai: an urban middle
class that could include a wide spectrum of urbanites, from white-collar
professionals, skilled industrial workers, and employees in service sectors
to the self-employed and freelancers; Ningbo-Shaoxing natives; and female
operagoers.
1 An investigation of the Yue opera constituency thus tells much
about the social and economic formations of the city’s population that fueled
the rise of Yue opera as a women’s culture as well as the stratiﬁcation of
the city’s popular culture in general.
Venues, Patrons, and the Opera
In Republican Shanghai, a hierarchy of venues for commercial performance
reﬂected the complex social stratiﬁcation of the audience, opera genres,
troupes, and performers. The various performance sites—teahouses, enter-
106


=== PDF PAGE 128 ===

tainment halls, and theaters—served people from various backgrounds and
embraced different cultural atmospheres. 
Westernization was part of modern theatrical reform in China. The ﬁrst
Western-style theater was perhaps the New Theater in Shanghai, built by
reformist Beijing opera actors Pan Yueqiao, Xia Yueshan, and Xia Yuerun
in 1908.
2 After the 1911 Republican Revolution, new-style theaters grad-
ually spread across the country and became the mainstream venue in Shang-
hai. In the 1930s and 1940s, in the colonial downtown area, a handful of
ﬁrst-rate modern theaters hosted spoken drama, drawing a core audience
of male intellectuals, while a score of large modern theaters featured major
Beijing opera and Yue opera troupes, with audiences centered on rich, pow-
erful male patrons for Beijing opera and upper- and middle-class female
patrons for Yue opera respectively.
Less equipped medium-size and shabby small theaters in and on the
peripheries of the city center, in Nanshi, Hongkou, and Zhabei, served as
venues for all kinds of local operas, geared toward lower- and middle-class
local audiences. Less prestigious Yue opera and Beijing opera troupes as
well as other local operas regularly staged productions in these theaters,
while itinerant troupes from nearby provinces and the countryside often
took over during the regular troupes’ summer and winter breaks. Teahouses
in these areas remained popular for itinerant troupes, storytellers, and story-
singers who entertained a mostly lower-class crowd.
3
Entertainment halls represented another new development in Republi-
can Shanghai’s public space. During the early Republican period, entertain -
ment halls provided a fashionable place for upper-class gentlemen to sample
modernity, drinking tea while looking out on the city’s skyline. Among
the most famous of these were the Great World (Da Shijie) and the New
World (Xin Shijie); both occupied multistory buildings in the downtown
shopping area and were miniatures of Shanghai’s entertainment world.
 Blinking neon lights illuminated the buildings’ exteriors, while inside, many
forms of popular entertainment could be found, from puppet and comic
shows, to storytelling, to various native operas. But during the 1930s, as
entertainment halls became increasingly popular among the urban masses,
upper-class patrons escaped to modern theaters, cafés, and hotel lounges,
leaving the entertainment halls to ordinary folks. A Shanghai municipal
government survey in the early 1930s shows that, on average, one per-
son from every working-class family in Shanghai visited the Great World
once per month.
4 A 1939 Japanese travelogue describes the Great World’s
popularity: “The Great World in the French concession is a dream world
Patrons and Patronage  107

=== PDF PAGE 129 ===

serving as an entertainment institution for the commoners, mainly lower-
class shop hands, laborers, and tourists. The entrance fee is twenty-two
cents.”
5 During the postwar period, entertainment halls declined sharply
in number. The Great World was the only independent entertainment hall
left, while a few others became attached to department stores and made
hardly any proﬁt.
6
For opera troupes, decisions on where to perform and whom to enter-
tain determined their social as well as economic status. While venues marked
the status of the performing troupe and the genre it represented, opera
troupes could advance along the hierarchy by performing at better places.
The constant movement of opera troupes and audiences among various loca-
tions points to a dynamic structure of entertainment that was at once hier-
archical and ﬂuid. Beijing opera, for example, regularly occupied the most
modern and grandest theaters in downtown but was also regularly featured
at the open fairground and entertainment halls attended by lower-class urban
crowds. While troupes performing in grand theaters for elite audiences cer-
tainly raised Beijing opera’s cultural status, performances at fairgrounds and
in entertainment halls inevitably imparted a lower-class character to the
genre. Beijing opera was, therefore, both upper and lower class, depend-
ing on how and where one viewed it. 
The rise of women’s Yue opera exempliﬁes the correlation among an
opera’s venue, audience, and status in that it shows how Yue opera’s entrance
into better theaters helped expand its middle-class constituency and hence
raised the genre’s status from lower-class entertainment to middle-class pas-
time. Before the mid-1930s, all-male Shengxian opera companies were reg-
ular features in entertainment halls and medium-size theaters in the
downtown area. In contrast, itinerant troupes and all-female troupes fre-
quented small teahouses and shabby theaters in the river-dock section of
Nanshi, where laborers and merchants from Ningbo and Shaoxing con-
centrated. Toward the late 1930s, as all-female troupes became increasingly
popular, they moved out of teahouses and into theaters. According to the
Shanghai Yue Opera Gazetteer, around and after 1936, all-female troupes per-
formed mostly in small and medium-size theaters, all-male and mixed
troupes in entertainment halls.
7 Then, after the Battle of Shanghai, all-female
troupes soon moved into a dozen or so medium-size theaters, while all-male
troupes disappeared.
8
Upon entering medium-size theaters, the better all-female troupes were
able to draw a large number of middle-class men and especially women,
108 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 130 ===

while the lower-class constituency remained in the teahouses with the itin-
erant troupes. Zhang Rongbiao and Zhang Yanqiu, an elderly couple liv-
ing in Chongren, Shengxian, performed Shengxian opera in Shanghai
during the 1930s until the outbreak of the Battle of Shanghai in August
1937 . They both stated that roughly equal numbers of men and women
attended the all-male or mixed Shengxian opera shows. Older people were
the most frequent visitors, while large numbers of middle-aged men also
attended. Zhang Yanqiu, who performed with both male-dominant and
all-female companies, added that more men than women attended per-
formances by all-female troupes. Why? Her explanation was that men
wanted to take advantage of young actresses: “I had a husband and was
thus spared. If not, men would probably have also come after me.”
9 Other
informants supported her description of the gender composition of audi-
ences, although they did not necessarily give the same reason. Xiao-Bai
Yumei, an actress who performed in place of her father with an all-male com-
pany in the New World entertainment hall in the mid-1930s, also described
an audience of mixed gender and age.
10 This situation began to change with
the rise of all-female troupes in the late 1930s. Shaoxing Opera Daily(Shaox-
ingxi bao), a tabloid published by a few Yue opera patrons, described the
relationship between the popularity of all-female troupes, the places where
they performed, and the growth of the female and middle-class audience
in 1941:
All-female troupes were active in small teahouses in the French concession,
Nanshi, and Xiaonanmen districts. Seats were cheap, only one dime or two.
Women went after all-female troupes like ﬂocks of ducks. All-female troupes
then swept through Shanghai. Although all-female troupes were popular,
the pitiful fact was that they mostly performed in small teahouses and there-
fore lacked a respectable audience. To change this situation, they began
gradual reforms while moving into performing halls in larger inns. The
 Commercial Inn in Nichengqiao was the ﬁrst to host all-female troupes, and
the Great Chinese Theater [Da Zhonghua] on Fuzhou Road was the sec-
ond. The reformed theaters adopted a new seating system in which the
viewer took a seat with a number that matched the number on the ticket;
this practice attracted many upper-class spectators. Then theaters raised
ticket prices to forty cents and competed for contracts with famous stars.
New plays were staged, elaborate costumes were added, and the theater
became full of vitality. The name of “ didu opera” has since then been
Patrons and Patronage  109

=== PDF PAGE 131 ===

imprinted on the minds of Shanghai residents. Now not only Shaoxing
natives care about the opera; people of various native-place origins all love
it and go to see it.
11
The relationship between Shanghai’s modern theater and the increasing
number of women opera spectators described here is of special importance.
The veteran Yue opera director Han Yi pointed out that there were few
public places in Republican Shanghai where women could engage in social
activities. Dance halls and popular music cafés that featured girls who
danced with customers as well as female singers were designed to enter-
tain modern men and were not appropriate for respectable women. Movie
theaters were mostly a meeting place for young lovers, and teahouses tradi -
tionally were a place for men. Opera theaters therefore became one of very
few public places for women and facilitated the increase in female opera -
goers by providing a venue where women could enjoy the opera and social-
ize while maintaining a sense of propriety.
During the course of reform in the 1940s, Yue opera became a regular
feature of some ﬁrst-rate large theaters, such as the Nine-Star Theater and
the Star Theater, which both had about nine hundred seats, compared to
the four hundred seats provided at the Dalai Theater. According to Han Yi
and Cheng Rong, both members of Yuan Xuefen’s reform staff in the 1940s,
New Yue Opera attracted younger, better-educated, and increasingly
female viewers. Cheng commented, “The core of the audience consisted
of young housewives around forty. They were educated, ﬁnancially well
off, and had plenty of time. They often brought their daughters, friends,
and mothers to the theater. Most men came as women’s companions. Older
audience members such as my mother had difﬁculty understanding the
new plays we put on during the reform.”
12 According to Han Yi:
During the Japanese occupation [1942–45], the level of the audience was
not high because of the low level of the venue [the Dalai Theater]. At the
time, Yue opera had already established a stable group of female viewers, and
middle-school girls were already a signiﬁcant part of the constituency—a con-
stituency of petty intellectuals began to develop. Shortly before the victory
over Japan and through the late 1940s, as the company [Xuesheng Company]
entered ﬁrst-rate theaters, such as the Nine-Star, Star, and Grand Shanghai,
the level of the audience rose further to include some college students. . . .
A large part of Yue opera’s audience at that time consisted of middle-
school girls and some boys, middle-class housewives and concubines of upper-
110 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 132 ===

class men. Men were also many, mostly medium or small merchants, shop
hands, ofﬁce workers, and functionaries....  I n  the postwar period, large com-
panies in Shanghai had a concentration of petty intellectuals who loved Yue
opera.
13
The testimony of Cheng Rong and Han Yi as well as other evidence indi-
cate that Yue opera’s constituency in mid-twentieth-century Shanghai was
made up of the middle class, including lower-middle-class male clerks and
upper-class housewives and concubines, and women across class lines. To
put Yue opera’s constituency in some perspective, the considerable num-
ber of upper-class women patrons gave the opera prestige vis-à-vis other
local operas, such as Shanghai opera and Huai opera, but did not match
the distinguished status upper-class men brought to Beijing opera and intel-
lectual elite men brought to spoken drama. Compared to lowbrow enter-
tainment such as Shanghai opera and Huai opera, on the one hand, and
spoken drama, on the other, Yue opera was in the middle in terms of the
educational level of its audience. Yue opera’s directors and playwrights in
the 1940s and 1950s were mostly petty intellectuals with high school edu-
cations, while its constituency had an average middle-school education.
Although college students were more likely to be attracted to spoken drama,
common folks with primary-school or lower educations made up the basic
audiences for Shanghai opera and Huai opera, respectively.
14 Mainstream
Yue opera thus can be characterized as a middlebrow entertainment for
Shanghai’s vast middle class, both men and women, with a medium level
of education, while lowbrow troupes served a lower-class constituency,
mostly at the city peripheries.
15
The Native-Place Factor
Republican-period Shanghai was a rapidly developing industrial and  com -
mercial center with a swiftly growing population. Among the roughly four
million people in Shanghai in the late 1930s, less than one-fourth were
Shanghai natives. Immigrants from northern and southern Jiangsu, Zhe-
jiang, and Guangdong constituted the majority of the city’s population;
the rest were from Anhui, Jiangxi, Shandong, and other provinces.
16 In big
cities like Shanghai, which could be intimidating to newcomers from small
towns and the countryside, immigrants from the same area tended to stick
together on the basis of native bonds strengthened by shared local dialects,
common customs and habits, behavior codes, and nostalgic sentimental-
Patrons and Patronage    111

=== PDF PAGE 133 ===

ity. For practical reasons, immigrants relied on native-place ties to help them
operate in the city and to ﬁnd friends, work, and places to stay. People from
the same native place thus tended to hold similar jobs and congregated in
the same areas.
17 Yet the uneven economic development in various regions
of China meant that immigrants from better-developed areas, such as Zhe-
jiang and southern Jiangsu, came more prepared to compete for jobs in the
city compared to those from less developed areas, such as northern Jiangsu
(Subei) and Anhui. Thus, native-place segregation compounded by class
stratiﬁcation informed a hierarchical structure in the popular politics of
native-place identity, popular culture, and business operation that put
Ningbo-Shaoxing natives at the top and Subei people at the bottom.
18
Poor Subei immigrants typically took up coolie jobs in the city, such as
rickshaw pullers, waste collectors, porters, and other types of low-paid work-
ers. Because of this, Subei people were generally stereotyped, stigmatized,
and discriminated against in urban Shanghai. Factory workers from Subei
usually took up the lowest-paying and toughest physical work. Subei natives,
for example, constituted the majority of the workforce in silk reeling mills
19
and performed the most menial jobs in cotton mills. 20 Even an educated
Subei person found it difﬁcult to locate a good job because of his easily
identiﬁable accent.
21 Shanghai natives, generally speaking, were not highly
represented in the middle and upper classes either. Many engaged in petty
trade and odd jobs. 
In contrast, a great number of immigrants from Guangdong, southern
Jiangsu, and Zhejiang came with more ﬁnancial resources and consequently
were better educated and better situated in business, factories, government,
public service, and educational institutions. While factory workers from
these areas usually found skilled work at higher pay, such as silk weaving
and knitting,
22 educated persons from these areas occupied the majority
of white-collar positions. A 1941 survey of white-collar employees in the
Shanghai Public Service Bureau shows only 16 Shanghai natives out of a
total of 238, along with 44 Zhejiang natives, 85 Jiangsu natives, and 62
Guangdong natives.
23
Zhejiang and southern Jiangsu immigrants composed the largest part of
Shanghai’s business class, intellectuals, and white-collar employees. Partic-
ularly well known was the near monopoly of the native banking business
by the Ningbo-Shaoxing immigrant group, known as the Ning-Shao
clique.
24 Several Japanese investigations pointed out the rapid growth of
the Ning-Shao clique’s ﬁnancial power during the 1920s and 1930s in rela-
tion to the formerly dominant Guangdong group. By the late 1930s, the
112 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 134 ===

Ning-Shao clique surpassed the Guangdong clique in its share of power to
become ascendant in Shanghai’s native banking system.25 One study shows
that, in 1941, among the larger native banks with capital in excess of 100,000
yuan, twenty-two belonged to the Ning-Shao clique, six belonged to the
Shandong clique, ﬁve were Cantonese, and four were controlled by Shang-
hai natives.
26 The power of Ning-Shao immigrants becomes even more obvi-
ous when one realizes that three among the “four famous men in Shanghai”
(Haishang sida wenren) were from the Ningbo-Shaoxing area. The godfather
of the Green Gang, Huang Jinrong, was from Yuyao; the famous business-
man Yu Qiaqing was from Ningbo; and the onetime president of the Shang-
hai Chamber of Commerce, Wang Xiaolai, was a Shengxian native. Only
Du Yuesheng was a Shanghai native.
27
Native-place economy and politics directly inﬂuenced the economy and
politics of Shanghai’s culture. Various native cuisines, dress, dialects, and
operas competed with one another to deﬁne the city’s cultural landscape.
Not coincidentally, the rise of the Ning-Shao bankers paralleled the emer-
gence of women’s Yue opera. As Ningbo-Shaoxing economic and politi-
cal power laid the foundation for its native opera, the success of the opera
in turn symbolized Ningbo-Shaoxing hegemony in the city. Yue opera not
only became the most popular genre in Shanghai but also came to repre-
sent Shanghai-Jiangnan culture in China, while neither Shanghai opera nor
Jiangbei opera ever gained such national recognition. The modern Shang-
hai dialect was ﬁrst inﬂuenced by the Suzhou dialect and later by the Ningbo
dialect. The famous modern Shanghai term ehle,meaning “we,” for example,
originated in Ningbo dialect but has represented a proud Shanghainese
identity since the mid-twentieth century.
The support of Ning-Shao immigrants was crucial to the increased promi-
nence of women’s Yue opera in “island” Shanghai in the late 1930s and early
1940s. With a steady inﬂux of war refugees from adjacent areas, the pop-
ulation reached ﬁve million, and local operas—Yue opera, Shanghai opera,
and Huai opera in particular—ﬂourished.
28 As local operas drew audiences
mainly from their native-place immigrants, the superior position of Ning-
Shao immigrants gave Yue opera an edge. Ning-Shao immigrants promoted
their native opera in several ways. Ning-Shao men of letters wrote new
plays for the opera and put together tabloid publications and fan maga-
zines to promote it, while Ning-Shao businessmen and their wives bought
expensive stage costumes and paraphernalia for the opera’s stars. Finally,
a loyal cross section of the Ning-Shao audience paid for tickets that cost
more than tickets for other local operas. 
Patrons and Patronage    113

=== PDF PAGE 135 ===

In 1939, Yue opera tickets were the most expensive among local operas,
with the exception of Beijing opera. A ticket for a Yue opera show cost
ﬁfty cents, but one for Jiangbei opera was only ten cents; Shanghai opera
performed in entertainment halls cost twenty cents plus the entrance fee,
but admission to a Yue opera was an additional forty cents. Yue opera could
charge more because its audience had a higher median income compared
to audiences for Shanghai and Jiangbei opera, and it was also more popu-
lar. Especially important for women’s opera was the large group of Ning-
Shao upper- and middle-class housewives and daughters, who had both
the time and the money to frequent the theater. The Shanghai-native mid-
dle class was proportionately smaller, and the Subei-native middle class was
very small. This large core middle-class female audience steadily channeled
the ﬁnancial resources of Ning-Shao immigrants to Yue opera, providing
the opera with a ﬁnancial base that far surpassed those of other local operas. 
Yue opera’s superior ﬁnancial situation enabled it to invest more in its
productions, and higher production values in turn attracted more viewers.
For example, Yao Shuijuan’s 1940 play The Romantic Death of Jiang the Fifth
(Jiang Laowu xunqing ji), scripted and directed by Fan Li and staged at the
Queen’s Great Theater, used realistic stage sets for the ﬁrst time in Yue opera
history, with an actual rickshaw and boat as well as realistic buildings appear-
ing onstage. These unusual stage properties cost so much that the theater
had to raise its prices by 20 percent. But the play was a sensation, and the
ﬁrst run lasted sixty-three performances.
29 A few such major productions
were enough to establish Yao Shuijuan as the top star in Yue opera.
Similarly, Yue opera actresses had the resources to dress well onstage as
a result of their higher incomes, which helped maintain their theater’s higher
value vis-à-vis other operas and their actors. During the Republican period,
costumes were the responsibility and personal property of the actors. Actors
had to make themselves appear attractive and digniﬁed onstage; otherwise,
they could lose their popularity or even their positions. The pressure to
dress well onstage was high, and many ordinary actors were hard pressed
to keep up with expectations. As Yue opera’s popularity gave its actresses,
especially the leading actresses, considerable incomes, they could afford
such expenses, unlike their counterparts in other local operas. Revenues
from ticket sales encouraged theater bosses to invest more willingly in
improving stage properties and theaters as a whole. 
All these factors added up to an enhanced outlook for Yue opera the-
ater, which gained higher status in the public eye. Within a few years, Yue
opera emerged as an urban modern theater and overtook Beijing opera to
114 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 136 ===

become the most popular opera in the region, attracting a mass urban audi-
ence that crossed native-place, gender, and class lines. In 1941, an observer
pointed out in Shaoxing Opera Daily: “Now not only Shaoxing natives care
about the opera. People of various native-place origins all love it and go
to see it.” Also in 1941, Shaoxing Opera Dailystated: “Before the war, 70 per-
cent of the audience for Shaoxing civil drama [one of the many names for
Yue opera] were Shaoxing natives and 30 percent Ningbo natives. After
the war broke out, Shaoxing natives composed only 40 percent of the whole
audience, and Ningbo natives 30 percent, while other natives increased to
30 percent. In this year, non-Ningbo-Shaoxing-native audience members
have increased to 60 percent.”
30 Though these statistics are difﬁcult to ver-
ify, the Yue opera audience’s historical expansion among various natives
from the Jiangnan region is undeniable. Further evidence shows that Yue
opera also began to attract attention from the mainstream media as, per-
haps for the ﬁrst time in its history, Shanghai Newspaper carried a positive
commentary on Yue opera on May 3, 1941.
31 Initially an opera of Ning-
Shao natives, Yue opera was being transformed into a general urban spec-
tacle as it simultaneously grew into a distinctly women’s opera in 1940s
Shanghai. 
Women Going to the Opera
Women made up the majority of Yue opera’s audience in Shanghai in the
mid-twentieth century. They were by far the most enthusiastic spectators,
fans, and patrons. Although men constituted nearly half of the audience,
it was the new female audience members who invigorated women’s Yue
opera and helped make it a great popular-culture phenomenon. 
Going to the opera could be quite an urban experience for women who
had not long ago achieved the right to enjoy themselves in the public space
and view commercial entertainment. Besides their strong preference for
romantic love stories, a specialty of women’s Yue opera, women loved to
attend Yue opera shows for a variety of social reasons. Going to the opera
expanded women’s horizons, exposing them to other sorts of urban expe-
riences. Riding a trolley bus, rickshaw, or private motorcar, walking among
the crowds on paved sidewalks between handsome high-rises, and passing
through the urban scene represented quite an adventure. Operagoing, like
shopping (and window-shopping) at downtown department stores, was
recreation for urban women, a refreshing break in their routines. Oppor-
tunities for socializing were another beneﬁt of operagoing, as women usu-
Patrons and Patronage    115

=== PDF PAGE 137 ===

ally attended plays with female companions. Knowledge of the opera, the
play, and the actresses provided good fodder for conversation; one could
even show off if one’s companions were less knowledgeable. Women also
obtained a great deal of information from the variety of shows with themes
ranging from contemporary social sensation (The Romantic Death of Jiang the
Fifth, 1940), to patriotic epic (Hua Mulan, 1939), to the lives of workers in
remote areas (Daughter of a Border T own[Biancheng nüer], 1943), to exotic
ethnic subjects (Desert Prince, 1947) in addition to great love stories about
famous historical and ﬁctional personalities. Enthusiastic fans used the
opportunity to try out their organizational skills with group activities and
amateur performances. For the rich, patronage was a way of deploying their
superior status and economic power in public. In short, going to the opera
represented an opportunity for women to engage the world at large, to cross
the boundaries of the family and the factory, and even to develop identi-
ties apart from their social stations.
The Economics of Viewing Opera
