Women’s Yue opera appealed to women from various backgrounds, includ-
ing middle-class housewives and schoolgirls, factory workers, businesswomen
and merchants, and, later, students and professional women. In other words,
in the middle decades of the twentieth century, women’s Yue opera attracted
a broad cross section of the female population in Shanghai. 
Factory workers made up a considerable part of the lower- to middle-
class audience for Yue opera in entertainment halls and small theaters.
32 A
1939 study done by a group of communist labor workers, Shanghai’s Indus-
tries and Workers(Shanghai chanye yu Shanghai zhigong), provides precious
ﬁrsthand observations of workers’ lives as well as valuable statistics.33 The
study states that among Shanghai’s 600,000 industrial workers in 1939, cot-
ton mill workers numbered more than 100,000, and silk-knitting and silk-
weaving workers were estimated at more than 40,000 each.
34 The majority
of these workers were women. 
To understand the economics of viewing opera for lower- and middle-
class audiences, it is helpful to put wages and opera ticket prices in per-
spective. The average wage of cotton mill workers was ﬁfty cents (half a
yuan) per day—certainly not a lavish sum. The workers were mostly from
southern and northern Jiangsu, although some were Shanghai natives.
35 On
weekends, workers went out for fun in groups. Many went to see Jiangbei
opera in theaters where the price of a ticket was ten cents, about one-ﬁfth
116 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 138 ===

an average daily wage; others spent twelve cents to see Shanghai story-
telling.36 Workers who earned less income could see the same entertain-
ment for just a few cents at the West Shanghai Great World, an open-air
fairground that regularly featured three Beijing opera troupes, two Jiang-
bei operas, one Shanghai local-dialect recitation, one magic show, two mar-
tial arts performances, and one storytelling show.
37 Yue opera was not
performed there, indicating the lack of interest among cotton-mill work-
ers, mostly non–Zhejiang natives, before 1939. By the early PRC years,
however, cotton-mill workers composed a large portion of Yue opera fans,
evidence of a growing interest in the genre among these non–Zhejiang
natives during the intervening decade.
The factory workers among the Yue opera audience during the Repub-
lican period were predominantly from silk-knitting and silk-weaving mills,
both among the better-paying industries. According to the 1939 Shang-
hai’s Industries and Workers, workers’ daily wages ranged from an average of
forty cents in silk-reeling mills to ninety cents at the French-owned trol-
ley bus company. An average daily wage for workers in silk-weaving mills
was eighty cents, and workers in knitting mills earned sixty cents a day.
38
The study also reveals a signiﬁcant correlation between workers’ native
places and their wages: the majority of silk-knitting workers were from
Shaoxing and Ningbo,
39 while the better-paid workers in silk-weaving mills
were natives of Zhejiang’s sericulture area, including Shengxian, Xinchang,
Dongyang, Hangzhou, and  Shao xing.
40 These workers enjoyed visiting
entertainment halls to watch local operas, and their native Yue opera was
their favorite entertainment. Many liked to sing, and some serious ama-
teurs occasionally organized performances for their own and their fellow
workers’ amusement.
41 Workers generally were able to purchase access to
some type of performance, depending on their personal budgets. In Octo-
ber 1939, one of the most popular shows, staged by the famous Xiao Dan-
gui and her troupe in the Old Dam Theater, charged ﬁfty cents a ticket,
42
while the entrance fee for the Great World was twenty-two cents. 43 It
seemed that male workers found entertainment halls more attractive, prob-
ably for the dynamic atmosphere and varieties of entertainment, but
female workers favored inexpensive seats in theaters, perhaps because they
preferred to quietly enjoy the show.
44
Shanghai’s Industries and Workers does not mention gender differentiation
among operagoers, which suggests that both male and female workers
enjoyed local operas from their native regions. By the early PRC period,
female workers had become the more enthusiastic fans of Yue opera. This
Patrons and Patronage    117

=== PDF PAGE 139 ===

conforms to other sources that argue for the growing portion of female, as
well as non-Zhejiang-native, audiences in the 1940s and 1950s. Indeed,
the increase in these sectors of the Yue opera audience made Yue opera the
most popular opera in modern Shanghai.
Female domestic helpers and servants constituted another group of Yue
opera’s loyal customers. The writer Wei Shaochang, a Shaoxing native and
the son of Wei Jinshan, the executive manager of the Zhonghui Bank owned
by Du Yuesheng, recalled his childhood encounter with Yue opera in the
1930s. His nanny loved Yue opera and often made the little boy ask his
mother for permission and money to go see the opera at the Great World
so that she could go with him. Little Wei liked to go, too, for the excite-
ment and the delicious snacks sold at the entertainment hall.
45
Children also attended Yue opera shows. Unlike Beijing opera, Yue opera
theaters admitted small children for free, perhaps a necessary accommo-
dation to women spectators. Children exposed to Yue opera at a young age
often carried their interest in the opera into adulthood. Wei Shaochang
later became the editor of several Yue opera fan magazines and tabloids,
including Shaoxing Opera Daily.
46 Wu Ping, a lifetime Yue opera fan who
nurtured her granddaughter’s interest in learning the art of Yue opera, had
her own ﬁrst contact with the opera in her preschool years as the com-
panion of a young lady in her neighborhood. Later, during her adolescence,
she often went to see Yue opera with a girlfriend. The two girls rode in one
rickshaw and shared a seat in the theater—Yue opera theaters allowed two
children to enter for one ticket.
47
Middle-class housewives and daughters were the core audience for
women’s opera. Republican Shanghai’s middle and lower middle classes con-
sisted of roughly two groups: one was made up of self-employed merchants
and shop owners, and the other of white-collar employees. Self-employed
merchants were a traditional feature of the urban economic structure, and
their numbers were great. Cheng Rong’s father, for example, was a mer-
chant whose income was sufﬁcient to provide Cheng with an education
through middle school, but her admission to teacher’s college (equivalent
to high school but subsidized by the government, which provided a small
stipend plus a tuition waiver) no doubt relieved the stress on family
ﬁnances.
48 For women from lower-middle-class families, attending Yue opera
shows might require some special effort. According to Zhang Xingzhou, a
merchant from Ningbo, the average Ningbo immigrant in Shanghai lived
a frugal life. Though the women usually did not work outside the home,
there was always plenty of work to do—cleaning, doing laundry, attend-
118 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 140 ===

ing to sideline family businesses, caring for children, shopping, and cook-
ing.49 Ningbo immigrants were notorious for their miserly ways, and
Ningbo women were discouraged from going out and spending money.
The frequent Yue operagoers, Zhang remarked, were those who had both
money and leisure. By this, he meant upper-class women.
50
Shanghai’s white-collar employees, according to Shanghai’s Industries and
Workers, outnumbered those in the rest of China—approximately 300,000
in three economic sectors. About 140,000 worked in traditional-style shops,
about 100,000 in foreign-owned businesses, and about 50,000 in modern
institutions owned by native capitalists. In the Chinese sectors, an average
monthly salary for junior employees, who made up more than half the white-
collar workforce, was between ten and twenty yuan, lower than the wages
of average factory workers; an average salary for mid-level employees was
about thirty yuan per month, except for some higher-paid employees in
the ﬁnancial sector. An average salary for mid-level bank employees, for
example, was between ﬁfty to sixty yuan per month. Employees of the
Shanghai China Bank were by far the best paid.
51 The authors do not give
a salary range for the one-third of white-collar workers who were employed
in foreign-owned businesses; however, they clearly indicate that salaries
in this sector tended to be higher than in Chinese sectors. 
Another set of numbers from 1941 also helps us make sense of the eco-
nomics of viewing Yue opera in wartime Shanghai. According to the Shang-
hai Public Service Bureau’s annual report, the salaries for 232 white-collar
employees in June 1941 ranged from 40 to 180 yuan per month, and 225
blue-collar employees earned from 22 to 60 yuan.
52 Meanwhile, Yue opera
tickets ranged from 80 cents to 1 yuan and 20 cents in 1941. In either case,
viewing Yue opera may not have been cheap, but at the same time, it was
still affordable for middle-class families. 
Middle-class men and women tended to have received better educations
in modern schools, and the education gap between them and the lower
class could not be ignored. The authors of Shanghai’s Industries and Workers
explain: 
China’s modern industry has less than thirty years of history. As the rural
economy disintegrated, blue-collar workers came mainly from poor peasant
families, while white-collar employees came mainly from landlord and rich
peasant families. The former were less educated and the latter better edu-
cated. In addition, in our country, industries that need skilled workers capa-
ble of handling complicated technologies are relatively few. This has
Patrons and Patronage    119

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===

resulted in a big gap in educational levels between the white-collar and blue-
collar workers and consequently divides them into two social categories.53
The two categories of workers corresponded with divisions in native-place
origins. Natives of the Jiangnan region constituted a large proportion of
Shanghai’s college students and intellectuals as well as its entrepreneurs and
white-collar workers. Although many white-collar men were not interested
in Yue opera, they did not mind letting the women in their families enjoy
it. The father of Yue opera fan Wu Ping, for example, was a college gradu-
ate who worked for the customs service during the 1940s. He had no inter-
est in Yue opera or any other local operas, but he did not mind giving his
daughter permission and money to attend Yue opera shows. According to
Wu Ping, the four family members each had his or her own favorite pas-
time. Her father read, her mother played mahjong, her older sister loved
pop songs, and she herself was crazy about Yue opera.
54 Middle-class women
were fond of going to see Yue opera. A new bride in Wu Ping’s neighbor-
hood often went to see Yue opera, most frequently in the afternoons, when
her husband was at work, but also sometime in the evenings, leaving her
husband home alone. The well-known female writer Su Qing, a Ningbo
native, admitted that attending opera was her favorite pastime.
55
The Fans
Finally, later during the 1940s, a new group, and, in a sense, a new gener-
ation, of Yue opera fans appeared. They were schoolgirls, female college
students, some female professionals, and new middle-class housewives, all
educated in the modern school system with Republican ideas of equal rights
for women, nationalism, and modernity. By the postwar period, the Repub-
lican educational system had made school education for middle-class girls
commonplace and college education a possibility. This generation endowed
the opera with new prestige as well as new stimulus for further reform. Cheng
Rong, the ﬁrst woman playwright for Yue opera, and Chen Shulian, the Yue
opera radio announcer, both graduated from teacher’s schools (equivalent
to high school) and taught primary school before they joined Yuan Xue-
fen’s company. Teng Jiazhen and her husband Song Zhiyou, both account-
ants and graduates of Dongwu University, were enthusiastic Yue opera
supporters. During the late 1940s and throughout the 1950s, Teng held mid-
 night snack parties at her home for actresses after their evening shows. She
also taught them to read and write. As a professional, Teng emphasized in
120 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 142 ===

interviews that she was not an old-fashioned adoptive mother, nor one of the
obsessed Yue opera fans.She just wanted to help the actresses.The actresses
in fact addressed Teng as “teacher.”56 Many middle-school boys and girls as
well as college students became Yue opera regulars; known as the “triangle
brand audience,” for they all wore triangular school badges, they were the
pride of New Yue Opera as they embodied the opera’s modernity.
Many of these young women were attracted to women’s opera for rea-
sons similar to those of their mothers’ generation. Chen Shulian commented: 
At the beginning, I was not interested in Yue opera or any local operas
[because local operas seemed vulgar and old-fashioned]. I had been a big
movie fan. I became attracted to Yue opera after I befriended Yuan Xuefen,
when she came to our station for a Yue opera radio program. The people
from the Xuesheng Company were different from those of other local opera
companies. Shanghai opera, Suzhou story-singing, and Shanghai comic
shows all performed on radio programs at our station. Even well-known actors
from Shanghai opera behaved just like lower-class folks in the marketplace,
with cigarettes on their lips all the time. Only people from the Xuesheng
Company looked respectable.
57
That was how Chen got involved with the Xuesheng Company and fell in
love with the reformed Yue opera.
Fan-club activities, which began in the early 1940s, reveal how Yue opera
served young women’s needs for socializing and personal development in
the modern context. The appearance of several fan clubs in the late 1940s
signaled young women’s self-conscious efforts to forge a community.
Through fan-club activities, women created a social space in which par-
ticipants could express their individual opinions and make decisions regard-
ing their clubs’ affairs. Fans indeed openly criticized and argued with one
another in tabloids, fan publications, and meetings, often unable to agree
on what should be done and how. Although admittedly frustrated by this
situation, members nonetheless took great pleasure in getting together with
other women with the same interests, exercising control and inﬂuence at
the same time. These fan clubs seem to have achieved very little of what
the members wanted, but they provided a space where young women could
develop a sense of their social selves while learning social and organiza-
tional skills.
One interesting feature of these fan clubs was the women’s attempt to
exclude men. Female fans’ monopoly over fan clubs indicates not only their
Patrons and Patronage    121

=== PDF PAGE 143 ===

enthusiasm for Yue opera but also their desire for a social space of their
own, free from male intervention. The women, consciously or not, tried to
expand their horizon beyond the home and school and did so in deﬁance
of male social dominance. It was to a certain extent a subversive practice,
though it took place on the periphery of society and did arouse some male
protest. One commentator, responding negatively to women’s monopoly
of fan clubs, wrote about the establishment of a fan club for Fan Ruijuan,
a popular young male impersonator, in the weekly tabloid Yue Opera News
(Yueju bao): 
According to the Founding Manifesto of the Fan Ruijuan Club [Juan She],
it seems that the guiding principle is to support Fan Ruijuan, enhance Fan’s
art, and associate all the Fan fans in fellowship with one another. If this is
to be the principle, then all the Fan fans, men and women, should be able to
join the club. It goes without saying that it is contradictory for the club to
limit its membership to women only. From this case, one can see how nar-
row-minded women are.
58
Another interview with the club’s president, Dong Qian, by a woman named
Xu Fangfeng, probably a freelancer for the tabloid and a Yue opera fan her-
self, offers a general sense of the purpose and attitudes of these fan-club
members. According to Dong:
The Fan Ruijuan Club now has more than 120 members. There are fourteen
volunteer workers; though the number is not great, we do not feel any lack
of personnel because everyone works hard. Members go to see the shows
on their own and convene when there is business to discuss. As for enter-
tainment, members all suggest that I organize fan-club performances because
everyone likes to sing. But organizing an amateur performance is not an easy
thing. There are plenty of ﬁve-minute enthusiasts among Yue opera fans. In
addition, we should not draw lines between one another if we want to per-
form together. In Shanghai, there are amateur performance clubs for Beijing
opera; Yue opera as the local number one certainly should have these clubs.
[I] am now writing a play for Miss Fan, tentatively titled Lonely Figure under
the Autumn Moon [Qiuyue guying]. It’s just an enjoyment for myself.
59
Dong Qian is obviously a well-educated, young, single woman from a
well-to-do family—typical of the new generation of middle- and upper-
middle-class young women fans in Shanghai in the mid-twentieth century.
122 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 144 ===

Patronage
Republican Shanghai, largely due to its semicolonial economy and politi-
cal system, was home to a large, mixed upper class composed of newly
afﬂuent entrepreneurs, high-ranking business professionals, high ofﬁcials,
the town strongmen, and the city’s fading old aristocrats and rural mag-
nates. During the turbulent years of the ﬁrst half of the century, Shang-
hai’s foreign settlements attracted rich aristocrats and modern intellectuals
alike with its exotic and extravagant lifestyle while providing a safe haven
for refugees ﬂeeing political persecution, warfare, and famine. Many lived
decadent lives, preoccupied with opium smoking and the company of cour-
tesans and opera actors. The young novelist Zhang Ailing painstakingly
describes in her ﬁction the lives of the declining elite in Shanghai’s foreign
settlements, witnessing the hopeless and self-indulgent existence of her
father, stepmother, and other relatives.
60
Patronizing and consorting with actors were traditionally prerogatives of
upper-class men; the privilege was challenged during the Republican period
when women entered into the practice. With the rise of minor operas, espe-
cially women’s Yue opera, a new gendered division of upper-class patronage
appeared. While upper-class men customarily attended Beijing opera and
patronized famous Beijing opera actors, the women socialized with Yue opera
actors and actresses. Upper-class women’s patronage of Yue opera began
in the 1920s with the establishment of the all-male Shengxian opera in
Shanghai and became a popular-culture phenomenon with women’s Yue
opera during the middle decades. Thus, for example, while Huang Jinrong,
the king of Shanghai’s Green Gang, was a great patron of Beijing opera, his
daughter-in-law was the adoptive mother of Xiao Dangui, the famous Yue
opera star. As housewives who did not have independent social status apart
from that of their husbands, these women did not have a formal role to play
in the public domain. Thus, for them, more than for men, going to the the-
ater and patronizing actors were not merely entertaining activities but also
a means of socializing, crossing boundaries, and empowering themselves. 
Women transgressing gender boundaries in modern Shanghai was a
favorite topic of public gossip. During the late nineteenth and early twen-
tieth centuries, when women were not supposed to appear in public enter-
tainment venues, courtesans attended Beijing opera plays in foreign
settlements; many became romantically involved with famous actors, while
some caused scandals. Even though the male-centered public culture ren-
dered them objects of male sexual desire, courtesans were able to turn the
Patrons and Patronage    123

=== PDF PAGE 145 ===

tables by making actors the targets of their own sexual desire, often at the
expense of their male customers.61 During the early Republican period, when
operagoing became a legitimate pastime for women, upper-class wives—
mostly courtesans who had become concubines—also began to have sex-
ual liaisons with actors. When all-male Shengxian opera became popular
in Shanghai during the 1920s and 1930s, upper-class women from the
Shaoxing-Ningbo area did indeed take advantage of the naive young actors
from the countryside. 
In interviews, the Yue opera actress Xiao-Bai Yumei emphasized that her
father, a famous female impersonator and renowned as Mei Lanfang in the
all-male Shengxian opera, was never corrupted and stated that he was
unusual in this regard.
62 Xiao-Bai Yumei inherited the famous name of her
father when she took over his role in 1933 at the age of twelve and per-
formed with popular actors of the time. Many famous actors smoked opium
and had liaisons with rich concubines, and some became victims of their
own decadent lifestyles. Xiao-Bai Yumei recalled a young man named Gao
Yuesheng, a popular star specializing in young male roles and Xiao-Bai
Yumei’s stage partner. Gao was at the peak of his career when the two per-
formed at the New World in the mid-1930s. Gao, however, indulged him-
self in liaisons with rich men’s concubines. Every day, as soon as the show
was over, there was always a car awaiting him outside. He obviously had
more than one lover. As time passed, the quality of his performance and
voice deteriorated, and eventually he had to quit. He died at the age of
twenty-seven. Xiao-Bai did not see how it could have been possible for an
actor to perform two shows every day while entangled in an excessive sex-
ual life. She was not speciﬁc about the cause of Gao’s death, perhaps because
it was obvious to her that he simply overextended and exhausted himself.
63
Gao’s story provides an interesting case in which multiple transgressions
of class and gender boundaries occurred. On the one hand, the actor under-
mined the elite’s supremacy by seducing their women and consuming their
wealth. The concubines, on the other hand, ridiculed male sexual domi-
nance and male authority by enjoying the actor’s sexuality at their hus-
bands’ expense. Concubines were able to engage in such sexual activity
behind their husbands’ backs largely because it was common Republican
era practice to maintain concubines in separate dwellings, away from prin-
cipal wives and children. Young concubines who had been courtesans and
did not have children were most likely to initiate extramarital affairs.
64 A
principal wife usually shunned sexual escapades, perhaps because her stake
in the family was greater.
124 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 146 ===

Deﬁance and transgression always had their limits and were subject to
punishment in Republican Shanghai. Unlike rich courtesans, who had more
autonomy over their own sexuality, concubines as well as principal wives
owed their status and ﬁnancial well-being to their husbands, who possessed
certain prerogatives with respect to the women’s sexuality. Thus, when
women’s opera and its actresses became publicly accessible, both concu-
bines and principal wives turned to these sources of legitimate involvement.
Concerned primarily with the well-being of the familial and social struc-
ture, the Chinese patriarchal system was largely silent on intimate rela-
tionships between women, which seemingly posed no threat to patriarchal
authority. As Xiao-Bai Yumei explained, husbands were not worried when
their wives became passionate about women’s Yue opera and close to the
actresses because these were “just women’s things.” Nothing serious could
happen between women—meaning there could not be a scandal resulting
from public misbehavior or pregnancy. Homosexual relations were rare and
of little concern, she believed. In fact, husbands were at ease when their
wives and daughters were attending women’s opera and consorting with
opera actresses.
65
Women’s opera also attracted upper-class wives because of the absence
of male performers. In most other performing groups—such as other local
operas, cinema, and spoken drama—actors and actresses worked and
sometimes lived together day and night. This was to some extent a pro-
fessional imperative, often compounded by ﬁnancial necessity, that pro-
voked titillation regarding modern lifestyles and suspicion of promiscuity
among the public. Sexual relationships between male and female stars were
a never-ending topic of tabloids and gossip, invoking the centuries-old
stereotype of actors as promiscuous. In contrast to the perceived promis-
cuity of other performing troupes, women’s Yue opera companies appeared
pure and clean, with a hint of virtue and chastity. Actresses, mostly unmar-
ried girls around twenty years of age from the countryside, looked as though
they were begging for protection from potential patrons, if not waiting to
be claimed.
66 Furthermore, the very absence of male actors backstage was
convenient for women patrons, permitting them to move in and out freely
and appropriate it as a woman’s space.
67
Adoptive Mothers and Sisters
“Adoptive motherhood” ( guofang niang) is a peculiar institution related to
wealthy women and their patronage of Yue opera actresses in modern
Patrons and Patronage    125

=== PDF PAGE 147 ===

Shanghai.68 It began with the appearance of women performers in Yue opera
in Shanghai in the 1930s and ended in the mid-1960s with the Cultural
Revolution, roughly the golden period of the opera in the city. An obscure
practice among Shanghai residents, adoptive motherhood became an insti-
tution within the context of Yue opera patronage. Generally, when a rich
patron decided to take her favorite star as an adoptive daughter, she would
select a date on which the ritual was to take place. For the ritual, which
was usually held at the adoptive mother’s home, the adoptive mother would
be seated when the adoptive daughter was brought in and knelt down before
her. The daughter kowtowed three times, thereby formally establishing
adoptive relations. The mother then presented the daughter with “gifts of
the ﬁrst meeting” ( jian mian li), which could include dresses, accessories,
stage costumes, or expensive jewels. Sometimes, the adoptive family would
even host a banquet to announce the adoption. Adoptive relations were
public and usually carried much prestige for both the mother and the daugh-
ter. For the mother, it was a way of showing off her wealth and status by
playing the role of protector of a star; for the actress, the relationship sym-
bolized access to the wealthy and powerful. Underneath the public and
symbolic meanings of the institution, the relationship itself could be as com-
plicated and diverse as any other. 
Upper-class patronage of Yue opera was important in shaping the
opera. Female patrons would take one or sometimes several of their favorite
actresses into adoptive relationships, showering them with gifts ranging
from cash, jewels, and offstage dresses, to stage costumes and decorations
made of embroidered brocade bearing the actresses’ names. The better and
more expensive the stage sets and costumes, the more prestige the adop-
tive mother gained in the public eye. Adoptive mothers also took care of
the actresses to varying degrees. They often took their adoptive daughters
home after evening performances and provided food for them, and actresses
sometimes stayed at their adoptive mothers’ homes for a few days or even
weeks. Some adoptive mothers even invited actresses to move in with them.
Often, an adoptive mother would buy a dozen or more front-row tickets
and invite friends and relatives to the actress’s show. Adoptive mothers could
also show off by taking their adoptive daughters, usually famous stars, to
perform at private gatherings.
69
The most exciting occasions, however, involved grand charity per-
formances, opportunities for adoptive mothers to show off their wealth and
power and for adoptive daughters to enhance their fame, ﬁnancial situa-
tions, and social esteem.
70 Charity performances were always held in ﬁrst-
126 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 148 ===

rate theaters with larger stages, more seats, and, most important, an
esteemed audience of celebrities who did not usually go to Yue opera the-
aters. Tabloid coverage and advertising for these charity performances were
profuse. As adoptive mothers called in to reserve tickets in the names of
their adoptive daughters, tabloids updated ticket sales daily, listing actresses’
names in order of the yuan amount and making the event into a public
pledge competition. The larger the yuan amount pledged to the star, the
more prestigious the star and her adoptive mother became. The rivalry con-
tinued onstage when actresses tried to display themselves at their best, in
terms of costumes and paraphernalia as well as performing skill. For these
reasons, both adoptive mothers and actresses considered participation in
a charity performance to be an honor and a chance to gain public expo-
sure while supporting a lofty cause.
The careers of the noted actresses Xiao-Bai Yumei and Yao Shuijuan illus-
trate how the adoptive mother system worked. Xiao-Bai Yumei’s adoptive
mother was the wife of He Lunzhang, a Ningbo native and comprador for
the American-owned Xinfu Trade Company. Xiao-Bai was introduced to
her adoptive mother by the woman’s daughter, a great fan of Xiao-Bai’s
who attended her performance every day after school. The family had three
private cars and employed seventeen domestic servants. Xiao-Bai’s adop-
tive mother was the principal wife, which explained why she could spend
so much money on Xiao-Bai. It seemed to be a point of pride for Xiao-Bai
that her adoptive mother was the principal wife. The adoptive mother was
very fond of the young Xiao-Bai Yumei, who was still a teenager. At the
adoption ritual, Xiao-Bai recalled, “My adoptive mother was so happy when
I called out loud ‘Mma!’ For that, she gave me a pearl necklace.”
71 Xiao-Bai
Yumei recalled with pride that, in the late 1930s, when she triumphed in
a charity performance, her adoptive mother gave her seventeen taels of
gold, which Xiao-Bai used to purchase a long-term lease on a house for her
family.
Unlike Hollywood stars or a few Beijing opera superstars at the time,
actors in Republican China were never rich, no matter how famous they
were. Xiao-Bai Yumei’s father, one of the most popular actors in all-male
Shengxian opera, earned sixty yuan monthly, which was enough to keep
a family of ﬁve comfortable. Xiao-Bai Yumei, however, earned in women’s
opera much more than her father did. Her income not only made her fam-
ily comfortable but also paid for her three brothers’ education. According
to Shaoxing Opera Daily, the highest monthly salary for Yue opera stars was
two hundred yuan in 1938 and six hundred yuan in 1940.
72 A reasonable
Patrons and Patronage    127

=== PDF PAGE 149 ===

extrapolation would indicate about four hundred yuan per month in 1939.
Compared to the earnings of factory workers and white-collar employees,
actors’ salaries may look high, but only a few famous Yue opera actresses
actually earned that much. Moreover, the money quickly disappeared—
to pay for costumes and daily expenditures for families living in the city
and relatives who remained in the countryside. There was always the ques-
tion of saving for the summer and winter off seasons, when actresses did
not earn salaries, as well as for the years after retirement, which usually
occurred when actresses were in their late twenties. Thus, for actresses, gifts
from adoptive mothers represented a source of income that contributed
signiﬁcantly to their personal ﬁnances.
Adoptive mothers helped Yue opera actresses not only ﬁnancially but
socially as well. The patronage Wei Shaochang and his parents bestowed
on Yao Shuijuan is a good example, illustrating how the rich and power-
ful Ning-Shao immigrants helped make successes of an actress and an opera.
When Yao Shuijuan made her debut in Shanghai in early 1938, a young
female fan introduced her to Wei Jinshan and his wife, who subsequently
took Yao as an adoptive daughter and deluged her with generous gifts, from
professional to personal items. Yao’s extravagant stage costumes and para-
phernalia, along with her performing skill, became a subject of public gos-
sip. It was said that she changed costumes eighteen times in one play. She
had, it was rumored, the most gorgeous brocade stage curtains with her
name embroidered on them; she was, in fact, the ﬁrst Yue opera actress to
use expensive logo curtains.
73 In 1939, with the backing of Wei Jinshan,
Yao Shuijuan obtained a lease on the Queen’s Great Theater and became
the ﬁrst Yue opera actress with her “own” theater, although it was actually
controlled by the Weis.
74
Wei Shaochang, then in high school, also became a fan of Yao Shuijuan’s.
He and two other Shaoxing natives published a couple of tabloid fan mag-
azines, The Song of Yue (Yue ou), and Shaoxing Opera News, to promote Yao
Shuijuan and Yue opera in general. Inspired by patron publications in honor
of famous Beijing opera female impersonators such as Mei Lanfang and Cheng
Yanqiu, Wei and his colleagues also published a special issue dedicated to
Yao and her art, the ﬁrst publication of the sort for a Yue opera actress.
75
These publications were ﬁlled with photos of Yao and articles and reports
that praised the actress and her art, portraying her as a modern conscien-
tious actress and her art as the best of its kind. The book also contains a line
of calligraphy by Mei Lanfang for Yao Shuijuan, which reads: “The queen
of Yue opera.” Mei had not yet met Yao, and he crafted the calligraphy for
128 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 150 ===

Wei Jinshan, who had requested it on Yao’s behalf. It was a smart idea that
helped lift the cultural-social status of Yao Shuijuan and Yue opera by equat-
ing Yao Shuijuan with Mei Lanfang, the “queen of Beijing opera.”
76
Patrons provided a social network, a sort of social protection, for
actresses faced with the theater owner’s monopoly of their trade. Actresses,
who were rural girls newly arrived in Shanghai, lacked experience and social
networks that might help them counter the power of the theater boss, who
tended to take advantage of his employees’ vulnerability. With powerful
patrons such as the Weis, however, Yao Shuijuan secured a long-term lease
on the Queen’s Great Theater, hence becoming somewhat self-employed.
Within a few years of her arrival, Yao Shuijuan became the most noted Yue
opera actress and a star in Shanghai’s public culture, or so it seemed. A
December 1940 survey by Drama Semi-monthly (Banyue xiju) indicates that
Yao Shuijuan’s shows had the highest viewing rate, with Xiao Dangui sec-
ond, and Ma Zhanghua third.
77 In fact, Xiao Dangui was at least as popu-
lar, as suggested by a saying of the time: “The three ﬂowers were surpassed
by the graceful; the graceful was surpassed by a laurel.” The three ﬂowers
were Shi Yinhua, Zhao Ruihua, and Tu Xinhua, the very ﬁrst generation
of women’s opera actresses; the “graceful” (juan) was Yao Shuijuan; and the
“laurel” ( gui) was Xiao Dangui. But Xiao Dangui and Ma Zhanghua were
not as lucky as Yao Shuijuan. Both died young and tragically, after each
tried to break free from her boss’s control. 
Even now, Wei Shaochang felt compelled to give credit to the adoption
practice by comparing the different situations of Yao Shuijuan and Xiao
Dangui: “Try to imagine,” Wei commented, “with a devil like Zhang Chun-
fan following Xiao, how could decent adoptive mothers and sisters dare to
get close to her? In contrast, Yao Shuijuan had the most powerful patrons
and was friendly with tabloid writers. The different situations of the two
stars could not but affect their careers and lives.”
78 Self-promotion notwith-
standing, Wei Shaochang’s story is not without merit. Actresses must have
found that the social and ﬁnancial support provided by adoptive parents
could be of some signiﬁcance. What is important is not that Zhang Chun-
fan was a devil but that he alone controlled Xiao’s life and career.
The adoptive parents system continued into the early PRC period. One
interesting example involves the actress Gao Jin, a promising actress in the
mid-1950s specializing in young male roles. Her family was quite poor,
and her brother was in the laundry business in Hong Kong. Gao wanted
to join her brother and could not obtain permission. But she knew a young
girl who admired her and her art and whose mother held a position of some
Patrons and Patronage    129

=== PDF PAGE 151 ===

responsibility at the local police station. The girl introduced Gao to her
mother, and the mother took Gao as her adoptive daughter and helped her
obtain permission to go to Hong Kong.
79
Patrons, of course, were not altruists. Some were suspected of being
involved in activities deemed indecent or deviant, such as suspicious adop-
tive father-daughter relationships or homosexual adoptive mother-daughter
relationships. An interesting commentary in Yue Opera Newshad this to say
about patrons and patronage:
Patrons all aim to satisfy their own desires. Their explicit purpose is to boost
the actresses’ popularity, but their implicit purpose is to appropriate the
actresses. What patrons do is merely ﬂatter the actresses, to win their favor.
The great patrons ﬂatter the great stars, and small patrons ﬂatter small stars.
To ﬂatter an actress, a patron has to give costumes and paraphernalia as
gifts, manage theaters, organize opera companies, publish special issues,
and everything else necessary to gratify the star. There are also some people
who join the claques as a favor to their friends. 
As for female patrons, they also have their purposes. Some want to be
respectable senior adoptive mothers, others want to ﬁnd concubines for their
husbands, still others are interested in homosexual relations with the
actresses. We ﬁnd no concrete evidence of homosexuality in Yue opera cir-
cles, but we know it certainly exists.
The formulae are as follows: male patrons (yang) ﬂatter young female char-
acters (yin), and yang plus yin equals neutrality. Female patrons (yin) ﬂatter
young male impersonators (yang—though fake yang, better than nothing). 
The author declared, “I think the most loyal patron is every ‘pure and clean’
audience member in every theater.” And he ended his comments with the
question: “Does the development of Yue opera rely on ﬂattery?”
80
The author was certainly critical, but actresses generally condoned the
practice of patronage and acknowledged that they beneﬁted from it. Xiao-
Bai Yumei as well as other actresses did not have many complaints about
adoptive mothers, even though the adoptive mother system has received
much criticism and negative portrayals in the ofﬁcial history and discourse
on Yue opera after 1949. During the Republican and early PRC period, all
popular stars had their loyal patrons, many of whom took the role of adop-
tive mother. A star usually had more than one adoptive mother and many
more special patrons. Xu Yulan, for example, had at least two adoptive moth-
ers, Zhao Naixue and the wife of Xu Datong, popularly known as “the king
130 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 152 ===

of paper manufacture.” Xiao-Bai had three adoptive mothers; one was the
wife of a comprador, and the other two were wives of Ning-Shao bankers.
When asked why women’s opera had such success in Shanghai, Xiao-Bai
Yumei responded with one sentence: “That’s because of the adoptive moth-
ers.”
81 Xiao-Bai might have had her personal success in mind with that
answer, but indeed the success of many top actresses like Xiao-Bai ensured
the success of women’s Yue opera as a whole.
Another type of patronage involved women professionals and emerg-
ing entrepreneurs in Republican Shanghai, who aided their favorite actresses
with their professional expertise and social experience. Zhao Naixue, an
excellent tailor and the adoptive mother of Xu Yulan, not only took care
of Xu’s life but moreover was responsible for many of Xu’s specially
designed stage costumes. Xu Yulan moved in with Zhao in 1946 and lived
with her for many years, until Xu got married in 1954. During Xu’s sick-
ness in 1946, Zhao acted as the actress’s parent, caring for her at her bed-
side and also defending her against her boss Zhang Chunfan’s demands
that she perform.
82 Xu’s own mother later came to Shanghai and lived with
her, but as a rural woman, her mother could not give her the kind of social
support that Zhao was able to provide.
Tang Diyin, owner of the Green Jade Pen Company, was a special patron
of Yuan Xuefen’s and supported many of the actress’s activities through her
business expertise, although Yuan was not exactly Tang’s adoptive daugh-
ter, and the two probably had a more equal relationship than most others.
Yuan met Tang at the Soviet Union Radio Station through Chen Shulian,
the radio announcer. Yuan agreed to advertise for Tang’s pens, not for money,
but because she supported hardworking independent woman like Tang.
83
According to Cheng Rong:
Tang Diyin’s success came from her hard work. Her parents discriminated
against girls and gave her no support. Tang was strong willed and managed
to become an apprentice in a stationery shop after graduating from primary
school. When I saw her in 1945, she wore very short hair and a straight
cheongsam. She had a lot of ideas, very experienced and with a strong con-
cept of women’s independence. All of us four sisters [Yuan, Tang, Chen, and
Cheng herself] were like that—we all wanted independence, not reliance
on men.
84
Tang’s was a small factory that employed about ten workers. It was set up
in two living rooms of a three-story townhouse on a lane that branched
Patrons and Patronage    131

=== PDF PAGE 153 ===

off the main roadway, a type of housing known as shikumen. Tang was not
really rich, and she supported Yuan with her expertise and connections rather
than ﬁnancially. During the Civil War period, between 1946 and 1949, Tang
acted as Yuan’s trusted representative, and Yuan relied on Tang during these
eventful and sometimes very difﬁcult days.
85
It should be noted that even though rich patrons adopted only popular
stars who specialized in young male and female roles, other actresses, with
little access to this institution, were not critical of it. Zhou Baokui, a well-
known actress specializing in elder female roles, remarked, “Only big stars
had adoptive mothers. Others did not have this beneﬁt. We had to spend
much of our monthly pay on buying stage costumes for ourselves.” She
may have been a little envious but certainly was not critical.
86
The adoptive mother system lingered into the early PRC period but
ended with the beginning of the Cultural Revolution in 1966. With the
revolution, a totally different sociology and political economy replaced
those of the 1930s and 1940s, which had encouraged the development of
upper-class women’s patronage of Yue opera.
Yue Opera in the Mass Media 
In Shanghai in the 1940s, Yue opera’s inﬂuence was not limited to opera-
goers. Modern means of mass communication, such as radio, the phono-
graph and audio recordings, and motion pictures, plus cheaply produced
lyric handbooks (see ﬁg. 3.1), made Yue opera a pervasive inﬂuence in urban
society and beyond in the early Chinese electronic age.
87 Shanghai’s res-
idents, men and women, old and young, even if they did not attend the
performances in person, were all exposed to Yue opera—constantly hear-
ing its lyrics and music played on radios and phonographs, reading its gos-
sip in the tabloid press, and seeing its images in theatrical posters, newspaper
advertisements, lyric handbooks, and photographs displayed in downtown
photo studios. Many lower-class women who could not afford to attend
performances by major troupes before 1949 and many white-collar men
who did not care to attend Yue opera were nevertheless familiar with it
through hearing gossip about its stars as well as through its famous plays,
lyrics, music, and character modeling. 
The relationship between the new high-technology media and popular
entertainment in mid-twentieth-century Shanghai was one of mutual
reliance, not much different from that of our day, in which high-tech media
depended on popular programming in order to develop, while popular forms
132 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 154 ===

used high-tech means to spread beyond the limits of traditional venues.
The most popular programming of the time included Suzhou story-
singing, Yue opera, Beijing opera, Shanghai opera, and comic shows, with
the story-singing and Yue opera being perhaps the most commercially viable.
The story of Yue opera reveals much about the formation of a mass-medi-
ated popular culture in modern Shanghai. 
Radio appeared in Shanghai in the 1920s and became popular during
the 1930s. Private ownership of radio sets was already prevalent in mid-
dle- and upper-class households, and women in these families were avid
listeners, since they spent the larger part of the day at home.
88 But the inﬂu -
ence of radio broadcasting went far beyond the ownership of radio sets.
For one thing, Republican Shanghai was already a very crowded place.
Most of Shanghai’s commoner population lived in shikumen , three-story
townhouses located on the narrow lanes that branched off the main
streets.
89 A radio playing in one household could easily be heard by a dozen
or so neighbors. Moreover, there were always some neighborhood shops
as well as department stores, restaurants, and teahouses playing radios to
attract potential customers off the streets. Movies made during the period
Patrons and Patronage    133
3.1  Cheaply produced Yue opera lyrics handbooks and popular promotional 
publications for Yue opera fans in the 1930s and 1940s. (Preserved at the 
Shanghai Municipal Library)

=== PDF PAGE 155 ===

often tried to create a lively atmosphere by playing radio shows, popular
songs, Suzhou story-singing, or Yue opera in the background. The Yue opera
fan Wu Ping remarked that when she was in primary school, she was always
distracted by Yue opera broadcasts forever playing in the department store
across the street from her classroom. “I was listening to the radio across
the street from my school all the time. I didn’t care that much about my
math. But I could tell which actress was on as soon as I heard the huqin play-
ing the opening scores, even before she started to sing.”
90
The ﬁrst Yue opera radio show went on the air in July 1939, performed
by Ma Zhanghua with Fu Quanxiang and later with Yuan Xuefen.91 Sanyou
Enterprises, a pharmaceutical company, sponsored the show in order to
advertise its products. Ma Zhanghua’s fame quickly spread via airwaves
throughout the city. She soon became known as “the lightning young male
impersonator” (shandian xiaosheng). The word shandian, “lightning,” connotes
speed while alluding to electronic devices, a perfect description of Ma’s
sudden success boosted by the radio program. Because she advertised a tonic
product for Sanyou Enterprises, Ma got another nickname, a more cynical
one perhaps, “the tonic young male impersonator” (buwan xiaosheng), vividly
illustrating how much her fame was associated with the radio advertising. 
Sanyou Enterprises, moreover, published a special issue accompanying
its radio program, Yue Opera Special Issue(Yueju zhuankan), which was printed
on cheap paper and distributed free of charge. The booklet of about three
hundred pages contains pictures of Ma Zhanghua, Yuan Xuefen, and other
popular actresses as well as the lyrics of popular pieces from Yue opera
plays, punctuated by advertisements for the company’s products. In one
typical example, Ma Zhanghua’s and Yuan Xuefen’s lyrics occupy the larger
part of the two facing pages. Below the lyrics is a clearly printed line: “From
6:00 to 7:10  P.M. during Ma Zhanghua’s and Yuan Xuefen’s Yue opera,
please call in and request your favorite pieces. Ma’s and Yuan’s women’s
Yue opera performs at the Dalai Theater in Hushe on Beijing Road; for
reservations call 92205.” And on the left side, an advertisement for Sanyou
tonics catches the eye.
92
In the ensuing decade, as with Xuesheng Company and the Soviet Union
Radio Station (discussed in chapter 2), major Yue opera companies used
radio to introduce new plays, enhance their public visibility, and promote
sales. Actresses made themselves available to listeners and answered their
questions during the ﬁxed daily programs, which usually ran one to two
hours.
93 Live broadcasts from the theater were another regular feature. Yue
opera companies contracted directly with radio stations for broadcasts; the
134 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 156 ===

two parties usually did not pay each other for these programs, but adver-
tising revenues went to the stations.
The phonograph played a similar role in enhancing Yue opera’s popu-
larity. A phonograph was a luxury item owned mostly by upper-class fam-
ilies; in movies made during the 1940s, the presence of a phonograph
indicated an upper-class lifestyle. The inﬂuence of phonographs and
records, however, extended beyond the upper-class living room through
a market network of phonograph and recording companies, radio stations,
theaters, bookstores, publishing houses, department stores, radio equip-
ment and repair stores, and even watchmakers’ shops. A contemporaneous
lyric pocketbook, Complete Book of Lyrics from Shaoxing Opera(Shaoxing xikao
quance), testiﬁes to a marketing network centered on Yue opera. The book
was published in 1939 and cost sixty cents, roughly the price of a ticket to
a Yue opera show. It contains seventy-nine pages of lyrics from various Yue
opera plays, about twenty pages of pictures of Yue opera stars, and many
pages of advertisements. This lyric pocketbook reveals an interconnected
world of entertainment, commerce, and the media. One page has a chart
listing radio stations along with station symbols, wave frequencies, phone
numbers, and addresses. The other side of the page is an advertisement for
a phonograph company. The ﬁrst page contains two coupons from a phono-
graph company, which the reader could use to request that the designated
radio station play two Yue opera pieces from the company’s records. The
next two pages list major Yue opera theaters, with phone numbers and
addresses, and display an advertisement for another phonograph company.
On the inner back cover is a list of distributors of the book, some of which
appear to be retailers of phonographs and records. The distributors in Shang-
hai include four Yue opera theaters, four phonograph companies, three radio
stations, a radio repair shop, and a bookstore. Distributors outside Shang-
hai include phonograph stores, watchmakers’ shops, radio equipment
shops, department stores, and various retail stores.
94
Compared to radio, the phonograph had its advantages—it could play
a recording over and over again, and it helped spread the inﬂuence of Yue
opera far beyond Shanghai’s radio boundaries. Qiu Wenguang, a Sheng-
xian native and a lifetime Yue opera fan who has lived in Taiwan since 1949,
recalled how young girls in Shengxian learned Yue opera by singing along
with phonograph and records: 
My most vivid memory goes back to 1943, when I ﬁnished the fourth grade
and returned home from my grandma’s place to go to Chongren Senior Pri-
Patrons and Patronage    135

=== PDF PAGE 157 ===

mary School. Almost all the twenty or more girls in the school were mem-
bers of an opera school. As soon as they joined the opera school, they sat
around a table, holding a piece of paper with lyrics in their hands, and sang
together loudly with great concentration....  
During these two years, I had a classmate by the name of Qiu Chutao.
Chutao’s family and mine were on the same street, less than one hundred
meters away from each other. We thus played together even after school.
His family owned a phonograph, and his older sister Xingyun and cousin
Aining (who was also called Yuxuan) often sang along with the records,
assisted by a palm-size lyric book. As I had heard them singing all the time
in these years, I had these lyrics down pat.
95
Yue opera actresses began to produce records as early as 1936, when Yuan
Xuefen made her ﬁrst recording for the French-owned Baidai Recording
Company.
96 After that, hundreds of recordings of numerous well-known
Yue opera actresses appeared in the marketplace. Printed lyrics and scripts,
used in conjunction with the phonograph and records, helped listeners
better understand and appreciate the opera and aided amateur artists in
improving their skills.
97
Shanghai’s press also played a role in creating a mass opera culture. Print-
ing shops could be set up in a downstairs living room, and cheap, recycled
paper could be used to produce low-cost handbooks, which could then be
sold at bargain prices for a few cents to maybe sixty cents each.
98 Most of
these publications were printed in a small, thin format so that they could
easily be carried around in a pocket or a woman’s purse. They were mass-
produced to feed a large urban public interested in the opera. Fans collected
these pocketbook lyrics, and amateurs used them as scripts when putting
together shows for themselves. 
Other publications included tabloids, Yue opera company promotional
issues, and fan magazines. During the New Yue Opera reforms, companies
began to put together playbills that were sold at theater entrances before
each performance. The playbill included introductions to the plot and cast,
information on new features in stage scenery and costumes, and inciden-
tal details about the stars and the production process. These playbills sold
so well that they generated a considerable side income for the editors, usu-
ally company staff members.
99 Yue opera fan Wu Ping loved the playbills
so much that she saved all the ones she bought at performances and care-
fully preserved them in a wicker suitcase along with many lyric scripts.
100
These publications connected a visual and audio world to a printed world,
136 Patrons and Patronage

=== PDF PAGE 158 ===

blurred the territorial boundaries of different regimes of mass communi-
cation, and helped diffuse the culture of Yue opera in Shanghai’s public
space.
Finally, it was the cinema that made Yue opera one of the most inﬂuen-
tial arts in the nation. Motion pictures acquired a mass audience in Shang-
hai during the Republican period. As a 1936 essay, “The Development of
Shanghai’s Cinemas,” noted:
Motion pictures...  a s  a  n e w  f o r m  o f  a r t  i n d e e d  won lots of people’s inter-
est and appreciation. The movie business developed rapidly. As time passes,
it not only comes to share the entertainment market with China’s traditional
operas but tends to surpass them in popularity. Right now there are between
thirty-three and thirty-six cinemas in Shanghai. Last year a movie revue,
Diantong Pictorial [Diantong huabao], charted all the cinemas on a Shanghai
street map and topped the map with a large-character headline, “Places Where
a Million People Spend Leisure Time Everyday!”
101
A decade later, a tourist book published in 1947 listed thirty-four movie
theaters, while the Shanghai Gazetteer(Shanghai zhi) listed forty “noted” cin-
emas in Shanghai.102 “A million people” is certainly a gross exaggeration,
and statistics indicate that the cinema shared the entertainment market with
other popular forms. Unlike local operas, however, the cinema drew a gen-
eral audience from the city’s young professionals and ofﬁce workers as well
as students, an audience that cut across native origins yet was character-
ized by its youth and modern-style education. 
During the postwar period, Yue opera actresses began to look at the cin-
ema as a means to expand the horizon of their art and reach out to the gen-
eral public. The ﬁrst to act in ﬁlms was Yuan Xuefen, and the ﬁrst Yue opera
ﬁlm was Sister Xianglin, produced in 1948 by the Qiming Studio. In the same
year, this studio also produced the noted dramatist Hong Shen’s Hope for
the Day at the Dawn When the Cock Begins to Crow(Jiming zao kantian), directed
by Ying Yunwei and starring Yuan Xuefen among others. Sister Xianglin
opened in September 1948 in four downtown cinemas, and copies of the
ﬁlm were distributed outside of Shanghai. The September program at the
Great Shanghai Theater (Da Shanghai Daxiyuan) featured the ﬁlm Sister
Xianglin in the afternoon and the Yue opera Coral (Shanhu yin), scripted by
the famous playwright Tian Han, in the evening. Both were productions
of Yuan Xuefen’s Xuesheng Company.
103 Another example shows how cin-
ema helped promote reformed Yue opera outside of Shanghai, even export-
Patrons and Patronage    137

=== PDF PAGE 159 ===

ing it back to its native province of Zhejiang. In early 1949, Xiao Yueying,
a leading Yue opera actress with a rural itinerant troupe in the Hangzhou-
Jiaxing-Huzhou area, watched the ﬁlm. Xiao was so impressed that she
decided to adapt the ﬁlm for the stage. Working solely from memory, she
and her troupe put together the play and performed it successfully for her
rural audience.
104 The most inﬂuential Yue opera ﬁlms, however, were made
in the 1950s and early 1960s and include The Butterﬂy Lovers (1953), Emerald
Hairpin(1962), andDream of the Red Chamber(1962) plus Sister Xianglin (1978).105
These Yue opera movies were popular and attracted a nationwide, cross-gen-
der audience; many of these people never attended Yue opera shows. 
Women’s Yue opera permeated the mass media and, through them, pen-
etrated modern China’s cultural consciousness. Radio, the phonograph,
the printing press, and ﬁlm together transformed an urban women’s culture—
Yue opera and its love dramas—into a media phenomenon with a lasting
inﬂuence on modern China’s cultural formation in the mid-twentieth
 century.

=== PDF PAGE 160 ===

139
Chapter 4
Staging in the Public Arena
One evening in May 1946, the atmosphere in the Star Theater was unusual,
as a special rehearsal of the New Yue Opera play Sister Xianglin was about
to begin. The play had been adapted from the great modernist and left-
wing writer Lu Xun’s 1924 short story “The New Year’s Sacriﬁce” (Zhufu).
Well-known intellectuals and dramatists occupied almost the whole bal-
cony, among them Tian Han, Hong Shen, Huang Zuolin, Shi Dongshan,
Fei Mu, Zhang Junxiang, Li Jianwu, Bai Yang, Ding Cong, and Hu Feng.
For the ﬁrst time in its history, Yue opera had the honor of entertaining
the cultural elite as a group.
1
Sister Xianglin tells a tragic story of an unfortunate peasant woman who
has married twice and been widowed twice. Both of her husbands died in
sickness and poverty, and wolves consumed her only son, Ah Mao. Local
superstitions have convinced her that she is to blame for her loved ones’
misfortunes, because of bad karma from her previous lives. At the house-
hold of Master Lu, where she works as a domestic helper, a fellow servant
tells her that the king of the underworld will cut her into two halves after
her death so that she may serve her two husbands in the other world. Vil-
lagers scorn her for bringing bad luck to whatever she touches, and Mas-
ter Lu eventually dismisses her for this reason. Wandering in the streets
with a beggar’s stick and basket in her hand, she asks passersby: “Tell me,
will the soul continue to live after a person dies? Does hell exist?” No one
seems able to answer her question, and people try to avoid her. Rejected
by all, having no place to go, cold and hungry, she perishes in the streets
one New Year’s eve. 
Lu Xun’s work had rarely been adapted for the stage, due both to its


=== PDF PAGE 161 ===

political sensitivity and to the difﬁculties of translating his artistry—its
nuance and rich meaning—into performance. This was the ﬁrst time since
the mid-1930s that a story by Lu Xun was performed onstage, an extraor-
dinary time when his work had become even more politically sensitive than
before in the eve of an all-out civil war between the Nationalist Party and
the Chinese Communist Party. It would have been impossible for left-wing
intellectuals to produce Lu Xun’s work on a Shanghai stage under the Nation-
alist government’s watchful eye. The leading cultural personalities were sur-
prised that Yue opera—a local opera largely by and for women, featuring
“conventional” love stories, and of little political signiﬁcance—was doing
what they could not do. The intellectuals had come to watch the play after
being invited by Xu Guangping, Lu Xun’s widow, and out of respect for
the master. Hardly expecting that a “vulgar” local opera would be able to
do justice to Lu Xun’s work, they were nonetheless impressed by the show.
Tian Han, for example, profusely praised the performance: “This is from
real life, humane, accurate, and convincing.”
2
The play also caught the attention of the Nationalist government and
eventually made Yue opera a site of contention between the two political
rivals during the ensuing civil war, from 1946 to 1949. Out of this conﬂict,
an unlikely alliance emerged between Yue opera actresses and CCP left-
wing intellectuals: intellectuals, with their pens and networks, helped cre-
ate positive modern images of Yue opera actresses in the city’s mainstream
media and began to penetrate Yue opera companies, through which they
were able to inﬂuence a large population characterized by relatively low
literacy, political apathy, and indifference to intellectual discourse.
The staging of Sister Xianglin in May 1946 set in motion a chain of land-
mark events in the ensuing years that culminated in the media drama in
the wake of Xiao Dangui’s tragic death. Unwittingly involved in the poli-
tics of the Civil War, the actresses fought their own battles and emerged
during this conﬂict-ridden period with a newfound identity and status. 
Politics and Culture in Postwar Shanghai
The post–World War II period in China witnessed another round of com-
petition between the two rival parties on all fronts. While the two sides
engaged each other ferociously on the battleﬁeld, they also fought an ide-
ological war on the urban front, competing to control or inﬂuence public
opinion. On August 31, 1945, two weeks after Japan surrendered, the
Nationalist Executive Committee issued guidelines for regulating the mass
140 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 162 ===

media. In September, the Nationalists ordered the conﬁscation of all news-
 papers, news agencies, magazines, and movie studios owned by Japanese
and puppet-government institutions or related individuals. From Septem-
ber 17 to 22, the government reorganized the two major privately owned
Shanghai newspapers, Shanghai Newspaper (Shen bao) and Daily News (Xin-
wen bao), taking control of their editorial boards.
3 On April 2, 1946, the
Shanghai municipal police issued a directive, “Censorship of Shanghai’s
Broadcast Programs (Draft),” ordering that “all radio stations in the city,
privately or publicly owned, should submit their broadcast programs to
this bureau for review two days in advance.”
4 Also in 1946, the Social Affairs
Bureau of the Shanghai municipal government ordered all theaters and opera
houses to register with its ofﬁce as a prerequisite for licensing. It then ordered
all actors, singers, dancing girls, and prostitutes to register, a policy deemed
humiliating by all actors and threatening by left-wing intellectual actors
active in ﬁlm and spoken drama. The government also took steps to orga-
nize Nationalist-controlled unions in the entertainment business as well as
in factories. At the same time, secret police relentlessly cracked down on
CCP activities, arresting and executing the Party’s underground workers. 
These government measures were, however, far from effective in check-
ing CCP activities. The Party reestablished its Shanghai Committee after
the Japanese surrender, led ﬁrst by Liu Changsheng and then by Liu Xiao
after he arrived in Shanghai from Yan’an in February 1946. In January 1947,
the CCP Central Committee established its Shanghai Branch, which in May
became the Shanghai Bureau, headed by Secretary Liu Xiao and Deputy
Secretary Liu Changsheng. The Shanghai Bureau contained subcommit-
tees charged with responsibility for Party activities related to factories, busi-
nesses, schools, women, and culture. The Committee on the Cultural and
Commercial United Front was then established under Secretary Zhang
Zhiyi and Deputy Secretary Sha Wenhan to lead and coordinate under-
ground activities in the ﬁelds of publishing, literature, theater, and music;
at the end of 1948, it became the Committee on Cultural Work, with Chen
Yusun as its secretary.
5
Under the leadership of the Shanghai Bureau and its subcommittees,
numerous grassroots cells were established in all walks of life, directly imple-
menting the Party agenda in the local environment. Systematic party build-
ing aside, CCP cadres founded, penetrated, and controlled many civil
organizations, such as teachers’ associations, student unions, associations for
ﬁlm industry artists, and women’s organizations, using them as legal ven-
ues for subversive activities against the government. The CCP’s cultural activ-
Staging in the Public Arena    141

=== PDF PAGE 163 ===

ities incorporated a wide variety of artistic forms, including the popular sing-
along movement, cartoon satire, and grassroots amateur performance.
The mass media—the press and the ﬁlm industry—remained the prior-
ity of the Party’s cultural work. As early as September 14, 1945, Mao Zedong
and Zhou Enlai, in the middle of peace negotiations in Chongqing, sent a
telegram to the CCP Central Committee in Yan’an and top leaders in the
central China base area with the following instructions: “The Shanghai New
China Daily[Xinhua ribao] and other Party-controlled newspapers published
under the aegis of non-Party associates should be published in Nanjing,
Wuhan, and Hong Kong as soon as possible. According to Nationalist reg-
ulations, newspapers can be published ﬁrst and register later. Act quickly—
the quicker, the better. Or we will get the worst of it.”
6 From August 1945
to June 1946, before the outbreak of civil war, a large number of left-wing
publications appeared. In Shanghai, CCP members published more than
a dozen newspapers and periodicals, including Times Daily (Shidai ribao),
Weekly (Zhou bao), People’s Weekly (Renren zhoubao), United Daily (Lianhe
ribao), Free Man (Ziyou ren), The Renaissance (Wenyi fuxing),Literary Gallery
(Wencui), and National Reconstruction Daily (Jian’guo ribao). The Southern
Committee of the CCP’s Central Committee, for example, secretly spon-
sored United Daily, although the registered publisher was an American. The
government shut down the newspaper on November 30, 1945, but the same
staff managed to publish United Evening News(Lianhe wanbao) on April 15,
1946, secretly sponsored by the CCP’s Shanghai Committee, only to have
the paper banned again on May 24, 1947 . In mid-1947, the government
forcefully asserted control over the left-wing press as the military conﬂict
between the two parties escalated and began to turn in the CCP’s favor;
by the end of the year, critical left-wing periodicals had virtually disap-
peared from the public domain.
7
Left-wing intellectuals, however, also inﬁltrated the editorial boards of
privately owned mainstream newspapers, the most famous of which were
Wenhui Daily (Wenhui bao) and Dagong Daily; the editors in chief of both
papers were CCP members.
8 CCP members and left-wing intellectuals more-
over worked as editors, reporters, and columnists for major newspapers while
contributing to government-controlled media such as The Central Daily
(Zhongyang ribao), Shanghai Newspaper, and Daily News. 
The CCP’s presence in the print media was a highly motivated, organ-
ized, disciplined, and effective force consisting of capable young men and
women. These journalists skillfully provided media support for student
demonstrations and popular unrest and inﬂuenced public opinion through
142 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 164 ===

criticism of unpopular government policies. To a certain extent, CCP under-
ground journalism was able to coordinate what in normal times would be
routine scrutiny of the government into an opposition front and reoriented
public sensibilities to the CCP’s advantage.
Likewise, the CCP and left-wing intellectuals, who had held irrefutable
prestige in Shanghai’s ﬁlm industry in the 1930s, quickly regained their
inﬂuence in the postwar period. In 1946, Yang Hansheng and other CCP
cultural cadres returned to Shanghai and reestablished the Lianhua Studio,
which was reorganized into the Kunlun Studio in 1947 . This studio pro-
duced many popular movies such as Eight Thousand Miles under Clouds and Moon-
light (Baqian li lu yun he yue), in 1947); River of Sorrow Flows East (Yijiang
chunshui xiang dong liu), in 1947; and Lights from the Windows of Thousands
of Families (Wanjia denghuo), in 1948. Between 1946 and 1949, by the CCP’s
account, two dozen “progressive” and inﬂuential features appeared in
Shanghai.
The Party was, however, slow in penetrating the world of popular enter-
tainment in the city, as left-wing intellectuals who despised the commer-
cial popular culture were in charge of CCP cultural operations. During the
entire Civil War period, no CCP cell was set up in any popular entertain-
ment form in Shanghai. Despite their low priority, entertainment forms
were not free of CCP inﬂuence, and the staging of Sister Xianglin was partly
a result of wide-ranging CCP underground grassroots activities.
Sister Xianglin and the CCP’s Underground Operation
One day in April 1946, backstage at the Star Theater, Yuan Xuefen, the
leading actress and head of the company, was putting on makeup while
director and playwright Nan Wei read a story aloud to her. Nan Wei had
recently read “Sister Xianglin—A Study of Female Characters in Lu Xun’s
Work,” an essay in Women and Literature (Funü yu wenxue), by Ding Ying.
The essay had introduced Nan Wei to Lu Xun’s The New Year’s Sacriﬁce and
inspired him to adapt the story for the Yue opera stage. Yuan found her-
self becoming totally absorbed in the vivid scenery and characters of Shao -
xing, which was so familiar to her, and as soon as Nan Wei had ﬁnished,
she announced: “You write the play. I will perform it.”
9 In little more than
a month, the play Sister Xianglin was born.
Who was Ding Ying? Where did Nan Wei get the book? “Ding Ying”
was one of the many pseudonyms Ding Jingtang used in the 1940s in order
to protect his true identity. The following account is based on an inter-
Staging in the Public Arena    143

=== PDF PAGE 165 ===

view (see ﬁg. 4.1), his essays, and his book Women and Literature.10 Ding Jing-
tang lost his parents when he was young. He left his hometown of Ningbo
and moved to Shanghai to live with his aunt, who was married to, in Ding’s
words, a “medium-size capitalist.” During his years at the Young Men’s
Christian Association (YMCA) high school, from 1936 to 1939, Ding and
his fellow students worked with the Student Union (Xue Xie), a CCP-
afﬁliated local association coordinating citywide student mobilization for
the War of Resistance against Japan. Their duties included circulating the
organization’s journal Student Life (Xuesheng shenghuo), organizing study
groups, and coordinating student activities in several middle schools along
what is now Beijing Road. Ding joined the Party in 1938. “Comrades of
our generation who joined the revolution during the War of Resistance
almost all experienced a similar passage,” Ding explained, “from reading
the May Fourth New Literature, advancing to elementary Marxist read-
ings and training in organizational activities, to joining the Party.”
11 In 1939,
Ding qualiﬁed to enter Dongwu University. In winter 1940, he became
editor of The United Voice (Lian sheng), ofﬁcially sponsored by the Asso-
ciation of Shanghai Christian Student Fellowships but actually under the
direction of the CCP Committee on Student Activities. This and all other
similar publications came to a full stop in December 1941, when Japanese
troops marched into “island” Shanghai upon the outbreak of the Paciﬁc
War. Owing to the extremely dangerous situation in the occupied city, the
CCP Shanghai Committee was disbanded, and in order to maintain some
underground forces in the city, Ding and his comrades were instructed to
lie low.
12
As soon as the war ended, in August 1945, Ding Jingtang resumed his
work of publishing journals propagating left-wing ideas. Within this con-
text, he wrote and published his study on Sister Xianglin. Ding recalled in
1982:
After the victory [over Japan], a former college classmate of mine asked
me to contribute and organize articles for the journal Progressive Women(Qian-
jin funü) on behalf of the owners, Liu and his wife. 13 At that time, all the
journals I was coordinating were student publications that lacked stable
 funding. These journals often appeared for a few months and disappeared
whenever the funding ran out. Now, with this opportunity, I invited Tian
Zhongluo (Yuan Ying), Zhou Jiling, Zhao Zi, and others to occupy this posi-
tion. After the victory in the War of Resistance, I had planned to write a
series of articles on female images in modern Chinese literature. At the time,
144 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 166 ===

I wrote two pieces. The ﬁrst one was that very article, “Sister Xianglin—A
Study of Female Characters in Lu Xun’s Work,” published in the second issue
of Progressive Women [November 1945] under the pen name Li Yang. I had
written it in October 1945. The article was later republished in a collection
of essays titled Women and Literature, published under the name “Ding Ying”
in February 1946 by the Shanghai Hujiang Book House.
14
The Nationalist government shut down Progressive Women after four issues,
when a member of the Nationalist Shanghai Committee accused the jour-
nal of printing CCP propaganda, and the Shanghai Hujiang Book House
existed only as a name Ding had made up for the publication of his book.
Women and Literature, not sold in bookstores owing to censorship of left-
wing publications, circulated among friends and the like minded. Through
a network of comrades, friends, and relatives, the book reached Nan Wei,
