and an accidental meeting between two high-school friends triggered the
landmark production of the Yue opera Sister Xianglin.15
Ding Jingtang had a friend and comrade named Wu Kang. The two were
classmates and fellow activists at the YMCA high school, and Ding brought
Wu Kang into the Party in 1939. Wu qualiﬁed for entrance to Shanghai
Zhijiang University, while Ding entered Dongwu University, and they both
received YMCA fellowships for poor students. With his aunt’s under-
standing, Ding offered his fellowship to Wu Kang; Wu was from a poorer
family, one fellowship was not enough to support him through school, and,
because he spent most of his spare time working for the Party, he was not
able to work for money. Although classrooms for both Dongwu and Zhi-
jiang Universities were in the same building of the Continental department
store on Nanjing Road, the two friends nevertheless stopped seeing each
other, following Party regulations on underground work. One day in 1946,
Ding ran into Wu in the street. Told that Wu’s brother-in-law Nan Wei
worked for the Xuesheng Company, Ding handed Wu a copy of Women
and Literature and asked Wu to give it to Nan Wei.
16 Hoping to draw his
brother-in-law close to the revolution, Wu in turn asked Ding to send some
friends to contact Nan. Ding recalled:
At that time, I had already been transferred to work under the Shanghai [CCP]
Cultural Committee [Shanghai Wenwei] and was in charge of the Party-led
Shanghai Youth Association of Performing Arts. Many young Party mem-
bers were engaged in various theatrical activities. So I sent Liao Lin to Nan
Wei, with the introduction “Brother Ah Kang [reference to Wu Kang] asked
me to come to see you.” Liao Lin [pen names Luo Lin and Ye Ping] was an
amateur spoken-drama veteran and a special correspondent in ﬁlm and the-
ater for the daily newspaper The New Current Affairs [Shishi xinbao].
17
Liao Lin thereafter became a frequent visitor to the Xuesheng Company.
Liao’s girlfriend Tong Lijuan, also a Party member, was working with the
Shanghai Women’s Association, an organization headed by Lu Xun’s widow
Xu Guangping but in fact under CCP direction. In Xu’s name, Tong Lijuan
and another female CCP member acted as editors of the two daily news-
paper supplements on women’s issues, Women’s News(Fuxun), for United Evening
News, and Women’s Life (Funü shenghuo), for The New Current Affairs. While
Liao Lin made friends with Nan Wei, Tong Lijuan befriended Yuan Xuefen.
The two young CCP cadres encouraged Nan and Yuan in their efforts to
stage Lu Xun’s work. Liao Lin told Nan Wei, “Putting Lu Xun’s work on the
146 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 168 ===

stage will be a high point in your life.” Tong Lijuan arranged for Yuan Xue-
fen and Nan Wei to meet with Xu Guangping, who gave the Xuesheng Com-
pany permission to adapt The New Year’s Sacriﬁce for the Yue opera stage and
also provided moral support. After the performance, Liao and Tong took
Yuan Xuefen and Nan Wei to the home of Yu Ling, the playwright and head
of the CCP underground Committee on Culture, where they met Tian Han
and engaged in a serious discussion of local-opera reform.
18 Thus, although
the production of Sister Xianglinwas an autonomous decision made entirely
by Yuan and her director, lower-level CCP cadres served as catalysts and
guided Yuan and Nan to contact with top CCP intellectuals. 
The staging of Sister Xianglin made Yue opera and its reforms a subject of
discussion in the mainstream media. Left-wing journalists and commenta-
tors loudly applauded the play as a milestone in Yue opera reform and a turn-
ing point that marked the transformation of a “vulgar” local opera into a
reputable and meaningful theater for social and cultural reform. These com-
ments appeared not only in left-wing newspapers, such as Wenhui Dailyand
The New Citizen Daily (Xinmin ribao), but also in the Nationalist-controlled
The Daily Newsand The New Current Affairs. Liao Lin, using the pen name Luo
Lin, reported in The New Current Affairsa week before the opening of the play
in an article headlined “Lu Xun’s Famous Fiction Adapted for the Yue Opera
Stage: Yuan Xuefen Plays Sister Xianglin, Setting a New Record in Traditional
Opera Reform.”
19 Three days after the play’s premiere, Liao’s article, titled
“Tian Han Discusses Yue Opera Reform with Yuan Xuefen and Nan Wei,”
again helped draw Yue opera into mainstream culture.
20 In another article,
using the pen name Ye Ping, Liao Lin wrote: 
The staging of Sister Xianglin is signiﬁcant. No one can deny the fact that
local opera is groping forward step by step....  W e  h o p e  the staging of  Sister
Xianglin will inﬂuence various local operas and make them bear more respon-
sibilities for the education of their audience.21
Wenhui Dailyalso published an interview with Yuan Xuefen by Mei Duo, a left-
wing journalist. The article, titled “Sister Xianglin Backstage,” cites Yuan: 
She [Yuan Xuefen] said that their Xuesheng Company has more than twenty
members who have worked together for many years. The purpose for their
getting together is not just to make some money but rather to reform Yue
opera and make it progressive....  S h e  s a i d  t h a t  b e c ause Yue opera (Shao -
xing opera) traditionally presented stereotyped scholar-beauty love stories,

=== PDF PAGE 169 ===

people naturally thought the opera worthless, but they can make the opera
socially meaningful by changing the content of the plays.22
A commentary by Yu Ping in World Morning News (Shijie chenbao) reads: 
The conversion of Sister Xianglinfor dramatic presentation opens a new path
for Yue opera and gives the opera a new life....  G e n e r ally speaking, the
conversion did not betray the spirit of Master Lu Xun’s original work.
Although the play emphasized a romance, it successfully manifested, through
the romance, the suffering and misfortune of ordinary rural women in feu-
dal society.
23
Another commentary, by Yu Yu in Daily News, praised Yue opera reforms
compared to the vulgarity of popular entertainment: 
While Beijing opera still ponders whether or not to reform, and when its
“imperial capital style” [Jingchao pai] relies on Break Open the Cofﬁn [Da piguan]
and Spinning Cotton [Fang mianhua] [deemed representative of vulgar erotic
plays], while the “vicious Shanghai style” [exing Haipai] relies on “striptease”
to survive, the “diduopera” [Yue opera] that they viewed with contempt walks
onto the great path of enlightenment one step at a time.
24
The inﬂuential playwright Tian Han, in his article “The Path toward
 Popularization of Performing Arts,” summarized the left-wing intellectual
elite’s view of Yue opera reforms and the staging of Sister Xianglin: 
Sister Xianglin moved me. I had occasionally seen didu opera, the so-called
Shaoxing civil opera, in places like the Great World [entertainment hall]
before the war. It did not catch my attention. Later, when I was in Chongqing
[during the war], I heard friends returning from “island” Shanghai praising
profusely the progressiveness of Shaoxing opera, saying that the opera had
developed to the extent that it could stage many socially meaningful plays
and that it now had its own opera houses ....  S till, I did not hope for too
much. Not until I attended the play Sister Xianglin was I convinced that the
opera’s achievement, due to its eight-year reform effort, has surpassed my
imagination.
25
Sister Xianglinmoreover caught the attention of the top CCP leadership and
ultimately inspired the Party to amend its underground cultural policy in
148 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 170 ===

Shanghai. In October 1946, Zhou Enlai, then leading the CCP team in
peace talks at Nanjing and spending a lot of time in Shanghai between meet-
ings, quietly attended the Xuesheng Company’s show Sad Moon over the Liao
Royal Palace, performed by Yuan Xuefen. Zhou’s family was originally from
Shaoxing, and Zhou himself had been fond of performing onstage in his
student years in Naikai High School in Tianjin. Impressed by Yuan Xue-
fen’s performance and the size of the audience, Zhou summoned Yu Ling
for an oral report on women’s Yue opera and its reforms. Before he left for
Yan’an, when the peace talks broke down in November 1946, Zhou twice
discussed local operas with the Shanghai underground. He told Yu Ling and
Liu Housheng, a CCP cadre and dramatist who later joined the Xuesheng
Company as head of the staff, “You should pay attention to this [Xuesheng]
company. I knew about Shaoxing opera, but I did not know that the opera
had such a large audience and was so inﬂuential in Shanghai.” He instructed,
“You should send Party members to help them improve their arts and, through
contacting them, respecting them, assisting them, patiently guide them
toward the road of revolution. They have an audience, and that is power.”
26
Recognizing the popularity and hence importance of local operas in
general, Zhou wanted them incorporated into the blueprint of the CCP’s
cultural work, instructing that Shanghai’s underground work in the enter-
tainment business should have a three-tier structure. The ﬁrst tier consisted
of overtly leftist spoken drama, which was entirely under Party control.
The second tier was the Kunlun Studio and the Audience Drama Com-
pany, both in the hands of left-wing intellectuals but featuring more pop-
ular productions. And the third tier included local operas and privately
owned movie studios, which indeed constituted the core of popular
entertainment. If the government were to crack down on leftist spoken
drama, as was almost certain to happen, then its personnel should move
to the second tier and from there to the third tier. Zhou stressed that the
Party ought to take local operas seriously because they had a consider-
able audience, but he cautioned the Shanghai underground to select upright
and respectable cadres to work in local operas.
27
Following Zhou’s instructions, the Shanghai underground committee sent
intellectual CCP members and afﬁliates to work with local opera troupes.
Party member Qian Yingyu and Party afﬁliate Wu Chen joined Yue opera
companies in 1947 . Later, in 1948, Liu Housheng himself entered the Xue -
sheng Company as a director who also headed the staff. By 1949, almost
all the ﬁve major Yue opera companies had one or two playwrights or direc-
tors who were CCP afﬁliates. Most of these men were veterans of spoken
Staging in the Public Arena    149

=== PDF PAGE 171 ===

drama. As the spoken-drama market continued to shrink during the post-
war period, and because the ﬁlm industry could absorb only a limited num-
ber of new participants, some veterans found employment in local opera,
calling this “jumping into the sea [of popular culture]” (xia hai). While spo-
ken-drama veterans worked for Yue opera companies, left-wing journalists
continued to support Yue opera reform and publicize new images of Yue
opera actresses in the print media, gathering public opinion behind the
actresses in a series of conﬂicts that followed the staging of Sister Xianglin.
Moreover, cultural celebrities such as Tian Han and Yu Ling became friends
and teachers of these young Yue opera reformers, providing much needed
advice and moral support. Tian Han and his wife An E also began to write
plays for Yue opera. The politically naive Yuan Xuefen and her colleagues
at the time did not know that these respected “good-hearted masters” (hao-
hao xiansheng) were associated with the CCP. In 1949, as the communist
army approached Shanghai, Liu Housheng and Wu Chen called a few meet-
ings with Yue opera actresses to discuss preparation for the imminent
takeover. In May, when the People’s Liberation Army (PLA) had overcome
the last Nationalist resistance in Shanghai, actresses from ﬁve major Yue
opera companies went on the air to welcome the PLA, telling Shanghai’s
populace to stay home and remain calm, as the PLA would not disturb the
common people.
28 On June 5, after the CCP took over Shanghai, the Party
convened a conference of the city’s cultural elites. Major Chen Yi addressed
the conference. The new government selected only two representatives from
the ﬁeld of popular entertainment: one was the famous Beijing opera actor
Zhou Xinfang, and the other was Yuan Xuefen.
The Union Incident: Actresses in Local and National Politics
The staging of Sister Xianglinin 1946 also caught the attention of the Nation-
alist Shanghai government and made Yuan Xuefen and her colleagues tar-
gets of political surveillance. The incident that occurred in the wake of
the show reveals how the national struggle between the CCP and the
Nationalists affected local politics within the Yue opera trade in postwar
Shanghai.
In July 1946, a group of Yue opera musicians, who were old masters of
the actor-centered system, and backstage hands, mostly men with unsta-
ble employment or unemployed, organized the Yue Opera Workers’ Union
in the context of the government-sponsored union movement—a means
of preempting the CCP’s labor movement but otherwise having no obvious
150 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 172 ===

focus and hence badly organized. Although the Yue opera union was ofﬁ-
cially afﬁliated with the Shanghai General Union under the Social Affairs
Bureau of the Shanghai municipal government, in reality it received little
leadership or discipline from any of these entities. The union activists, the
majority of whom were lower-class folks with little understanding of and
no access to national politics, cared only for their own concrete interests.
The union’s purposes, its organizers declared, were to offset the power of
theater bosses who had organized themselves into the Yue Opera Employ-
ers’ Union, safeguard members’ right to work, negotiate salaries on behalf
of its members, and maintain its members’ welfare. The union was instantly
popular among most Yue opera employees because it promised to ﬁght
employers on behalf of union members. Actresses and staff in most Yue
opera companies joined the union, and the unionists invited Yuan Xuefen
to chair the board of directors. Suspicious that this might be just another
way to squeeze money out of the actresses, Yuan declined to have any-
thing to do with it. But other popular actresses, including Fu Quanxiang
and Yin Guifang, were initially enthusiastic about the union and its ideas.
Fu Quanxiang offered her home to serve as headquarters for the union, and
Yin Guifang chaired the board with Fu Quanxiang, Zhu Shuizhao, Xu Yue -
xing (staff member), and Zhang Zifan (director) serving as board members.
Unfortunately, Yuan Xuefen’s suspicion was not without foundation. Before
long, the union fell into the hands of some unscrupulous Shengxian-native
men whose only concern was to collect money from actresses. They
harassed companies with random demands for “membership fees,” and the
union’s promise that it would protect the rights of employees turned out to
be empty. The union quickly lost its popularity as people realized that it
was nothing more than a charity for the unemployed former actors and a
“rice bowl” for some local opportunists, as Fu Quanxiang put it.
29 Moreover,
as money ﬂowed into the pockets of the opportunists, the union’s ﬁnancial
resources dried up, and the board members, most of whom were popular
stars, ended up paying the union’s bills out of their own pockets. Yin Guifang
resigned on August 25, and other board members followed suit.
30
The extortion of contributions from female stars by male associates—
whether local troublemakers, retired actors, theater bosses, or members of
male-dominated native-place organizations—was a common phenomenon
of the time, according to Fu Quanxiang and other actresses. Female stars
and their male associates were all part of a complicated native-place and
professional network that obligated young actresses to contribute to the
men’s welfare on the basis of native-place ties and former teacher-disciple
Staging in the Public Arena    151

=== PDF PAGE 173 ===

relationships. This moral pressure was often backed by the threat of van-
dalism or personal attack. No one liked to be squeezed, but actresses took
different approaches to the situation. They usually had to accede to at least
some requests—sometimes giving cash contributions and at other times
performing for private parties or fund-raising events.
31 While Yin Guifang
and Fu Quanxiang seem to have represented the attitude of many actresses
in their willingness to negotiate and cooperate with the unionists, Yuan
Xuefen refused to deal with them at all. Xiao Dangui and her Dangui Com-
pany, another major Yue opera company of the time, had nothing to do
with the unionists, because Xiao and the company were ﬁrmly in the hands
of Zhang Chunfan, a strongman in charge of the Employers’ Union. 
The unionists were angry. Not being in a position to harm Zhang, they
blamed Yuan Xuefen for leading the rebellion against the union and plot-
ted retaliation. On August 27, 1946, as Yuan rode in her personal rickshaw
to the Soviet Union Radio Station for a regular broadcast program, a man
ambushed her by pouring a bag of dung over her head. A few days later,
Yuan received a threat instructing her to leave Shanghai within three days
unless she wanted a taste of “peanuts” (the meaning of this term was made
explicit by an enclosed bullet).
Yuan’s persecution generated a wave of sympathy in both the main-
stream and the tabloid press. On August 29, Dagong Daily, a left-inclined
mainstream newspaper, reported these incidents with the headline “Refus-
ing to Join the Opportunist Organization, Yuan Xuefen Execrated and
Ambushed—Hit with Dung on Her Way to the Radio Station.” Accord-
ing to the report, when the unionists insisted that Yuan head the board,
she complained to the Social Affairs Bureau and refused to join the union,
since its members were troublemakers, not Yue opera workers. The union-
ists tried to collect union fees from Yuan, even though she was not a mem-
ber. Finally, decisively spurned by Yuan, the unionists resorted to base
methods.
32 A World Morning Newsheadline read “Let an Honest Person Live,”
and the headline in The New Citizen Daily was “Misfortune for a Progressive
Actress of a Traditional Opera.”33
Tian Han was indignant. Using his personal inﬂuence, he called a news
conference to support Yuan and stage a general protest against govern-
ment measures aimed at checking left-wing activities as well as cleansing
obscenity in popular entertainment. About two hundred cultural celebri-
ties, administrators of professional associations, and reporters attended,
among them Guo Moruo, Hong Shen, and Xu Guangping, the linguist Zhao
Jingsheng, the famous “butterﬂy” ﬁction writer and editor Yan Duhe, head
152 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 174 ===

of the Entertainment Association Dong Tianmin, and head of the Performers’
Association Liang Yiming. At the news conference, held on September 10,
1946, a shy but determined Yuan Xuefen discussed her persecution in terms
of women’s liberation and theatrical reform. She described herself as a pro-
gressive actress devoted to Yue opera reform and complained that the union-
ists had attacked her because she refused to join their union. “I hope social
celebrities and journalists will extend their righteous hands to me and uphold
justice.”
34 She also stated: “ It is a small thing if it concerns only my career.
But if this kind of behavior is allowed to continue, then what safeguard is
there for other Yue opera actresses? Or for women’s right to work in gen-
eral?”
35 Xu Guangping also spoke about women’s social status and encour-
aged Yuan and other Yue opera actresses to unite in seeking their own
salvation.
36
It is interesting to note that the celebrity status of left-wing intellectuals
and their direct connection with top CCP leaders such as Mao and Zhou
placed them outside the local Party network and allowed them to act freely
without the Party’s restrictions on underground work. It was, moreover, the
duty of the Party’s local branch to look out for the safety of these left-wing
celebrities. While Tian Han and other celebrities relied on their personal
inﬂuence to gather public support for Yuan Xuefen and used Yuan’s case to
highlight the general discontent of the actors as a whole, the Party’s local
branch had a different perspective and would not risk the underground net-
work for such an event. Yu Ling, head of Shanghai’s underground Commit-
tee on Culture, disapproved of holding a news conference for Yuan. Having
learned that invitations had already been issued, he became concerned ﬁrst
with the safety of Guo Moruo and advised Yuan Xuefen to lie low and not
to overstate her case: “You should be careful with your statement. Simply
say that you are just an actress trying to make a living with your own labor,
and you are persecuted because of this. Stop there. Do not make Guo Moruo
speak, as everyone is watching him.”
37 Yuan, as we have seen, developed her
own strategy, but Guo indeed did not speak at the news conference.
While left-wing intellectuals gave Yuan tremendous moral support and
helped her win the battle of public opinion, the unionists took advantage
of the government’s anti-leftist policy to avenge their grievances against
her. Their persecution of Yuan was primarily a means of keeping their
hold on the young actresses, but it was the government’s anti-leftist stance
that gave them a political pretext for their unlawful actions and allowed
them to get away with it. The Shanghai edition of the Nationalist home
newspaper, Central Daily: Shanghai Edition(Zhongyang ribao: Shanghai ban),
Staging in the Public Arena    153

=== PDF PAGE 175 ===

engaged in a personal attack on Yuan as well as a general attack on her Yue
opera reform. An article, “Yuan Xuefen Exaggerates a Small Incident” (Yuan
Xuefen xiaoti dazuo), blamed Yuan for overreacting to these trivial inci-
dents, accused her of staying out of the union merely to be different, and
refuted her progressiveness with the claim that Yue opera “reform” was noth-
ing but a gross deception.
38 The police conducted a halfhearted investi-
gation of the case that yielded no satisfactory result, and the unionists
continued to harass Yuan in the ensuing months. 
If the left wing and Yuan won the battle of public opinion, then the union-
ists, though suffering bad publicity, managed with some success to con-
tinue to squeeze the young actresses. On October 8, 1946, the union
sponsored a fund-raising performance for its welfare foundation. Yin
Guifang, Zhu Shuizhao, Fu Quanxiang, and several other popular actresses
formed the main cast.
39 Yuan Xuefen and the Xuesheng Company of course
did not participate. Probably as a result, in November, three men intruded
into Yuan’s dressing room, demanding that she go with them to perform
at a private party they had arranged. They informed her that if she refused
to go, then they would claim one-third of her monthly pay as compensa-
tion for their “loss.” In December, she received another threat demanding
a cash contribution to the union.
40 By the end of the year, Yuan Xuefen
resolved to retire. Left-wing intellectuals and journalists sent her off with
a chorus of praise in the print media, honoring her achievements in Yue
opera reform as well as her upright personality.
Though the exact reason for Yuan’s retreat is not clear, it is possible to
surmise several of the problems that faced her. Her ongoing conﬂict with
the unionists certainly caused the theater bosses to worry about possible
negative effects on the box ofﬁce. It also seems to have caused friction among
fellow actresses; some thought she was too harsh with the unionists, who
were, after all, fellow Shengxian natives, while others disliked her simply
because she was different. In addition, her reform agenda appeared too rad-
ical to Yue opera management and some of the other actresses. The New
Yue Opera reforms seemed to have come to a crossroads. According to the
recollections of Cheng Rong, who spent days and nights with Yuan dur-
ing this time, Yuan took to heart the comments she received from the main-
stream and left-wing media and carefully collected all the newspaper
clippings on the topic.
41 She became even more reluctant to perform trite
love stories as she was now subject to so much left-wing expectation after
Sister Xianglin. But she was unsure of how to make Yue opera a vehicle of
social reform while continuing to retain its audience.
42 Other reform
154 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 176 ===

actresses, including Yin Guifang, would not go that far. 43 Yin’s attitude
toward Yue opera reform, emphasizing the centrality of love drama in Yue
opera, was probably more popular among Yue opera professionals, includ-
ing most Yue opera stars and some playwrights such as Xu Jin. Yuan Xue-
fen’s elitist inclinations not only caused her conﬂict with the unionists and
theater bosses but also isolated her to some extent from her fellow actresses
in and outside of the Xuesheng Company. In January 1947, Yuan retired
and left the stage. Fu Quanxiang joined the company, which was renamed
the Dongshan Company, in the main female role.
Looking back at the New Yue Opera reforms after more than half a cen-
tury, Yuan still remembered the frustration she felt at the time and her dis-
may at the lack of a clear vision about reform among her fellow actresses,
describing their mental status as hunhun e’e, meaning nonreﬂective or lack-
ing in understanding.
44 Fortunately for Yuan, history would prove that her
insistence on independence of and respect for the actresses and their pro-
fessional work indeed provided the spirit as well as a clear vision for the
New Yue Opera reforms. Her courageous rejection of the status quo, how-
ever unpopular at the time, demonstrated to her fellow actresses that resis-
tance and change were possible.
The Ten Sisters of Yue Opera: Forging a New Collective Identity
During her retirement, Yuan reﬂected on the situation of Yue opera reform
and concluded that the root of the actresses’ vulnerability lay not so much
in the harassment from the union opportunists but in the near monopoly
exercised by the Yue opera management. In an article published in Daily
News, she wrote:
In Yue opera theater, the theater owners invest in the theater, managing
the box ofﬁce and paying the actresses’ salaries. The employers also inter-
vene in the production. They have their eyes on proﬁt only and never care
about social signiﬁcance or the professional standards of the production. For
them, a good play is one that sells well. Often, better plays are forced from
the stage simply because the sales do not exceed 70 percent (since they pre-
fer 100 percent ticket sales) owing to recession or bad weather. The theater
owners especially like to stage traditional plays, because old plays sell as
well as new plays while costing much less to produce. For traditional plays,
actresses need only to put on ready-made, one-style-for-all costumes and
go onstage, with no consideration for the particular historical setting of the
Staging in the Public Arena    155

=== PDF PAGE 177 ===

act; as such, employers need not pay for new costumes, stage properties,
musical accompaniment, lighting, and other production costs, nor need they
pay for the staff members. As the theater bosses aim entirely for proﬁt, it is
impossible for those of us who seek Yue opera reform to work with them.
45
Besides greed and bias against Yue opera reform, theater owners were also
pressed by ﬁnancial difﬁculties resulting from the postwar recession. The-
ater bosses often threatened to break Yue opera contracts in favor of ﬁlm
screenings whenever sales were slow. The pressure from theater owners
was intense, and actresses constantly worried about job security. Without
job security, it was impossible for actresses to achieve artistic autonomy,
and without reform, the status of Yue opera and its actresses could easily
slide back to their previous low levels, and actresses would remain subju-
gated to theater bosses. Contemplating these issues, Yuan Xuefen began
to entertain a bold idea—to build a theater of her own, a dream so ambi-
tious that few actresses had ever imagined it. But why not? If none of them
could create a theater on her own, maybe together they could. 
Yuan’s idea was that leading actresses would stage a grand show to raise
funds for the creation of their own theater and a Yue opera school for young
girls. They could experiment with reforms with the theater, which would
attract a wider audience while further improving the status of Yue opera.
The revenues would be entirely theirs, and they would use the proﬁts to
establish a Yue opera school, which would help secure their employment
after they retired from the stage. When Yuan Xuefen shared her idea with
other actresses, they all became excited and jumped into it immediately. After
all, despite existing frictions, actresses faced similar problems and shared
similar aspirations, and the need for collective action far outweighed their
disagreements. 
First came the announcement that the show was to open on August 18
and end on September 21. On July 29, 1947, ten leading actresses gathered
at a Western-style restaurant on Fuzhou Road and produced a contract
signed by all ten in the presence of a lawyer. The contract was indeed a
manifesto and succinctly summarized the purposes and principles of the
fund-raising production: 
After several meetings, we have found that we all share the same goals of
sustaining the art of New Yue Opera and ensuring the welfare of our fellow
actresses. We hereby decide to act together as cofounders in organizing
a company for the construction of a new-style theater. From now on,
156 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 178 ===

cofounders shall be mutually responsible and do their best to ensure the
smooth operation of the theater and performances, and so to forge a bright
future for Yue opera. The cofounders of the organization have absolutely
equal rights and responsibilities. With this contract signed, cofounders shall
work to raise investment capital, either through personal connections or
through collective fund-raising performances and radio broadcasting, and
to attract shareholders. Each cofounder shall do her best to work toward these
goals and shall not break the agreement or neglect her responsibilities.
46
The ten leading actresses who signed the contract were Yin Guifang, Fu
Quanxiang, Yuan Xuefen, Xiao Dangui, Fan Ruijuan, Zhang Guifeng, Xu
Yulan, Zhu Shuizhao, Wu Xiaolou, and Xu Tianhong—now known as the
“ten sisters” of Yue opera (see ﬁg. 4.2). 
Nan Wei, Han Yi, and Cheng Rong, in the meanwhile, scripted a new
play for the fund-raising show, For the Love of the Country(Shanhe lian), which
transposed the story of Alexandre Dumas’s The Three Musketeersinto an ancient
Chinese setting drawn from the Ming historical novel Histories of the States
in the Eastern Zhou Dynasty (Dongzhou lieguo zhi). The play tells a compli-
cated story of desire, jealousy, conspiracy, and double crosses in a court
struggle involving the king of Liang; his lusty minister; a beautiful princess
and her lover, a prince of the Cao kingdom; an evil royal concubine; two
righteous palace maidservants; and two righteous generals. The story had
no obvious political message but instead focused on an intriguing good-
versus-evil kind of melodrama played out on a grand scale (see ﬁg. 4.3). 
The play’s greatest attraction obviously lay in its all-star cast and grandiose
stage sets. The ten sisters dedicated themselves wholeheartedly to the fund-
raising project and wanted the show to be a great event. They had often
staged fund-raising performances for other organizations, but this was the
ﬁrst time such an event would beneﬁt their own cause. Yin Guifang, for exam-
ple, gave up a rare opportunity to make a Yue opera movie out of one of her
popular plays. It would have been the ﬁrst Yue opera ﬁlm had Yin decided
to participate. Yuan Xuefen voluntarily played the smallest role, in order to
refute her critics’ accusation that she initiated the production to enhance her
own fame. The actresses were electriﬁed and wanted everything to be per-
fect: the theater, the costumes and stage setting, and the media coverage.
The young actresses, however, underestimated the difﬁculties their
project was to face. Yuan Xuefen had consulted Yu Ling, who had expressed
doubts about the project’s feasibility. But Yuan was too excited, and per-
haps too naive, to heed Yu Ling’s warning. For one thing, many sponsors
Staging in the Public Arena    157

=== PDF PAGE 179 ===

were interested in getting their hands on the money the actresses were
expected to make. For many, it was simply unthinkable that these young
girls could or should keep the proﬁts for themselves.
The ﬁrst major expense was to rent a theater. The actresses wanted a
large theater for their event and decided on the Grand Golden Theater,
which was unoccupied for the summer season. But the rental terms the
actresses obtained from Zhou Xinfang, the famous Beijing opera actor who
held the long-term lease on the theater, were harsh: the actresses not only
had to pay a vast sum for rent but also had to furnish one month’s salary

for Zhou’s entire company during the summer recess. The cost for the the-
ater in the end amounted to one-fourth of the box ofﬁce revenue for all
the performances. Zhou certainly was a shrewd businessman and made a
huge proﬁt from the rental while depriving the theater employees.
47
The actresses had another expense, to be expected in the case of such
magniﬁcent fund-raising, and that was to pay various native-place thugs
so that they would not cause trouble.
48 The unionists, understanding that
they were excluded from a share in this grand production, called a press
conference to attack the actresses for having abandoned their teachers
and “affronted their ancestors” (qishi miezu).
49 The Nationalist soldiers also
wanted their share, and since they had not received free tickets, they rushed
into the theater one day, expelling the audience and taking their seats. The
actresses had no choice but to accommodate them.

4.3  The ten sisters of Yue opera staged For the Love of the Country (Shanhe lian), 1947, to
raise funds for building their own theater. Although the actresses did not achieve their
goal, the performances deeply impressed audiences and the general public and did
much to raise Yue opera’s social status. (Shanghai Yueju zhi 1997)

=== PDF PAGE 181 ===

The government and the CCP were both aware of the large sums of
money the show was drawing into the theater. Yu Ling assigned two men
to help Yuan Xuefen and the actresses with accounting and logistics, while
the actresses, probably in an effort to avoid being squeezed by the govern-
ment, also invited Wang Canzhi, daughter of the revolutionary martyr Qiu
Jin and an ofﬁcial at the tax bureau of the municipal government, to head
the proposed Yue opera training school. But this did not appease the gov-
ernment. Ofﬁcials were skeptical that the young actresses—mostly illiter-
ate, apolitical, knowing nothing but their trivial opera plays—could have
come up with such an ambitious fund-raising effort solely by themselves,
and they suspected that the revenue would ﬂow into the hands of the CCP
underground. Halfway through the show’s run, the Social Affairs Bureau
suddenly issued an interdiction, charging that the actresses had not prop-
erly completed registration procedures, and appointed a special board of
trustees to oversee the funds raised by the production. The trustees included
Wang Canzhi, Wang Xiaolai, and a few other government ofﬁcials. The ban
against the show was lifted only when the actresses agreed to place the funds
under the trustees’ control for three months.
50 Up until then, the show had
stimulated public enthusiasm, and tickets were selling well. The government’s
disruption had a negative effect on the box ofﬁce, and the second phase of
the production did not go so well. Other elements may also have been at
work. Not least was competition from the China Great Theater (Zhong-
guo Daxiyuan), where Beijing opera superstars—including Mei Lanfang,
Meng Xiaodong, Zhang Junqiu, Ma Lianliang, and Zhou  Xinfang—
performed from September 3 to 13 in celebration of Du Yuesheng’s sixti-
eth birthday; these dates coincided with the second phase of the ten sisters’
production. Most Yue opera stars also performed at Du’s private parties to
show their respect for him. Yuan Xuefen, as usual, did not go. 
When the performances concluded, the revenue was less than had been
expected. After settling bills for costumes, salaries, and the theater rental,
there wasn’t much left. The trustees then froze the funds for a few months,
while inﬂation mounted. When the trustees ﬁnally released the fund, after
entertaining themselves with a banquet, the account was worth only
ninety-six taels of gold. In the end, it was only enough for the actresses to
rent a small two-story house with three rooms. They ﬁxed up a small stage
downstairs and put up a plaque that read “Shanghai Yue Opera Experimental
School.” The government never issued a permit for this school.
The fund-raising project, though it did not succeed as intended, was a
big success in that it projected a positive, upbeat image of the Yue opera
160 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 182 ===

actress to the public. On August 11 and 12, a week before the opening
night, the ten sisters launched a promotional radio campaign. Each went
to the station to sing pieces from the new play and to answer calls from
the audience. The two-day promotion climaxed with the ten actresses
singing together in a chorus. Mainstream newspapers as well as tabloids
covered the fund-raising show with eye-catching headlines, and the phrase
“ten sisters of Yue opera” permeated Shanghai’s mass media, marking the
beginning of a new collective identity for the actresses. 
The tabloid Robin Hood (Luobinhan), for example, ﬁrst reported the ten
actresses’ initiative on August 5 in an article headlined “Ten Leading Yue
Opera Actresses Successfully Cooperated.” The report notes: 
This organization [Yuelian Company, founded by the ten sisters] is an inno-
vation in any opera circle, for it is rare and precious that ﬁrst-rate actresses
might join hands like this.
51
A few days later, another article in the tabloid praised Yue opera actresses
and criticized Beijing opera:
The Yue opera circle unites tightly under the leadership of Master [Xian
Sheng] Yuan Xuefen (as everybody addresses her). The actresses are work-
ing hard to collect funds for a Yue opera school and a theater of their own.
In contrast, Beijing opera, though esteemed as the National opera and with
so many famous actors, does not compare. It is such a pity that famous Bei-
jing opera actors all take care only of their own business; they cannot rival
these young ladies full of progressive ideas and actions.
52
On August 28, the tabloid carried a report mocking the unionists. Accord-
ing to the news, the Yue Opera Workers’ Union convened a press confer-
ence on Teachers’ Day to air its complaints that Yue opera had fallen under
the spell of women. “Today is Teachers’ Day, and everyone should pro-
mote respect for teachers,” the union spokesman was quoted as saying. “But
the younger generation of Yue opera performers pays their teachers no
respect—they enjoy drinking the clear water but forget its source. We there-
fore ask you reporters to uphold justice for us.” The article continues:
The original purpose of this press conference was to ask the ten leading
actresses, including Yuan Xuefen and Fu Quanxiang, to each hire one male
Yue opera teacher for their companies, so as to help the teachers make a liv-
Staging in the Public Arena    161

=== PDF PAGE 183 ===

ing. One of the union representatives’ comments caused the assembled jour-
nalists much consternation. He said, “We are not as rich as Yuan Xuefen,
and today’s reception has not been easy for us to prepare. We have spent
ten days collecting to make possible even these simple snacks.” He even
addressed the reporters as “comrades.” Having heard him, the reporters could
only smile at one another. Feeling sorry for the unionists, the reporters
refrained from consuming the snacks.
53
Mainstream newspapers reported this event differently. The New Citizen
Evening News published interviews with each of the ten actresses. Yuan Xue-
fen was quoted as saying, “To accomplish anything requires cooperation
and collective action. One person can do nothing. We all work for the future
of New Yue Opera, not for personal fame.”
54 Yin Guifang remarked, “I have
been thinking about training more young actresses in this transitional period,
when an older generation has already passed and the new is yet to come.
But I cannot make it happen alone. So when Xuefen talked with me about
the Yue opera school, I was really excited. This undertaking will fail,
though, if it must depend only on us ten sisters. We need lots of friends to
care for the school and to help. Support us.”
55 An enthusiastic Tian Han
published an article in The New Citizen Evening Newstitled “United, You Have
Power!” in which he pointed out that it was already a great achievement for
the actresses to have brought off such a production.
56
Thus, in the process of this grand fund-raising, a new collective iden-
tity emerged among the leading Yue opera actresses, one of progressive,
modern, and autonomous art workers. Correspondingly, this self-conscious
identity was successfully projected in the news media in the image of the
young, upbeat, and reform-minded actresses of Yue opera. It was also dur-
ing this process that Yue opera actresses began to appear as subject-agents
in the news media, making the news themselves and deﬁning their own
positions, rather than being reported and commented upon as before. In
this context, Yue opera actresses took on a central role in the Xiao Dangui
incident and staged a self-directed social drama in the public arena. 
The Xiao Dangui Incident: Competing Narratives
On October 13, 1947, less than one month after the end of the fund-rais-
ing performances, one of the ten sisters, Xiao Dangui, committed suicide
at her home. Xiao Dangui was from a poor peasant family in Shengxian.
162 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 184 ===

She was given away as a child bride at the age of eight and joined a girls’
opera school, the Gaosheng Theater, at ten. Hardworking and talented, Xiao
distinguished herself during her school years and took up a leading role imme-
diately after her apprenticeship. During the 1930s, she performed with the
Gaosheng Theater all over Zhejiang and made a good impression on Shang-
hai audiences as well. In early 1938, Xiao caused a sensation when she per-
formed with the Gaosheng Theater in “island” Shanghai (see ﬁg. 4.4). 
Qiu Guangxian, owner of the Gaosheng Theater, was also a Shengxian
native and the highly disciplined patriarch of the school. Qiu’s admoni-
tion to the students may be summarized in the two sentences he often
uttered: “Carry yourself pure and clean; perform honestly and earnestly.”
He not only hired the best teachers so that the students would receive good
training in the operatic arts but also safeguarded the girls’ moral and sex-
ual purity. He set up strict rules forbidding socialization between men and
women within the school and even hired a female manager to supervise
the girls’ everyday lives. When the school toured urban centers such as
Hangzhou and Shanghai, Qiu always kept his eye on the students, not allow-
ing them to socialize with local audiences lest they be seduced by money,
sex, and the corrupt urban life, especially that of colonial Shanghai. In 1939,
after a successful eight-month run in Shanghai and in the midst of a boom
Staging in the Public Arena    163
4.4  Stage photo of Xiao Dangui in Qing-style
dress. (Zhongguo Yueju 1989)

=== PDF PAGE 185 ===

in women’s Shengxian opera, Qiu decided to leave Shanghai. A strict dis-
ciplinarian, he could no longer prevent his actresses from becoming pop-
ular stars—the objects of public desire and the subjects of their own fates.
Qiu applied a harsh punishment called “red all around,” beating all the girls
if one girl did something wrong. Even this could not stop the actresses from
sneaking out of the dorm or receiving gifts behind his back. Losing con-
trol and fearing the consequences of the girls’ behavior, Qiu took the troupe
back to eastern Zhejiang to perform in Ningbo, Shaoxing, and Shengxian.
57
In 1940, Qiu retired and disbanded the Gaosheng Theater. At this point,
Zhang Chunfan came and recruited Xiao and several veteran Gaosheng
Theater actresses. Xiao returned to Shanghai and quickly became one of
the most popular Yue opera stars in the city. When Xiao and her fellow
actresses ﬁrst came back to Shanghai, they all lived in the backstage area
of the Zhedong Theater, where they performed. But before long, Xiao
moved upstairs where Zhang Chunfan and his wife and children lived. Xiao
and Zhang soon became sexually involved and lived together for about six
years before Xiao’s suicide.
58
Zhang Chunfan, the defendant in the case, was also a Shengxian native.
He came to Shanghai in 1930 to work in a silk factory and later became
the manager of two small silk mills. In early 1940, seeing the boom in
women’s Shengxian opera in Shanghai, Zhang decided to seek his fortune
in the opera business. He organized a troupe with Xiao Dangui as the lead-
ing actress and made a fortune in the next several years. Zhang was a capa-
ble man in his own way. By all accounts, he was greedy, controlling, and
bullying, on the one hand, and generous, chivalrous, and useful to his friends,
on the other. Zhang was a tough self-made local strongman with little edu-
cation. He gathered a gang of followers who would not hesitate to use vio-
lence against his enemies and also organized and headed the Yue Opera
Employers’ Union. With his tough style and connections within the trade
and through the Shengxian native-place organization, Zhang made him-
self one of the most powerful men in Yue opera circles.
59
At ﬁrst, Xiao’s death seemed like just another sensational suicide of a
popular female star. On the next day, October 14, the Leyuan Funeral
House, which had held Ruan Lingyu’s funeral twelve years before and
where Xiao Dangui’s body now lay, became the center of attention. While
newspaper headlines were preoccupied with Xiao’s suicide, tens of thou-
sands of spectators crowded the Leyuan Funeral House. Press reports were
eager to point out similarities between the two tragic deaths of famous
actresses twelve years apart. Dagong Daily reported on October 15: “All
164 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 186 ===

the Yue opera stars in Shanghai went to mourn [Xiao]. The situation after
Xiao’s death is comparable to that after the death of the movie star Ruan
Lingyu years ago. Both incidents created a public stir. For this reason, the
police take the incident seriously and are investigating the cause of Xiao
Dangui’s suicide.”
60 Mainstream newspapers, however, seemed unwilling
to get into reporting on sex scandals, the so-called peach-colored news,
as the despised tabloid press had done with the Ruan Lingyu incident.
Trying to offer some explanation for Xiao’s suicide, the Dagong Dailyreport
came up with three points, most likely inﬂuenced by family members liv-
ing with Xiao and Zhang. First, Xiao’s business was losing money, and she
was indebted for up to 400 million yuan, an especially heavy debt because
she had quit performing after the departure of her stage partner Xu Yulan.
Second, Xiao was frustrated because she felt that public reaction to her
performance during the recent fund-raising show was not as favorable as
for other actresses. And third, the report vaguely implied that she had
some family trouble.
But events soon took a different turn on October 16, the day of Xiao’s
funeral, when the remaining nine Yue opera sisters charged that Zhang’s
abuse of Xiao was the cause of the actress’s demise. Using their power as
public icons, the actresses staged a social drama in which they were both
directors and protagonists, setting up Zhang Chunfan as the defendant in
a public trial. On that day, Yue opera theaters throughout the city can-
celed scheduled performances in solidarity in order to pay respects to the
deceased Xiao. Many Yue opera actresses attended the funeral, where they
became the main attraction for fans and the general public. About twenty
thousand Yue opera fans and Shanghai residents once more crowded the
funeral house; a few hundred of them broke into the funeral parlor, dam-
aging the furniture, and a portrait of the deceased Xiao went missing.
61
Focusing on the dramatis personae, represented as “the nine sisters of
the deceased,” media coverage quickly responded to the actresses and their
charges against Zhang. Dagong Daily reported:
Zhang Chunfan has now become the target of the people. The nine sisters
met yesterday [October 16]. They request that no one cooperate with
Zhang and that Zhang be thrown out of the Yue opera business. The Guo-
tai Theater was bought with Xiao Dangui’s personal savings, and therefore
Zhang should not be allowed to manage its business. They [the nine sisters]
should take charge of the business of the theater. The proﬁts from the the-
ater will be used to take care of Xiao Dangui’s grave and annual memorial
Staging in the Public Arena    165

=== PDF PAGE 187 ===

service, and the rest should go to her family [meaning her natal family in
Shengxian] for their daily use.62
On October 18, Shanghai Newspaper’s headline read: “Actress Xiao Leaves
2.1 Billion in Property, the Yue Opera Circle Makes Two Points—Suicide
Had Nothing to Do with Financial Difﬁculties, Xiao Had No Dubious Sex-
ual Relationship.”
63 The article continued: “they [the nine sisters] will con-
tinue to press Zhang Chunfan and, at the same time, call for the support
of the public in upholding justice in order to ﬁnd out the real cause of Xiao’s
suicide.”
64 As the actresses requested, Zhang handed an inventory of Xiao’s
belongings and property to the mediator, Huang Jinrong, the godfather
of Shanghai’s notorious Green Gang whose daughter-in-law was Xiao’s
adoptive mother. In the meantime, the nine sisters were reportedly plan-
ning a huge memorial service for Xiao, and a Yue opera play relating the
story of her suicide was soon to open; according to the article, it was
expected to cause another public sensation.
65 In fact, more than one Yue
opera company staged the story. Xiao’s suicide soon became a popular sub-
ject not only for Yue opera but also for other entertainment forms such as
Suzhou story-singing and Shanghai opera.
On October 20, Dagong Daily published an interview with Leng Shan,
Xiao’s director, whom Zhang accused of having had a dubious relation-
ship with Xiao. In this interview, Leng appeared as an honest intellectual
who, accepting the suggestions of his friends in spoken drama, had entered
Xiao’s company to help reform Yue opera. Xiao was portrayed as morally
scrupulous and an eager student of modern arts, unlike most other actresses
in the company. The relationship between Leng and Xiao was purely one
between a leading actress and her director, or between a student and a
teacher. Watching movies and discussing performing skills were, accord-
ing to Leng, part of Xiao’s preparation for pursuing her goal of acting on-
screen. In the evening of October 7, Leng recounted, he and Xiao set out
to see a Hollywood movie. It was a little early, so they went for a walk.
They became involved in a discussion about acting skill and never made
it to the movie. The next morning, Leng told the reporter, he was sum-
moned to Zhang’s house, where Zhang demanded that he tell the truth
about the previous night. Leng told the reporter: “I want to make a clear
statement about my pure friendship with Xiao. But my sincere oath did
not win understanding from a jealous Zhang, and in the end, a weak woman
had to resort to suicide. I feel sorry and deep sorrow for her.” Other news-
166 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 188 ===

papers also focused on Zhang’s unfounded jealousy and his subsequent mal-
treatment of Xiao, which, according to popular belief, ultimately caused
the actress’s suicide. It seems that by this time Zhang was already presumed
guilty in the public eye.
66
As the social drama and the theatrical performance of Xiao’s tragedy
unfolded, the police were obliged to take action and arrested Zhang as “the
main suspect in the case of Xiao’s suicide.”
67 On October 31, newspapers
published excerpts from Zhang’s lengthy statement of self-defense, writ-
ten in police custody. Zhang described a nearly consummated affair
between Xiao and Leng, portraying Leng Shan as immoral and Xiao as a
confused woman who would have been seduced by Leng if not for Zhang’s
advice. But his self-defense had little impact on the police detectives. That
same day, after more than ten days of investigation, the police charged
Zhang as an accessory to Xiao’s suicide and took him in handcuffs to the
jail at the District Prosecution Ofﬁce. The police spokesman made a state-
ment at a news conference: “After Xiao Dangui committed suicide, the police
took Zhang Chunfan into custody and investigated him in order to
strengthen the law, reassure the public, and make the truth known.” The
main evidence of Zhang’s offense, the ofﬁcer said, was his refusal to accept
Xiao’s repeated avowal that her body was pure and clean and his subse-
quent maltreatment of Xiao. Zhang may have even told Xiao to kill her-
self, the police ofﬁcer reasoned.
68 Then, on November 5, the trial opened,
and the public prosecutor charged Zhang with abetting Xiao’s suicide.
Since Zhang Chunfan had long been presumed guilty in the public eye,
the court trial was at ﬁrst presented in the daily newspapers much as the
preceding newspaper trial had been—the same story told, and with the
same witnesses. “Yuan Xuefen,” a Shanghai Newspaperreport on the trial read,
“is an outsider. It is only because Xiao’s death was so pitiable that she, as
Xiao’s sworn sister, appeals to the court to redress the injustice done to
Xiao.”
69
The story was accompanied with a shot of a frustrated Zhang Chun-
fan, in long gown, walking into the court under police escort, and another
showing Yuan Xuefen, wearing a plain cheongsam and smiling, posing for
the reporter in front of the courtroom, with Leng Shan, wearing glasses
and a Western-style suit, in the background (see ﬁg. 4.5). The contrast is
interesting. Leng Shan’s appearance as a young Western-style intellectual
is juxtaposed to the older Zhang Chunfan’s traditional outﬁt and style, with
Yuan Xuefen smiling conﬁdently in the foreground.
Staging in the Public Arena    167

=== PDF PAGE 189 ===

On December 27, the district court acquitted Zhang, for charges against
him did not constitute positive evidence, and released him soon after the
verdict.70 Zhang emerged from the jail after two and half months, to ﬁnd
himself disgraced in the public eye and deprived of a chance to beneﬁt
from the wealth that Xiao had left behind her. Xiao Dangui’s older brother,
from her hometown of Changle Township in Shengxian, was recognized
as her legal heir and inherited Xiao’s property and personal belongings.
71
Throughout the incident, the nine actresses, cast as the nine sisters,
assumed a central role in the public arena, affected the progress of events,
and re-created themselves as powerful public ﬁgures in the mainstream
media. Although their initial demand that they be permitted to take over
168 Staging in the Public Arena
4.5  A Shanghai Newspaper (Shen bao) article on the Xiao Dangui incident, 
November 5, 1947 . Left: Yuan Xuefen in the foreground with Leng Shan to her right.
Right: Zhang Chunfan under police escort.

=== PDF PAGE 190 ===

the Guotai Theater, so as to realize the dream of having a theater of their
own, soon proved without legal basis, they succeeded in compromising the
prestige of theater owners, casting them in the image of a defeated Zhang
Chunfan. How did this happen? How could these young actresses dare to
pursue a local strongman like Zhang Chunfan? And what was their moti-
vation? In a 1995 interview, Fu Quanxiang responded: “We were outraged.
We just wanted to avenge the deceased Xiao Dangui [da baobuping]. I don’t
know where I got the guts. Zhang Chunfan’s men threatened us, saying,
‘Do you want to keep your hands? Do you want to keep your legs? Or do
you care about your pretty face?’ It was scary. We were really bold.”
72 Yuan
Xuefen, who assumed the leadership in this ﬁght, and Xu Yulan, who had
been Xiao’s stage partner not long before Xiao’s death, provided similar
answers.
73
The actresses suggested that they had no other motivation than to avenge
the deceased Xiao Dangui. They had good reason to exact vengeance from
Zhang Chunfan. One of the most powerful theater bosses in the trade,
Zhang was monopolistic and ruthless and had no respect for the actresses.
The actresses were also angry about the way Zhang had treated Xiao. As
they saw it, Zhang, a married man, took control of Xiao’s sexuality and
used her artistic talent to advance his dominance in the Yue opera busi-
ness. Moreover, the actresses believed that Zhang, while beneﬁting from
Xiao’s talent, abused her privately at home. It was quite obvious to the
actresses that Xiao would not have resorted to suicide if Zhang had not
humiliated her and treated her unjustly.
Many actresses had the impression that Xiao was an unhappy woman
whose weak personality made her easy prey for Zhang.
74 They reasoned
that Xiao had committed suicide out of desperation for several reasons: her
popularity was in a decline, due to Zhang’s hostility to Yue opera reform
and his insistence that she play erotic characters; as her career drew to an
end, she would become ﬁnancially less useful to Zhang and thus subject
to more abuse; and Zhang had already showed signs of disliking her. While
it is not certain that Xiao had feared for her future because of tepid ticket
sales for her shows, the actresses’ reasoning was grounded in the reality of
wife/concubine abuse permitted by the polygamous patriarchal system. To
the actresses, Zhang appeared to be just such a local strongman who had
no hesitation about abusing his power. Xiao’s suicide once again conﬁrmed
him as a bully.
The actresses’ enormous sympathy for Xiao and their anger with her
abuser derived from their identiﬁcation with certain tragic elements in
Staging in the Public Arena    169

=== PDF PAGE 191 ===

Xiao’s case. The abuse Xiao suffered was, for the actresses, part and par-
cel of the more general abuse of women in the Yue opera trade and in
society at large. Ma Zhanghua’s distress at the hands of another theater
boss, Lu Gendi, was still a fresh memory. Fu Quanxiang herself had endured
misery in her ﬁrst marriage as the second wife of the owner of the Shen-
zhou Inn, and she had recently initiated her own divorce. Yuan Xuefen
was a self-proclaimed celibate. Xiao-Bai Yumei, Shi Yinghua, and Yao Shui-
juan all had unhappy marriages. 
In a broader sense, Xiao Dangui’s tragic death became an outlet for
women to speak their feelings through public agitation. When the nine
sisters launched the public campaign charging Zhang Chunfan as Xiao Dan-
gui’s true murderer, they spoke not only for themselves, and for the
deceased, but also to the sensibilities of many women. Yue opera fans, con-
cubines as well as principal wives, knew their grievances, and some were
able to identify Xiao’s tragedy with their own suffering. During the uproar
after Xiao’s death, Dagong Dailyreported an episode in which a Yue opera
fan, Zhang Jinfeng, tried to kill herself. Zhang Jinfeng was obsessed with
Xiao’s case. She went to the Leyuan Funeral House to attend Xiao’s funeral
and followed the news passionately every day, so much so that she neg-
lected her housekeeping chores. Her mother-in-law scolded her, saying,
“If you are so concerned, why not go with Xiao yourself?” On the morn-
ing of October 26, Zhang Jinfeng drank a large quantity of gasoline in an
attempt to kill herself. Her husband took her to the hospital, and her life
was saved.
75
The actresses won public support not only because they articulated
women’s sensibilities, elicited by Xiao’s tragic death, but also because they
succeeded in claiming the virtue of qingbai, or purity and cleanliness, through
Yue opera reform. They had cleansed the opera of sexual obscenity and
earnestly produced decent, sometimes socially meaningful, love dramas,
and they had created a public image of themselves as respectable, modern
art workers through the staging of Sister Xianglin and recent fund-raising
events. If there was any doubt about Xiao’s qingbai, Xiao had puriﬁed her-
self by giving up her life. The irony was, however, that the actresses were
able to prevail as modern public ﬁgures only because they were ﬁrmly
grounded in a traditional rendering of women’s virtue. Zhang Chunfan, in
fact, invoked the concept of qingbaito criticize Xiao’s wrongdoing and jus-
tify the pressure he placed upon her. In his statement to the police, he
stressed that he pressed Xiao because he was concerned about her qingbai,
which was in jeopardy because of Leng Shan’s seduction. Zhang’s Sheng-
170 Staging in the Public Arena

=== PDF PAGE 192 ===

xian supporters had even fabricated a sensational story of the alleged seduc-
tion.76 Their story, like that of the unionists, was hindered by its outdated
language and style to the extent that it sounds laughable to the modern
reader. But in the end, only because the actresses had acquired the virtue
of qingbai could they summon up enormous social sympathy, try the
alleged abuser in the public forum, and turn a sexual scandal into a social
drama of justice for women.
The Republican Actress T ransformed in the Public Sphere
In May 1935, upon the death of Ruan Lingyu, Lu Xun pointed out with
great pain the silence of the actress in the middle of the public uproar:
“Gossip is frightening” is a sentence that we read in movie star Ruan 
Ling yu’s suicide note, found after her death. After a lot of empty chatter,
generated by the heartrending incident of her tragic death, public atten-
tion has gradually cooled. As soon as the drama Fragrant Lingyu Vanishes with
the Wind (Lingyu xiangxiao ji) stops playing, the whole incident will fade
away, leaving no trace, just like Ai Xia’s suicide a year ago. Their deaths
were a grain of salt dropped into the endless sea of gossip, adding some
ﬂavor. But soon, the gossip trade will be the same as before—tasteless and
meaningless.
77
Contending that the media’s sensational descriptions of Ruan Lingyu’s case
were just another form of dalliance with women, Lu Xun continued:
She [Ruan Lingyu] was slandered [by the media] with colorful inventions
but had no way to clear herself. Couldn’t she ﬁght, some say? But since she
did not have a home newspaper [ jiguan bao], how could she ﬁght? She had
grievances, and injustice had been done to her. But since she did not have
a clear enemy, whom should she ﬁght? If we can put ourselves in her shoes,
then we may understand the truth in her statement “gossip is frightening,”
as well as the truth in people’s conviction that her suicide had to do with
the media.
78
Ruan Lingyu’s words ﬁnally reached the public arena only after her lam-
entable suicide. A voice as weak as this, crying that “gossip is frightening,”
needed suicide to pierce indifference. Many died voiceless, only to excite
even more sound and fury in the gossip media. Actresses served as the
Staging in the Public Arena    171

=== PDF PAGE 193 ===

objects of a public culture of erotica—to be imagined, described, discussed,
interpreted, and spoken for. If they were unfortunately entangled in sex-
ual scandal, a favorite subject of the gossip media, the cost of their redemp-
tion was sometimes nothing less than death: only thus could they
communicate protest and recover their innocence in the public eye, as we
have seen repeatedly in the cases of Ai Xia, Ruan Lingyu, Ma Zhanghua,
and Xiao Dangui. 
Remarkably, the death of Xiao Dangui in October 1947 invoked a social
drama that was very different from anything the public had seen. In 1935,
male intellectuals seized on Ruan Lingyu’s death to lecture the public and
the gossip media on women’s liberation and theatrical reform, but twelve
years later, Yue opera actresses took center stage in the media to speak for
themselves and seek justice for Xiao Dangui. This change clearly signaled
the ascent of Yue opera actresses in the public arena, from silent objects
of sexual scandals or reformist discourses to articulate subject-agents with
full social status.
