=== PDF PAGE 194 ===

173
Chapter 5
The Opera as History
On October 1, 1949, Yuan Xuefen climbed to the balcony of the Gate of
Heavenly Peace (Tiananmen), along with a few hundred representatives
to the First Chinese People’s Political Consultative Conference, to witness
Chairman Mao Zedong declare the founding of the People’s Republic of
China. Yuan was one of four actors selected to represent the local-opera
profession nationwide; the other three were the famous Beijing opera mas-
ters Mei Lanfang, Cheng Yanqiu, and Zhou Xinfang (see ﬁg. 5.1). Yu Ling,
the former chair of Shanghai’s CCP underground Committee on Culture
and now head of the Division of Arts in Shanghai’s new government, had
sent Yuan, along with Zhou Xinfang, to Beijing as a special representative
of local operas. From then on, Yuan’s personal fortunes were tied to her
relationship with the new government. 
At ﬁrst, Yuan recalled, she did not want to attend the meeting. She told
Yu Ling that she was not interested in politics, because politics was dirty,
and she wanted only to be a good actor. Unsettled by her sudden status as
an equal of such important cultural ﬁgures as Mei Lanfang and Zhou Xin-
fang, Yuan told Yu Ling that she did not deserve the honor. Yu Ling sim-
ply asked, as Yuan recalled: “You say you don’t like politics. Do you know
what politics is?” Yu Ling continued: “Politics is ﬁrst of all to make clear
what is right and what is wrong.” Once in Beijing and after being received
by Premier Zhou Enlai, Yuan changed her mind. Zhou told her that he had
secretly attended one of her performances in 1946 and had known of her
for some time. She found the premier to be extremely nice and friendly,
quite the opposite of what she had imagined high ofﬁcials to be. She was
equally surprised to discover that her old friends Yu Ling, Tian Han, and


=== PDF PAGE 195 ===

Liu Housheng had been members of the CCP underground and were now
ofﬁcials of the new government. 1
Yuan joined the Communist Party in 1954. In 1955, she was appointed
head of the newly established ofﬁcial Shanghai Yue Opera House. A decade
later, she was persecuted during the Cultural Revolution, like many other
ofﬁcials, but her standing was restored immediately afterward, and she
remained in the position until she retired in 1985. She was also a long-term
representative to the National People’s Congress and a member of its ﬁfth,
sixth, and seventh Standing Committees. In the meantime, she starred in
a few New Yue Opera classics and two of the most inﬂuential Yue opera
ﬁlms, Butterﬂy Lovers(1953) and Sister Xianglin(1978).
2 Yuan and her colleagues
at the Shanghai Yue Opera House performed internationally and enter-
tained numerous foreign diplomats as representatives of China’s traditional
arts. Her political and artistic stature, combined with Yue opera’s popular
success, made Yue opera one of the most important in the nation.
Beginning in the late 1980s, however, Yue opera was caught in the trend
of a general decline in native operas, suffered a sharp downturn in its pop-
ularity, and quickly lost its place in mainstream popular entertainment. In
174 The Opera as History
5.1  Yuan Xuefen with three Beijing opera actors, representatives of the traditional
opera circle at the First National Political Consultative Council, 1949. From left: 
Cheng Yanqiu, Yuan Xuefen, Mei Lanfang, and Zhou Xinfang. (Yuan 2002)

=== PDF PAGE 196 ===

the ensuing two decades, Yue opera performances were few and the audi-
ence was small. The few remaining Yue opera troupes relied entirely or par-
tially on government subsidies to pay for salaries and production expenses.
By the end of the twentieth century, it became clear that Yue opera’s glory
as a shining popular-culture phenomenon had passed. Signiﬁcantly, the rise
of Yue opera actresses from lowly female entertainers/prostitutes to
respected professional art workers, popular media icons, and even mem-
bers of the cultural elite proved to be irreversible, despite the genre’s decline. 
How did the communist revolution and the PRC state affect Yue opera
and its actresses? The vicissitudes of the opera and its actresses in the lat-
ter half of the twentieth century unfolded under the heavy-handed inter-
vention of a socialist state during the early PRC period through the
Cultural Revolution and within the context of waves of modern economic
development and urban migration in the post-Mao era of “reform and open-
ing [to the world]” (gaige kaifang) in the 1980s and 1990s. While the story
of Yue opera and its actresses was still evolving, interpretations of this very
story were put forward, seeking not only to deﬁne Yue opera’s history but
also to inﬂuence its future development. This chapter explores the dynamic
relationship between the opera and its actresses, on the one hand, and the
communist revolution and the PRC state, on the other, as well as the nar-
ratives about this relationship produced during the half-century after 1949.
The discussion develops through a reading of two productions about the
history of Yue opera: the 1965 ﬁlm Stage Sisters (Wutai jiemei), directed by
Xie Jin, representing a communist interpretation of Yue opera’s history, and
the Yue opera play by the same title, produced in 1998 by the Shanghai
Yue Opera House, revealing an understanding of Yue opera’s history and
self-identity as deﬁned by Yue opera people themselves.
Narrating the Yue Opera Story in Film 
The ﬁlm Stage Sisters tells the story of two Yue opera actresses from the
Shengxian countryside who take different life paths after entering the dan-
gerous metropolis of 1940s Shanghai. Zhu Chunhua chooses to abide by
her teacher’s admonition, “carry yourself pure and clean; perform earnestly
and honestly,” and she eventually joins the CCP-led “progressive forces”
( jinbu liliang), a CCP term describing social forces sympathetic to the left-
ist agenda and critical of the Nationalist government in Republican Shang-
hai during the Civil War. By contrast, Xing Yuehong, longing for wealth
and the status of a bourgeois housewife, marries a theater boss in order to
The Opera as History    175

=== PDF PAGE 197 ===

secure her desired lifestyle. She ultimately falls victim to the evil theater
boss, who soon tires of her and then sacriﬁces her, under order of Nation-
alist ofﬁcials, in an effort to obstruct Chunhua’s progressive activities. Finally,
after Liberation, in 1949, the theater boss ﬂees Shanghai for Taiwan, while
Chunhua invites Yuehong to join the revolution and come back to the stage. 
Conceived in 1957 and produced in 1964, Stage Sisters was a major suc-
cess for the forty-one-year-old Xie Jin, the PRC’s master ﬁlm director of
communist melodrama.
3 Like his The Number Five of Women’s Basketball
(Nülan Wuhao), of 1957, and Red Detachment of Women(Hongse niangzi jun),
of 1960, Stage Sisters explores the fate of women before and after Libera-
tion. Born and raised in Shangyu, a county adjacent to Shengxian, Xie Jin
was familiar with the lives of Yue opera actresses of the past, and he was
struck by the contrasts in the status and lives of these actresses before and
after Liberation.
4 His two co-screenwriters, Xu Jin and Lin Gu, were also
from Zhejiang, and Xu Jin was, as discussed in previous chapters, a mas-
ter playwright of the Yue opera love drama. In Stage Sisters , as in Xie Jin’s
other two ﬁlms, women’s lives serve as vehicles for communist melodrama.
Such stories typically start with oppressed, exploited, and victimized
women before Liberation and end with “liberated” and happy women, who
are grateful to the Communist Party and fully devoted to the revolution.
The ﬁlm Stage Sisters weaves the story of Yue opera and its actresses into
this master narrative of the communist revolution, which in turn sets the
framework for interpreting Yue opera’s history. It should not be surpris-
ing that the ﬁlm sought to construct and present the history of Yue opera
as part of the melodrama of the communist revolution, for the new state
emphasized the fundamental importance of ideology and propaganda, of
which entertainment was an important aspect. Xie Jin clariﬁed the main
theme of the ﬁlm:
We heard many life stories detailing entertainers’ struggles against the old
ruling class—these were contradictions in life. The dramatic contradiction
in Stage Sisters is manifested in the ideological conﬂict between the two sis-
t e r s... ,  which to a certain degree reﬂects intense and complex class con-
tradictions of the time ....  T his process of intensifying contradiction, in
fact, tells us that people live in a complex class-stratiﬁed society and always
have to choose between two paths—if they do not choose the revolution-
ary path, they will be used by the enemies, and their conduct will make the
enemies happy and their friends grieved....
Local-opera singers in the old society occupied a low position and were
176 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 198 ===

called “tenth-grade humans.” Thus, there is a certain amount of tragic ele-
ments in certain scenes; . . . the mood becomes exalted only after the
appearance of the Party’s leadership in the third chapter.
5
The ﬁlm opens with off-screen  choral singing, alerting the audience to
the ﬁlm’s main theme:
The green water winds its way around the mountains, 
And the sound of opera singing ﬁlls the theatres.
Tears and laughter are commonplace on the stage; 
Who would know there is real-life drama offstage?
6
The scene is set in 1935 and focuses on a traditional stage built on a hill-
side in the eastern Zhejiang countryside , where a women’s Shengxian
opera performance is in progress. In front of the stage, various venders
and gambling groups mingle with audience members, forming a typical
performance scene. Suddenly, the audience is disturbed by a teenage girl
running away from her pursuers, a man and a woman with rope in their
hands. The girl is none other than Zhu Chunhua, escaping the fate of
being a child bride, for she is scheduled to be sexually united with her “child
husband” ( yuan fang) that night. Teacher Xing of the troupe, Yuehong’s
father, pities the girl and takes her in as a student. Before long, the hard-
working Chunhua proves herself to be a good actress, and she and Yue-
hong become stage partners and sworn sisters. A local wealthy landlord
desires the two sisters, but he is soon put off by Chunhua’s strong person -
ality and her overt disgust at his sexual advances. Setting his eyes on Yue-
hong, the landlord conspires with the corrupt police force to storm the
troupe’s performance one day, under the pretext of banning the morally
corrupt women’s opera, and tries to kidnap Yuehong amid the chaos. Chun-
hua ﬁghts off the police and rescues Yuehong but is arrested in the end.
Yuehong’s father uses up his savings to secure Chunhua’s release and, hav-
ing been seriously wounded during the ﬁghting, dies soon thereafter. His
last words to the sisters are “Carry yourself pure and clean; perform
earnestly and honestly.” The owner of the troupe shrewdly sells the sis-
ters into a three-year obligation to a theater owner in Shanghai. For this,
he receives a large sum of money and tells the sisters he has used it all to
pay for the burial of Yuehong’s father. 
In Shanghai, the two sisters soon emerge as rising Yue opera stars, but
they then follow separate paths. Manager Tang, the theater boss, heart-
The Opera as History    177

=== PDF PAGE 199 ===

lessly abandons his lover, Shang Shuihua, the former queen of Yue opera,
now overshadowed by the younger sisters, and takes Yuehong as his new
lover. Humiliated and seeing no future for herself, Shang commits suicide. 
The second part of the ﬁlm depicts a struggle between the CCP-led pro-
gressive forces and the corrupt Nationalist establishment, as manifested in
the struggle between Chunhua and Manager Tang over Yuehong’s fate.
Greatly disturbed that Yuehong has fallen into Tang’s hands and by Shang’s
death, Chunhua adopts progressive ideas about right and wrong, as taught
to her by the reporter Jiang Bo, a CCP underground worker under the lead-
ership of Brother Lin. She decides to stage a production of Lu Xun’s The New
Year’s Sacriﬁcein place of the traditional Yue opera plays, which she now con-
siders obscene and meaningless. In cahoots with the Nationalist Social Affairs
Bureau, which has banned the play, Tang cancels his contract with Chun-
hua’s troupe. Chunhua counters Tang’s effort to ruin her by organizing Yue
opera actresses for a performance that would raise funds for building a the-
ater of their own. The day before the performance, as Chunhua is walking
home, a hooligan jumps out and throws lime powder on her face, damag-
ing her eyes. The Nationalist commissioner Pan and the theater boss Tang
name Yuehong as a coconspirator in an attempt to mislead the public into
believing that the incident was part of a dispute between the two actresses.
The trial is set in a clear-cut good-versus-evil format: Chunhua, the plain-
tiff, and Jiang Bo, who occupies the journalist’s seat during the trial, repre-
sent the good, while the hooligan (the defendant), the Nationalist judges,
Tang, and a Nationalist secret agent seated in the audience represent the
evil. Yuehong, meanwhile, is caught in the crossﬁre; the trial ends in chaos
when Yuehong faints. 
At the end of the ﬁlm, which is set in 1950, Brother Lin and Jiang Bo,
both in army uniform, send Chunhua and her troupe off to participate in
land reform in their hometown as part of an entertainment propaganda team.
On the very stage where Chunhua and Yuehong performed ﬁfteen years ago,
Chunhua stages The White-Haired Girl(Baimao nü), an early Communist clas-
sic from Yan’an that tells the story of how the old society turned a woman
into a ghost and how Liberation restored her. The story line thus parallels
Chunhua’s efforts to restore Yuehong to society, and the ﬁlm ends with Chun-
hua vowing to perform revolutionary drama for the rest of her life.
7
There is thus no doubt that Stage Sisters presents a Party version of the
history of Yue opera and its actresses, a history that meshed closely with
the Party’s history of modern China in general. The director clearly
expressed the ﬁlm’s overt political mission: “Through the vicissitudes of
178 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 200 ===

two stage sisters in a changing theatrical environment, through the differ-
ent life paths Chunhua and Yuehong choose to take, the ﬁlm conveys the
love and hatred of the author and what the author wants the viewer to con-
template.”
8 The ﬁlm’s author wanted to present an uplifting melodrama of
the communist revolution and wanted the viewer to conclude that the his-
tory of Yue opera and its actresses was determined by a grand political strug-
gle in the twentieth century.
How much of the ﬁlm is based on real life and how much is  self-
congratulatory CCP propaganda? The story of Stage Sistersis based on Yuan
Xuefen’s experience during the 1940s, but it also draws liberally on the life
stories of various people surrounding her, most of whom are discussed in
previous chapters: fellow actresses Fu Quanxiang, Ma Zhanghua, and Xiao
Dangui; theater bosses Lu Gendi and Zhang Chunfan; and CCP under-
ground workers Yu Ling and Tong Lijuan. 
Yuan and Fu entered the same all-girls training school in the Shengxian
countryside in 1933, when Yuan was eleven years old and Fu was ten. Each
recalled the other with great fondness, as a companion from school days.
“I was very naughty, and Yuan Xuefen too,” Fu conﬁded. “While everyone
could easily see my troublemaking, not everyone knew how mischievous
Yuan was, because she was more thoughtful even when she was naughty.
We were a good pair.”
9 Yuan recalled with amusement her ﬁrst meeting
with Fu. Yuan’s father would not give Yuan permission to join the opera
school, but a stubborn young Yuan insisted and left home without any lug-
gage. At the school, Yuan met Fu, who was from a nearby village. The two
little girls struck up a conversation, telling each other their stories. When
night fell, however, Yuan found herself without bedding. “Fortunately,” Yuan
said, “Fu offered to let me sleep with her under the same quilt.” 
After three years of study, the girls graduated in 1936; the school became
a troupe and went to Shanghai to try its luck. As indicated in the ﬁlm, the
two friends followed different paths. While Yuan (Zhu Chunhua in the
ﬁlm) resisted the temptations of money and fame and conducted her life
in a manner that was pure and clean, others were caught up in the tumul-
tuous currents of city life. Fu had joined a religious sect with Yuan but could
not adhere to the ascetic lifestyle. In 1941, at age eighteen, she quit the
sect and became the second wife of the owner of the Shenzhou Inn, Zhu
Xiangyun.
10 Five years later, after much hardship, Fu ﬁled for divorce and
ended the marriage.11 The characters Xing Yuehong and Shang Shuihua
also rely on the life stories of Yuan’s stage partner, the famous “lightning
young male impersonator” Ma Zhanghua, and the Yue opera erotic star Xiao
The Opera as History    179

=== PDF PAGE 201 ===

Dangui, both of whom suffered at the hands of theater bosses and died
tragically while embroiled in sex scandals.
The character of Manager Tang is an obvious reference to the theater
bosses Lu Gendi and Zhang Chunfan, who were implicated in the deaths
of Ma Zhanghua and Xiao Dangui respectively and executed by the new
government as counterrevolutionaries in 1951.
12 On the political left, the
character Brother Lin is modeled after the playwright Yu Ling, chair of the
CCP Shanghai underground’s Committee on Culture during the Civil War
and director of Shanghai’s cultural affairs immediately after Liberation in
1949. Another important character, the reporter Jiang Bo, clearly repre-
sents a collective image of lower-level CCP underground workers who facil-
itated Yuan’s staging of Sister Xianglin and supported her in the press during
the union incident. 
Though derived from real-life stories, the ﬁlm politicizes and polarizes
these stories in terms of the struggle between the two rival political par-
ties, whereas the reality, as discussed in previous chapters, was more com-
plicated. For example, Lu Gendi and Zhang Chunfan were not Nationalist
ofﬁcials or afﬁliates, and the man who attacked Yuan Xuefen with a bag of
dung had nothing to do with Zhang Chunfan, nor was the attack ordered
by Nationalist ofﬁcials. The unionists had little political commitment and
acted for personal revenge, although they did take advantage of the gov-
ernment’s anti-leftist attitude. Such complicated everyday local politics are
simpliﬁed in the ﬁlm so that it ﬁts into a grand narrative of the struggle of
good versus evil, or the CCP versus the Nationalists. In fact, the ﬁlm reﬂects
the politicizing practice unfolding in real life in the early PRC period, as
seen in the new government’s execution of Lu and Zhang as class enemies,
ostensibly in response to the request of Yue opera actresses who had held
a public meeting complaining about Zhang’s oppression.
13
Yuan Xuefen, however, endorsed the ﬁlm version of Yue opera history,
or, rather, she helped create this particular version of history. For Yuan’s
career was completely tied to the Party after 1949, and her views inter-
twined with those of the Party to form the basis for an ofﬁcial interpreta-
tion of Yue opera history. During production, the ﬁlm team consulted Yuan
and her cohorts many times and, given Yuan’s artistic and political stature
and her status as the representative of the genre, any ofﬁcial interpretation
of Yue opera history had to privilege Yuan’s career and perspective. 
The revolution reinvented Yuan as a member of the PRC political and
cultural elite, not only occupying high ofﬁcial positions but also enjoying
a personal relationship with Premier Zhou Enlai and his wife Deng
180 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 202 ===

Yingchao. She was described by local ofﬁcials as “someone who had direct
access to top leaders in Beijing” (tongtian). Looking back, Yuan deemed the
performance of Sister Xianglin in 1946 to be the turning point in her life and
career. The play not only introduced her to the CCP before 1949 but also
assured her career success after Liberation. Ideologically correct and artis-
tically meritorious (thanks to Lu Xun’s craftsmanship and Yuan’s thought-
ful rendition), Sister Xianglinshowcased the best of Yue opera’s revolutionary
modern-attire plays and was bound to become a classic under the new gov-
ernment. The play not only exempliﬁed Yuan’s artistic achievement but also
suggested her strong sense of right and wrong even before Liberation. The
play was restaged at three politically signiﬁcant moments: in 1956, on the
twentieth anniversary of Lu Xun’s death; in 1962, on the twentieth anniver-
sary of Mao Zedong’s talk on art and literature in Yan’an; and in 1977, shortly
after the Cultural Revolution and on the forty-ﬁrst anniversary of Lu Xun’s
death. Each restaging of the play in the PRC period reafﬁrmed Yuan’s posi-
tion in Yue opera circles and in national cultural politics. 
In a sense, polarized interpretations of the initial staging of Sister Xianglin
were inevitable given the life-and-death struggle between the two parties
in these decades, and indeed, left-wing intellectuals began to politicize the
show in the wake of the performance. The Nationalist Shanghai Social Affairs
Bureau also became increasingly suspicious that Yuan was a CCP member
and put her under surveillance, while tacitly allowing the unionists to black-
mail and physically attack her. The bureau thought that the ten sisters were
holding their fund-raising performances to raise money for Communist
troops in northern Jiangsu and tried to block the project. Bureau archives
show that, in 1948–49, Wu Kaixian, the head of the bureau, personally mon-
itored the Xuesheng Company, while the bureau ordered the company to
submit its program of performances for approval two weeks in advance.
14
Even in 1996, some Nationalist-afﬁliated Shengxian natives who moved to
Taiwan in 1949 and who are now amateur Yue opera researchers in Taipei
still believed that Yuan was an underground CCP agent.
15
The State and Yue Opera in the Early PRC Period
The ﬁlm’s account of the history of Yue opera and its actresses was also
informed by the complex transformations shaping the genre and the lives
of the actresses in the early PRC period during which the ﬁlm was pro-
duced. Having set itself up as the liberator of the masses, who were exploited
and oppressed by the old ruling class, the CCP was, at least in theory, look-
The Opera as History    181

=== PDF PAGE 203 ===

ing out for the good of the common people, and many CCP leaders were
indeed sympathetic to the needs of disadvantaged, lower-class people.
Unlike workers and peasants, whose status in the revolution was relatively
clearly deﬁned, lower-class actors and especially actresses were regarded
by the CCP with both sympathy and suspicion and identiﬁed as people in
need of both liberation and reformation. Zhou Enlai’s view of Yue opera
actresses, articulated in 1946, clearly reveals this approach:
They [Yue opera actresses] were all children from poor families and became
actresses because there was no other way to make a living. When they became
popular in Shanghai, adoptive mothers and fathers all pursued them, and
evil forces in society surrounded them and corrupted them. A few among
them began to realize their status as the insulted and exploited and thus
wanted to be progressive and learn from progressive spoken dramatists led
by the Party’s underground forces.
16
Zhou accordingly instructed the Shanghai underground to help Yue opera
actresses and draw them into the revolution. 
Li Taicheng, an ofﬁcial at the Shanghai Cultural Affairs Bureau and later
head of the bureau after the Cultural Revolution, had a similar perspective
on the Party’s attitude and policy toward actresses in the early PRC period:
Actresses in the old society were the insulted and injured. In order to make
a living and build a foundation for survival, actresses had to deal with hooli-
gans, theater bosses, young mistresses, and rich concubines. Tong Zhi ling
[a noted Beijing opera actress], for example, had an unusual relationship
with Dai Li [head of the Bureau of Investigation of the Nationalist Com-
mittee on Military Affairs, a notorious fascist organization], but we did not
purge her for that. She was only an actress. If she were not, then it would
have been a political matter. The Communist Party was in fact very sym-
pathetic to these people. It was not that we had to use them to glorify the
image of the Party—there were a lot of actresses with clean personal his-
tories [who were willing to serve the Party].
17
State policy toward popular entertainment in the early and mid-1950s
sought to bring entertainment ﬁrmly under state control through the so-
called three transformations (san gai), which applied to the entertainer, the
structure of the industry, and the stage performance. 
With entertainers, the state sought to educate them in socialist ideas
182 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 204 ===

and make them advocates for the new society. The lowly entertainer of
the past now acquired the status of art worker and thereby not only became
the equal of others in the new society but also was further honored as, in the
state’s term, an “engineer of the soul” who inﬂuenced people with power-
ful arts. In order to showcase this transformation, the state identiﬁed many
ﬁgures similar to Yuan Xuefen in various local operas, such as Ding Shi’e
in Shanghai opera, Chang Xiangyu in Henan clapper opera, Guan Sushuang
in Hebei clapper opera, Hong Xiannü in Guangdong opera, and Mei Lan-
fang and Zhou Xinfang in Beijing opera, to name just a few, and made them
part of the new cultural and political elite.
Within Yue opera, the state-run Shanghai Yue Opera House collected
well-known actresses, such as Fan Ruijuan, Fu Quanxiang, Xu Yulan, and
Wang Wenjuan. Most of them were given political status as members of
the People’s Congress or the Political Consultative Conference. After posi-
tions in Shanghai were allocated, other noted or even less well-known
actresses moved out of Shanghai with the hope of acquiring similar status
in other cities. In 1954, Zhu Shuizhao and her Yunhua Company became
the Nanjing City Yue Opera Company,
18 and Gao Jianlin and her com-
pany settled in Xi’an,19 while in 1959, Yin Guifang and her Fanghua Com-
pany relocated to Fuzhou. 20 The transformation of such actresses into
members of state elites raised the status of entertainers in general and encour-
aged others to follow the Party as well.
In effecting structural transformation of the entertainment industry, the
state sought to replace the old market-based structure with a centrally planned
popular culture and economy. In Shanghai, the new entertainment indus-
try was organized in tiers, with a few state theaters directly under the cen-
tral government ( guojia juyuan) at the top, followed by local state-owned
institutions under provincial or municipal governments (guoying jutuan), state-
run troupes collectively owned by the employees (xin guoying jutuan), and
private troupes receiving state aid (minban gongzhu jutuan). The theaters were
also placed under state ownership and tied loosely to various performance
units. The government ﬁrst created four state-owned institutions in major
native-opera genres in the city: the Shanghai Yue Opera House, with the
status of a state theater; the Shanghai Beijing Opera House; the Shanghai
People’s Shanghai Opera Company; and the Shanghai People’s Huai Opera
Company.
21 These state-owned companies were meant to showcase the best
of each genre and lead opera reform in general. Before the Cultural Revo-
lution, the ﬁeld of popular entertainment included a planned entertainment
culture represented by state-owned institutions and their productions along
The Opera as History    183

=== PDF PAGE 205 ===

with the market-oriented popular entertainment provided by the collectively
owned troupes. Employees in state-run institutions were on the government
payroll and enjoyed a stable monthly salary and beneﬁts, while those under
collective ownership relied on the market value of their performances.
Depending on the market and the quality of each troupe’s performances,
some actresses could enjoy a very good income, much better than those on
the state payroll, while others had to rely on government-subsidized health
care and welfare beneﬁts when box ofﬁce revenues were low.
22 In the mid-
1950s, the government spent a considerable amount of money to safeguard
the welfare of entertainers, providing everything from unemployment and
health beneﬁts to winter clothing and quilts.
23 At the same time, the state
not only directly controlled the productions of state-owned companies but
also asserted inﬂuence on collectively owned troupes by inserting adminis-
trators and Party cells in these troupes. Thus, before 1949, entertainers were
largely on their own, and their livelihood depended on their ability to draw
customers; after 1949, the state took responsibility for both the content of
the production and the entertainers’ livelihoods.
24
Along with transforming the entertainer and the industry, the state sought
to transform stage productions. According to Li Taicheng: “After Libera-
tion, the administration exercised complete leadership over art and enter-
tainment and did away with all social [market] inﬂuences.”
25 Although the
extent to which government control of entertainment was in fact “com-
plete” is an open question, there is no doubt that during the early PRC
period, state intervention had a great impact on stage performances as a
whole. For the state, popular entertainment was to be reformed, along with
the entertainer, so that it could educate the people with socialist ideas. 
The new state took a populist stance toward local operas, promoting them
as China’s native folk arts and sending playwrights, directors, and composers
as well as political leaders to native-opera troupes to help develop politi-
cally correct productions with high artistic values. While providing unprece-
dented intellectual, media, and ﬁnancial support to local operas, and hence
helping to raise their artistic sophistication to a new level, state intervention
limited possibilities for exploring various subjects and styles. As the self-
designated heir to the New Culture Movement, the state quickly banned
plays it deemed morally corrupt or politically incorrect, including ghost
horror stories and erotic plays such as the Yue opera Widow Ma’s Innand the
Beijing opera Break Open the Cofﬁn.
26 At the same time, it elevated and pro-
moted revolutionary modern-attire plays conveying the Party line and prop-
agating state policies on current affairs. 
184 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 206 ===

The state, however, was unsure about how to treat love drama. On the
one hand, the new state sanctioned love drama with its strong pro-women
position, which countered feudal remnants such as gender discrimination,
arranged marriage, and the exploitation of women in the traditional family
system. The constitution gave women rights equal to those of men, and in
1950, for the ﬁrst time in Chinese history, the Marriage Law banned polyg -
amy and granted women equal rights to family property and to divorce. The
propaganda campaign launched in 1953 to promote the new law identiﬁed
free love, as opposed to traditional arranged marriage, and women’s liber-
ation in general as fundamental components of China’s socialist culture and
the basis for forging a new socialist citizenry. The state’s promotion of gen-
der equality and free marriage gave love drama in Yue opera, and in popu-
lar entertainment overall, a great boost, as it granted political legitimacy to
an already popular theatrical subgenre. On the other hand, the ofﬁcially
endorsed form of revolutionary heroism left no room for indulgence in the
petty, bourgeois kind of love sentiment. Audiences, however, continued to
support love drama and were uninterested in the new revolutionary plays.
As revolutionary plays lacked popularity and erotic plays were banned, love
drama thrived.
Yue opera love drama peaked both artistically and in popularity in the
1950s and early 1960s, a period that witnessed the production of several
Yue opera masterpieces, including Tale of the Western Chamberand Dream of the
Red Chamber. Just as the transformation of The Butterﬂy Loversduring the Repub-
lican period exempliﬁes the way in which folk traditions nurtured the opera’s
growth, so the staging of Tale of the Western Chamberand Dream of the Red Cham-
ber shows how a mature Yue opera absorbed the cream of  tradi tional liter-
ature and created theatrical masterpieces of love in the complex context
of the early PRC period.
The Shanghai Yue Opera House’s new rendition of Tale of the Western Cham-
ber illustrates this process. Adapted from Wang Shifu’s famous miscellany-
opera play of the same title, the Yue opera Tale of the Western Chamberis a tragic
love story about a talented young scholar, Zhang Gong, and Cui Ying ying,
the beautiful daughter of a late prime minister. The play was ﬁrst produced
in 1953 by order of Premier Zhou Enlai to entertain the visiting North Korean
president, Kim Il-Sung, upon the signing of the Sino-Korean Treaty.
27 In the
1955 revision of the play, Yuan Xuefen depicted the character of Cui Ying -
ying as a sensitive, lovesick maiden torn between her upbringing, with its
Confucian notions of women’s propriety, and her desire for love. Although
Yingying’s love for Zhang Gong eventually overcomes the constraints of
The Opera as History    185

=== PDF PAGE 207 ===

feudal morality, and the union of the two lovers deﬁes feudal authority,
their subsequent forced separation attests to the evils of feudalism.28
During the Qing dynasty, Tale of the Western Chamber was banned as an
“erotic play” (yin xi), and in the Republican period, various local operas often
performed it as such. It is said that Zhang Gong, as played by some rural
or low-level itinerary troupes, extended his arm from behind the curtained
bed, swinging egg whites on his ﬁngers, a gesture considered extremely erotic
at the time.
29 Other versions, including those in women’s Yue opera and
Beijing opera, had the character Hongniang, Yingying’s maid, act out erotic
gestures from her place outside the room where Cui Yingying and Zhang
Gong are supposedly making love.
30 In the 1953 and 1955 Yue opera pro-
ductions, all the explicit sexual episodes were eliminated, and romantic love
governed the narrative. The strong subtext of the young lovers’ erotic desire
