for each other was veiled by the actresses’ skillful depiction of the charac-
ters’ emotional development (see ﬁgs. 5.2, 5.3).31
The state used Yue opera love drama to represent the achievements of
Chinese native opera in the international community. Yue opera was the
ﬁrst native opera to be performed internationally. At the Geneva Con-
vention in 1953, Premier Zhou Enlai entertained foreign diplomats and jour-
nalists with the new Yue opera color ﬁlm The Butterﬂy Lovers. In 1955, the
Shanghai Yue Opera House, touring as the Chinese Yue Opera Troupe,
performed two Yue opera masterpieces, The Butterﬂy Loversand Tale of the West-
ern Chamber, in the German Democratic Republic and the Soviet Union.
32
These two plays and, after 1958, Dream of the Red Chamber, became interna-
tionally known through frequent performances overseas and at home for
foreign guests. The Shanghai Yue Opera House became an important cul-
tural ambassador for the PRC state. In 1962 and 1963, when a number of
Yue opera plays were made into color ﬁlms and screened nationwide and
internationally, Yue opera’s inﬂuence reached new heights. As the Yue opera
playwright Cheng Rong remarked:
Yue opera had always had low status and was not worthy of being talked
about. As a Yue opera playwright, Xu Jin could not even ﬁnd a wife before
Liberation. He was married after Liberation. At that time [in the 1940s], we
were all embarrassed to tell people that we were doing Yue opera. I thus even-
tually cut off communication with my former classmates, because they could
not understand why I became involved in Yue opera—as if I were no good.
Only after Liberation did Yue opera gain high esteem and come to be per-
formed nationwide and even overseas, representing our country.
33
Perhaps exaggerating somewhat, Cheng Rong nonetheless attested to
the fact that Yue opera was at its political and artistic zenith during the
1950s and early 1960s. Although Yue opera had already been prominent
in Shanghai’s entertainment market before 1949, the new state acknowl-
edged Yue opera’s artistic achievements and further raised its status by mak-
ing it into a national theater, widely known as China’s second-largest native
opera, second only to Beijing opera.
Most actresses felt optimistic and ﬁnancially more secure under the new
government. Wu Xiaolou, for example, had entered an all-girls opera school
in 1934, when she was around nine years old, and joined the Shanghai Yue
Opera House as a leading actress playing elderly male roles after Liberation.
The youngest among the Yue opera ten sisters, she was enthusiastic about
188 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 210 ===

the revolution, and being part of the state-run Shanghai Yue Opera House
was her way of joining the revolution. She willingly took a salary cut and
never expected to receive such generous ﬁnancial beneﬁts in her retirement:
Comparing the situations of actresses in the old and new societies, actresses
now enjoy a higher political status and [level of] human dignity. Although
we were very popular among audiences, we had no political status and were
looked down upon by others. After Liberation, party leaders treated us so
nicely, nicer than my own father. Moreover, when I joined the state-run insti-
tution, I was prepared to receive no salary. In the end, however, I received
a monthly salary of three hundred yuan, plus free health care. Now, my retire-
ment pension is more than one thousand a month plus free health care. I can
be an in-patient whenever necessary. In the old society, I watched old Yue
opera teachers dying in the streets.
34
Wu’s sentiment was shared by other elderly actors and actresses. The
couple Zhang Rongbiao and Zhang Yanqiu, Yue opera performers in the
1930s, were comfortable, though not rich, living in retirement on govern-
ment money in their hometown of Chongren, Shengxian, in 1995. Wu also
alluded to another beneﬁt Liberation brought to young actresses: they no
longer had to deal with hooligans, theater bosses, and unscrupulous men
trying to take advantage of them.
Not everyone, however, was initially as enthusiastic as Wu about the
new government and state-run institutions and missed out on what the
state had to offer. Jia Guilian, a member of the Yulan Company, led by Xu
Yulan, refused to participate when Xu merged the company with the People’s
Liberation Army in Beijing in 1952; this was yet another way of joining
the revolution and becoming nationalized. After two years of service, the
Yulan Company was sent back to Shanghai and joined the Shanghai Yue
Opera House. Zhou Baokui, who had spent her entire career with the Yulan
Company, commented in 1996 that Jia had “calculated badly” and now
lacked a stable salary and free health care.
35 This may not have been impor-
tant at the time, as popular young actresses could probably make at least
as much as the income offered by the state. But in the long run, those on
the state’s payroll could count on secure employment and better beneﬁts,
not to mention the status of being members of a national opera house.
The ﬁlm Stage Sisters, made during this so-called golden age of Yue opera,
reﬂected these positive changes in the status of Yue opera and the lives
of its actresses. Indeed, it is indisputable that the communist revolution
The Opera as History    189

=== PDF PAGE 211 ===

raised the status of local operas and beneﬁted Yue opera actresses and enter-
tainers in general by absorbing some entertainers into a national political
and cultural elite while providing others with ﬁnancial security and social
standing. 
Problems with Cinema’s Master Narrative
Were there no problems, then, with Stage Sisters’ communist melodrama and
the polarization of good and evil manifested in its dichotomized repre-
sentations of the CCP versus the Nationalist Party and of pre-Liberation
society versus post-Liberation society? One problem with communist
melodrama is that it denies agency to the people the CCP claims to have
liberated from the evils of the Nationalists. It attributes agency only to the
two battling forces, making objects of everyone caught between the two.
In this way, it construes the relationship between the Party and Yue opera
actresses as one between the liberator and the liberated and thus allows
the Party to take full credit for the actresses’ upward mobility. The melo-
drama therefore disregards the subjectivity of the people who created Yue
opera as a popular phenomenon—their views and the strategies they
adopted or forged in order to get through life. One striking example of
this oversight involves the character Xing Yuehong, who is presented as a
typically weak and misled woman, destroyed by evil forces and rescued
only because of the triumph of good forces. By casting her as such a pathetic
creature, the ﬁlm does not give her credit for resistance or self-salvation.
In real life, however, Fu Quanxiang, on whom the character is partially based,
divorced her ﬁrst husband entirely of her own volition. Like Fu, many other
actresses managed to overcome adversity, one way or another, without the
Party’s help.
Signiﬁcantly, the ﬁrst draft of the ﬁlm, titled Detailed Outlines, has a some-
what less clear-cut narrative and makes better sense of Yuehong’s choices.
Before taking his last breath, Yuehong’s father tells her to ﬁnd a good hus-
band and quit this difﬁcult acting vocation. Later on, disappointed in her
marriage to Manager Tang, Yuehong returns to acting and tries to abort
her pregnancy in order to free herself of Tang’s control. At a crucial moment,
Yuehong bravely, and also shrewdly, rescues Chunhua from the hands of
Nationalist agents and helps make the fund-raising performance a success.
The sisters are thus reconciled, and Yuehong joins the progressive forces
before Liberation.
36 This early version of Yuehong’s marriage to Tang reﬂects
the ideals and life strategies common among Yue opera actresses at the time,
190 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 212 ===

and in portraying Yuehong’s resistance to Tang, after she realizes the mar-
riage was a mistake, it at least allows her some agency. After a subsequent
editorial process that sought to “deepen the main theme” and “reﬁne the
plot line,” however, Yuehong’s marriage to Tang is recast as a categorical
mistake, and the ﬁnal production denied Yuehong any agency at all.
37
Even the main character, Zhu Chunhua, is presented as a person who
would be nothing without the Party. Chunhua’s protests to Tang upon Shang
Shuihua’s death only help Tang boost his public image, while she is
depicted as at her wit’s end in ﬁghting the dark forces. It is the left-wing
journalist Jiang Bo who thereafter supports and guides Chunhua step by
step, introducing her to Lu Xun’s story about Sister Xianglin, writing arti-
cles supporting her in the news media, and guiding her through the com-
plex courtroom battle. At the same time, the ﬁlm tries to make clear that
Chunhua’s, or Yuan’s, struggle would not have had a chance of success were
it not for Liberation. As Jiang Bo tells Chunhua when the actresses are work-
ing on the fund-raising project for their own theater: “The Xinhu Theater
is a small stage, and society is a large theater. There is a big boss behind
Manager Tang, and behind the big boss there is the American. These people
control the large theater....  I f  s ociety remains the same and the big boss
remains in control, we cannot possibly have our own theater.”
38 Liberation
becomes the only solution to all of the problems facing the actresses, ren-
dering their spontaneous activities trivial. 
In real life, Yuan Xuefen displayed an exceptionally strong will and stub-
bornly pursued her own agenda of making Yue opera and its actresses both
modern and respectable. Yuan not only followed a lifestyle based on qing-
bai but also performed that virtue onstage. From the time of her youth, Yuan
was reluctant to participate in what she considered lighthearted and
obscene plays and chose instead to specialize in the roles of serious
women. In 1942, when she started the New Yue Opera reform movement,
she made it a principle not to sing at private parties. Chang tanghui, the prac-
tice of performing for rich, powerful patrons in their homes, was a tradi-
tion from imperial times and continued into the Republican period. For many
popular entertainers, chang tanghuicould be an important source of income,
a way of exchanging favors with the host or go-between, and a chance to
establish or renew ties with upper-class patrons. Under the inﬂuence of
the Republican rhetoric of theatrical reform, and inspired by the model
intellectual actors in ﬁlm and spoken drama, some entertainers, such as Yuan
Xuefen, began to see the practice as feudalistic and humiliating, contrary
to their desired new status as art workers. Even when Madame Chiang Kai-
The Opera as History    191

=== PDF PAGE 213 ===

shek (Song Meiling) invited Yuan in 1946 to perform at her house, Yuan
declined, saying that Madame Chiang could see her performance in a the-
ater; the invitation had come through Wang Xiaolai, former president of
the Shanghai Chamber of Commerce and perhaps the most powerful Sheng-
xian native in Shanghai. In Yuan’s mind, her decision was not directed toward
Madame Chiang Kai-shek but was a matter of principle and consistency
in building her self-esteem and character.
39 Left-wing journalists found only
political value in Yuan’s action. The CCP-controlled newspaper United Evening
News reported the episode, implicitly politicizing Yuan’s refusal.40 Three
decades later, Yuan’s biography presented this small episode as evidence
of her political choice.
41
While communist revolution was not her agenda, to some extent because
she had no idea what communism was at the time, some of her under-
takings inadvertently coincided with the interests of the left, such as her
staging of Sister Xianglin and her refusal to be involved with the Yue Opera
Workers’ Union, which was sanctioned by the Nationalist Social Affairs
Bureau. By making these two incidents the center of the Yue opera story,
however, the ﬁlm neglects the most important development of the time,
one initiated entirely by the actresses themselves, namely the New Yue
Opera reforms of which Sister Xianglin was a product. What mattered to
Yuan at the time was not her “discovery of the Party,” as she never dis-
covered the Party until after Liberation, but access to the mainstream media
and a powerful left-wing social network.
42 Because of Sister Xianglin, Yue
opera made headlines in mainstream newspapers for the ﬁrst time in its
history, initiating a continuous stream of reportage about its reforms and
its actresses over the next few years; coverage of Yue opera became a  regu -
lar feature, making Yuan Xuefen a positive public ﬁgure in the mainstream
press. 
Sister Xianglin also introduced Yuan Xuefen and other Yue opera actresses
to women’s activist groups, members of the left-wing cultural elite, and high-
level CCP Shanghai underground leaders. Yuan Xuefen established rela-
tionships with such inﬂuential individuals as Xu Guangping, Tian Han, and
Yu Ling as well as with the young intellectuals Liao Lin, Mei Duo, and Tong
Lijuan. This social network opened up a new world for Yuan and the other
actresses. Yuan Xuefen and Yin Guifang were invited to attend seminars
and tea parties for youth and women organized by Xu Guangping and
 performed twice for fund-raising events beneﬁting the Chinese Children’s
Foundation (Zhongguo Fuli Hui) sponsored by Madame Sun Yat-sen
(Song Qingling). Yuan and the Xuesheng Company also participated in
192 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 214 ===

various leftist-sponsored activities. The Xuesheng Company, for example,
sent representatives to the memorial conference for Tao Xingzhi, a well-
known left-wing intellectual, and to the public memorial service for Wen
Yiduo and Li Gongpu, both of whom had been assassinated by National-
ist special agents in 1946.
43 By participating in these functions, and pro-
viding popular material for left-wing media, Yue opera actresses also
supported the left wing and the CCP underground, albeit unknowingly.
The relationship between Yue opera actresses and the CCP was mutually
beneﬁcial and required continual negotiation after 1949. In any case, it was
hardly a relationship between a liberator on one side and those waiting to
be liberated on the other.
The ﬁlm’s communist melodrama also presents a one-sided view of how
Liberation beneﬁted Yue opera and its actresses, neglecting any possible
negative effect the post-Liberation reform process had on the opera. After
the government ﬁnished restructuring the entertainment system in 1958,
it turned its focus to productions that would reﬂect the heroism and patri-
otism of the working people in building socialism during the Great Leap
Forward. From 1958 onward, Yue opera was under great pressure to change
the emphasis of its repertoire from costume love dramas to revolutionary
modern-attire plays. Numerous plays about heroic struggles against the
Nationalists and the Japanese invaders in the past and about ongoing class
struggles were hastily staged to help disseminate government propaganda
for political campaigns; these productions, however, attracted few view-
ers and usually ended after only a few performances.
44 The situation became
increasingly tense when Mao himself delivered two speeches on culture,
at the end of 1963 and in mid-1964. Mao’s instructions were echoed by
the radical leftist Zhang Chunqiao, then head of the CCP Shanghai Min-
istry of Propaganda, who instructed the art and entertainment industries
to concentrate on the momentous achievements of the thirteen years since
Liberation. As Li Taicheng, then deputy head of the Shanghai Cultural
Affairs Bureau, recalled:
We were so afraid when we heard of Chairman Mao’s two instructions in
1963 and 1964. Mao also commented [on another occasion] on a report
sent to him about the storytelling campaign that Beijing opera is all about
emperors, kings, generals, and ministers, while Yue opera is all about scholar-
b e a u t y  s t o r i e s ....  B e c ause of Mao’s instructions, the CCP Ministry of Prop-
aganda was a little nervous, and Zhang Chunqiao thus declared “ten great
merits for focusing on the thirteen years since Liberation.”. . . Mao’s instruc-
The Opera as History    193

=== PDF PAGE 215 ===

tions were [issued in] only a few sentences, but the wind began to blow.
Wind coming from nowhere could become so strong that it would shake
heaven and earth.
45
In 1964, the recently released, instantly popular Yue opera ﬁlm Dream of
the Red Chamber was quietly recalled, signaling the end of good fortune for
love drama and anticipating the great disaster soon to come. In fact, an
increasingly radical state cultural policy had already damaged the popu-
lar-culture market. Even before the Cultural Revolution, most state-owned
institutions, including the Shanghai Beijing Opera House, the Shanghai
People’s Shanghai Opera Company, the Shanghai People’s Huai Opera
Company, and major theaters, had been running deﬁcits for several years,
despite the tax exemption they received for staging modern-attire revolu-
tionary plays.
46 The Shanghai Yue Opera House was perhaps the only state-
owned institution that did not lose money, because of revenues from several
popular Yue opera movies made during the 1950s and early 1960s. It, too,
was losing money on stage performances of modern-attire revolutionary
plays, and revenues from its limited staging of love dramas, with special
permission from the Cultural Affairs Bureau, could not make up for the
losses. The overall market for popular entertainment was shrinking.
In short, just as the Party assumed the role of liberator of the people,
so it made itself a designer of culture, directing artists and audiences onto
what it considered to be the right path. This elitist attitude again blinded
the Party to the crucial roles artist agency and audience reception played
in cultural development. The state’s privileging of the communist revolu-
tion in Stage Sisters obscured many other forces at work in the history of
Yue opera. The rise of women’s Yue opera and its actresses was a ramiﬁ-
 cation of several modern processes: the actresses’ struggle for full social
 status, the formation of an urban bourgeois culture with a strong female
component, and an urban-based theatrical reform in the context of nation-
building rhetoric. The social, political, and economic upheaval that
occurred from the early PRC period through the Cultural Revolution dras-
tically altered Shanghai’s bourgeois modernity, which had supported Yue
opera’s success, and the Cultural Revolution was the coup de grâce. 
The Storm
Stage Sisters , along with the 1963 ﬁlms The Second Month in Early Spring
(Zaochun eryue), directed by Xie Tieli, and Jiangnan in the Northern Country
194 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 216 ===

(Beiguo Jiangnan), directed by Shen Fu, had become an object of criticism
even before its 1965 release. According to the Party’s leftist radicals, led
by Mao and his wife Jiang Qing, these ﬁlms propagated not the heroic rev-
olutionary spirit but bourgeois humanism, which had its roots in the bour-
geois orientation of the early leftist ﬁlms produced in Shanghai in the 1930s.
Stage Sisters was thus a “poisonous weed” ( ducao) threatening the garden of
socialist art and entertainment with its bourgeois sentimentality, as expressed
in the two sisters’ deep feelings for each other. Mao and Jiang Qing used
such cultural criticism to weaken rivals within the Party who were allied
with Liu Shaoqi, then PRC president, who had been in charge of the Party’s
underground work in large cities in the 1930s and 1940s. Stage Sisters, they
claimed, was guilty of propagating the wrong line, one that emphasized
underground work, as opposed to Mao’s correct line of building revolu-
tion in the countryside in order to surround and ﬁnally take over the bour-
geois cities.
47
These charges against Stage Sisters reﬂect an important division in
approaches within the CCP: the Maoist rural-based, tough proletarian
approach versus the urban-oriented, bourgeoisie-friendly approach advo-
cated by Liu Shaoqi and Deng Xiaoping after the founding of the People’s
Republic of China. Though it had its roots in the 1930s, when Mao emerged
victorious from intraparty struggles and the Nationalists’ military campaign
to wipe out the CCP, this division reemerged in the 1950s; it became increas-
ingly clear in the early 1960s in the wake of the great famine that resulted
from the Great Leap Forward. Mao had been forced to resign as president
of the state in 1962, when the national economy hit rock bottom and twenty
million to thirty million people died of starvation. Liu Shaoqi took over
and consolidated ongoing economic adjustment policies, which were later
labeled capitalistic by hardcore Maoists. On the cultural front, Liu’s new
approach encouraged the production of numerous sentimental revolutionary
melodramas, including the three ﬁlms noted above. Yet, as the economy
was recovering, Mao was gathering his forces. He noted in his two direc-
tives on cultural affairs issued in late 1963 and mid-1964:
There are a lot of problems that involve a lot of people in the various art
forms—local operas, storytelling, music, painting, dance, ﬁlm, poetry, and
literature. Socialist reform has achieved very little in many institutions.
The majority of [artistic] associations and their publications (it is said a
few are good) basically have not followed the Party’s policy over the past
ﬁfteen years. They have behaved like high ofﬁcials and not tried to get close
The Opera as History    195

=== PDF PAGE 217 ===

to the workers, peasants, and soldiers so as to reﬂect socialist revolution and
construction [in their arts]. In recent years, they have even fallen to the edge
of revisionism.
48
The rhetorical campaign Mao and his allies waged against certain ﬁlms
and operas between 1963 and 1965 was followed by the campaign criti-
cizing the Beijing opera play Hairui Dismissed from Ofﬁce (Hairui baguan),
penned by the historian Wu Han, which became the prelude to the Cul-
tural Revolution.
49
During the Cultural Revolution, leftist radicals purged the entertainment
industry of all “feudal” and “bourgeois” productions, leaving a handful of
thoroughly revolutionary ﬁlms and operas, mostly about heroes and hero-
ines led by Mao in armed struggles against the Nationalists and Japanese
invaders in the 1930s and 1940s. The radical state now asserted nearly com-
plete control over cultural production and consumption, and all it could
offer in performance art were the ten so-called model operas, which fea-
tured the same dry narratives of the heroic communist revolution. Yue opera
was hard hit, as it focused on the bourgeois love stories and the feudal cos-
tume plays that ran counter to proletarian arts. Between 1966 and 1971,
almost all Yue opera companies in Shanghai were disbanded, properties
were disposed of, and artists were sent to labor in the countryside and fac-
tories or otherwise devoted themselves entirely to political activities. After
President Richard Nixon’s visit to China and the signing of the Shanghai
Communiqué in 1972, the state slightly loosened its policy toward local
operas. In 1973, the Shanghai Yue Opera House was reorganized into the
Shanghai Yue Opera Company and staged its ﬁrst public performance of
a modern-attire revolutionary play, Song of the Dragon River(Longjiang song),
copied from the model Beijing opera. Thus, less than a handful of gender-
straight modern-attire revolutionary plays constituted the only Yue opera
performances during the entire decade of the Cultural Revolution.
50 The
damage done to Yue opera was so severe that the genre never recovered,
except for a short period following the end of the Cultural Revolution. 
Yuan Xuefen and many other well-known actresses were politically per-
secuted and physically tortured by the young rebels in the Shanghai Yue
Opera House during the Cultural Revolution. In 1977, having barely sur-
vived, Yuan and the Shanghai Yue Opera House once again staged Sister
Xianglin. At this moment of rehabilitation, Yuan reﬂected on her sufferings
during the Cultural Revolution and waxed nostalgic about the passage of
time. Sensing that this might be her last performance as Sister Xianglin,
196 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 218 ===

she wrote an essay recalling her ﬁrst performance of the play thirty-two
years before:
At that time, I was young and naive, having never read Marxist-Leninist works.
I did not comprehend the greatness of Lu Xun and had only vague feelings
about the things I liked and disliked in society. I simply felt sympathetic to
Sister Xianglin’s misfortune. I had seen and was familiar with female ﬁgures
like the character Sister Xianglin. I just wanted to adapt The New Year’s Sac-
riﬁce for the stage....
The performance attracted considerable attention. The Nationalist reac-
tionaries dispatched secret service agents to tail me, and they assaulted me
with dung, libel, and other means. Reactionary newspapers threatened that
if I “turned to the left, there would be no way out.” But progressive forces
of the time supported me and encouraged me, pointing out that the mis-
fortune of Sister Xianglin was not simply a question of who killed her but a
criticism of the social system. I then began to understand that the distress
of Sister Xianglin in Lu Xun’s story condemned the reactionary social sys-
tem, the foundation of the old society. This theory enabled me to see the
social origin of Sister Xianglin’s tragedy. From then on, Sister Xianglin led me
to progressivism and revolution.
51
Yuan blamed the Nationalists for all her adversities in the three years before
Liberation and did not doubt that the CCP underground supported her
otherwise lonely struggle against all odds. Two Party scholars, Zhang Lihui
and Gao Yilong, later elaborated on this theme in their 1984 biography of
Yuan: 
After the staging of Sister Xianglin, progressive forces and reactionary forces
both paid attention to Yuan Xuefen and the Xuesheng Company. During
those turbulent and confusing days, the inevitable questions [Yuan and other
actresses] faced were: Which path to take? And whom to follow?
52
Yuan’s statement and her biography are in line with the communist mas-
ter narrative of Yue opera’s history as featured in Xie Jin’s ﬁlm. At this time
Yue opera was enjoying a huge but short-lived revival. The Yue opera ﬁlm
Dream of the Red Chamber was released in 1978 and instantly broke box ofﬁce
records across the nation, and the wide-screen color ﬁlm of Sister Xiang lin
attracted numerous viewers throughout the nation and in Hong Kong. In
Shanghai, enthusiastic spectators crowded theaters to see Yue opera clas-
The Opera as History    197

=== PDF PAGE 219 ===

sic love dramas. While the Shanghai Yue Opera House regained its pres-
tigious title, several district-level state-owned Yue opera companies also
revived. Then, during the late 1980s, Yue opera’s market began to shrink,
and the genre slid into the same desperate position as other local operas
that had long been relying on government subsidies. The reason for this
negative change, according to several Yue opera practitioners and schol-
ars, was the lack of a new generation of operagoers due to the disruptions
caused by the Cultural Revolution.
53 It seemed to many that the new state
brought Yue opera to its glorious peak only to destroy it. When asked to
assess the relationship between state control and the fate of Yue opera, vet-
eran Yue opera director and reformer Han Yi responded:
Before Liberation, there were many inadequacies in terms of funding, the
quality of personnel, and internal organization. The most difﬁcult problem
was the lack of funding for artistic experimentation. After Liberation and
with the state’s support, artistic accomplishments reached to a new height,
only to stagnate because artistic considerations had to be subordinated to
bureaucracy and the Party’s orders....  I t  i s  p ossible that Yue opera would
be better off today if not for Liberation and the Cultural Revolution.
54
Yue opera’s downturn in the 1980s thus invited Yue opera people to reassess
the genre’s relationship with the communist revolution and to reﬂect on
their hopes for the opera’s renewal.
The New Context of the Post-revolution Era
After Mao’s death, China’s national objectives underwent dramatic changes.
The landmark Third Plenary Session of the CCP Tenth Central Com-
mittee, held at the end of 1979, passed a resolution to stop considering
“class struggle” the center of the Party’s platform and to shift its focus to
“socialist modernization.”
55 The Party began to reinstate market-driven
economies, slowly moved away from the Soviet model of a planned econ-
omy, and allowed a capitalist economy to coexist with socialism. In 1980,
the CCP government established four Special Economic  Zones—Shenzhen,
Zhuhai, Shantou, and Xiamen—in which to develop a capitalist economy.
In 1984, another fourteen large coastal cities, including Shanghai and Tian-
jin, were opened to foreign investment.
56 In 1992, during Deng Xiaoping’s
southern tour to Guangdong and Shanghai, his famous speeches ﬁnally crys-
tallized the Party’s determination to focus on economic development and
198 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 220 ===

the policy of “opening and reform.” 57 Deng believed that China “should
borrow and use all those advanced economic models that reﬂect the laws
of modern mass production developed by countries around the world includ-
ing developed capitalist countries.”
58 His methodology blurred the line
between socialism and capitalism and freed the government to use all avail-
able means for China’s development. Deng’s famous “cat philosophy”—
”White cat or black cat, as long as it can catch the mouse, it is a good
cat”—became the motto of the reform era, and the Maoist ideology and
politics of class struggle crumbled.
Under the leadership of Deng Xiaoping and his successor, Jiang Zemin,
economic development became the Party’s focus, and ideology was assigned
a supporting role. Reinstating a market economy was key not only for eco-
nomic stimulation in general but also for cultural production. As the state
now prioritized putting money into people’s pockets over reforming their
souls, artists and entertainers were released from their obligation to assist
the state in this endeavor and could explore artistic themes that inspired
them, as long as they posed no explicit challenge to the CCP leadership.
The 1980s and 1990s witnessed the emergence of a lively entertainment
market characterized by a diversity of cultural activities, including revived
native-opera and story-singing performances, movies and television dra-
mas directed by veterans and new generations of Chinese directors, and
new cultural forms inﬂuenced by a globalized mass culture, such as rock
and roll, karaoke and dance halls, and foreign ﬁlms.
The entertainment market in the reform era differed from that of the pre-
vious decades in two ways. First, unlike during the early PRC period and
the Cultural Revolution, productions featuring revolutionary and politically
correct patriotic narratives, considered “main melody” (zhu xuanlü) works by
the Party, no longer dominated; instead, along with main-melody produc-
tions, which represented the ofﬁcial ideology, the Party allowed the devel-
opment of a diverse cultural market. Second, the later post-Mao years
witnessed a general decline in “traditional” entertainment forms, such as ﬁlm,
native operas, and story-singing, following a brief boom when the enter-
tainment market returned, and the rise of new cultural forms inﬂuenced by
globalized mass entertainment that appeals to younger consumers. Accord-
ing to Li Taicheng, head of the Shanghai Cultural Affairs Bureau from 1977
to the mid-1980s: 
From the Bureau for Public Health, I was asked [by the Party] to go back
and take charge of the Shanghai Cultural Affairs Bureau on January 10, 1977 .
The Opera as History    199

=== PDF PAGE 221 ===

I began by restoring some native-opera troupes, and Yue opera resumed per-
formances initially with modern-attire plays, such as Spring Tide [(Sanyue
chunchao), of 1978] and Yang Kaihui [(Zhonghun qu), of 1977]. By the 1978
Chinese New Year, tickets for native-opera and story-singing performances
were no longer as coveted as before. Suzhou story-singing was the ﬁrst to
decline in popularity, because it was too slow and its audience was aging.
There were still people attending shows in the daytime, but few showed
up for the evening performances. On the other hand, libraries were full of
people. In the latter half of 1978, Beijing opera took its downhill turn. But
Yue opera, comic shows, and Shanghai opera were still popular. After 1979,
many genres lost their appeal, including Yue opera. But dance and comic
shows remained pretty popular. It is clear that there was a generational
change in the audiences. Comedy became popular, ﬁlm declined, TV drama
entered people’s homes, and newspapers became extremely popular. Later,
pop songs, karaoke, dance halls, and television boomed, and native operas
crashed.
59
In Shanghai, although native operas no longer dominated the entertain-
ment market, along with movies, as they had before, they still managed
to survive, thanks in part to government subsidies that helped sustain a
few state-owned institutions, including the Shanghai Yue Opera House,
the Shanghai Beijing Opera House, the Shanghai Shanghai Opera House,
the Shanghai Huai Opera Company, and the Shanghai Kun Opera Com-
pany. Facing increasing challenges from globalized mass entertainment
and the demands of new generations of consumers, native operas were
under great pressure to revive themselves and ﬁnd their niches in the new
marketplace or become museum pieces. Each of these genres has been try-
ing in its own way to attract new audiences, but none so far has been very
successful.
Yue opera in Shanghai has experienced perhaps the most painful down-
turn, given its tremendous popularity and national inﬂuence during the early
PRC period and after the Cultural Revolution. The decade between the
late 1980s and the late 1990s may have been Yue opera’s most difﬁcult and
uncertain period, during which it had to rely on government stipends or
corporate charity for survival. Only four Yue opera companies remained,
the Shanghai Yue Opera House and three second-tier state-owned troupes
in the Hongkou, Jing’an, and Luwan districts. According to Lu Shijun, a
researcher at the Shanghai Yue Opera House and editor of Shanghai Yue
200 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 222 ===

Opera Gazetteer, the decline in Yue opera was reﬂected in the “three fews”:
few operagoers, few performances, and few Yue opera professionals.60 As
Lu observed, the more performances the troupes put onstage, the more
money the government lost on Yue opera, because box ofﬁce revenues did
not make up for the expense of each performance.
61
How to revitalize Yue opera became a hot topic in Yue opera circles and
concerned scholarly communities. Many symposia and conferences were
held to discuss related issues, and conference volumes and anthologies were
devoted to answering this very question. The title of one collection pub-
lished in 1994, T oward Glory Once Again (Chungxin zouxiang huihuang),
reﬂects the general aspirations of those who participated in these meet-
ings and contributed to the publications.
62 There was, however, a range of
opinion concerning the nature of the problem, and opera professionals and
scholars, as well as the leadership of the Shanghai Yue Opera House, includ-
ing Yuan Xuefen, put forth a wide variety of solutions. While many Yue
opera practitioners welcomed the attention, they also felt overwhelmed
by the sheer quantity of often contradictory opinions and expressed the
desire to be left alone to work on their art. As one Yue opera director com-
plained at a 1995 symposium: “No one seems to have a clear idea about
the main theme [of Yue opera]. In the past, it was anti-feudal. What is it
now? For example, the main-melody theme was thought to be not right
for Yue opera, but later it was deemed right again. It is now both right and
not right.” He then pleaded with the various theorists: “Quality produc-
tions need time to mature. Please do not put too much pressure on the
artists!”
63
Quietly but consistently, in the midst of these heated debates, Yue opera
practitioners have been making their own choices. They have selected top-
ics that stray from main-melody themes and stuck with those that are more
mundane, as reﬂected in a series of new productions such as Xishi Returns
to Her Home Country (Xishi gui Yue), of 1989; The Lotus Girl (Lianhua nü), of
1991; The Jade Hairpin(Yuzan ji), of 1996; and Cao Zhi and Zeng Luo(Cao Zhi
yu Zeng Luo), of 1996.
64 In the meantime, Dream of the Red Chamber con-
tinued to be the most popular Yue opera play. Then, in August 1999, the
Shanghai Yue Opera House successfully staged a special production of this
Yue opera classic for the newly constructed Shanghai Grand Theater. Yue
opera was the ﬁrst native opera to be performed in this palace of modern
performing arts, itself a landmark of Shanghai’s contemporary architecture.
The play created a sensation; local audiences as well as people from Hong
The Opera as History    201

=== PDF PAGE 223 ===

Kong and other cities packed the eighteen thousand seats for every per-
formance. The everlasting popularity of Dream of the Red Chamberhelped Yue
opera artists understand that Yue opera’s artistic strength lay in its ability
to explore love and passion, not grand nation-state narratives. 
Same-Sex Love in Women’s Yue Opera: Artistic Identity Redeﬁned
The 1998 Yue opera play Stage Sisters was produced during this Yue opera
artists’ soul-searching process. Although represented as an adaptation of
Xie Jin’s 1965 ﬁlm, from which it takes its title and basic story, the Yue
opera play is in fact not at all like the ﬁlm.
65 It tells a different story about
Yue opera’s history and the relationship between the two actresses. While
the ﬁlm derives its dramatic tension from conﬂicts framed in terms of good
versus evil, or the CCP versus the Nationalist Party, the play does away
with such political polarization and focuses instead on the theme of sis-
terly love. The theme song makes this clear from the outset and reminds
the audience throughout the performance what the play is all about:
Tough times are hard, they sing year in, year out;
Homeless anywhere, yet they make their home everywhere. 
Exhausting tragic and happy stories on the stage, 
Never forgetting sisterly love in the real world.
Several important changes depoliticize the play’s story. The two left-
wing ﬁgures, Brother Lin and the journalist Jiang Bo, are eliminated and
replaced by a secondary character, Ni Tao, a Shengxian native and pro-
gressive youth who loves his native opera and tries to help the actresses
deal with the challenges and temptations of the metropolis. He introduces
spoken drama and the patriotic play Wen Tianxiangto the actresses and later
writes the play The New Year’s Sacriﬁcefor Chunhua to perform. The bad char-
acters are also drawn in less political tones. While Commissioner Pan, of
the Nationalist Social Affairs Bureau, and the Nationalist secret agent are
eliminated, Manager Tang is rewritten so that he is no longer a character
with direct ties to the Nationalists but a politically insigniﬁcant local strong-
man who bullies his way through social relationships and exploits actresses
in his employ. The story line of the second half of the play is altered accord-
ingly. Politically charged developments such as the staging ofThe New Year’s
Sacriﬁce , the fund-raising performance, the lime-powder attack on Chun-
hua, and the courtroom drama either disappear completely or fade into the
202 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 224 ===

background, given only a few lines of treatment. Perhaps the most impor-
tant change between the ﬁlm and the play is the ending. While the ﬁlm
ends with the Party returning the humiliated Yuehong to society after
Liberation, the play conveniently ends in the winter of 1946, avoiding Lib-
eration and its signiﬁcance to the Yue opera story altogether.
By cutting out these political story lines, the play makes room for explor-
ing the relationship between the two actresses. Certain long-standing Yue
opera dramatic techniques are deployed to develop the theme of sisterly
love. One central device is the “drama within the drama” (xi zhong xi), which
is only hinted at but not fully employed in the ﬁlm. The play makes excel-
lent use of this device to develop a second theme of heterosexual love,
intertwining the motif of the butterﬂy lovers with the main theme of sis-
terly love. The two themes work together extremely well in building emo-
tional development, thanks to the peculiarly ﬂuid cross-dressing plays of
this all-female theater. The two actresses—Qian Huili, as Xing Yuehong,
and Shan Yangping, as Zhu Chunhua—are both skilled performers of the
cross-dressing plots seen in many Yue opera plays. While Shan Yangping
specializes in young female roles and excels at playing cross-dressing char-
acters, such as Zhu Yingtai and Meng Lijun, Qian Huili is a popular male
impersonator noted for her performances of Jia Baoyu and many other
young lovers. In Stage Sisters, the drama within the drama allows her to imper-
sonate the young male lover and thus enriches an otherwise gender-straight
performance. 
The four scenes of the drama within the drama punctuate the structure
of the typical love story: unity-split-unity. The play opens with the two
actresses, Chunhua and Yuehong, on a traditional stage in Shaoxing in the
early 1940s. They are performing the classic “Eighteen-Li Farewell,” in which
the cross-dressing Zhu Yingtai promises a happy Liang Shanbo her little
sister in marriage; the sister looks exactly like Yingtai. Their lively acting
wins an enthusiastic response from the audience. This scene not only helps
set the temporal and spatial frame for the story but also introduces the emo-
tional status of the two main characters, as a pair of young partners onstage
and offstage. This union, however, breaks down, and the second and third
dramas within the drama bring the emotion of the play to a double crisis.
Chunhua and Yuehong go onstage for one last performance together after
Yuehong announces her marriage to Manager Tang. The two have just had
a ﬁght, as Chunhua tried in vain to dissuade Yuehong. The program is “Meet-
ing at Yingtai’s Chamber,” in which Shanbo comes to request Yingtai’s hand
in marriage, only to learn that he is too late. The heartbroken lovers then
The Opera as History    203

=== PDF PAGE 225 ===

sing farewell to each other. In the course of their performance, the two
actresses cannot help but improvise lyrics that comment on their own
farewells to each other:
Chunhua: Sending Brother Liang off to the door, I hold your hand, not
letting you go. [Pulling Yuehong into a kneeling position side by side with her.] Don’t
you remember that we swore on the ancient theater that we would never
separate?
Yuehong: Not to separate, we still have to separate; you and I are not des-
tined to be together for all our lives. [Turning to face Chunhua and raising her hand
to wipe Chunhua’s tear, but Chunhua turns her face away in protest.] Not letting go, I
still have to go; there is never such a thing as sending off your Brother Liang
to the door.
Chorus [from the side]: It is impossible to tell if this is about the passion in
the play or the passion between the sisters, and there is no way to keep the
person who shares the same origin but turns out to belong to somebody
else!
The act ends with an emotional climax: Liang Shanbo (Yuehong) bids fare -
well to Zhu Yingtai (Chunhua) and runs offstage, and while Yingtai cries
out for Brother Liang, another voice calls out in a panic that Shang Shui-
hua has killed herself. Shocked by Shuihua’s fate and feeling desperate about
Yuehong’s future, Chunhua collapses.
The third drama within the drama occurs when Yuehong is committing
suicide after Manager Tang has accused her of being unchaste, locked her
in her bedroom, and convinced her that Chunhua has betrayed her. Only
after a desperate Yuehong has swallowed some poison does she receive a
secret message from Chunhua warning her about Tang’s trick and telling
her that she, Chunhua, is coming to rescue her. The message comes too
late, however, and a dying Yuehong sings “Shanbo at his last moments,”
interspersing her own lyrics about her longing for Sister Chunhua mixed
with the original lines expressing Shanbo’s longing for Yingtai:
Having no chance to sing with Sister Chunhua again, 
I play the dying Shanbo by myself one last time.
I request that my relatives bury me next to the ancient stage 
And erect two stone tablets inscribed
With Sister Chunhua’s name in red and Sister Yuehong’s in black.
204 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 226 ===

I cannot be with her again in this life, 
But I will deﬁnitely be her sister in my next life.
For audiences familiar with the classic story of the butterﬂy lovers, these
dramas within the drama—intertwining the beautiful love story of the het-
erosexual butterﬂy lovers, performed by two actresses, with a tale of same-
sex love between two sisters—enhance and intensify the play’s emotional
effects.
In the last act, Chunhua searches for Yuehong, who is nowhere to be
found in Shanghai. Sadly, Chunhua returns to Shaoxing to mourn Teacher
Xing on the ﬁfth anniversary of his death. Yuehong, who was rescued and
has been recovering in hiding, appears when she hears Chunhua’s prayers
for her, and the two sing a happy piece from “Eighteen-LiFarewell” on the
ancient stage of their hometown. The curtain falls at the end of the piece
with the local audience applauding the two sisters. The play is thus pri-
marily a story about the love between the sisters, the telling of which is
enhanced by references to the classic love story of the butterﬂy lovers. 
The play’s depiction of sisterly love has its roots in the tradition of the
same-sex culture of women’s Yue opera. This culture may be understood
along two lines: “homosexual relationships” (tongxing lian) and “sisterly love”
( jiemei qing). 
Homosexual relationships existed in the Yue opera circle, at least at the
level of gossip and the so-called mosquito tabloids ( wenzi xiaobao), cheap
irregular publications featuring scandalous rumors and chitchat. Numerous
allegations about homosexual relations surface to this day, and homosex-
ual love is reported to have been quite common among such new profes-
sional women as nurses, doctors, and schoolteachers in Shanghai in the
1930s and 1940s. Reliable written documentation is lacking, however, in
fan magazines, established tabloids such as Shaoxing Opera Dailyand Yue Opera
News, mainstream newspapers, and ofﬁcial documents. Homosexual rela-
tionships likely existed, not necessarily among actresses but rather between
actresses and unmarried independent professional women from the audi-
ence. The general attitude toward such relationships was one of uncertainty,
with neither strong support nor opposition. 
While homosexual love existed in the twilight zone between rumor and
actuality, sisterly love, or same-sex nonsexual relationships, was the main
form in the culture of women’s opera. Sisterhood ﬁrst developed through
the days of all-girls opera schools, when teenage girls slept under the same
The Opera as History    205

=== PDF PAGE 227 ===

roof, ate from the same wok, trained and suffered disciplinary beatings
together, and traveled and performed together. Sharing the same native-
place origin, training background, and aspirations for a better life, these young
actresses developed a strong sense of bonding after entering the metropo-
lis of Shanghai. They addressed one another as “older sister” (jie) or “younger
sister” (mei), and sworn sisterhood was common among Yue opera actresses.
Other than the most famous Yue opera ten sisters formed in 1947, there were
many sets of sisters in the Yue opera circle, and adoptive sisterhood was also
common between actresses and young women in the audience. The clos-
est relationships between actresses may have formed between  long-standing
stage partners, most well known among them being Yin Guifang and Zhu
Shuizhao in the 1940s and Xu Yulan and Wang Wenjuan in the late 1940s
through 1960s. Xu and Wang were perceived as very close to each other,
even though both were married and had children. 
Both nonsexual sisterly love and homosexual relationships were marginal
in society at large and subordinate to the dominant heterosexual relation-
ship. Yue opera actresses all wanted to get married and to marry well in
order to have good standing in society, and some did so at the expense of
sisterly love, as depicted in both the ﬁlm and the Yue opera Stage Sisters.
The relationship between Yin Guifang, a popular male impersonator, and
Zhu Shuizhao, a specialist in young female roles, tells much about sisterly
love crossed and destroyed by heterosexual relationships.
According to Yin Guifang’s recollections, she and Zhu Shuizhao ﬁrst
met and became friends when they performed together in Shenjiamen, Zhe-
jiang, in 1934. They performed together once again in 1937, after the out-
break of the War of Resistance against Japan. In Huangyan County, they
were persecuted by the police and conﬁned to the county jail for ﬁfty-three
days.
66 After being released from jail and before bidding each other fare -
well, the two became sworn sisters. Separately, each went to Shanghai, and
they once again became stage partners in 1940. During the ensuing years,
the two were close friends and well-established partners onstage. Together,
they joined in the New Yue Opera reforms, performed numerous popular
love dramas onstage, and participated in the Yue opera ten sisters’ fund-
raising performance in 1947 . Yin Guifang wrote about her complex feel-
ings toward Zhu Shuizhao: 
The director ordered me to be intimate with my lover—who, needless to say,
is my only long-term stage partner Zhu Shuizhao. We are lovers onstage,
and our feelings toward each other increase every day; as nine out of ten plays
206 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 228 ===

we perform are conventional love stories, we have come to forget that we are
actually love sisters. This practice has been very helpful for our acting on the
s t a g e ....  B u t  b e c ause of this, naturally we grow affectionate toward each
other. Zhu Shuizhao, my dear stage partner and lover, I am deeply in love
with you. I forgive any possible shortcoming in my only stage partner and
lover, so the sweet ambience on the stage would not be disturbed—for the
body of the actress is in the arm of the stage! Looking back, the masses appre-
ciated Yue opera because private feelings between men and women are always
dominant in every [Yue opera] play.
Many people, oh no, only those gossipers often intentionally create ridicu-
lous rumors as if to tease me. Am I angry? No—because I have decided to
treat these gossipers as characters in a play. Those who cannot tell the dif-
ference between onstage and offstage are just too nervous.
67
In this seemingly confusing passage, Yin made it clear to people who spec-
ulated about homosexual relations between herself and Zhu that the rela-
tionship was one of nonsexual sisterly love. She also explained to readers
that the director had demanded that she and Zhu become immersed in their
roles and portray authentic heterosexual love onstage, and that perform-
ing love dramas with Zhu resulted in the growing affection between them.
Obviously, the intense love dramas onstage combined with the intimate
relationship offstage led to public speculation about a homoerotic rela-
tionship between the two actresses. Notice that while Yin tried to clarify
the nonsexual nature of her relationship with Zhu, she confessed her deep
love for Zhu, a same-sex love enhanced by the heterosexual love they per-
formed onstage every day. 
In 1947, Yin was engaged to a male patron who later decided that he
really wanted to marry Zhu. Zhu married the man, and Yin remained sin-
gle all her life. The two sworn sisters never performed together again: Zhu
organized her own troupe and shifted her specialization to young male roles
because it was easier to ﬁnd a stage partner in young female roles, while
Yin Guifang’s career suffered from the lack of stable stage partners in young
female roles.
68 In 1954, Zhu and her company moved to Nanjing, and she
committed suicide when she was persecuted during the Cultural Revolu-
tion. Yin and her company moved to Fuzhou in 1958. She was tortured
and became partially paralyzed during the Cultural Revolution but was reha-
bilitated and returned to Shanghai in the 1980s. Yin passed away in 2000
at the age of eighty. As in Stage Sisters, the actresses’ intense sisterly love in
real life was enhanced by the heterosexual love they played onstage, which
The Opera as History    207

=== PDF PAGE 229 ===

was then destroyed by a heterosexual relationship. Unlike the story in Stage
Sisters, there was no obvious reconciliation and reunion for the two actresses,
aside from Yin’s fond memories of Zhu, published in her memoirs after the
Cultural Revolution.
69 Zhu Shuizhao, however, did not leave any words
in print about her relationship with Yin.
For most Yue opera actresses in Shanghai in the mid-twentieth century,
sisterhood was the primary relationship in their daily lives, and the strug-
gle for a better life, respectability, and status was the major concern. The
political struggle between the CCP and the Nationalists was in the back-
ground or, for many, simply irrelevant. For the creators of the Yue opera
Stage Sisters, sisterly love emerged naturally as the main theme after they
decided to depoliticize the original plot of the ﬁlm. The shift in focus to
sisterly love made the play truer both to Yue opera history and to the genre’s
artistic identity as a theater of love. As the composer Su Jinzou explained:
“From the very beginning, we made the decision to make the political tone
as light as possible. Everyone felt that the Yue opera Stage Sistersshould have
its own artistic characteristic and should emphasize sisterly love.”
70
Scriptwriter Xue Yunhuang hesitated before identifying sisterly love as the
theme, observing: “It was not the case that we set the main theme ﬁrst and
then developed the play around it. I started by thinking how to develop a
main thread and a few characters. I concentrated on sisterly love because
Yue opera was good at that.” It is revealing that, unlike the director of the
ﬁlm version, who wanted to make sure the ﬁlm’s main theme was under-
stood and properly expressed, the Yue opera producers focused on the
opera’s artistic characteristic, identiﬁed as the exploration of human emo-
tions, and the main theme then emerged naturally. As Xue Yunhuang noted:
“Everyone agreed that [Stage Sisters] was not a political play and should focus
on sisterly love, and that there was no need to develop a political thread. . . .
Moreover, the spectators would not be interested in politics. They would
want to see sisterly love.”
71 Su Jinzou similarly stated clearly that Yue opera’s
artistic characteristic is elaboration on human feelings, or yanqing.72
Neither Xue nor Su is in any sense a political radical or dissident. Both
are Party members, and Xue once served as vice president of the Shang-
hai Yue Opera House. They are mainstream personalities representing fairly
common views in the ﬁeld of local operas. Moreover, the creation of the
Yue opera Stage Sisters, as they and the two leading actresses emphasized,
was very much a collective effort. The scriptwriter, the score composer,
the director, and the leading actresses all actively participated in shaping
the ﬁnal product. Much was decided during rehearsals, rather than at the
208 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 230 ===

scriptwriter’s or director’s desk. “Usually, Yue opera is a synthesized art,
and everyone participates in the production,” Xue explained. “During the
rehearsal, if one person dislikes a detail and others do not disagree with
her or him, then the detail is gone.” He gave two examples of the troupe’s
improvisational approach. Xue had tried to incorporate the courtroom
scene, following Yuan Xuefen’s suggestion, but without success, because
no one in the rehearsal liked it. In contrast, everyone liked the drama
within the drama, and so he replaced the courtroom scene with Yuehong’s
suicide, making for a third drama within the drama. Moreover, the ﬁrst
version of the play more closely resembled the ﬁlm and retained such
details as Chunhua’s performance of The New Year’s Sacriﬁce, the attack with
lime powder, and Manager Tang’s accusation that Yuehong was the insti-
gator. Only after much feedback from both audiences and experts did
the team settle on the ﬁnal, 1999 version. Apparently, there was consensus
among Yue opera artists and audiences as to what would make a good Yue
opera play.
73
Xue and Su, therefore, spoke not only for the artistic characteristic of
the Yue opera Stage Sisters but also for the genre’s distinctive artistic iden-
tity as a theater of love stories. In the reform era, Yue opera artists were
able to reclaim the legitimacy of their theater’s concern with the private
and the everyday and refocus its productive energies on the love drama or,
more generally, the genre of yanqing.
The state’s new cultural policy endorsed the Yue opera artists’ approach
by showering awards on Stage Sisters. In 1998, the play was selected to par-
ticipate in the First Shanghai International Festival of Arts and was one of
three that won an Excellent Play award. At the end of 1999, the Shanghai
Yue Opera House sent the troupe to Beijing to participate in celebrations
commemorating the ﬁftieth anniversary of the founding of the People’s
Republic and to enter the competition for the Wenhua Awards sponsored
by the Ministry of Culture, the most prestigious ofﬁcial performing arts
awards in the nation. In 2000, Stage Sisters won the Ninth Wenhua Award
for New Plays, and the two leading actresses won the Wenhua Award for
Best Performance. The actresses also won the Plum Blossom Award for Chi-
nese opera from the National Association of Dramatists.
Yue Opera History Reconstructed
The Yue opera Stage Sistersnot only helped the genre claim an artistic posi-
tion for itself in the cultural landscape of the new era but also aided it in
The Opera as History    209

=== PDF PAGE 231 ===

distinguishing its history from the communist melodrama presented by
the 1965 ﬁlm and by Yuan Xuefen and her biographers. Both Su Jinzou and
Xue Yunhuang considered the current version to be closer to “historical
reality” (lishi zhenshi). To be sure, no one wanted to deny the facts of Yuan
Xuefen’s relationship with the CCP underground and left-wing intellectu-
als, but as Xue pointed out, “Yuan being attacked with a bag of dung was
her personal fate, not political struggle. It was not a struggle between the
two lines, and the class struggle interpretation does not accord with his-
torical reality.”
74 Xue and Su Jinzou both thought that the Party did not
have the level of inﬂuence on Yue opera’s development before 1949 that
the ﬁlm suggests; the play only softens this exaggeration while clarifying
other facts in Yue opera history. The new character Ni Tao, for example,
who replaces Brother Lin and Jiang Bo, is obviously a tribute to such his-
torical personalities as Nan Wei, Han Yi, and Xu Jin, who were crucial to
the Yue opera reforms of the 1940s. 
From the vantage point of the post-revolutionary era, Yue opera prac-
titioners were able to take a long view of their own history and place the
communist connection in perspective. The Yue opera Stage Sistersis, in this
sense, a story about Yue opera told by Yue opera people themselves. The
director’s notes, printed on the playbill, poetically describe this story: 
A pair of young sworn sisters leave their hometown in Shaoxing and come
to blaze new paths for life in Shanghai, a place where “even the streets were
paved with gold.” They take care of each other and share the hardships of
life—a story full of love and resentment, joy and sorrow, and partings and
u n i o n s  i s  u n f o l d i n g ....  
People say that the rivers and mountains of Shengzhou [Shengxian] have
spirit and are delicate, and that they have nurtured native daughters and a
native opera as beautiful as themselves. Coming from her native land, Yue
opera has overcome many hardships and absorbed many inﬂuences in the
metropolis, now trickling like a rivulet, now ﬂowing in waves....  
Tough times are hard, they sing year in, year out;
Homeless anywhere, yet they make their home everywhere. 
This is the song sung by the Yue opera people themselves—life is like a song!
75
This reading of Yue opera history emphasizes its native rural origin and
the long, continuous process of urban migration and transformation, car-
ried out generation after generation by native daughters. It is, in this sense,
210 The Opera as History

=== PDF PAGE 232 ===

an ethnographic view of Yue opera history as a way of life maintained by
a group of people, as well as an expression of their native culture, negoti-
ated within the dynamics of a cosmopolitan urban society. As long as the
dynamic tension between the Zhejiang countryside and Shanghai the great
metropolis persists, Yue opera as a way of life will continue.
Yue opera was indeed an art developed by Zhejiang immigrants in Shang-
hai in the last century, and it still is today. From 1949 until the early 1980s,
immigrant actresses such as Yuan Xuefen, Fan Ruijuan, Fu Quanxiang, Xu
Yulan, and Wang Wenjuan dominated Yue opera. During this period, when
the state prevented urban in-migration in order to control urban popula-
tion growth, the locally trained younger Yue opera performers were largely
overshadowed by their more successful seniors. The reinstitution of a mar-
ket economy beginning in the 1980s has resulted in a new wave of urban
migration, and Shanghai has once again become a popular destination. Sig-
niﬁcantly, new generations of immigrant Yue opera performers contributed
much to the revival and maintenance of Yue opera in Shanghai after the dis-
ruptions of the Cultural Revolution. The last two decades of the twentieth
century witnessed the rise of a new generation of Yue opera stars who were
recent immigrants from Zhejiang and surrounding areas. The majority of
the younger generation of ranking performing artists in the Shanghai Yue
Opera House—including Zhao Zhigang, Fang Yafen, Wang Zhiping,
Zheng Guofeng, Zhang Ruihong, and Zhang Hailing—were from Zhejiang
and surrounding areas. The two stars of Stage Sisters, Qian Huili and Shan
Yangping, were from Zhuji and Jiande, Zhejiang, respectively. Youngsters
from Zhejiang composed the majority of Yue opera students in the Shang-
hai Native Opera School (Shanghai Xiqu Xuexiao) and were on their way
to becoming the next generation of Yue opera stars in Shanghai.
While the pattern of urban migration and expression of native culture
has remained a basis for Yue opera’s development in Shanghai, the terms
and conditions of that migration and cultural expression are very differ-
ent from before. The generations of Yue opera performers that have grown
up in the PRC period have had a much smoother experience than their
predecessors did, thanks to the struggles and ground-breaking achieve-
ments of these predecessors and to changes in modern Chinese society
since the early twentieth century. In the past, most entertainers came from
poor families who chose their low-status professions only to make a liv-
ing. Nowadays, entertainers in China are accepted members of society,
and stars even enjoy unusually high status, as public idols and cultural
celebrities, much like entertainers elsewhere in the world. Moreover, female
The Opera as History    211

=== PDF PAGE 233 ===

entertainers now enjoy the same privileges as their male counterparts, as
social celebrities and art professionals. The stigma that used to be attached
to actresses is a reminder of how much the gender norm has changed in
the past century. The Chinese state also grants many popular stars an elite
political status; the young Yue opera star Qian Huili, for example, is a mem-
ber of the Shanghai Political Consultative Conference and was appointed
vice president of the Shanghai Yue Opera House in March 2003. Although
Qian Huili, like Xing Yuehong, migrated from Zhuji to Shanghai, she
asserted that she does not personally identify with the character. “Not at
all. I have never had any hardship, as Xing Yuehong does. I have always
been cheered, protected, and supported by everyone. My career has been
smooth sailing all along.”
76 By “everyone,” she meant the government and
the leadership of the Shanghai Yue Opera House as well as her colleagues.
Shan Yangping, who plays Zhu Chunhua in Stage Sisters , however, had a
somewhat different answer to the question: “Xing Yuehong is a prototype
of immigrant actresses. Although the present-day society is perhaps dif-
ferent from before Liberation, the allure of the great metropolis is the same.
America is also like that—very tempting. There are very few actually like
Chunhua, and I cannot say that I stand on such a high moral ground as
she does.”
77 Indeed, the allure of a better life has always driven genera-
tions of Zhejiang’s native daughters to other countries, but the desire for
self-expression has been equally strong. Thus, during the difﬁcult years
of the 1980s and 1990s, some promising actresses, like Xing Yuehong, gave
up their careers and moved to the United States and Japan. Many stayed,
however, and worked to sustain Yue opera in Shanghai while making names
for themselves, and some have returned from abroad to resume their act-
ing careers.
78
The new generations of Yue opera performers found employment in state-
owned institutions stable and secure, although it did not necessarily make
them rich. Under the current system, a considerable portion of the  govern -
ment’s budget for culture is devoted to preserving and developing native
operas as treasures of traditional folk arts, which are seen as endangered
by a globalized mass popular culture. City and district governments under-
write the Shanghai Yue Opera House and a few district Yue opera compa-
nies, guaranteeing artists and administrators monthly salaries plus health
and retirement beneﬁts. This system provides baseline security to employ-
ees of these companies, and the diverse cultural market offers artists oppor-
tunities to earn extra income through various channels of production, to
help support their companies and meet personal expenses. Shan Yangping,
212 The Opera as History

for example, starred in a Yue opera television drama in 1991, ﬁnanced jointly
by Shanghai Television and China Central Television, and the ﬁrst Yue opera
karaoke video compact disc (VCD), “Song from the Heart” (Xinqu), in 1995,
sponsored by Eastern Television.
79 Wang Zhiping, another noted young
actress, with the backing of her home institution, the Shanghai Yue Opera
House, invested her own money and collected private funds for the pro-

80 Moreover, the
government also allows private, independent studios for native operas. The

ple, began her private studio in June 2002 and has already staged a num-

Looking back at the end of the twentieth century, Yuan Xuefen and other
senior Yue opera artists, now enjoying their old age, cannot help but feel-
ing nostalgic about the good old days of Yue opera (see ﬁg. 5.4) The younger
generations, however, seem less sentimental and sometimes appear, in the
eyes of their seniors, to be too content with the current situation. They,
too, certainly would like to see Yue opera become more popular, but it is
not entirely within their power to make this happen. Every era has its own
popular culture, and as a social and cultural phenomenon, Yue opera
belonged to the twentieth century. The opera will continue to exist as part
of China’s cultural tradition, as a representative genre of native opera, but
it no longer represents signiﬁcant social movements of the time.
