215
Chapter 6
A Feminine Aesthetics
Produced in 1958 and ﬁlmed in 1962, the Yue opera rendition of the
 eighteenth-century novel Dream of the Red Chamber was perhaps the greatest
and most popular Chinese love story of the twentieth century. It is impos-
sible to assess the impact of women’s Yue opera in shaping a modern cul-
ture of love in twentieth-century Chinese popular culture without talking
about the play. An incident in Shanghai in the early 1970s vividly illus-
trates the play’s popular inﬂuence. A young man was running to catch a
bus, but the bus conductor, a young woman, closed the door, and the bus
pulled away without him. The young man cried out jokingly to the con-
ductor, parodying a line from the popular Yue opera play: “Cousin Lin, I
have come too late!” Bystanders all smiled, and the conductor blushed with
embarrassment. The words the young man spoke are from a line delivered
by the heartbroken Jia Baoyu when he arrives at his beloved Lin Daiyu’s
chamber shortly after her tragic death, and everyone on the street knew
the scene by heart. That the Yue opera play was still very much alive in
people’s memories during the Cultural Revolution, even though the radi-
cal state leadership banned it, suggests just how popular the play must have
been before the Cultural Revolution. 
In August 1999, the Shanghai Grand Theater staged a new production
of the play, starring Qian Huili and Shan Yangping, which turned out to
be a rare commercial success at a time when the genre had long passed its
golden age and native operas all faced declining popularity. Subsequent tours
of the play in Beijing in 1999 and 2001 were equally successful. Again, in
April 2004, the Shanghai Yue Opera House sent the play to tour several cities
in the northwest and southwest, including Xi’an, Lanzhou, Xining, Chengdu,


=== PDF PAGE 237 ===

and Chongqing. Local audiences received the play with great enthusiasm,
and many viewers sang along with the actresses during the performance.1
The play’s lasting popularity is a clear indication of the centrality of the
love theme in modern China’s popular culture and the strong inﬂuence of
women’s Yue opera in shaping the modern culture of love. 
The Poetics of Yue Opera: T oward a Feminine Aesthetics
Women’s entrance into the entertainment market as both actors and audi-
ence resulted in the feminization of opera content and style as well as of
the operatic body. While major changes occurred nationwide, many devel-
oped ﬁrst and most intensely in Shanghai, where modern Western ideas,
traditional Chinese aesthetics, and the quickly forming metropolitan tastes
of urban residents mingled, calling for new-style theaters. Every theatri-
cal form in Shanghai answered this call in its own way and experimented
with a variety of new strategies. Special effects and machine-operated stage
props, ﬁrst adopted by Beijing opera, new drama, and other local operas in
Shanghai, created visual spectacles that catered to the tastes of a develop -
ing, curious metropolitan audience. An emphasis on visual effects and emo-
tive acting, in contrast to the traditional emphasis on singing and restricted
acting within a spare stage setting, became trademarks of Shanghai-style
Beijing opera.
2 Shanghai opera responded to the need for theatrical reforms
with modern-attire plays, in which actors and actresses appeared onstage
in Western suits and cheongsams, with Western-style hairdos and leather
shoes (the women in high heels), and performed amid modern living-room
settings. The intellectuals’ spoken drama, by contrast, rejected the popu-
lar taste for pure spectacle and adhered to its own ideas of social and the-
atrical reform modeled on Western drama. 
Women’s Yue opera emerged amid a variety of evolving theatrical forms
as one of the most successful new theaters in the 1940s through the 1960s.
Striking a balance between a naturalistic acting style and the modern love
drama, and enhanced further by a feminized theatrical spectacle, women’s
Yue opera created a modern poetics of human emotions that helped set trends
for theatrical renewal in general. Yue opera’s feminized poetics—evident
in the way it developed its music, stage design, singing, and acting style
so as to best elaborate a feminized narrative of qing, or feelings—provoked
a unique and intense sensibility in the modern urban audience, not only
among fans but also among those who did not care much for Yue opera’s
love drama. Many middle-aged and older Chinese men who are not Yue
216 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 238 ===

opera fans have claimed that they like Yue opera because it is beautiful. For
many from the north, the Yue opera ﬁlm The Butterﬂy Lovers(1953) or Dream
of the Red Chamber (1962) might be the only Yue opera play they have seen
in its entirety. While many admitted that they had little patience for Yue
opera’s scholar-beauty cliché in general, they emphasized that Yue opera
music and singing “please the ears” (hao ting) and its costumes and stage set-
tings “please the eyes” (hao kan).
3
Yue opera fans appreciated the opera for its feminine poetics and the
love drama it elaborates. Fans of various ages give basically one reason for
their appreciation of Yue opera: Yue opera is beautiful. Wu Ping, a lifetime
Yue opera fan who began her association with the opera as a preteen in the
1940s, recalled that she was drawn to Yue opera by its beautiful actresses,
costumes, and singing as well as its love stories.
4 A group of middle-aged
women, the next generation of Yue opera fans, also emphasized Yue opera’s
beauty and its elaboration of human feelings. Li Jiang, who was born and
raised in Beijing, was passionate about Yue opera, even though she was a
northerner. She said repeatedly that Yue opera is “so beautiful”—the stage
scenery, the costumes, the actresses, the singing and acting styles, and the
music—all “so gentle and incredibly beautiful.” Li Jiang’s ﬁrst encounter
with Yue opera was in 1958 or 1959, when Fan Ruijuan and Fu Quanxiang
performed The Butterﬂy Lovers in Beijing. At the time, she was impressed by
the beautiful actresses and stage set but was too young to appreciate the
art. Her real introduction to Yue opera occurred in 1962 through a cluster
of Yue opera ﬁlms, including Dream of the Red Chamber and Emerald Hairpin. Li
used the word yangpai(foreign style), which Wu Ping also used, as opposed
to tuqi (rustic) to describe Yue opera aesthetics. She explained that Beijing
opera is too stiff and tough, and ping opera a bit too rustic and also a little
tough for her taste, even though these operas are from her northern home-
land. “I enjoy only Yue opera,” she said.
5 Fans also indicated that they appre-
ciated Yue opera’s dramatization of qing, especially “human feelings” (renqing)
and “affection,” “passion,” or “sentiment” (ganqing). The comments of these
fans echo the remark of the popular Yue opera star Yin Guifang, who said
in 1947 that “love and private feelings” ( er nü si qing) would always be the
center of Yue opera plays, because the audience appreciated it.
6
The vocabulary Yue opera fans used to express their appreciation for the
opera resonates with that used by Yue opera scholars and the performers
themselves. Yue opera scholars and artists have consistently described Yue
opera aesthetics as beauty (mei) that is soft, gentle, ﬂuid, and ﬂexible (rou).
The word rou is often used with yin (feminine), as opposed to yang(mascu-
A Feminine Aesthetics    217

=== PDF PAGE 239 ===

line). The term yin rou(feminine and soft) deﬁnes Yue opera’s particular kind
of beauty, and its antonym, yang gang(masculine and tough) often describes
the kind of beauty Beijing opera embodies. Indeed, soft feminine beauty has
characterized the idiosyncratic Yue opera aesthetics since the 1940s. 
Yue opera singing and character modeling are perhaps the two elements
that contribute most to its softness. About Yue opera singing and music,
the Yue opera director Huang Sha noted: “The softness of the tunes and
melodies is a distinctive feature of Yue opera as a genre. It is the main ele-
ment that contributes to the formation of Yue opera’s sentimental poetic
aesthetics.”
7 Yue opera music achieved its softness by doing away with the
deafening gongs and drums commonly used in Qing major operas and build-
ing on the pleasing folk melodies of the Jiangnan region where it had orig-
inated. Actresses sing with their natural voices, and their singing is like a
natural extension of their speech; its purpose is not to display elaborative
vocal techniques but to express a character’s complicated emotions. Com-
pared to vocals in Beijing opera and Italian opera, both of which involve
complicated techniques and special training, Yue opera singing appears
almost effortless and, accompanied by traditionalsizhu (lit., “silky string and
bamboo”) instruments such as the bamboo ﬂute and the huqin, a two-string
instrument played with a bow, moves listeners with its unassuming inti-
macy (see ﬁg. 6.1). The speech and singing use mostly Shengxian Man-
darin (Shengxian guanhua), in which vowel sounds are close to those of
standard Chinese (making it understandable for northern people as well)
and consonant tones are softer. In the early stage of women’s Yue opera,
actresses mainly used the sigongscale system, in which the huqin’s two strings
are treated as the sixth and third notes of the scale (somewhat similar to
the Western system’s A major) and deﬁnes the primary key area; this pro-
duces a lively, blissful tone but lacks a layer in the lower-note range. Dur-
ing the November 1943 premiere of The Fragrant Princess (Xiangfei), Yuan
Xuefen and her accompanist Zhou Baocai created a chai scale system, or
chai diao, in improvisation. Compared to the sigong system, the primary key
area of thechai scale system is where the huqin strings are treated as the ﬁfth
and second notes of the scale (somewhat similar to the Western system’s
G major); this system has a lower-note area that enables the actress to elab-
orate on more dramatic changes in the character’s emotions and sentiments.
8
Since then, the chai scale system has developed into a rich resource for Yue
opera music and singing that are uniquely expressive of sentimental feel-
ings. According to Yue opera musicologist Lian Bo: “The chai scale system
took shape in Japanese-occupied Shanghai in the early 1940s. It mainly
218 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 240 ===

expresses the sad and plaintive feelings prevalent in a troubled time. The
chai scale system, however, is also lovely, graceful, and charming, typical
of Jiangnan folk tunes nurtured by the region’s natural environment and
cultural traditions.”
9
As it carries the lyrics, narrative, and emotions, singing is the soul of every
successful Yue opera play of lasting inﬂuence. A 1983 statistic from the Chi-
nese Record Company, which had a monopoly on the record industry until
cassette tapes became popular in the late 1980s, attests to the importance
of Yue opera music to the genre’s popular identity: the Yue opera Dream of
the Red Chamberwas the best-selling record, with more than three million copies
sold, and the Yue opera The Butterﬂy Loverswas the third-best seller.
10
The creation of modern female images in Yue opera was another key
element in deﬁning the genre’s feminine aesthetics. Traditional operatic
images of women had been designed and deﬁned by men in Beijing opera
and other Qing major operas according to an orthodox gender system that
prioritized women’s virtue over their physical beauty. On the opposite side
of this moralistic orthodoxy was a public culture of erotica that objectiﬁed
A Feminine Aesthetics    219
6.1  Yue opera musical instruments. (Shanghai Yueju zhi 1997)

=== PDF PAGE 241 ===

women’s bodies as sexual parts, as seen in numerous minor-opera produc-
tions in the late Qing and early Republican periods. These were two man-
ifestations of the same misogynist system that subordinated women to men.
The situation began to change during the Republican period, as actresses
and female patrons became increasingly important. Minor operas had to
cleanse themselves of their misogynist erotic performances, and major
operas such as Beijing opera also began to reformulate their female images.
Beijing opera critics have long celebrated the female images created by the
four great female impersonators, such as Mei Lanfang’s aristocratic beau-
ties, Cheng Yanqiu’s suffering but virtuous women, Shang Xiaoyun’s right-
eous militant heroines, and Xun Huisheng’s intelligent and lively maids.
These female images, however, were created by men and more or less
reﬂected men’s ideas of women, and they never succeeded in attracting a
female audience, as attested by the predominance of men in Beijing opera
audiences throughout the Republican period. 
Women’s Yue opera set a new standard for operatic images of women.
The female images in Yue opera embodied not only the new era’s idea of
autonomous women but also a modern urban fascination with female phys-
ical beauty, offering a variety of lively female characters enhanced by del-
icate ornamentations. Popular appreciation of decorated female physical
beauty was especially well developed in Shanghai, which was located in a
region renowned for producing beautiful women and proud of being called
“the Paris of the Orient” in recognition of its leading position in China’s
fashion industry. Much talk within the Yue opera trade and among female
spectators concerned ways of enhancing the actresses’ physical beauty
onstage.
11 Women’s Yue opera used soft, fresh synthetic colors instead of
the strong, dark primary colors that dominated Qing major operas. It also
led the way in character modeling, using Western-style techniques such as
oil- instead of water-based makeup to enhance the actresses’ feminine
beauty. Women’s Yue opera was among the ﬁrst to use silk extensively for
its stage costumes. Silk was softer than cotton and came in more delicate
colors. The Jiangnan-Shanghai area was the center of China’s silk produc -
tion, and Shengxian natives composed a large portion of the workforce in
Shanghai’s silk industry. Being expensive and delicate, silk costumes
demanded great expertise in their care, and this expertise was more easily
found among Zhejiang natives than among other native-place groups. Most
stars could afford to have experts tend to their costumes, but since ordi-
nary Yue opera actresses knew how to care for their own costumes, the use
of silk was more common in Yue opera than in other operas.
12
220 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 242 ===

As women patrons competed with one another in costuming their favorite
actresses, and actresses competed in creating enchanting female images,
Yue opera directors were pressured to provide innovative stage designs that
would set off the fresh female images.
13 New designs for stage sets, lights,
and props in pinkish and greenish shades along with silk costumes evoked
feelings of warmth, gentleness, and ﬂuidity. This stage effect worked in
concert with the actresses’ naturalistic acting style to create a new aesthetic
of theatrical presentation—a feminine representation of the sentiments of
love drama. 
Yue opera’s new female imagery quickly won the hearts of a large audi-
ence, especially women. The Yue opera fan Wu Ping loved seeing actresses
in beautiful costumes and enjoyed the eye-pleasing stage scenery. At the
time, she recalled, acting skills were not as sophisticated as in later days,
and audiences paid more attention to the overall appearance of the stage
elements—the scene designs, the costumes, and the hairstyles. She espe-
cially enjoyed the performances of two actresses, Qi Yaxian and Zhu
Shuizhao. Qi, said Wu Ping, was good at playing young tragic female roles
and sang her lyrics very clearly, making it easier for the audience to fol-
low the plot. Wu Ping acknowledged: “Tragedies were my favorite kind of
story. Because I loved Qi Yaxian, I went to see her performances all the
time. I followed her performance no matter what new play she put on the
stage.” She also idolized Zhu Shuizhao because Zhu had such a beautiful
appearance and was a good performer. “Zhu Shuizhao was the [commonly
acknowledged] queen of Yue opera at that time!” Wu emphasized, “She was
very beautiful.”
14 It is apparent that Wu Ping embraced Yue opera as a whole:
for its visual presentation, its acting styles, and its sentimental love stories.
Cheng Rong, another early Yue opera fan who later joined Yuan Xuefen’s
company, was drawn to the opera partly because its imagery of ancient
women was so convincing to her—she had been collecting cigarette boxes
with depictions of ancient beauties and felt as if these ﬁgures had come to
life before her very eyes on the Yue opera stage.
15
Wu Ping admitted that plays about peasant lives did not interest her.
She considered them too rustic, and only well-composed stories with
gorgeous stage sets appealed to her. For this reason, she was also attracted
to Shanghai opera’s modern-attire productions. She did not like Beijing
opera, however, in part because it was difﬁcult for her to follow the north-
ern dialect and because of its emphasis on acrobatic ﬁghting. But no less
important, she deemed the characters “ugly” ( nankan) or “not good-look-
ing” (bu haokan). Her complaint: “The young female roles in Beijing opera
A Feminine Aesthetics    221

=== PDF PAGE 243 ===

wore dark-colored, old-fashioned clothes and sported depressing hairstyles
and makeup.”16 Yue opera was totally different. Almost forty years later,
the Yue opera’s ten sisters’ grand fund-raising performance of For the Love of
the Country, in 1947, was vivid in her mind. Wu Ping recounted in consid-
erable detail the plots and characters, and who played which roles. She
recalled the spectacle with relish, with all the popular stars appearing onstage
together in their best costumes. Like many Shanghai women then and now,
Wu Ping’s tastes may be summed up in two opposing terms: yangpai, mean-
ing “foreign style” with the implication of being modern and urban, and
tuqi, or “rustic,” which suggests backward and provincial. Not surprisingly,
she used the term yangpai for Yue opera and the term tuqifor Beijing opera.
17
The next generation of Yue opera fans, such as Li Jiang and her friends,
expressed similar views. 
Yue opera’s aesthetics began to gain nationwide inﬂuence during the
early years of the People’s Republic. At a 1952 national opera conference,
about two dozen local operas, including Beijing opera, ping opera, and
Shanghai opera, presented as many as one hundred of their best produc-
tions.
18 Yue opera playwright Cheng Rong, who attended the conference,
recalled: “All the local operas had almost the same appearance. They all
looked like one another, as they all imitated Beijing opera. You had no
idea what opera it was until the actors opened their mouths [to sing or
speak]. [But] when Yue opera went onstage, you saw the stage suddenly
illuminated. It was so fresh and so beautiful. The stage design, hairstyles,
and costumes were all so different and nice looking.”
19 Despite Cheng’s
clear partiality with regard to her own opera, her comments contain an
important element of truth. Beijing opera, by virtue of its status as the
opera of the capital and its historical inﬂuence, served as a model for
numerous local operas. Beginning with the 1952 opera conference, how-
ever, Yue opera’s fresh style and feminine aesthetics began to make a nation-
wide impact on other local operas, through many national and regional
opera conferences, numerous nationwide tours, and, most important per-
haps, ﬁlms of such masterpieces as The Butterﬂy Lovers and Dream of the Red
Chamber. Many local operas, especially minor operas, learned from Yue
opera’s stage design and character modeling and enhanced their own
production values.Within a few decades, local operas began to look some-
what similar again, and audiences could not readily distinguish one opera
from another until the actors spoke or sang—all local operas resembled
Yue opera, with soft, ﬂuid costumes, refreshing stage colors, and delicately
ornamented characters. 
222 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 244 ===

Male Impersonators: Women’s Ideal Men
The “young male impersonator” (nü xiaosheng) arguably made the most impor-
tant contribution to the femininity of Yue opera aesthetics. The opera the-
orist Gong Hede suggested:
Yue opera is a soft and beautiful feminine opera . . .  that is performed by
women and is good at telling women’s stories. It is women’s favorite opera.
The key to the establishment of this feminine opera is the young male imper-
sonator. The standard male impersonator should be handsome, delicate, and
bookish and have a natural grace and elegant bearing....  T h e  f a c t  t h a t  t h e
young male role is played by women determines that Yue opera’s main theme
is the love story and soft beauty is its main style.
20
Contrary to Gong’s claim, the male impersonator did not “determine” the
love story as the main theme of Yue opera; historically, as discussed in pre-
vious chapters, it was the love drama that facilitated the rise of women’s
Yue opera. There is no doubt, however, that the young male impersonator
has shaped the distinctive feminine aesthetics that made Yue opera the mas-
ter theater of love drama. More speciﬁcally, young male impersonators in
Yue opera embody women’s ideal men—elegant, graceful, capable, caring,
gentle, and loyal. 
The male impersonator began to take leading roles in women’s Yue opera
in the late 1940s and soon thereafter became the biggest attraction for Yue
opera fans. Li Huikang, a retired Yue opera playwright and researcher at
the Shanghai Institute for Research in Arts, considered the rise of the young
male character to be an indication of the maturity of women’s Yue opera,
21
but Lu Xun’s comments on the gendered spectatorship of Mei Lanfang’s
performances are perhaps more useful for understanding the Yue opera case.
Men and women, Lu Xun noted, saw different things in Mei’s performance:
men observing the women created by men, and women observing men play-
ing women. Similarly, in Yue opera, it may be said that women observed
the men created by actresses, while men watched women performing men.
The theory remains simple: just as Mei Lanfang and his cohorts created
women more or less from a male perspective and on the male body, so Yue
opera’s male impersonators created men more or less from a female per-
spective and on the female body. 
Yin Guifang, as Li Huikang pointed out, was, at the end of 1946, per-
haps the ﬁrst performer to establish the young male role as a lead in Yue
A Feminine Aesthetics    223

=== PDF PAGE 245 ===

opera, having just founded the Fanghua Company. Yin was an excellent
actress who successfully created numerous well-known representations of
men, including Prince Luolan in Desert Prince, Jia Baoyu in various theatri-
cal adaptations of Dream of the Red Chamber , and Jin Yuqing in The Prodigal
Son (Langdangzi) of April 1947, with script by Xu Jin and directed by Yan
Jin (see ﬁgs. 6.2–6.4). Yet, while Yin created women’s ideal men in Prince
Luolan and Jia Baoyu, she also staged a criticism of the kind of men who
disappoint women. In the modern-attire playThe Prodigal Son, for example,
the young schoolteacher Jin Yuqing meets and falls in love with Li Ping.
The two get married and establish a nice family. Yuqing unfortunately begins
to spend time with a playboy and ends up falling into traps of the urban
underworld. He becomes involved with a prostitute, Yang Liusi, and
spends days and nights in her brothel, which also features a gambling hall,
a bar patronized by alcoholics, and an opium den. Yuqing eventually runs
out of money and is thrown out of the brothel. Meanwhile, Li Ping has
had to take work as a domestic servant in order to provide for her father-
in-law and daughter Xiaoqing. One day, their house catches ﬁre and burns
down. That night, an exhausted Yuqing ﬁnally comes home, only to see
Xiaoqing crying alone amid the ruins of the house. He takes Xiaoqing to
an abandoned hut and tries to make a living by begging and by gleaning
and reselling scrap. Then the daughter falls ill, and Yuqing has no money
to buy her medication. Desperate, he steals a small amount of cash from a
woman outside a pawnshop, but the woman turns out to be his wife, who
has just pawned her coat to buy medicine for his father. Yuqing is greatly
ashamed. As he walks along the Huangpu River, Yuqing decides that he
can no longer bear to recall what he has done. Just as he is about to jump
into the river, he encounters an old friend, Zhu Fuhua, and Zhu helps Yuqing
get back on the right track. The production hit the theater world with a
bang and ran two shows every day for a full month. The last song Yuqing
sings on the bank of the Huangpu River became a popular piece. 
The play spoke a truth for women, that men’s selﬁsh and irresponsible
behavior was the direct cause of many broken families and destitute
women. While echoing government propaganda against drug abuse and
gambling, Yin’s play put forth a gendered critique of the situation faced
by many struggling Shanghai families. The ending in particular reﬂects
women’s views, as Jin Yuqing repents of his past wrongdoings and comes
back to restore his family. For most middle-class women at the time, divorce
was debilitating because women still lacked independent social status out-
side of marriage as well as the means to achieve ﬁnancial independence;
224 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 246 ===

even for working-class women, who earned their own wages, divorce was
the last resort in dealing with an alcoholic or gambling-addicted husband.
The best women could hope for when their husbands abused drugs or took
up gambling, therefore, was that their men would one day ﬁnd help, repent,
and come back to them. 
Xu Yulan was another inﬂuential male impersonator. Her debut as a young
male impersonator was in the 1947 comedy It’s My Fault(Shi wo cuo), with
a script by Tao Xian and directed by Shi Lei, about two childhood sweet-
hearts, Zhao Wenjun and Li Yuzhen. The parents of both families agree
on a marriage contract, but Wenjun later changes his mind, when he decides
that Yuzhen is not an adequate match and wants a daughter from a wealth-
ier family. His mother, however, cannot wait for the marriage to take place
and for grandsons to be born. On the wedding night, Wenjun says to Yuzhen
that they should be like brother and sister, rather than husband and wife.
Li Yuzhen then suffers considerably from her husband’s coldness and her
mother-in-law’s complaints about her failure to produce a grandson. Dur-
ing the civil-service examination year, Wenjun leaves home for the capi-
tal and earns a jinshi degree. Meanwhile, Yuzhen, who can no longer bear
her mother-in-law’s ill treatment, decides to end her life. At the moment
when she is about to jump into the river, Yuzhen unexpectedly encounters
her long-lost birth father, who takes her to the capital city. On the night
of the moon festival, Wenjun hears a woman’s voice chanting poems. When
he climbs up the wall to take a peek, he discovers that it is a beautiful young
lady; he instantly feels respect for her and falls in love with her. He sends
a go-between to the young lady to arrange a conference with her, and to
his great embarrassment, the lady he meets is none other than Li Yuzhen,
his wife. Overcome with regret, Wenjun kneels down before Yuzhen, apol-
ogizes, and asks for her forgiveness. The play, ending with their happy
reunion, reﬂects at once women’s criticism of men’s disloyalty and their
readiness to forgive the men when they return home. The song Zhao Wen-
jun sings when he apologizes to Li Yuzhen, “It’s My Fault,” which became
quite famous, praises Li’s, and hence women’s, virtue while criticizing and
making fun of his own mistakes with humorous lyrics.
22
In their long careers as young male impersonators, Yin Guifang and Xu
Yulan created many women’s men on the Yue opera stage and attracted thou-
sands of female fans. Fans developed crushes on their favorite actresses and
sometimes confused their love for the actresses with their feelings for the
characters their favorite actresses created. They wrote hundreds of letters
to their idols, some of which found their way to tabloid newspapers, while

In the mid-1950s, the Fanghua Company staged Baoyu and Daiyu(Baoyu
yu Daiyu), starring Yin Guifang. The play created a sensation everywhere
it went, and Yin Guifang’s Jia Baoyu was a big hit. In 1959, when Yin Guifang
and the Fanghua Company were leaving Shanghai and relocating to
Fuzhou, Fujian, thousands of her fans packed the Shanghai railway station
to see her off. Many were in tears, and as the train was about to depart,
people jumped onto the tracks to form human barriers and blocked the
train for some time.
25 Some fans missed her (or perhaps the Jia Baoyu she
created) so much that they took trains to Fuzhou just to see her and her
performance in Baoyu and Daiyu.
26
During the opening run, in 1958, of the Shanghai Yue Opera House’s
Dream of the Red Chamber , in which Xu Yulan played the affectionate Jia
Baoyu, fans called out for her every night outside the backdoor of her
house: “Jia Baoyu, Xu Yulan, come out. Please let us see you, please.”
27 Later,
after the premiere of the 1962 ﬁlm version, Xu Yulan became a nationwide
popular heroine whose appeal went beyond the women who were her fans
and reached out to men. “It was said,” Xu recalled, “that the ﬁlm was usually
only shown at county-level cities. But for some reason, my hometown Xin-

the CCP Central Committee approved the screening after Xu Yulan wrote

Vietnamese War. The young soldiers were overjoyed to see Jia Baoyu with
their own eyes. They were not alone in their excitement; a local girl threw
herself into Xu’s arms upon recognizing the actress’s Jia Baoyu.
29
Explaining her interpretation of the character Jia Baoyu, Xu wrote: “Jia
Baoyu’s love for Lin Daiyu contains his respect for women, his pursuit of
the ideal, and his encompassing concerns for his lover’s well-being. . . .
He is indeed an elegant, graceful, and talented young master above the
masses.”
30 Such a young man became an ideal type not only for women
but also for men desiring to ﬁnd true love in their lives. 
One important function that Yue opera’s male impersonators perform is
to make young female roles even more feminine. The typical lovers on a
Yue opera stage, both male and female, are beautiful, delicate, and grace-
ful, and their speech and singing are soft and sentimental. If Yue opera male

impersonators feminized the male role, then the young female roles are

an incarnation of a celestial, possesses unearthly frail beauty. When she sobs
in the Grand View Garden, the scene and the sound are so sad that birds
and ﬂowers cannot bear it—birds ﬂy away and ﬂowers fall like snow.
31 When
Wang Wenjuan shed tears onstage or on-screen, thousands of viewers could
not stop themselves from crying along with her. There is no way to quan-
tify how many tears the Yue opera Dream of the Red Chamberhas earned from

Wenjuan’s body has since set the standard for later theatrical and ﬁlmic re-
creations of this popular ﬁctional beauty. 
Feminine Cast, Feminine Opera
But how “feminine” could women’s Yue opera be if the company and the-
ater owners, directors, and playwrights were mostly men? The answer lies
in the ways in which performance art is materialized on the performer’s
body. Generally speaking, any theatrical production is as the performer
presents it onstage at that particular moment. On a more particular level,
actor-centered performance was characteristic of all native Chinese operas.
In the early period of women’s Yue opera, for example, most actresses were
illiterate and there was no director or playwright. For the actress, prepa-
ration for a performance involved learning the rough story line and her
part and memorizing some basic lines. Most of the performance was impro-
vised onstage, through spontaneous interactions with stage partners cou-
pled with an awareness of the audience’s reactions. In the old “improvised
performance” (lutou xi ) system, common in local operas, actors were free
to perform anything their way as long as the audience liked it. Talented
actors could carry an audience for a long time with their singing and act-
ing without exhausting the lyrics and thus dominated the stage, while less
experienced or less talented actors often ran out of lines and devices quickly
and could not keep themselves onstage long enough to make an impres-
sion on the audience. 
The role of the actor was so important that even when modern theater
professionals entered Yue opera troupes in the 1940s, the actor-centered
system of performance did not change. Women’s Yue opera continued to
230 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 252 ===

grow around a constellation of superstars in the 1950s and early 1960s,
each of whom developed her personal style of singing and acting through
long-term improvisational experience and attracted her own set of loyal
fans. Even though the authority of the director and playwright has become
more ﬁrmly established in the past few decades, it has not deprived
actresses of their personal style and their power of ﬁnal interpretation. 
In the ﬁnal analysis, then, the all-female cast was the basis for Yue opera’s
unique femininity. The peculiar feminine aesthetics of Yue opera are
indeed built on the bodies of the actresses, just as the all-male cast of the
late Qing period Beijing opera embodied a masculine aesthetics. The point
is not simply that gendered qualities are constructed; it is, rather, a ques-
tion of who constructs what and out of what materials. Thus, if the femi-
ninity projected by the four great female impersonators of Republican era
Beijing opera was largely a male construction of the female that helped
deﬁne the masculine, then Yue opera’s construction of the male on the female
body yielded a kind of masculinity that served women’s interests and helped
deﬁne the feminine. In a general sense, the all-female cast was the key to
Yue opera’s creation of a feminine dynamic onstage. 
The importance of the all-female cast becomes clearer when women’s
Yue opera is compared with ping opera in Tianjin and boy-singers’ opera
in Taipei, on the one hand, and gender-straight performances within Yue
opera itself, on the other. Ping opera and boy-singers’ opera also featured
young male impersonators, but male actors always were a strong pres-
ence in the cast and dominated male roles in general. Although actors in
young male roles adopted a rather feminine style and young male imper-
sonators were popular, these theaters still fell short of establishing the
dominance of feminine aesthetics on the stage, owing to the presence of
male bodies. 
Gender-straight Yue opera, in contrast, never fully caught on with
national and international audiences, despite the CCP’s long-term support
for gender-straight performances. The ﬁrst gender-straight Yue opera
troupe was born in the Siming Mountain area in eastern Zhejiang, as part
of the CCP-led New Fourth Army’s propaganda team, during the War of
Resistance against Japan. The Siming Mountain Yue opera focused entirely
on revolutionary and war themes, and its repertoire of predominantly mod-
ern-attire plays, as well as the CCP’s modernist ideology, required gender-
straight performances in which men and women played the roles of their
own biological sex. The Siming Mountain Yue opera was in fact a very dif-
ferent opera that developed in parallel with women’s Yue opera, which was
A Feminine Aesthetics    231

=== PDF PAGE 253 ===

centered in Shanghai and spread throughout Zhejiang during the same
period. Except for their common origin and use of the same native Sheng-
xian Mandarin, the two operas had little in common, for they developed
very different melodies, musical accompaniment, costumes, stage sets, per-
formance styles, and content. In the early PRC period, Siming Mountain–
style Yue opera troupes spread rapidly across the province in coordination
with political campaigns such as land reform, implementation of the New
Marriage Law, and the Cultural Revolution, staging such revolutionary,
modern-attire plays as The White-Haired Girl, Sorrow of the Poor People(Qiong -
ren hen), and An Antique Coin (Luohan qian). This Zhejiang tradition of
gender-straight Yue opera was quickly pushed to the sidelines after the Cul-
tural Revolution, when political propaganda went out of fashion, and was
overshadowed by a popular revival of women’s Yue opera. As the “little
ﬂower” (xiao baihua) troupes, featuring only young actresses, mushroomed
throughout the province, only one or two government-supported gender-
straight Yue opera troupes remained at the ofﬁcial Zhejiang Yue Opera
House.
32
In Shanghai, women’s Yue opera has dominated since the late 1930s,
and experiments with gender-straight Yue opera proved even more difﬁcult
and unfruitful. During the latter half of the twentieth century, the ofﬁcial
Shanghai Yue Opera House followed the state’s lead and invested a great
deal of resources in developing gender-straight Yue opera. The opera house
produced a number of revolutionary modern-attire plays and trained sev-
eral generations of actors, though with limited success. Only a couple of
costume plays were somewhat popular, and a handful of actors achieved
name recognition among audience members, with only two, Shi Jihua and
Zhao Zhigang, acquiring signiﬁcant numbers of fans.
33 Created by state
ideology but never really embraced by audiences, male Yue opera actors
remained marginal in a genre dominated by women. It is no accident that
the most popular actor, Zhao Zhigang, is also the most feminine, and his
popularity owes not only to his excellent acting skills but also to his rela-
tively small build and feminized singing and acting style, which make him
more acceptable to Yue opera fans. Li Jiang and Cheng Liping liked Zhao
precisely for his femininity, and although others did not care for him, Li
Jiang and her friends agreed that Yue opera is all about the young male
impersonators and the all-female cast.
34
Complete feminization of male roles materialized on the female body
in women’s Yue opera has resulted in an even deeper feminization of female
232 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 254 ===

roles, making Yue opera performance exceedingly soft, and created a unique
environment that nurtured an exquisite feminine performance of hetero-
sexual love that has no match in gender-straight or mixed-gender theaters.
Commenting on the women’s opera of his time, the Ming dynasty literati
Zhang Dai, an opera connoisseur, remarked: “Though not perfect, women’s
opera charms people as it is so pretty and coquettish, easy and relaxed,
and soft and enchanting.”
35 Although Zhang Dai was referring to Kun
opera, his description easily ﬁts the feminine beauty of women’s Yue opera.
Zhang Dai’s appreciation of the feminine beauty of women’s opera as well
as his reservations on the art of women’s opera found echoes in the opin-
ions of contemporary Chinese male intellectuals, as discussed earlier in
this chapter. 
This feminine beauty of women’s opera, combined with the modern urban
sophistication nurtured by the city of Shanghai, became the trademark of
Yue opera and gave the genre its unique artistic and aesthetic identity. With
these characteristics, women’s Yue opera, as distinct from gender-straight
Yue opera, won a signiﬁcant place in China’s theatrical traditions. 
Women’s Voices, Women’s Narratives
As women’s Yue opera developed a distinctive modern feminine aesthet-
ics on the bodies of its actresses, it also represented a distinctive woman’s
voice speaking to the public through the opera. 
During the early Republican period, the intellectual and political elite,
mostly men, dominated the discourse of women’s liberation and gender
equality, making it part of nation-building ideologies, while women’s
voices, expressed by a handful of women writers such as Ding Ling, Bin
Xin, and Lu Yin, sounded weak and uncertain. In her discussion of women
writers during and after the May Fourth period, the literary scholar
Wendy Larson thus concluded that by the early 1930s there was no sub-
ject position available for women, and distinctive women writers, such as
Ding Ling, began to shift the focus of their writing “from love to revolu -
tion,” or from a more feminine to a more general (read “masculine”)  leftist
discourse.
36 Women’s voices became loud and clear in wartime Shang hai,
with best-selling women writers, such as Su Qing and Zhang Ailing, as
well as the so-called young lady writers of popular literature.But the war -
time boom for women writers did not endure in the postwar period. From
the early PRC period up to the end of the Cultural Revolution, women
A Feminine Aesthetics    233

=== PDF PAGE 255 ===

writers and women’s writing were framed within a general communist ide-
ology and assumed little gender-speciﬁc characteristics. Larson’s study fur-
ther shows that literature in contemporary China has retained its
“traditional masculine prestige.”
37 Contemporary women writers such as
Wang Anyi and Dai Qing, Larson contended, were not able to take a dis-
tinctive women’s position, and those who did, such as Can Xue, were then
marginalized for it.
38
If well-educated women writers largely echoed dominant masculine
ideologies, as Larson argues, then it should come as no surprise that
actresses did so all the more, for in general they had less literary education
than women writers and were thus less prepared to create their own rhet-
oric. As Yin Yicui, the former head of the CCP Shanghai Ministry of Prop-
aganda, has remarked: “Among the branches under the Ministry of
Propaganda, the print and electronic media often have reservations about
Party’s policies, while the entertainment circle is most willing to follow
the Party’s directions.”
39 She failed to point out, however, that popular-
culture artists have always both used and misused Party rhetoric to
advance their own agendas. 
While feminist scholars such as Wendy Larson are dismayed by women’s
lack of a subject position from which to speak, women may be seen as hav-
ing adopted various subject positions as necessary strategies for asserting
themselves, taking, shifting, and, whenever possible, creating new posi-
tions in search of a better place from which to speak. Thus, what matters
is that women did make themselves enunciating subjects and tried to occupy
meaningful positions in the public space.
The performing women of twentieth-century China constituted a new
type of enunciating subject whose speeches broke the male monologue in
the realm of public culture. Because of the increasing presence of women
in public culture, and as female voices became stronger and more assertive
over time, women’s performances were likely to produce new meanings per-
tinent to female experiences. This is perhaps most apparent in the case of
women’s Yue opera, but even during the Cultural Revolution, when women’s
Yue opera was temporarily sidelined, revolutionary heroines ﬁgured promi-
nently in model operas and ﬁlms. Although they may not have developed
their own grammar and rhetoric, women in entertainment spoke for them-
selves by taking advantage of various existing rhetorical strategies and by
engaging in a dynamic dialogue with mostly male elite discourses. From
wartime through the early 1960s, Yue opera actresses managed to tell their
stories onstage with borrowed languages ranging from the traditional rhet-
234 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 256 ===

oric of womanly virtue to modern discourses of nationalism and Marxism.
These narratives were as varied as women’s experiences and their rela-
tionships with different rhetorics. 
In the play Widow Ma’s Inn , for example, the performers used several
rhetorical strategies in rendering a well-known character. Originally a
chuanqi story from the Tang period, by the early twentieth century, Widow
Ma’s Inn had become a typical misogynist play that portrayed women as
seductive, dangerous, irrational, stupid, driven by uncontrollable sexual
desire, and possessed of a weak sense of morality. Women, the play sug-
gested, should therefore be contained, controlled, and educated by men.
In an opera script printed in the 1930s or 1940s, Widow Ma’s attempt to
seduce Di Renjie meets with Di’s ﬁrm rejection and results in Di lecturing
the widow on female chastity. An ashamed Widow Ma then becomes deter-
mined to remain chaste and to raise her son properly. Her son eventually
passes the civil-service exam and marries the daughter of the minister, who
invites Widow Ma to the capital for the wedding. When Widow Ma arrives
at the household of the minister, she realizes that he is none other than Di
Renjie. She is so ashamed that she wants to commit suicide, but Di is able
to persuade her to stay. He then memorializes the emperor, and a memo-
rial board is bestowed upon Widow Ma in recognition of her having safe-
guarded her chastity and properly raised her son. Di thus once again saves
the widow from falling into disgrace.
40 In the early 1950s, the PRC gov-
ernment banned the play as “obscene theater,” but then, in 1956, a new
edition of the play, produced by the Hezuo Company, turned the story
into an overt criticism of Di Renjie, the hypocritical Confucian moralist
who betrays his lover, Widow Ma, because of his political ambition.
41 Three
discourses underlie these two versions of the story: a traditional misogyny
that makes women sexual beings for men to desire and to control at the
same time; a leftist discourse of women’s liberation; and a Neo-Confucian
puritanism shared by moralists from the Qing to the PRC periods.
In her performance in the 1940s, Xiao Dan’gui vividly portrayed the
young widow’s strong sexual desire, her longing for love, and her sense of
shame when rejected. Xiao, however, made the widow commit suicide upon
recognizing that the minister and father-in-law of her son is Di Renjie, the
young scholar she encountered twenty years before.
42 In China’s long his-
tory, female suicide was, as summarized by Margery Wolf, “the weaker’s
weapon,” a socially acceptable way for a desperate woman to protest against
and take revenge on the ones who had wronged her, or to clear her good
name when it was compromised by rumors and accusations.
43 Particularly
A Feminine Aesthetics    235

=== PDF PAGE 257 ===

in the context of chaste widows in the late imperial period, as pointed out
by Susan Mann, women’s suicide as an autonomous action was respected
and digniﬁed.
44 With her suicide, Xiao’s Widow Ma ultimately rejects Di’s
patronage, implicitly implicates Di, and transforms herself from a pathetic
widow into a digniﬁed person. Xiao’s portrayal of Widow Ma reveals not
only her appropriation of the May Fourth rhetoric about the suffering Chi-
nese woman in the old society but also her struggle to give human dignity
to women’s sexual desire and the sexually desiring woman in the face of
the old misogynist demand for women’s chastity and the new reformist
purge of eroticism. Sadly, the tension in Xiao’s treatment of Widow Ma
mirrored the tension in her own life and anticipated her tragic death. Her
sexual relationship with her boss Zhang Chunfan put her in an ambiguous
and shameful status between an old-fashioned concubine and the illegal
lover of a married man, which made her vulnerable to charges of moral
depravity. Her performance of supposedly obscene plays, moreover, made
her appear unclean and unprogressive to the modernist intellectuals who
otherwise felt sympathy for her as the victim of the feudal evil man Zhang
Chunfan. Ironically, Zhang accused her of being unchaste when she tried
to develop a relationship with modern forces through her director Leng
Shan. In the end, Xiao died to restore her dignity. 
Yuan Xuefen, perhaps the most intellectual among Yue opera actresses
before 1949, represents a different case. Yuan passionately echoed the left-
ist rhetoric of women’s liberation and gender equality in her quest for inde-
pendence, equality, and respectability for women. Writing in the mid-1940s,
she noted:
Women are unfortunate, especially women in our country. Having suf-
fered feudalism and experienced extreme pain in human life for thousands
of years, women still get the worst of it even today when the rhetoric of
gender equality is bandied about ....  D on’t you see that only women are
asked to be virtuous wives and good mothers? Men are never asked to be
“virtuous husbands and good fathers.” Why is that? Being respectful should
be mutual and equal [between husband and wife], and sacriﬁce should also
be worthwhile. The so-called “virtuous wife and good mother” is only a lofty
phrase men use to make women their slaves.
45
Apparently, Yuan found in the discourse of women’s liberation and gender
equality powerful articulations of her personal struggle for independence
236 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 258 ===

and respectability, and the characters she created on the Yue opera stage,
such as Sister Xianglin and Zhu Yingtai, reﬂected her understanding of
women’s liberation. 
Fu Quanxiang, another actress, was inspired to express her life experi-
ence through her portrayal of courtesans. Fu’s ﬁrst marriage to Zhu
Xiangyun, owner of the Shenzhou Inn, in 1941, was certainly not a happy
one. For this marriage, Fu called an end to her three-year commitment to
a religious life, alienated her good friend and colleague Yuan Xuefen (with
whom Fu had joined the sect), and earned herself a bad name. Tabloids
accused her of breaking her religious vows and of marrying for money,
interpreting her marriage as another example of an innocent girl being cor-
rupted by the evils of urban society.
46 When asked about her personal life,
Fu simply replied: “Actresses all had similar experiences in their personal
lives in the past, and there is no special value to discussing these things
n o w ....  I  don’t want to think about these unpleasant things in the past,
and they have all gone after I cried myself out at my meeting with Master
Chen Letian.
47 Now, I care only to leave my art with the younger gener-
ation, and my reﬂections on life have been crystallized in the theatrical
images I have created.”
48 Her goal, she said, was to convincingly portray
a few courtesans. Fu has created several courtesan characters, including Jiao
Guiying and Du Shi’niang, who suffer humiliation and betrayal at the hands
of their lovers and kill themselves in protest. Another character, Li Wa,
destroys her beautiful eyes to make her literati lover focus on his book, not
her beauty, so that he will pass the civil-service examination.
49 Using the
May Fourth rhetoric of the suffering woman in feudal society, Fu managed
to register her own grievances against male exploitation of women and their
love, sex, and labor. 
Qi Yaxian’s story provides yet another example of women shaping their
own narratives by appropriating various discourses. Qi was born into a poor
family in Shanghai.
50 According to Qi’s recollections, her father picked up
petty jobs only now and then and failed to take responsibility for supporting
the family; he was the kind of person who would lend the family’s last few
dollars to other people while allowing his own family to go hungry. He
also had a bad temper and beat his wife and children when he was unhappy.
About her mother, Qi wrote:
My mother was an ordinary housewife: hardworking, diligent, and frugal,
embodying many of the traditional virtues of the old generations of Chi-
A Feminine Aesthetics    237

=== PDF PAGE 259 ===

nese women. She was skinny but could bear a great deal of hardship in life.
She gave birth to ﬁve children. I am the fourth, with three older brothers
and one younger sister. Married to such a man, with such a heavy family
burden, Mother raised us children one by one, with tears in her eyes, endur-
ing hardship with a dogged will.
51
Because of the family’s poor ﬁnancial situation, Qi Yaxian never had a chance
to go to school, and her brothers all started to work as apprentices at a
young age. As the elder daughter, little Yaxian helped her mother with
housekeeping and contracted needlework to add to the family income.
When Qi was twelve, her mother got her a position as a utility girl in a
Yue opera company, as she did not want the child to toil in factories or
work in a dance hall serving male customers, and that was the beginning
of Qi’s Yue opera career. Qi recalled:
[Mother] was poor all her life. In her suffering and hardships, the only thing
that could console her was to attend women’s opera performances. Paying
a few cash for a seat far from the stage in a small, run-down theater, she
enjoyed performances of familiar scholar-beauty stories. She cried for the
people of the past, sympathized with female characters’ misfortune, and
cheered for happy marriages at the end. With these performances, Mother
could, for a moment, forget her worries and her family’s miserable condi-
tion. She was certainly delighted that her daughter was now to join the
opera.
52
Qi eventually became a popular star whose specialty was “tragic female roles”
(beidan) in “bitter plays” (kuxi). She explained her personal relationship with
tragic roles:
In my childhood, there were more tears than joy. Tragic plays composed
the largest group of the plays I learned in girls’ opera school. The plays I
performed after my apprenticeship were also mostly tragedies. Because I had
suffered poverty and struggled in my life, and because I was particularly famil-
iar with the bitterness in women’s lives, my acting in tragic plays was vivid
and quite moving.
53
Two May Fourth themes ran through these various narratives in Yue
opera. First, women are almost always suffering, and even Yuan Xuefen’s
generally more assertive characters cannot escape it. While the elite dis-
238 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 260 ===

course on women’s suffering in imperial China’s patriarchal system was one
obvious origin of this narrative, it is imperative to take note of another
source—a folksong form popularly known as “crying tone” ( kuku diao) sung
by women throughout China’s countryside in olden times, from which
sprang a number of minor operas including Yue opera, boy-singers’ opera,
and ﬂower-drum opera. Qi Yaxian’s remarks about suffering women in bit-
ter plays, therefore, should be understood not simply as echoes of the elite
discourse but also as a women’s narrative rooted deeply in women’s his-
torical experiences. 
Second, most Yue opera plays took the form of the scholar-beauty love
story. As Zhang Ailing and Su Qing argued during the 1940s, women artists
tended to focus on love stories because a woman’s real-life drama had always
centered on her marriage. However, by using the framework of the love
drama, women’s Yue opera also offered multifaceted narratives pertaining
to women’s experiences. While Beijing opera’s imperial narratives reﬂected
traditional elite (male) views and tastes that neglected women’s experiences,
and spoken drama exempliﬁed modern intellectual (again mostly male) con-
structions of nation-state ideology that subordinated women’s concerns to
national interests, women’s Yue opera became a vehicle by which women
could tell their own stories and create their own public expressions in the
scholar-beauty formula. If the May Fourth intellectuals appropriated the
popular crying tone within a traditional women’s culture, then the scholar-
beauty formula appears to have been the opposite case, in which women
took on the May Fourth construction of women’s liberation and free love
in order to articulate their particular aspirations.
Intellectuals were critical of this appropriation. Women, they claimed,
needed to be freed from feudal bonds so that they could better serve the
cause of national salvation and help build a strong nation. In his 1945 essay
“Old and New Women in Chinese Theater,” the leftist writer Yang Han-
sheng presented a review of changing theatrical representations of Chi-
nese women over time, from the feudal era, through the May Fourth period,
to the time of the War of Resistance against Japan. Yang noted that in
operas during the Yuan, Ming, and Qing dynasties, Chinese women suf-
fered all kinds of hardship and oppression. After the Republican Revo -
lution, however, women rose up under the banners of “science” and
“democracy,” and the new women appeared onstage. Yang listed several
female characters featured in scripts by famous elite writers, such as Hu
Shi, Tian Han, and Guo Moruo, that exempliﬁed women’s struggle to lib-
erate themselves from feudal bonds and embrace free love and marriage.
A Feminine Aesthetics    239

=== PDF PAGE 261 ===

Yang went on to describe the female characters produced within the war
culture of the 1930s:
After September 18th [1931, when Japanese troops occupied Manchuria]
and before July 7th [1937, the ofﬁcial outbreak of the War of Resistance],
the new women of China appeared onstage as heroines. 
We all remember the play Seven Women in a Thunderstorm[(Baofengyu zhong
de qige nüxing), by Tian Han]. The women protagonists in this play were
no longer vexed by love questions centered on their personal lives but con-
cerned with national salvation and resistance to the Japanese invasion. The
goal of their effort is no longer free love but national liberation. Compared
to the heroines of the May Fourth era, these women have clearly made a
great leap forward....  
After the war broke out on July 7, China’s new women all stood up and
rushed to take up various forms of work for the divine cause of resistance.
For this reason, a great number of heroines and women warriors appeared
in our wartime theaters....  Today’s Hua Mulan, for example, does not need
to go home; she probably would have already dispatched herself heroically
to the occupied area to ﬁght the enemy.
54
To Yang’s dismay, his model Hua Mulan soon disappeared from the stage,
and love drama once again dominated wartime Shanghai’s popular culture.55
The intellectual and political elites were apparently not able to impose their
agenda on Yue opera and other popular genres. Even during the early PRC
period, Yue opera continued to produce women’s narratives built around
their mundane experiences, and audiences continued to prefer Yue opera
love dramas to dry state-sponsored revolutionary plays. 
Yue Opera’s Dream of the Red Chamber: A Feminine Reinvention
The Yue opera Dream of the Red Chamber is a telling example of women’s
inﬂuence on the novel’s reinvention as a great love story that deﬁned the
popular sentiment of love in the twentieth century. Cao Xueqin’s eigh-
teenth-century novel Dream of the Red Chamber is an important part of
China’s cultural heritage and has inﬂuenced many aspects of modern Chi-
nese culture, from specialized scholarship to popular entertainment. The
novel gained fame among literati men and women immediately after its cre-
ation and remained popular throughout the eighteenth and nineteenth cen-
turies, even though the Qing state tried to ban it.
56 The novel’s reputation
240 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 262 ===

continued to grow during the twentieth century, when literary and schol-
arly interests made its study a specialized ﬁeld of inquiry known as Redol-
ogy (Hongxue). It was considered one of the “four great novels” (sida qishu)
of the Ming and Qing dynasties, the other three being The Water Margin,
Romance of the Three Kingdoms, and Journey to the West (Xiyou ji). Unlike the
other three novels, however, Dream of the Red Chamber was not successfully
integrated into popular entertainment and did not reach a large illiterate
or semiliterate population until the 1940s. Yi Bing, a Yue opera playwright
and administrator, was part of the leadership of the Shanghai Yue Opera
House in the early 1950s. He observed in 1959:
Many stories from Dream of the Red Chamber had been staged in Kun opera,
Beijing opera, and other local operas. But they were not as fortunate as plays
adapted from Romance of the Three Kingdoms, The Water Margin, and Journey to the
West, many of which have been reworked by generations of artists and are
still an important part of the contemporary stage, attaining immortal artis-
tic status. But most plays adapted from Dream of the Red Chamber were forgot-
ten [until recent times].
57
Yi Bing attributed this past lack of success in adapting Dream of the Red Cham-
ber for the opera stage mainly to the inability of theatrical arts to capture
the “rich and broad social content” of the novel. For a Marxist like Yi Bing,
the phrase “rich and broad social content” referred to Mao Zedong’s
instruction that the novel be read as a textbook of China’s feudal society.
58
A more convincing explanation, however, may be found in the domi-
nance of love drama in Republican Shanghai’s popular entertainment, for
it was within this context that the novel became a popular modern love
story. Beijing opera and other male-centered major operas from the Qing
period had little interest in the novel’s focus on domestic affairs and love
relationships, or the tedious details about court politics; in contrast, they
drew widely on stories about the Monkey King in Journey to the West, the
rebels in The Water Margin, and the great military strategist Zhuge Liang in
Romance of the Three Kingdoms. It was only in the twentieth century, with the
growing interest in themes related to love and women’s roles in society,
that popular entertainment began to draw on stories from the novel Dream
of the Red Chamber . The Beijing opera master Mei Lanfang was among the
ﬁrst to stage Lin Daiyu, the novel’s female protagonist, but his solo piece,
Daiyu Buries Flowers (Daiyu zanghua), was based on traditional elite male
interpretations of a young lady saddened by the sight of falling ﬂowers, a
A Feminine Aesthetics    241

=== PDF PAGE 263 ===

metaphor of her own passing beauty. It was certainly not one of Mei’s more
successful plays, despite his graceful singing and dance. In the 1940s, the
novel gained increasing favor with popular-entertainment genres and was
adopted by Suzhou story-singing, Shanghai opera, Yue opera, and ﬁlm. A
contemporary source pointed out: “1944 is the year of Lin Daiyu. The whole
city is staging Lin Daiyu, from Beijing opera and ﬁlm to the Yue opera Lin
Daiyu, which ﬁnally opens at the Nine-Star Theater!”
59 Together, these pro-
ductions made the novel a popular love story. 
Women’s Yue opera troupes began to stage solo acts drawn from the novel
in the early 1940s. Zhi Lanfang’s Daiyu Buries Flowers, recorded around 1941,
appears to be the earliest extant record of such performances.
60 Yuan Xue-
fen staged Lin Daiyu (Lin Daiyu) in 1944, and Yin Guifang and her stage
partners ﬁrst performed the story in 1945.61 In the mid-1950s, Yin Guifang
and her Fanghua Company put on Baoyu and Daiyu, which immediately
became a big hit in Shanghai and Beijing. In Shanghai, the play was sold
out for one hundred consecutive days, with two shows daily. During its
Beijing tour, the company had to move the performance to the Labor The-
ater, a four-thousand-seat open-air theater, and extended the show for sev-
eral days.
62 Over the course of her acting career, Yin Guifang played the
protagonist Jia Baoyu many times, in four different versions, and was remem-
bered as one of the most successful Jia Baoyus. Her portrayal transformed
the character from a lighthearted playboy, as he was commonly perceived
at the time, into a caring young man noted for his passionate and tender
feelings for women and his loyalty to his lover.
63
In 1958, the Shanghai Yue Opera House produced a new version of the
story penned by Xu Jin, with Xu Yulan as Jia Baoyu and Wang Wenjuan as
Lin Daiyu. This version was also a hit. It toured widely within and outside
of China, greatly enhancing Yue opera’s national and international repu-
tation. Then, in 1962, the play was made into a color ﬁlm that, perhaps
more than anything else, helped popularize both Yue opera and the novel,
making Jia Baoyu and Lin Daiyu household names throughout the nation. 
Cao Xueqin’s Dream of the Red Chamber, as many scholars have noted, is
much more than just a love story, as it deals with many different aspects
of human life, such as religion, politics, money, disaster, sex, marriage and
family, and fate as well as love and desire. It is monumental as both a polit-
ical novel and a yanqing novel containing 120 chapters and featuring more
than seven hundred characters ranging from monks and nuns, slaves and
entertainers, peasants and servants to merchants, ofﬁcials, aristocrats, and
imperial concubines. In addition, another two hundred or so names are men-
242 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 264 ===

tioned in the narrative.64 Two main themes run through the book: the polit-
ical and the emotional. The political theme develops around the vicissi-
tudes of the Jia household, whose rise, fall, and revival within a span of
several generations are contingent upon court politics and the emperor’s
unpredictable temper but are also affected by corruption and power strug-
gles within the complex multigenerational household. The other theme
develops around the emotional world of Jia Baoyu, the teenage son of the
Jia family, contrasting his appreciation for young, unmarried women and
their pristine world inside the Grand View Garden with his disgust for adult
men and their dirty, competitive world outside. 
In the novel, Lin Daiyu and Jia Baoyu are human incarnations of two
celestial beings. Baoyu is, as his name indicates, a precious stone that was
left unused by the legendary ﬁgure Nü Wa when she ﬁnished repairing the
fallen sky. The stone was ﬁrst assigned a position as the “divine compan-
ion of heavenly ﬂowers” (shenying shizhe), ﬂowers being a common metaphor
for young women in Chinese literature. While wandering along a heav-
enly river, he saw a lonely little herb. He took a liking to it and nurtured
it with sweet dew, the cream of heaven and earth, and the herb grew, day
by day, into a female celestial being. She used to say that she had nothing
like sweet dew to give him in return for his favor, but should he decide to
visit the earth, she would certainly accompany him and pay him back with
all her tears. Baoyu is thereafter born with a piece of jade in his mouth,
and Lin Daiyu, her surname composed by two “wood” radicals indicating
her herbal origin, turns into the most beautiful, delicate, and sentimental
of all women. Xue Baochai, another celestial being, is born with a golden
lock that matches Baoyu’s jade. When Lin Daiyu and Xue Baochai move
in with Baoyu’s family, a romantic triangle forms among the three protag-
onists, and the stage is set for a love tragedy to unfold. Lin Daiyu sheds
tears for her love all her life as she can never be sure that she will be united
with him in marriage, owing partly to her powerless position as an orphan
and partly to Baoyu’s love for Baochai and the pleasure he takes in the com-
pany of young women in general. After Daiyu has exhausted her tears, she
returns to heaven, and Baoyu eventually returns as well after completing
his earthly duty to the Jia household, having passed the civil-service exam-
ination and fathered a son with his wife Baochai. 
Although the relationship between Jia Baoyu and Lin Daiyu serves as
the emotional center that helps hold the numerous threads together, it is
only one part of Baoyu’s love, which covers at least two dozen young women
and a couple of young men, that is fully described in the novel, reﬂecting
A Feminine Aesthetics    243

=== PDF PAGE 265 ===

the male-centered polygamy common in traditional Chinese society. Only
about a dozen chapters, eight of them in the ﬁrst thirty-four chapters, fea-
ture details of Baoyu’s relationship with Daiyu. Lin Daiyu dies in the ninety-
seventh chapter, and, before and after, each of the lovely young women
living in the Grand View Garden leaves, one after the other, to meet her
own, in most cases tragic, fate. Near the end of the story, as the world of
the Grand View Garden crumbles, Jia Baoyu deserts it and disappears into
the world of the Buddha. The novel, however, ends with the beginning of
a revival of the Jia household as Baoyu’s young nephew, Jia Lan, passes the
ﬁrst level of the civil-service examination and the new emperor revises the
late emperor’s punishment against the family. 
The Yue opera Dream of the Red Chamberelects to focus on the Baoyu-Daiyu
love story, knitting together details from the dozen or so chapters that
speciﬁcally feature the relationship between the two, while the political
fortunes of the Jia household fade into background and most of the other
important characters are not even mentioned. It is interesting to note that
Cao Xueqin, likely being limited by his male perspective, never described
female homosexual relationships in the Grand View Garden, where many
beautiful young women and only two young men, Jia Baoyu and his pre-
teen nephew Jia Lan, lived closely together. Baoyu, who loves and is loved
by these women, is the center of this feminine world, and Baoyu’s love for
Daiyu is the most intense but not the only and exclusive love. Taking a
woman’s perspective, the Yue opera play elevates Lin Daiyu to a position
comparable to that of Jia Baoyu and makes the love relationship between
the two equal and exclusive. The feminine perspective of Yue opera thus
helps reinvent the novel as a modern love story in two ways: ﬁrst, by focus-
ing on the emotions unfolding in the feminine world of the Grand View
Garden while treating the masculine world outside only as background and,
second, by focusing on, hence promoting, a monogamous love relation-
ship between Baoyu and Daiyu while omitting, hence demoting, the tra-
di tional male-centered polygamous system of love relationships as well as
the male same-sex love relationships described in the novel. 
In the 1958 version of the play, Lin Daiyu, played by Wang Wenjuan,
is still quite young when her mother, the sister of Jia Baoyu’s father, dies
and her maternal grandmother, the matriarch of the Jias, has her delivered
by boat to the Jia household. Daiyu has no siblings, and her father dies
soon thereafter. Living alone in the rich, powerful, and complex house-
hold of her uncle, Daiyu is sensitive about her vulnerable situation. Jia Baoyu,
played by Xu Yulan, in contrast, is the only surviving son by the principal
244 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 266 ===

wife of his father, a high ofﬁcial and master of the household. Unusually
good-looking and intelligent, Baoyu is the prince of the household, spoiled
by his grandmother and mother and indulged in the company of his beau-
tiful sisters and cousins. Growing up together and sharing unconventional
views, Baoyu and Daiyu develop a deep affection for each other (see ﬁg.
6.6). But the adults choose Xue Baochai, daughter of the sister of Baoyu’s
mother, as Baoyu’s bride because they consider Baochai to be a better
inﬂuence, somebody who can help them in their efforts to curb Baoyu’s
“wild” behavior and set him on track to prepare for the civil-service exam-

marrying Cousin Lin and conceal their real plans from Daiyu. On his wed-
ding night, Baoyu collapses when he sees that his bride is really Baochai
and learns that his Cousin Lin has died. A few days later, a heartbroken
Baoyu visits the Bamboo Lodge and pours his heart out before Daiyu’s bier.

Keeping his promise of eternal love to Cousin Lin, he walks out of the pros-
perous Jia household, abandoning this dirty world once and for all. 
The play depicts the development of love between the two protago-
nists in a series of episodes organized into several main acts including “Daiyu
Enters the Jia Household” (Daiyu jinfu), “Reading Tale of the Western Cham-
ber” (Du Xixiang), “Daiyu Buries Flowers,” “Daiyu Burns Poems” (Daiyu fen-
gao), “The Perfect Gold-Jade Marriage” (Jin-Yu liangyuan), and “Baoyu
Mourns for Daiyu and Leaves Home” (Kuling chuzou). 
Baoyu and Daiyu are awakened to their love for each other when, sur-
rounded by ﬂowers in the Grand View Garden, they break the taboo and
steal a reading of the forbidden love story between Cui Yingying and Zhang
Gong in Tale of the Western Chamber. Then, left in a powerless position by the
death of her father, Daiyu becomes worried about the future of her love
for Baoyu and “sheds tears from winter to spring, and from summer to
autumn,” as the lyrics describe it. 
In “Daiyu Buries Flowers,” one of the most poetic scenes, Daiyu collects
and buries fallen ﬂowers amid a gorgeous spring scene in the garden and
is saddened as she thinks of her own fate. She sings:
Within the broad expanse of heaven,
Where could I ﬁnd a tomb for the blossoms?
Better to shroud the fragrant remains in silk
And bury the beauty with a handful of clean earth. 
Pure you come and pure you go,
Not stuck in the ditch and getting mired. 
People consider me silly burying ﬂowers,
But who will bury me when I die?
When spring leaves and takes youth away,
Who would notice the fallen blossoms or the dead maid?
When Daiyu overhears the wedding plans for Baoyu and Baochai with-
out knowing the details, she collapses at the thought that Baoyu has betrayed
her. Throwing her poems into the brazier, Daiyu sings her last words: 
All my life 
Poems and books are my close friends in the inner chambers;
Brushes and ink are my blood and ﬂesh.

These poems are not ﬁshing for fame and compliments;
All I hope is to ﬁnd a soul mate who can appreciate them.

diately cries out for Cousin Lin, singing in tears: “Where are you, Cousin

Both Cousin Lin and I are painfully ill,
