Seeing that his pleading has no effect on the matriarch, who has always
let him have his way till now, Baoyu soon realizes that everybody is in on
the plot. He rushes out to ﬁnd his Cousin Lin, only to collapse when Baochai
announces that Daiyu has died. 
The last act, “Baoyu Mourns for Daiyu and Leaves Home,” is set in the
Bamboo Lodge, several days after the wedding. Walking into Daiyu’s cham-
ber and faced with her bier, Baoyu is immediately overcome by a wave of
sadness and cries out: “Cousin Lin, I have come too late!” Only Daiyu’s faith-
ful maid, Zijuan, stands by the bier as Baoyu pours his heart out. Looking
around the once familiar place, now so empty without its mistress, Baoyu
is making up his mind: 
A mistake is made ﬁrm with all the irons in the world;
A wrong marriage is made by a single red rope. 
The broken sky could be ﬁxed by the Goddess Nü Wa,
But it is impossible to repair broken hearts. 
Having left this ﬁlthy world,
You are as clean as snow and ice. 
How then could my clear conscience compromise with these 
vulgar minds?
Sobbing, Zijuan says softly: “Young Master, please go back now. It is late.”
The temple bell tolls in the background. Murmuring, “Yes, go back now,
go back now,” Baoyu walks out, throws his jade to the ﬂoor, and disap-
pears into the twilight. The curtain falls as the chorus sings: “He throws
248 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 270 ===

away the jade and rids himself of gold; he gives up this dirty world and
leaves behind the prosperous nest built upon bloody competition.”
In the novel, scattered throughout the last thirty or so chapters after Daiyu
has departed are descriptions of Baoyu’s desperate efforts to communicate
with Daiyu, waiting for her every night in his dreams and visiting Zijuan at
the Bamboo Lodge to ask about Daiyu’s last days. He needs to ﬁnd mean-
ing in Daiyu’s death and in his love for her. The bond between the two lovers
is ﬁnally resolved only when Baoyu awakens to the Buddhist truth that all
worldly desires are but illusions that bring only suffering. In fact, the Bud-
dhist theme is so strong that it was possible for the classical scholar Wang
Guowei to read the novel as a Buddhist text that points out the vanity in
human affairs and shows that deeply felt loss can awaken people to the Bud-
dha’s truth. The Yue opera play, however, makes Baoyu’s mourning for Daiyu
a simple display of his unfailing love and casts his abandonment of the world
as an act of loyalty to his lover. As the scriptwriter Xu Jin suggested, the
emotional journey on which the play takes the viewer will be completed
only if Baoyu can convince the viewer of the sincerity of his love and matches
Daiyu’s sacriﬁce with his own.
65 Indeed, not until Baoyu throws away his
jade and walks out into nothingness is the great love tragedy born. 
Signiﬁcantly, almost all renditions of the novel in popular forms in the
1940s and 1950s focus on a monogamous love story and depict a feminine
world centered on Jia Baoyu, the feminine teenage boy. For example, Huay-
ing Studio’s 1944 ﬁlm Dream of the Red Chamber, written and directed by Bu
Wancang and starring Yuan Meiyun and Zhou Xuan, has a similar plot and
character development as those in the 1944 Yue opera Lin Daiyu and the
1958 Dream of the Red Chamber. The ﬁlm also centers on the domestic and fem-
inine world of the Grand View Garden and features a group of predomi-
nantly female characters, with only the minimal presence of male characters
including Baoyu’s father and a few male servants. Furthermore, as in Yue opera,
the character Jia Baoyu is played by a woman, the noted actress Yuan Meiyun.
This feminization of Cao’s novel in Shanghai’s popular entertainment of the
mid-twentieth century provides yet more evidence of a general trend that
is most clearly manifested in women’s Yue opera. 
It was the unique feminine aesthetics developed in women’s Yue opera
that gave the feminine narrative of Dream of the Red Chamber its perfect form.
Yue opera’s actresses ﬂeshed out, on their own bodies, a constellation of
enchanting female ﬁgures and the feminine Jia Baoyu, and these images
were so popular that they became standard portraits of these characters.
Yue opera renditions of the novel, with the ﬂuid operatic acting style, body
A Feminine Aesthetics    249

=== PDF PAGE 271 ===

posturing, and cutting-edge character modeling, helped bring Cousin Lin
and Jia Baoyu to life. Moreover, the beautiful arias featured in the Yue opera
play, enhanced by lyrics drawn from the novel’s poems, carried the audi-
ence through an intense emotional journey with the protagonists. If these
hundreds of poems in the novel deﬁne the work’s poetics, then Yue opera’s
arias fully bear this poetics. The arias circulated across the nation, through
the 1962 ﬁlm and millions of records, and came to symbolize what the story
was all about. The 1944 ﬁlm, limited by its realistic style, had no such fem-
inine operatic aesthetics in place to help enhance the feminine narrative,
to articulate the complexity and intensity of the characters’ emotions, or
to bear out the special and traditional poetics of the novel. Thus, as the
1944 ﬁlm quietly faded into the past, the movie industry was content to
simply ﬁlm the 1958 Yue opera play rather than make another ﬁlm. 
The Yue opera ﬁlm was immensely popular. It attracted four hundred thou-
sand viewers when it was ﬁrst released in Hong Kong in 1962. It was an
instant success in Mainland China, but the ﬁlm was quietly removed from
circulation soon after its premiere as the radical leftist leadership tightened
its grip on the cultural market. When it was rereleased in 1978, however, it
set a box ofﬁce record nationwide. Across the country, many places showed
the ﬁlm around the clock for several days in order to make maximum use
of their copies within their limited time slots.
66 Large numbers of viewers
watched it several times and shed tears in the theaters. One Yue opera fan
went to see the ﬁlm every day after work until the local run was over, and
many collage-educated young men living in and outside of Shanghai began
to appreciate Yue opera after viewing the ﬁlm.
67 The Yue opera play also
brought the story to millions of the illiterate and semiliterate, and numerous
people read the novel after viewing the play in the theater or on the screen. 
The Yue opera Dream of the Red Chamber greatly popularized the novel while
increasing the genre’s inﬂuence and prestige. Yue opera’s remaking of the
novel into a great love tragedy helped shape the popular reception of Cao
Xueqin’s novel in modern China more than any other interpretation or schol-
arly work. Even Chairman Mao’s instruction to read the novel as a text-
book on the feudal society has had less effect on the book’s popular
reputation. Known as a master theater of the love drama and with a clus-
ter of popular plays such as The Butterﬂy Lovers, Tale of the Western Chamber, and
Dream of the Red Chamber, women’s Yue opera inscribed its feminine imprint
on the culture of love and instilled a feminine sentiment into popular sen-
sibilities regarding gender and sexual relationships in modern China. 
250 A Feminine Aesthetics

=== PDF PAGE 272 ===

251
Conclusion
For at least ﬁve hundred years, from the mid-Ming to the last decades of
the twentieth century, local operas represented a prominent part of pop-
ular culture and an important aspect of Chinese life, and their transforma-
tions were integral to epochal social and political upheavals. In the
twentieth century, the massive entrance of Chinese women into the public
realm and their active participation in popular culture resulted in a pro-
found transition of opera culture, from the realm of men to an artistic and
social world in which actresses and female spectators ﬁgured prominently.
The feminization of the operatic body gave rise to a distinct feminine opera
culture of the twentieth century. While the transition was a general
process, it was manifested most intensely in the phenomenon of women’s
Yue opera in Shanghai. 
The early development of Yue opera comprised two major transfor -
mations—from rural to urban and from all male to all female—which
occurred in the late nineteenth and ﬁrst half of the twentieth centuries.
The increased prominence of Yue opera, and of minor operas in general,
may be seen as an expression of many modern processes, such as the dete-
rioration of the rural economy, the rise of treaty-port cities as industrial
and commercial centers, and mass urban migration. Major political and
ideological revolutions of the period also facilitated the transformation of
Yue opera and of minor operas more generally. The 1911 Republican Rev-
olution lifted the ban against women in the entertainment market that had
been imposed by Qing law and custom; the subsequent May Fourth New
Culture Movement promoted ideas of women’s liberation and free love and
marriage as opposed to arranged marriage and hence sanctioned love themes

=== PDF PAGE 273 ===

that were central to minor operas. The emergence of Yue opera as a female
opera, then, should be viewed not as a special case in cultural development
that ran contrary to the trends of the time but as a phenomenon that most
intensely expressed the gendered nature of these historical processes. 
The success of women’s Yue opera in Shanghai was a ramiﬁcation of the
city’s social formation, characterized by key factors of gender, class, and
native-place ties, and revealed the city’s basic cultural characteristics—
bourgeois, colonial (domestically as well as internationally), and feminine.
Yue opera’s constituency came mainly from three powerful new groups
in Republican Shanghai that helped deﬁne the city’s social landscape: an
urban middle class; Zhejiang, more speciﬁcally Ningbo-Shaoxing, immi-
grants; and women. First, as the largest industrial city and the most impor-
tant commercial and ﬁnancial center, Shanghai was home to a great number
of capitalists, modern businessmen, and white-collar professionals as well
as apprentices at printing houses, shop hands in modern stores, and indus-
trial workers. The concentration of these new groups in Shanghai nur-
tured China’s ﬁrst generation of the urban middle- and lower-middle class.
These wage earners or wives of wage earners made up the majority of
Yue opera’s audience. Second, Shanghai was a city built by settlers.
1 Divi-
sions among various immigrant groups from different native places,
though not necessarily always so clear-cut, were a basic factor in the city’s
social life and political economy. If most Subei natives and many Shang-
hai natives composed the lower layers of society, members of the new
bourgeoisie were mostly natives from the more economically developed
Jiangnan region. More speciﬁcally, the powerful Ningbo-Shaoxing busi-
ness group and intellectuals strongly backed Yue opera, and the opera’s
success in turn symbolized the rising power of this native-place group in
the city. Third, Shanghai had a high, perhaps the highest, concentration
of new types of women, which included factory workers, shop assistants,
domestic helpers, middle-class housewives, students, ofﬁce workers, and
professionals such as accountants, teachers, nurses, doctors, and actresses.
Mostly young immigrants in a young metropolis marked by a semicolo-
nial cosmopolitanism, these women found unprecedented, though still quite
limited, freedom in pursuing their personal interests, among which was the
choice to patronize public entertainment. During this important period of
transition in gender norms, women’s Yue opera provided opportunities for
various women to expand their lives beyond their social stations. 
When historical conditions were right, the actions of young actresses
made it all happen. When a group of girls from the poor countryside of
252 Conclusion

=== PDF PAGE 274 ===

Shengxian joined the ﬁrst all-girls opera school in 1923, when female
Shengxian opera troupes frequented Shanghai in the mid-1930s, and when
female troupes joined refugees ﬂowing into the falling city in the late
1930s, they probably did not think they were making history. But their
struggle for economic survival, human dignity, sexual autonomy, and social
status against all sorts of gender- and class-based exploitation constituted
an important dimension of China’s modern history. Key to the actress’s
struggle was the need to rescue herself from a traditional public culture
of erotica that had ﬁxed its voyeuristic gaze on her sexual body and to
transform herself from the lowly female entertainer into a modern artist.
This would be possible only if she could transform her opera from a bawdy
peasant form into a reﬁned and socially meaningful urban theater. Fortu-
nately, the actresses’ struggle converged with two modern processes: the
formation of an urban bourgeois culture with a strong female component
and the dissemination of elite nation-building rhetoric in theatrical
reform. Through a series of efforts to reform Yue opera, actresses turned
the opera into a master theater of the modern love drama and an urban
theatrical spectacle. While Yue opera’s romanticized and digniﬁed love
stories appealed to middle-class and female audiences, the patriotic mes-
sage attached to the principal love drama won political legitimacy for the
opera. 
As Yue opera actresses remade the opera into a decent theater, they simul-
taneously achieved the status of art workers. Their quest for respect, dig-
nity, and full social status won the support of a large middle-class female
audience, left-wing intellectuals, and a new public educated in the rheto-
ric of women’s liberation. Actresses’ struggle against class- and gender-
based traditional contempt toward the female entertainer culminated in a
series of public events in the late 1940s. Sister Xianglin, the union incident,
the collaborative fund-raising performances, and the Xiao Dangui inci-
 dent, staged in both the theater and the mass media, were all part of a public
drama that discursively transformed the public deﬁnition of the actress. The
dramatic transformation of Yue opera and its actresses was a central event
that took place in the limelight, and the resulting prominence of a women’s
culture gave the city’s modern culture a strong feminine ﬂavor. 
Feminine V alue
Romantic love and love drama had dubious reputations in modern China’s
public culture. While they dominated popular literature and entertainment,
Conclusion    253

=== PDF PAGE 275 ===

they occupied a lower position in the overall cultural hierarchy, looked
down upon by elitist artists and cultural connoisseurs as trite and criti-
cized by intellectual and political elites as self-indulgent. Various male
authorities since the Qing period thought that the emotions of personal
love belonged to the feminine, private sphere and were hence trivial mat-
ters. From such an elite male perspective, it is easy to dismiss the femi-
nine opera and its love drama as insigniﬁcant and inconsequential.
Educated elitist women struggling to break conventional gender norms
also tended to sideline Yue opera as a women’s cultural ghetto in which
self-indulgent female fans enjoyed actresses performing stereotypical
feminine clichés.
2 This bias against the feminine opera culminated during
the Cultural Revolution, when women’s Yue opera was banned, and the
Maoist notion that “times have changed, men and women are the same”
tried to make women like men.
3 Opposition to feminine opera continued
in the post-revolution era. A recent commentary, provoked by two Yue
opera productions— The Butterﬂy Dream, of 2001, and the Shanghai Grand
Theater version of Dream of the Red Chamber, of 1999—provides a clear exam-
ple of how disturbing Yue opera’s femininity can be to its critics. The author,
displaying a superﬁcial and impressionistic knowledge of Yue opera,
attacked the opera, from its music to its male impersonators, and denounced
it with indignation:
Yue opera is simply the sediment of the dirty and rotten elements in the
long river of Chinese opera tradition, and it brazenly exhibits and elabo-
rates the decadent and frivolous sentimentality from the Five Dynasties [907–
60] to the present ....  Y u e  o p e r a  distorts the true character of many
intellectuals in history as it depicts them in a sickly manner. Yue opera cas-
trates traditional intellectuals’ innate unswerving determination, as expressed
in the motto that scholars can be killed but cannot be humiliated, and steals
their aspiration to bring peace to the world starting from self-cultivation and
ordering the family and the state [xiushen qijia zhiguo pingtianxia].
4
Combining an old Confucian discourse on gentlemanly conduct with
the more recent discourse of nation building, the author expresses an
unreﬂective male elitist prejudice against Yue opera’s femininity, contrast-
ing it to the masculine tradition in Chinese opera represented by Beijing
opera. As harsh as it is, there is nothing new in this commentary. 
Yue opera has been loved and censured for its renowned femininity ever
254 Conclusion

=== PDF PAGE 276 ===

since the 1940s. As a feminine theater featuring emotionally intense fem-
inine narratives, Yue opera provoked strong emotional reactions in view-
ers, and where its admirers saw beauty, its critics saw decadence. Yue opera
fans were notorious for their unrestrained public expressions of love for
their icons, while critics expressed scorn for the opera and its fans. It is
remarkable that despite such elitist masculine prejudices and political efforts
to shape Yue opera into a revolutionary theater, women’s Yue opera thrived
as a master theater of the love drama. Its aesthetics and narratives were
articulations of modern urban feminine aspirations that helped set the fash-
ion for popular entertainment in twentieth-century China. The opera was,
therefore, an expression of the rise of feminine power in Shanghai and in
Chinese society in general. 
Despite many differences, both women’s Yue opera and Western “chick
ﬂicks” (ﬁlms that appeal especially to women, often focusing on romance
or relationships) are energetic, melodramatic, urban, popular, and femi-
nine, and both have provoked unsettling feelings in elitist feminists as well
as in masculine mainstreamers. Moreover, both clearly symbolize the
growth of feminine power in public culture, a kind of self-authorized
female representation that embarrasses various (including feminist) ortho-
doxies and deﬁes simple deﬁnitions.
5 In Yue opera, as in chick ﬂicks, we
see women’s aspirations for the right to agency, independence, respect,
and love as autonomous human beings. Through the opera, Chinese
women refuted traditional misogynist views that subjugate them to men
while they simultaneously negotiated with the modern revolutionary rhet-
oric that subjects them to the nation. The main political and intellectual
discourses on women’s liberation criticized the feminine yin as being tra-
ditional (read “backward”), soft, weak, and small-minded and tried to sub-
ordinate women to the masculine yang nation-building causes, but the
women of Yue opera sought to rectify and lift the feminine as equal to
the masculine. In this way, the women of Yue opera used seemingly con-
ventional feminine values to challenge male dominance and to empower
themselves. 
The theory of performance and subject formation articulated by Judith
Butler is useful in explaining the construction of femininity in Yue opera.
As Butler argued when discussing the construction of “sex”:
Crucially, then, construction is neither a single act nor a causal process ini-
tiated by a subject and culminating in a set of ﬁxed effects. Construction
Conclusion    255

=== PDF PAGE 277 ===

not only takes place in time, but is itself a temporal process which operates
through the reiteration of norms; sex is both produced and destabilized in
the course of this reiteration. As a sedimented effect of a reiterative or rit-
ual practice, sex acquires its naturalized effect, and, yet, it is also by virtue
of this reiteration that gaps and ﬁssures are opened up as the constitutive
instabilities in such constructions, as that which escapes or exceeds the norm,
as that which cannot be wholly deﬁned or ﬁxed by the repetitive labor of
that norm. This instability is the deconstituting possibility in the very
process of repetition, the power that undoes the very effects by which “sex”
is stabilized, the possibility to put the consolidation of the norms of “sex”
into a potentially productive crisis.
6
Butler’s argument for the construction of “sex” as a performative process in
everyday life sheds light on how women could change gender norms while
seemingly acting to afﬁrm them. Instead of following the male elite’s denial
of feminine value, the women of Yue opera exercised considerable self-
authorization in exploring feminine aesthetics and women’s narratives in
the form of love drama. With the opera’s popular success, the women of
Yue opera helped establish private feelings and mundane experiences as
major themes in artistic representations of modern life, making the “fem-
inine” concerns of the everyday as important as the “masculine” public dis-
course of nationalism and modernity, as represented by spoken drama and
Beijing opera. Thus, the women of Yue opera advanced the modern process
of signiﬁcation of the feminine, making it equal to, yet still different from,
the masculine. 
The Revolution
This urban process, however, was met with the rural-based communist rev-
olution that came to dominance after 1949. To be sure, many different
forces helped shape Yue opera’s vicissitudes before the Communist Party’s
left-wing intervention of the late 1940s. Riding on the trends of indus-
trialization and urban in-migration, women’s Yue opera quickly established
itself in Shanghai in the 1930s and 1940s with the support of an emerg-
ing middle-class, especially female, audience, at a time when women’s edu-
cation and careers outside the home had already begun to change gender
mores. Yue opera’s rise in Shanghai can be understood only within these
urban-centered fundamental social and economical processes. 
Actresses’ struggle for survival and respect, however, converged with
256 Conclusion

=== PDF PAGE 278 ===

the left-wing program for social and gender equality, and their relation-
ship with the communist revolution was for the large part mutually beneﬁ-
cial. The CCP underground forces aided Yue opera actresses’ struggle in
the 1940s, and during the PRC period, the state elevated the status of
entertainers to engineers of the soul, admitted their representatives into
the new cultural and political elite, and made entertainment a lofty pro-
fession while providing ﬁnancial security for entertainers. Most actresses
welcomed the social changes brought about by the new state and sup-
ported it with their performances. Although the CCP cannot be credited
as the sole liberator of the suffering actresses, there should be no ques-
tion that the PRC state, and the communist revolution, had a positive
impact on the actress’s transformation from member of the underclass to
full member of society. 
The PRC state’s intervention in the arts, however, had more question-
able effects on the genre’s development. In restructuring the entertainment
business in the 1950s, the state pulled together intellectual and material
resources and produced a cluster of Yue opera masterpieces of love drama,
which boosted the opera’s national and international inﬂuence. This devel-
opment rested, however, on the artistic foundations laid by Yue opera
reforms of the 1940s and should not be credited solely to state interven-
tion. In the long run, the state’s ideological and administrative control of
art production was detrimental to Yue opera, and to performance art in gen-
eral, as it limited artists’ creativity and their ability to respond to their audi-
ences. As state control of art intensiﬁed during the Cultural Revolution,
allowing only one kind of revolutionary heroic art, art and entertainment
withered. Even when the state shifted to a laissez-faire policy during the
reform era that began in the 1980s, Yue opera and other native-opera gen-
res were not able to reclaim their dominant position in the entertainment
market. 
Neither Yue opera’s glorious achievements in the 1950s and 1960s nor
the genre’s waning popularity in recent decades was due entirely to the
PRC state, although in both cases, the state and the communist revolu-
tion were contributors. Several developments in the late twentieth cen-
tury sent Yue opera and other native operas into decline. First, a globalized
mass-entertainment culture captured the imaginations of younger gener-
ations after China reopened itself to Western inﬂuence in the 1980s. The
freshness of things Western seems to have rendered traditional art forms
obsolete in the minds of young people. Second, thanks to economic and
social development, there were no longer as many desperate people rely-
Conclusion    257

=== PDF PAGE 279 ===

ing on grassroots low-brow performances as a way of making a living.
Third, the Shanghai municipal government imposed regulations on free-
ﬂowing troupe formations and performances in an attempt to maintain min-
imal control over anti-government or pornographic revues. Thus, while
Yue opera continued to be part of the entertainment scene in Shanghai,
it was no longer as bustling as it had been in its golden period (1930s–
60s), when the opera rallied novel artistic imaginations and tremendous
youthful energies. 
Thus, the impact of the communist revolution on Yue opera, especially
the 1949 Liberation and the Cultural Revolution, is only of historical sig-
niﬁcance but is not historically signiﬁcant (to borrow a Joseph Levenson
expression) because it was not the decisive factor in the rise and decline
of Yue opera.
7 The communist revolution and the story of Yue opera are
better understood as two separate phenomena that interacted with each
other, both shaping and being shaped by fundamental historical transfor-
mations in social, economic, and political systems in twentieth-century
China. Moreover, in the long run, the urban-centered primordial moder-
nity, as reﬂected partly in Yue opera, is no less a revolution than the rural-
based grandiose communist movement. 
Periodization
Yue opera’s history, as a process separate from national revolution, sug-
gests a periodization different as well as independent from the division in
political history marked by the founding of the People’s Republic of China.
Until recently, the 1949 divide has been taken as a standard in modern
Chinese history, reﬂecting the tremendous impact of the regime change
on the lives of the Chinese people and on the international order. This
division, however, has been increasingly problematic since the 1990s, as
the People’s Republic became a subject of historical inquiry and as China
entered a post-revolution period amid the global post–Cold War period.
Reassessing the role of the communist revolution in China’s moderniza-
tion process, China scholars questioned the usefulness of the 1949 divide.
Political historian Nishimura Shigeo, for one, argued that the regime change
of 1949 did not interrupt the process of building a modern nation-state in
China.
8 William Kirby and his students pointed out, from an economic
history angle, continuities in the building of state-sponsored and state-con-
trolled large-scale industries across 1949.
9
258 Conclusion

=== PDF PAGE 280 ===

Similarly, Yue opera also shows continuities across 1949 in modern devel-
opments in cultural and social aspects, indicating the necessity of treating
the period between the 1920s and the 1980s as a coherent temporal frame-
work deﬁned by several particular social and cultural processes. Of primary
importance in this regard is the prominence of a public women’s culture
centered on Yue opera, a phenomenon that epitomized the contradictions
and conﬂicts in the rise of women in urban society. The Yue opera phe-
nomenon emerged during a period when the new gender ideology had just
begun to transform traditional gender praxis, hence the necessity for women
to carve out a separate space of their own in which their new experiences
in public life would be comfortable. The home base provided by women’s
Yue opera was an open space, and the actresses and female audiences were
highly visible ﬁgures who moved publicly in and out of theaters and pro-
duced various social dramas in the mass media. The cultural activism of
the women of Yue opera as a social/cultural group was unprecedented, and
it helped normalize women’s presence in social life and remake gender norms
in the city’s public culture. 
The trend of women going outside the family and into the public realm
in urban society received a big boost after 1949 when the state mobilized
women to join the workforce. The new state’s intervention further expanded
and legitimated women’s activism in the public domain, and the subsequent
Cultural Revolution pushed this trend to a climax. Partly because women
all over the country became increasingly active in public life, the popu-
larity of women’s Yue opera expanded beyond the city limits and became
a national phenomenon. However, having grown strong in conjunction with
the general trend of women’s entrance into social life, women’s Yue opera
began to lose its grip on younger women and rather suddenly lost popu-
larity in the 1980s, signifying the end to a phase in the cause of women’s
liberation, and a positive end it is. Younger generations of women, grow-
ing up in a society in which women performed public duty alongside men
and gender equality was the norm, no longer shared the experiences and
aspirations of their predecessors; they saw in Yue opera a women’s culture
that was outdated and embarrassing, which reminded them of women’s
lowly status in the past. 
More broadly, Yue opera’s history was part and parcel of the modern
transformation of popular entertainment in Shanghai that began in the
1930s and ran across 1949 and through the Cultural Revolution, despite
the increasingly dominant leftist radicalism of the early PRC period. This
Conclusion    259

=== PDF PAGE 281 ===

period, from 1949 to 1976, witnessed a revolutionary state pushing the entire
society into an all-around socialist transformation. The construction of a
socialist culture was of paramount importance in this ambitious plan, and
the socialist transformation of popular entertainment was a key element in
the state’s effort to disseminate its ideology to the grassroots. In 1951, the
state announced the policy of three transformations—which applied to
the entertainer, the structure of the industry, and the stage  performance—
and launched an all-out operatic reform campaign. The state’s effort to
gain full control of the entertainment industry culminated during the Cul-
tural Revolution, when the industry was allowed to offer only a dozen or
so state-approved theatrical productions. The radical ideology and praxis
of this period gave the impression of a total rupture in cultural develop-
ment. Interestingly, however, the perceived rupture may be viewed as an
extreme push in the same direction. Close examination of the situation
reveals that, underneath the surface of radical rhetoric and practice, sev-
eral threads of cultural modernity ran through the early PRC cultural
restructuring, including institutionalization, the push for professionalism,
and citizenry building.
10
Institutionalization in popular entertainment developed in two ways:
market self-ordering, through competition and negotiation, and government-
imposed regulation. Shanghai’s popular-entertainment market grew rap-
idly with the inﬂux of immigrants and the city’s expansion in the early
twentieth century. There was not, however, much institution building to
speak of up to the period of the War of Resistance against Japan because
local opera troupes were unstable in terms of formation and movement,
and the split sovereignty in semicolonial Shanghai compromised govern -
mental efforts to regulate the market. Real institution building took place
in the 1940s, especially after the end of World War II. As China regained
full control of the city and the foreign concessions were abolished, the
Nationalist Shanghai municipal government set out to regulate the enter-
tainment market. It issued a series of laws and regulations requiring per-
formance companies, theaters, and dance-hall entertainers to register with
the Social Affairs Bureau. The government also censored pornographic
and politically dangerous performances and attempted to control union-
ization in the industry. Meanwhile, through a process of ﬁerce competi-
tion and constant negotiation, the entertainment market matured and
stabilized; order began to emerge, with star troupes and performers
becoming established in various trades while the less successful were
pushed to the periphery. In the Yue opera trade, for example, a cluster of
260 Conclusion

=== PDF PAGE 282 ===

such superstars as the so-called nine sisters and ﬁve large companies (Xuesh-
eng, Fanghua, Yulan, Dongshan, and Shaozhuang) dominated the mar-
ket. Meanwhile, interest groups within the trade took advantage of the
government-directed union campaign and set up the Yue Opera Workers’
Union and the Yue Opera Employers’ Union in order to increase their power
through institution building. This trend of institutionalization continued
after 1949, refashioned completely within a framework of socialist ide-
ology and centrally planned culture. The thorough socialist reorganiza-
tion of the entertainment business represented radical measures of modern
institution building beyond political and ideological differences between
the Nationalists and the CCP. By the end of the Cultural Revolution, the
landscape of Shanghai’s entertainment industry had stabilized, albeit in
a socialist fashion, with regulated performance companies, venues, pro-
fessional organizations, and educational units as well as governmental
administrative organs. In the post–Cultural Revolution era, when the gov-
ernment saw the need to reinstate an entertainment market, the success-
ful institution building of the previous period made it much easier for the
government to open and control a market. The open market of popular
entertainment, in which spontaneous street performances and itinerant
troupes and performers moved freely, had become a memory of the “pre-
modern” past. 
Professionalism was another important aspect in the continuous process
of cultural modernization in the 1950s. Most important in this regard was
the development of a script-based, director-centered system in theatrical
production that gradually replaced the previous mode of production cen-
tered on actors’ unscripted improvisational performances. This process began
in the late 1930s and developed throughout the 1940s within local opera
troupes active in Shanghai. In the 1950s, the state sent professional script -
writers, directors, and stage designers to every opera troupe and made sure
that every production followed a standard procedure, starting from a com-
plete script that was actualized onstage under a director. This greatly
enhanced professionalism and modernized aesthetic standards in local
operas and led to a cluster of masterpieces in the early PRC period, includ-
ing the Yue operas Tale of the Western Chamber, Dream of the Red Chamber , and
Pursuing the Carp(Zhui yu); the Shanghai operasAn Antique Coinand The Slave
Mother (Wei nuli de muqin); and the Huai operasQin Xianglian (Qin Xiang-
lian) and The Blue Bridge (Lanqiao hui). The push for professionalism was so
strong that general improvement in the artistic quality of local-opera pro-
ductions was achieved at the cost of the actors’ creativity and their ability
Conclusion    261

=== PDF PAGE 283 ===

to give their roles individuality through idiomatic singing and acting. Today,
talented self-authorizing actors of the past are legendary ﬁgures, and improv-
isational performance has become a legacy of the premodern tradition of
native operas. 
Citizenry building was an integral part of modern China’s nation build-
ing and another aspect of the continuous development of a modern cul-
ture in the early PRC period. Despite political and ideological differences
between the Nationalists and the Communists, both worked to forge among
the people a sense of one nation and one people—an understanding of
the correlation between personal and national interests as well as the urge
to take on one’s duty to the nation. The Republican effort at citizenry build-
ing is easily identiﬁed in various versions of the New Life Movement, patri-
otic discourses against foreign invasions, and nationalistic rhetoric against
the warlords and the Communists. School education and the mass media
served as the primary channels for such construction. During the early PRC
period, however, the state made popular entertainment another important
venue, pushing the traditional idea of “education through entertainment”
(yu jiao yu le) to the limit. By means of a centrally planned culture, the rhet-
oric of revolutionary patriotism permeated all media and most theatrical
productions, helping to create a uniform socialist language for the nation’s
uniﬁed citizenry. Through the socialist transformation of stage perform-
ances and performers—two of the three transformations in 1950s oper-
atic reform—the new state not only made the lowly entertainers members
of the citizenry but moreover constructed an imagined national commu-
nity of citizens as reformed entertainers popularized state ideology through
the staging of socialist arts. This radical citizenry building reached its cli-
max during the Cultural Revolution, when revolutionary heroic arts encour-
aged citizens to work and die for the country. At the end of this period,
the idea of China (Zhongguo) and Chinese (Zhongguoren) had taken root
in the common people’s political consciousness and informed their identity
formation. 
The New Century
Around the turn of the twenty-ﬁrst century, Yue opera began to experi-
ence a small revival with a few well-received productions by the Shang-
hai Yue Opera House, including Stage Sisters in 1998, The Meilong T ownship
(Meilong zhen) in 2000, and The Butterﬂy Dreamin 2001 along with the spe-
262 Conclusion

=== PDF PAGE 284 ===

cial production of Dream of the Red Chamber for the Grand Theater in 1999.
This is part of a revival in the cultural market for performance art in Shang-
hai that began with the opening of the Grand Theater in 1998. Supported
by a powerful economic upturn in the 1990s, Shanghai once again aspires
to rebuild a cosmopolitan spirit and restore its leading position in making
culture. The Grand Theater and a group of landmark buildings, including
the Shanghai Museum and the Museum for Urban Planning, erected at the
center of the city around the People’s Square, symbolize this ambition. In
recent years, the Grand Theater has contracted with many international
companies as well as ﬁrst-rate troupes nationwide to perform on its stages,
and its calendar is nearly full year-round. Citywide, about two dozen ven-
ues feature a variety of performances ranging from acrobatic displays, com-
edy shows, Yue opera, Beijing opera, and dance, to pop, rock and Western
music concerts, Russian ballet, Irish folk dance, and Broadway musicals.
Yue opera was the ﬁrst among local operas to stage at the Grand Theater,
with the ever popular Dream of the Red Chamber, indicating that the genre has
retained much of its charm for many people in the city and around the nation.
Playing up the cultural aspirations of the city, Yue opera artists made it their
goal to restore the brand name “Shanghai Yue opera” nationwide. Between
2002 and 2006, the Shanghai Yue Opera House has accordingly organized
several well-publicized performance tours to Zhejiang, the southwest, the
northwest, and the northeastern regions showcasing a sophisticated mod-
ern urban culture as well as the special charm of women’s opera. 
The reform era has also witnessed a new wave of urbanization and urban
migration. As Shanghai becomes increasingly open to new immigrants,
many Zhejiang natives have once again found in Yue opera a desirable career
in the metropolis. This trend will continue into the conceivable future, since
the government has made urbanization a strategic goal for China’s devel-
opment in the decades to come. Thus, the same dynamics between the Zhe-
jiang countryside and Shanghai that supported the twentieth century’s Yue
opera boom in Shanghai are once again working to revitalize the opera
(see ﬁgs. 7 .1, 7 .2). 
Moreover, economic development in the past quarter century created
a sizable population of young professionals and entrepreneurs in the city,
many of whom are women. Contemporary female fans of Yue opera are
found not only in theaters but also on the Internet. College students and
white-collar workers have created at least two dozen Web sites devoted to
Yue opera in general or to speciﬁc leading actresses. One such Web site,
Conclusion    263

=== PDF PAGE 285 ===

Shan Yangping Forum (Yangping Luntan), a lively chat board devoted to
the beautiful actress Shan Yangping, is ﬁlled with young women’s unre-
strained expressions of love for her and for one another as Shan fans.
11
Another Web site devoted to the handsome male impersonator Qian Huili
is similarly ﬁlled with young women’s confessions of love for their idol.
12
This group of well-educated fans, like those of the past, is the most vocal
and visible group among all Yue opera fans, but the majority of the Yue
opera audience is still composed of middle-aged women. It remains to be
seen whether Yue opera will be able to expand its inﬂuence among this
younger group and how the new generation of Yue opera fans and actresses
will shape Yue opera (see ﬁg. 7 .3). 
At the beginning of the twenty-ﬁrst century, women’s Yue opera is
enjoying a small revival, and not much seems to have changed: the same
264 Conclusion
7 .1  A middle-aged Yue opera fan rehearses 
at a party at Xiao-Bai Yumei’s residence, 1996.
(Photo by the author)

=== PDF PAGE 286 ===

patterns of love drama persist, and the opera has never tired of telling
women’s narratives presented in exquisite feminine aesthetics to a pre-
dominantly female audience. Relationships between audiences and stars
also remain more or less the same: fans fall in love with their idols because
of both artistic and sexual attraction. Adoptive mother-daughter relation -
ships are no longer as formal and ostentatious as before, yet it is common
knowledge within Yue opera circles that most leading actresses have spe-
cial patrons, mostly overseas Chinese businesswomen and businessmen.
The most obvious difference is the changing status of Yue opera actresses.
Conclusion    265
7 .2  A young Yue opera fan learns to perform at a party at
Xiao-Bai Yumei’s residence, 1996. (Photo by the author)

=== PDF PAGE 287 ===

The old stigma attached to female entertainers belongs ﬁrmly in the past,
and for Yue opera performers today, acting not only represents job secu-
rity but also is a respected profession that provides fame and celebrity sta-
tus for the stars. 
Thus, the social and economic processes that once supported Yue opera
seem to have been revived in the last quarter century of the reform era.
Especially since the 1990s, Shanghai has once again emerged as the cen-
ter of China’s modern economy. A new middle class is developing as immi-
grants ﬂow into the city, and female white-collar workers symbolize the
new women of Shanghai. The question is, Will women’s Yue opera thrive
once again in the new century? 
266 Conclusion
7 .3  The Yifu Tianchan Theater, a major stage for native-opera performances 
in contemporary Shanghai. (Yue yuan shen yun 2001)

=== PDF PAGE 288 ===

The answer is probably negative, for the social context is very different
from that of the early twentieth century. During the ﬁrst four decades of
the People’s Republic, Shanghai grew into a huge residential city, with older
generations of settlers becoming Shanghainese and the newcomers being
shaped by the city rather than shaping it. At the same time, society has
become so open to women that women in Shanghai no longer need Yue
opera to help broaden their social horizons. In short, members of the new
bourgeoisie in Shanghai have found less sociological and emotional con-
nection with the opera, although they may deem it aesthetically and per-
haps sexually attractive. As Yue opera makes itself a permanent feature of
Shanghai’s cultural scene, it no longer has the glamour and popularity of
its heyday, as popular-culture preferences have shifted to a globalized mass
entertainment represented by domestic and international rock and popular-
music stars and, most recently, such pop-culture representatives as Chi-
nese rappers. Thus, although Yue opera will continue to be a highly
developed art form in the city’s entertainment scene, it is no longer an impor-
tant social phenomenon. 
In today’s China, when native operas are reduced mostly to relying on
government subsidies in order to survive, Yue opera has joined Beijing
opera as a representative of China’s living cultural tradition. Both operas,
however, still claim the loyalty of a small segment of the population made
up mostly of the middle aged and elderly. The divide along gender lines
between Beijing opera and Yue opera, in terms of content, style, and audi-
ence, is still apparent. Yue opera continues to produce love dramas, while
Beijing opera retains its focus on grand imperial or nation-state narratives.
This is evident in several new productions by the Shanghai Beijing Opera
House, such as Cao Cao and Yang Xiu (Cao Cao yu Yang Xiu) and The Pros-
perous Reign of the Taizong Emperor (Zhenguan shengshi). In Lu Xun Park, in
northeastern Shanghai, on a sunny weekend, coteries of Yue opera fans,
mostly retired women, gather at the pavilion on top of the hill and rehearse
their favorite pieces; down the hill, across the lake, Beijing opera fans, mostly
elderly men, get together at the corridor and entertain themselves by singing
well-known pieces. Beijing opera and Yue opera, the products of two dif-
ferently gendered cultures in two different eras, have left deep marks on
China’s history and, as living traditions, continue to offer entertainment
for the population and inspiration for artists around the world. 
Conclusion    267

Preface
1. As Wendy Larson argues in her study of modern China’s women writers. Larson
1998. 
2. Fuller 1980, 235. 
3. Hall 1980. 
Introduction: Opera, Gender, and the City 
1. “Mandarin ducks and butterﬂies” ﬁction is a subgenre in Republican period popu-
lar literature that focuses on love stories. At ﬁrst, the term was pejorative and referred
particularly to love stories written in classical Chinese in the 1910s and early 1920s; even-
tually, it came to refer to all popular love stories of the Republican period.

9. In a more general sense, I have beneﬁted from reading a wide range of scholarship

=== PDF PAGE 297 ===

on Chinese opera. In Japan, there are some very interesting studies of Chinese operas,
such as those by scholars Tanaka Issei and O¯ ki Yasushi. See Tanaka 1985 and O¯ ki 1989.
Although I occasionally draw on these works, I do not attempt to discuss these studies
here mainly because most are concerned primarily with operas in history, while my focus
is cultural history manifested in one particular opera.
A small number of works of history and comparative literature have recognized, to
varying degrees, the importance of studying local operas as a means of gaining access
to popular mentality and sociality in China. In his study of the Boxers, Esherick astutely
noted the ways in which rebel heroes from opera repertoires provided local peasants with
role models, as well as the inspiration to rebel, in a time of crisis (Esherick 1987), while
Mackerras devoted a book to the social organizations of Beijing opera in the late eigh-
teenth and nineteenth centuries (Mackerras 1972). 
There is also a group of important articles scattered in various journals and collec-
tions, including Tanaka, “The Social and Historical Context of Ming-Ch’ing Local Drama”
(1985); Ward, “Regional Operas and Their Audiences” (1985); Arkush, “Love and Mar-
riage in North Chinese Peasant Operas” (1989); Bensen, “The Manipulation of Tanci in
Radio Shanghai during the 1930s” (1995); Stock, “Huju and Musical Change” (1997);
and Bender, “Shifting and Performance in Suzhou Chantefable” (1999). 
Other modern scholars of Chinese culture and society have studied the Chinese elite’s
use of opera as a means of transmitting elite messages; see, for example, Hsiao-T’i Li,
“Opera, Society, and Politics” (1996), and Hayford, T o the People(1990), 127–32. Rebecca
Karl used the case of Jingju reform in Shanghai in the 1900s to illustrate features of Chi-
nese nationalism in the late Qing in Staging the World(2002), while Joshua Goldstein dis-
cussed the “nationalization” of Beijing opera in the Republican period in “Theatrical
Imagi-nations” (1999a) and “Mei Lanfang and the Nationalization of Peking Opera, 1912–
1930” (1999b).

24. Yang Yi 1993, 335. The concept of Haipai, or Shanghai-style, culture is elusive and
difﬁcult to deﬁne, and much has been said about it since the 1930s when some Beijing-
based elite writers led by Shen Congwen criticized the commercialization of Shanghai
writers such as Shao Xunmei, Mu Shiying, and Shi Zhecun, labeling them “Haipai” writ-
ers. The term then became a pejorative description of things and styles of commercial-
ized Shanghai until after the Cultural Revolution. In recent decades, with the rapid economic
development of the city, the term “Haipai” has gained more prestige than it ever enjoyed,
and a few serious scholarly works appeared to rectify Shanghai-style culture. For recent
discussions on Shanghai-style literature and the Beijing-Shanghai debate in the 1930s, see
Wu Fuhui 1995. Hanchao Lu also brieﬂy discusses Shanghai style and its commercial, mod-
ern characteristics in comparison with “Beijing style” (Jingpai) as a more conservative, hin-
terland culture of China. See Hanchao Lu 1995. Other important works on the topic include
Yang Dongping 1994; Yang Yi 1993; and Zhang Gansheng 1991.

48. While Zhang Ailing created a variety of female characters in her best-selling ﬁction,

men’s attitudes, food, housing, and travel. In 1944, Miscellany (Zazhi), a best-selling lit-

280 Notes to Pages 23–30

=== PDF PAGE 302 ===

and their histories in the past two decades, including Wang Lin’s work on ping opera in
Tianjin (Wang Lin 1991), the work on Hebei clapper opera by Ma Longwen and Mao
Dazhi (Ma and Mao 1982), Zeng Yongyi’s work on boy-singers’ opera in Taiwan and
Fujian (Zeng 1988), and Luo Di’s work on opera and Zhejiang (Luo Di 1991). Japanese
and Western scholarship on Chinese opera and opera history has focused largely on Bei-
jing opera and paid very little attention to other local operas and even less to folk operas.
Except for a small number of articles and chapters, I am not aware of substantial research
on the history of minor operas and local major operas other than Beijing opera outside
Mainland China and Taiwan. Drawing on the example of Shengxian little opera, the fore-
runner of Yue opera, and an extensive reading of scholarly works in Chinese on various
minor operas, I offer an overview of the origins and growth of minor operas in the late
nineteenth and early twentieth centuries.

65. Early versions of the play were based on the Buddhist idea of retribution and rein-
carnation, a framework inherited from oral traditions within folk culture. Liang Shanbo
and Zhu Yingtai, as the play and other folk traditions had it, were in fact reincarnations
of Golden Boy and Jade Girl, who served the Jade Emperor in his heavenly court. The
two had fallen in love with each other at a party given by the Heavenly Empress (Wangmu
Niangniang) and had accidentally broken the Glazed Cup. The angry Jade Emperor pun-
ished them by sending them down to suffer three lives on earth (in some versions, they
have seven lives), separated from each other and devastated by lovesickness. According
to this framework, Liang Shanbo and Zhu Yingtai were the last incarnations of Golden
Boy and Jade Girl. After Zhu Yingtai and Ma Wencai jump into Liang Shanbo’s grave,
the three take their dispute to the court of the King of the Underworld (Yanluo Wang).
Checking his Book of Life and Death (Shengsi bu), the king discovers the true identities of
Liang and Zhu and sends them to the Jade Emperor, as they have now completed their
three incarnations on earth. Ma Wencai is sent back to earth, where he happily marries
his predestined bride. Liuyin ji [1930s?]; and Liang Shanbo baojuan 1924. Also see Ding Yi
1992b.

6. Even getting married could hurt an actress’s popularity. Several retired Yue opera
284 Notes to Pages 54–63

=== PDF PAGE 306 ===

actresses I interviewed in Shanghai in 1995 and 1996 told me that fans did not like their
favorite actresses to be married or have affairs with men, and the careers of many well-
known actresses went downhill after they married. There are also many whispers about
homosexual relationships among Yue opera practitioners and audience members, and those
affairs also hurt the actresses involved. In these cases, it seems that what mattered was
the fact that an actress was taken away from the public by an individual.

and night beside the Great Wall for forty-nine days. Her endless tears ﬂowed like a river
and eventually brought down the Wall.

41. Fan Li and Wen Zhong are both pseudonyms, pronounced the same as the names

3. Patrons and Patronage
1. My take on the Shanghai middle class is in agreement with that of such scholars
as Wen-hsin Yeh and Hanchao Lu. Yeh discussed the “petty urbanites” as the middle and
lower-middle class in Republican Shanghai, while Lu regarded as middle class all the res-
idents of shikumen, the three-story townhouses built on narrow lanes that led off the main
roads. Indeed, the majority of Yue opera audience members were “petty urbanites” liv-
ing mostly in such houses regardless of their occupation and gender. See Wen-hsin Yeh
1992; and Hanchao Lu 1999.

29. Shanghai Yueju zhi 1995, vol. 2: 104–5. Shaoxingxi bao, February 2, 1941.

32. Republican Shanghai’s factory workers represented a new industrial class that
enjoyed relatively stable wages and generally earned more than junior white-collar work-
ers and clerks as well as lower-class laborers and domestic servants. Many of them, as
Haochao Lu argued, earned wages that easily placed them in the ranks of the lower to
middle class. See Hanchao Lu 1999. 
33. Zhu, Hu, and Xu 1939. According to Zhang Qi, a respected authority on the
communist labor movement, the Chinese Communist Party sponsored the book project.
Party and labor movement leaders in Shanghai, including Liu Changsheng, Liu Ningyi,
Ma Chungu, and Zhang Qi, organized the investigations and the writing of the book;
the communist historian Gu Zhun conducted the ﬁnal editing. Zhu Bangxing, Hu Lin’ge,
and Xu Sheng were ﬁctitious names, and the Yuandong Publishing House in Hong Kong
was simply a fabrication. The book was actually printed in Shanghai. Chapters were writ-
ten by communist labor movement cadres who penetrated various industries as ordinary
workers and gained ﬁrsthand knowledge of the conditions. The project’s purpose was to
provide the CCP with data on which it would develop its labor movement guidelines.
See Zhang Qi 1984.

and ¥0.131 for each chi (1 chi = 1.0936 feet) of cloth. Shanghai jiefang qianhou wujia ziliao
huibian 1958, 89. These statistics and those that follow cannot be taken as absolutely accu-
rate, given the severe inﬂation of the late 1930s and possible errors in the sources, which
are almost impossible to verify. The numbers, however, do provide a general sense of
the economics of viewing opera.

42.Yue ou1939, vol. 4 (December). Also, according to Xiao-Bai Yumei, who performed
at the New World during the late 1930s, Yue opera was one of the most popular local
operas performed there. Within the small theater where Yue opera was staged, several
rows of benches were placed in front of the stage with a rope closing off the area. Spec-
tators paid an entrance fee of twenty-ﬁve cents at the door. Those who paid another
forty cents to the Yue opera company were seated on the benches and served tea and a
hot-pressed towel, while those who chose to stand and view the performance from behind
the closed area did not have to pay the extra fee. Yue opera usually had afternoon shows

Notes to Pages 117–125    291

=== PDF PAGE 313 ===

lost interest in married actresses, as if they were then no longer available. For reasons
discussed in this chapter and in greater detail in chapter 2, married actresses inevitably
lost their popularity. Many popular actresses thus chose to remain single until their early
thirties, very late given the marriage conventions of the time.
67 . Wei Shaochang, whose parents were patrons of Yao Shuijuan, attributed Yao’s rise
in part to this circumstance: “The fact that there were only actresses backstage facili-
tated the development of adoptive mother and sworn sister relationships between
actresses and rich patrons.” Wei 1982, 95.
68. Adoptive family relations—such as adoptive mother, father, brother, and sister—
were commonly known in traditional Chinese society. In northern China, an adoptive
mother was dubbed gan ma and an adoptive father was called gan die; in the Yangzi River
delta, the terms were guofang xiang for an adoptive mother and guofang ye for an adoptive
father. This practice was especially popular among gangsters and underworld organiza-
tions in modern periods. The women’s Yue opera practice may be seen as an indication
of the circle’s involvement with gangsters, an inevitable and frequent phenomenon for
lowbrow entertainment genres of the time. But just as adoptive relationships were not
unknown among the general population, adoptive relationships in women’s Yue opera
did not necessarily have gangster connections.

23. Shijie chenbao, May 8, 1946. The 1946 production of the Yue opera Sister Xianglin
adds the thread of a love story between Sister Xianglin and the son of the Lu family, Ah
Niu. The two are childhood sweethearts, but class difference and the arranged marriage
system make it impossible for them to be married. As discussed in chapter 2, it was a
common practice of the New Yue Opera reforms to add a love story to patriotic or socially
meaningful plays so as to cater to the tastes of the Yue opera audience. Yuan Xuefen and
Nan Wei consulted with Xu Guangping, and a supportive Xu gave her consent to this
addition. Left-wing intellectuals such as Tian Han also expressed understanding of the
necessity of this addition. All agreed, however, that the addition was trivial at best and
294 Notes to Pages 139–148

=== PDF PAGE 316 ===

distorted the spirit of Lu Xun’s original story at worst. In subsequent revisions and restag-

7. Conclusion
1. Shanghai’s settlers included people from foreign countries. Although this group was
demographically insigniﬁcant, its social and cultural inﬂuence should not be overlooked. 
2. Chinese women who were educated in the elite rhetoric of women’s liberation and
gender equality tended to have these kinds of views. 
3. See Jin Jiang 2001; and Zheng Wang 2001. 
4. Yang Hua, “Qingbo de Yueju” (Frivolous Yue opera). Available at http://www.xys.org
(posted May 2002). There are many obvious factual errors in this short Internet piece,
indicating that the author is not an opera specialist. 
5. Catherine Yeh’s study on Shanghai courtesans at the turn of the twentieth century
provides another illustration of how public women and their performance of love dra-
mas (both in real life and in role-play games) helped redeﬁne a modern culture of love
while assigning more favorable roles to their profession and to themselves within this
culture. See Catherine Yeh 2006. 
6. Butler 1993, 10. 
7 . Levenson wrote: “Intonation matters, in English as well as in Chinese. We may
describe an item in the historical record as historically (really) signiﬁcant, or as (merely)
historically signiﬁcant. The distinction is between an empirical judgment of fruitfulness
in time and a normative judgment of aridity in the here and now” (Levenson 1958, 85).
